最后我还想指出：一切伟大的文学，对于向它学习的那点灵明，都是温柔的。它以*领会*（apprehension）施教，而不以*理解*（comprehension）施教——后者正是许多哲学家力图做到的事，而他们在尝试中打碎了瓦罐，泼洒了其中的东西。文学懂得人，懂得人的所能。哲学呢，或许并不「像愚钝的蠢人所设想的那样刻薄乖张」，但它大多数从业者的毛病，在于力图去*理解*（comprehend）宇宙。而那能够理解宇宙的人，*ipso facto*（由此事实）也就理解了上帝，并且 *ipso facto* 成了一位超神，有能力废黜上帝，而且怀着智性自负的傲慢，十足准备把这一企图付诸实行。

## Lecture III Children’s Reading (I) / 第三讲 儿童的阅读（上）

===Original===
Wednesday, January 24, 1917

I have often wished, Gentlemen, that some more winning name could be found for the thing we call Education; and I have sometimes thought wistfully that, had we made a better thing of it, we should long ago have found a more amiable, a blither, name.

For after all it concerns the child; and is it quite an accident that, weaning him away from lovely things that so lovelily call themselves “love,” “home,” “mother,” we can find no more alluring titles for the streets into which we entrap him than “Educational Facilities,” “Local Examinations,” “Preceptors,” “Pedagogues,” “Professors,” “Matriculations,” “Certificates,” “Diplomas,” “Seminaries,” “Elementary or Primary, and Secondary Codes,” “Continuation Classes,” “Reformatories,” “Inspectors,” “Local Authorities,” “Provided” and “Non-Provided,” “Denominational” and “Undenominational,” and “D.Litt.” and “Mus. Bac.”? Expressive terms, no doubt!—but I ask with the poet

> Who can track
> A Grace’s naked foot amid them all?

===Chinese===
1917 年 1 月 24 日，星期三

诸位先生，我常常希望，能为我们称之为「教育」的这桩事找到一个更动人的名称；我有时也怅然暗想，倘若我们把它办得更好一些，恐怕早已找到一个更可亲、更欢悦的名字了。

说到底，这桩事关乎儿童；我们断掉他对那些可爱事物的眷恋——那些事物以何等可爱的名字称呼自己：「爱」、「家」、「母亲」——把他诱进我们设下的街衢，却只拿得出这些名目来作招牌：「教育设施」、「地方考试」、「塾师」、「教书先生」、「教授」、「入学试」、「证书」、「文凭」、「学堂」、「初等或小学与中等规程」、「补习班」、「感化院」、「督学」、「地方当局」、「官设」与「非官设」、「教派」与「无教派」，乃至「文学博士」与「音乐学士」——难道这全然出于偶然？这些字眼，无疑个个传神！——但我愿随诗人一问：

> 在这一切中间，谁能追踪
> 一位美惠女神赤裸的足迹？

===Original===
Take even such words as should be perennially beautiful by connotation—words such as “Academy,” “Museum.” Does the one (O, “Ode on a Distant Prospect of Clapham Academy!”) call up visions of that green lawn by Cephissus, of its olives and plane trees and the mirrored statues among which Plato walked and held discourse with his few? Does the other as a rule invite to haunts (O God! O Montreal!) where you can be secure of communion with Apollo and the Nine? Answer if the word “Academy” does not first call up to the mind some place where small boys are crammed, the word “Museum” some place where bigger game are stuffed?

And yet “academy,” “museum,” even “education” are sound words if only we would make the things correspond with their meanings. The meaning of “education” is a leading out, a drawing-forth; not an *imposition* of something on somebody—a catechism or an uncle—upon the child; but an eliciting of what is within him. Now, if you followed my last lecture, we find that which is within him to be no less, potentially, than the Kingdom of God.

I grant that this potentiality is, between the ages of four and sixteen, not always, perhaps not often, evident. The boy—in Bagehot’s phrase “the small apple-eating urchin whom we know”—has this in common with the fruit for which he congenitally sins, that his very virtues in immaturity are apt, setting the teeth on edge, to be mistaken for vices. A writer, to whom I shall recur, has said:

===Chinese===
就连那些凭内涵理应四时常美的词——比如「学院」（Academy）、「博物馆」（Museum）——也遭同一劫数。说起前者（啊，「克拉珀姆学院远眺」的颂歌！），可曾唤起刻菲索斯河畔那片青青草地的景象，唤起那里的橄榄树与悬铃木，唤起柏拉图在其间踱步、与寥寥数友晤谈的一尊尊映水雕像？说起后者，它通常可曾引人想起（上帝啊！蒙特利尔啊！）那些你能确信与阿波罗和九位缪斯相通的处所？请回答：倘若「学院」一词首先浮上心头的不是填塞小男孩的地方，「博物馆」一词首先浮上心头的不是填塞更大猎物的地方，那才怪呢。

然而「学院」「博物馆」，乃至「教育」，原都是健全的词，只消我们肯让事物与词义相符。「教育」的本义是引出，是导出；不是把某种东西——一本要理问答，或一位叔父——*强加*于儿童身上，而是把他内在的东西诱发出来。而你们若跟上了我上一讲的思路，便会发现他内在的东西，就潜能而言，无异于上帝之国。

我承认，这份潜能在四岁到十六岁之间并不总是显山露水，或许也不常显露。这孩子——用白芝浩（Bagehot）的话说，「那个我们熟悉的吃苹果的小顽童」——与他生来就为之犯罪的那种果实有一处相通：未熟时期的德性往往酸得倒牙，容易被错当作恶行。有一位作家——下文我还要提到他——说过：

===Original===
> If an Englishman who had never before tasted an apple were to eat one in July, he would probably come to the conclusion that it was a hard, sour, indigestible fruit, “conceived in sin and shapen in iniquity,” fit only to be consigned to perdition (on a dust heap or elsewhere). But if the same man were to wait till October and then eat an apple from the same tree, he would find that the sourness had ripened into wholesome and refreshing acidity; the hardness into firmness of fibre which, besides being pleasant to the palate, makes the apple keep better than any other fruit; the indigestibility into certain valuable dietetic qualities, and so on. …

In other words—trench, manure, hoe and water around your young tree, and patiently allow the young fruit to develop of its own juice from the root; your own task being, as the fruit forms, but to bring in all you can of air and sunshine upon it. It must, as every mother and nurse knows, be coaxed to realise itself, to develop, to grow from its individual root. It may be coaxed and trained. But the main secret lies in encouraging it to grow, and, to that end, in pouring sunshine upon it and hoeing after each visitation of tears parentally induced.

Every child wants to grow. Every child wants to learn. During his first year or so of life he fights for bodily nutriment, almost ferociously. From the age of two or thereabouts he valiantly essays the conquest of articulate speech, using it first to identify his father or his mother amid the common herd of Gentiles; next, to demand a more liberal and varied dietary; anon, as handmaid of his imperious will to learn. This desire, still in the nursery, climbs—like dissolution in Wordsworth’s sonnet—from low to high: from a craving to discover experimentally what the stomach will assimilate and what reject, up to a kingly debonair interest in teleology. Our young gentleman is perfectly at ease in Zion. He wants to know why soldiers are (or were) red, and if they were born so; whence bread and milk is derived, and would it be good manners to thank the neat cow for both; why mamma married papa, and—that having been explained and thoughtfully accepted as the best possible arrangement—still thoughtfully, not in the least censoriously, “why the All-Father has not married yet?” He falls asleep weighing the eligibility of various spinsters, church-workers, in the parish.

===Chinese===
> 假如一个从未尝过苹果滋味的英国人在七月里吃上一个苹果，他多半会断定这是一种又硬又酸、难以消化的果实，「于罪中受孕，在孽里成形」，只配打发去受永罚（扔上垃圾堆，或扔到别处）。可假如同一个人等到十月，再吃同一棵树上的苹果，他就会发现：那股酸味已经熟成有益而爽口的酸；那份坚硬已经熟成紧实的果肉纤维——不仅可口，还使苹果比任何别的果实都耐存放；那份难消化已经熟成某些可贵的滋养品质，如此等等。……

换言之——给你的小树松土、施肥、锄草、浇水，耐心地让幼果凭自身的汁液、从自己的根部长成；而你的本分，在果实成形期间，不过是把空气和阳光尽量多地引到它身上。正如每一位母亲和保姆都晓得的，必须哄劝它实现自身、发育起来、从它独有的根上生长。哄劝与训练都使得；但主要的秘诀在于鼓励它生长；为此，就要把阳光倾注在它身上，并在每一次父母招惹出的啼哭过后锄土中耕。

每个孩子都想生长，每个孩子都想学习。生命头一年光景，他为自己的身体养分而搏，几乎带着凶劲。打两岁上下起，他便奋勇去征服清晰的语言：先用它从芸芸「外邦人」里认出谁是他的父亲或母亲；继而下令要更丰盛、更多样的伙食；再往后，这语言便充当他专横求知意志的侍女。这愿望还在育婴室里，便已——像华兹华斯一首十四行诗里的「消融」那样——自低处攀向高处：从想用亲口实验弄清肠胃肯吸收什么、拒绝什么的渴望，一直升到对万物目的的一种王者般雍容的兴趣。我们的小少爷在锡安安逸自在。他想知道当兵的为什么是（或曾经是）红的，是不是生来就红；面包和牛奶从何而来，把两样都记在那头体面的母牛账上、向它道一声谢是否才算知礼；妈妈为什么嫁给爸爸——待这件事得到解释并被郑重接受为最妥当的安排之后——又依然郑重其事、绝无讥评地追问：「那天上的父怎么还没有娶亲呢？」他入睡时还在掂量，教区里各位待字闺中、替教会办事的女善人，哪一位最合适。

===Original===
His brain teeming with questions, he asks them of impulse and makes his discoveries with joy. He passes to a school, which is supposed to exist for the purpose of answering these or cognate questions even before he asks them: and behold, he is not happy! Or, he is happy enough at play, or at doing in class the things that should not be done in class: his master writes home that he suffers in his school work “from having always more animal spirits than are required for his immediate purposes.” What is the trouble? You cannot explain it by homesickness: for it attacks day boys alike with boarders. You cannot explain it by saying that all true learning involves “drudgery,” unless you make that miserable word a mendicant and force it to beg the question. “Drudgery” is *what you feel to be drudgery*—

> Who sweeps a room, as for thy laws,
> Makes that and th’ action fine.

—and, anyhow, this child learned one language—English, a most difficult one—eagerly. Of the nursery through which I passed only one sister wept while learning to read, and that was over a scholastic work entitled *Reading Without Tears*.

===Chinese===
他满脑子是问题，兴之所至便问，有所发现便满心欢喜。随后他被送进一所学校，据说这学校存在的目的，正在于这些问题或同类问题还来不及问出口就先替他解答了：可看哪，他不快乐！或者说，玩耍时他还算快乐，在课堂上做那些不该在课堂上做的事时也够快乐；他的老师却写信回家，说这孩子功课上不去，「因为身上的活泼劲儿总比眼前的用处所需的多出许多」。毛病出在哪里？你不能拿想家来解释：因为走读生和寄宿生害的是同一路病。你也不能说一切真学问都免不了「苦功」，除非你让这个可怜巴巴的词扮成乞丐，逼它去乞题。「苦功」者，*你感到是苦功的东西才是苦功*——

> 谁若扫一间屋子，如遵你的法度，
> 屋子与这桩事功便都精美。

——况且，无论如何，这孩子学过一门语言——英语，一门顶难的语言——而且学得兴致勃勃。在我自己经过的那间育婴室里，学认字时哭过的姊妹只有一位，而她哭，是为了一部题为《读书不哭》（Reading Without Tears）的课业读本。

===Original===
Do you know a chapter in Mr. William Canton’s book *The Invisible Playmate* in which, as Carlyle dealt in *Sartor Resartus* with an imaginary treatise by an imaginary Herr Teufelsdröckh, as Matthew Arnold in “Friendship’s Garland” with the imaginary letters of an imaginary Arminius (Germany in long-past happier days lent the world these playful philosophical spirits), so the later author invents an old village grandpapa, with the grandpapa-name of Altegans and a prose-poem printed in scarecrow duodecimo on paper-bag pages and entitled “Erster Schulgang,” “first school-going,” or “first day at school”?

> The poem opens with a wonderful vision of children; delightful as it is unexpected; as romantic in presentment as it is commonplace in fact. All over the world—and all under it too, when their time comes—the children are trooping to school. The great globe swings round out of the dark into the sun; there is always morning somewhere; and forever in this shifting region of the morning-light the good Altegans sees the little ones afoot—shining companies and groups, couples and bright solitary figures; for they all seem to have a soft heavenly light about them.
> He sees them in country lanes and rustic villages; on lonely moorlands … he sees them on the hillsides … in the woods, on the stepping-stones that cross the brook in the glen, along the seacliffs and on the water-ribbed sands; trespassing on the railway lines, making shortcuts through the corn, sitting in the ferryboats; he sees them in the crowded streets of smoky cities, in small rocky islands, in places far inland where the sea is known only as a strange tradition.
> The morning-side of the planet is alive with them: one hears their pattering footsteps everywhere. And as the vast continents sweep “eastering out of the high shadow which reaches beyond the moon” … and as new nations with *their* cities and villages, their fields, woods, mountains and seashores, rise up into the morning-side, lo! fresh troops, and still fresh troops, and yet again fresh troops of these school-going children of the dawn.
> What are weather and season to this incessant panorama of childhood? The pygmy people trudge through the snow on moor and hillside; wade down flooded roads; are not to be daunted by wind or rain, frost or the white smother of “millers and bakers at fisticuffs.” Most beautiful picture of all, he sees them travelling schoolward by the late moonlight which now and again in the winter months precedes the tardy dawn.

===Chinese===
威廉·坎顿（William Canton）先生的书《看不见的游伴》（The Invisible Playmate）里有一章，你们可知道？那一章里，正如卡莱尔在《旧衣新裁》（Sartor Resartus）中借一位虚构的特弗尔斯德罗克（Herr Teufelsdröckh）先生的虚构论著立说，正如马修·阿诺德在《友谊的花环》（Friendship’s Garland）中借一位虚构的阿米尼乌斯（Arminius）的虚构书信行文（在早已逝去的较幸福年月里，德国曾把这类好作哲学游戏的精灵借给世界），这位较晚的作者也杜撰出一位乡村老祖父，顶着阿尔特甘斯（Altegans）这个一副祖父派头的名字，作下一篇散文诗，印成稻草人似的三十二开小册，纸张粗劣如包装纸，题为《开学首日》（Erster Schulgang），意即「头一回上学」或「开学的第一天」。

> 这首诗开篇便是一幅关于孩子的奇美异象；出乎意料，也令人欢喜；事实本属寻常，呈现却极浪漫。全世界——连同世界底下、时辰一到的那一部分——孩子们正成群结队赶往学校。大地球从黑暗转入阳光中旋转；总有什么地方正当清晨；而在这片晨光流转的地域里，善良的阿尔特甘斯永远看得见那些上路的小小的身影——光辉的队列与三五成群，双双对对与明亮的独行客；因为他们身上仿佛都笼着一层柔和的天光。
> 他看见他们在乡间小路和质朴的村落里；在孤寂的荒原上……他看见他们在山坡上……在林间，在峡谷里横渡小溪的踏脚石上，沿海崖，在波纹如织的沙岸上；他们越过了铁道线，抄近路穿过麦田，坐在渡船里；他看见他们在烟气都市拥挤的街衢，在小小的岩石岛上，在远离海岸、只把海当作一个离奇传闻的内陆地方。
> 这颗行星的清晨一侧，被他们搅得生机勃勃：他们啪嗒啪嗒的脚步声无处不闻。而当浩大的陆块扫过「那道远及月外的高影，向着东方涌出」……而当新的民族带着*他们自己的*城市与村落、田野、森林、山峦与海滨升入清晨一侧，看哪！新的一队，又一队新来的，再来一队新来的，这些黎明时分赶路上学的孩子。
> 在这场童年不停歇的活动画卷面前，天气与节令算得了什么？这些小小的人儿在荒原上、山坡上踏雪跋涉；蹚过被水淹的道路；风雨霜冻，或是「磨坊主与面包师挥拳相斗」扬起的那片白茫茫，都吓不退他们。最美的一幅画是：他看见他们在冬月里偶尔先于迟来黎明的那种残月光下，赶路去上学。

===Original===
That vision strikes me as being poetically true as well as delightful: by which I mean that it is not sentimental: we know that it ought to be true, that in a world well-ordered according to our best wishes for it, it would be *naturally* true. It expresses the natural love of Age, brooding on the natural eager joy of children. But that natural eager joy is just what our schools, in the matter of reading, conscientiously kill.

In this matter of reading—of children’s reading—we stand, just now, or halt just now, between two ways. The parent, I believe, has decisively won back to the right one which good mothers never quite forsook. There was an interval, lasting from the early years of the last century until midway in Queen Victoria’s reign and a little beyond, when children were mainly brought up on the assumption of natural vice. They might adore father and mother, and yearn to be better friends with papa: but there was the old Adam, a quickening evil spirit; there were his imps always in the way, confound them! I myself lived, with excellent grandparents, for several years on pretty close terms with Hell and an all-seeing Eye; until I grew so utterly weary of both that I have never since had the smallest use for either. Some of you may have read, as a curious book, the agreeable history called *The Fairchild Family*, in which Mr. Fairchild leads his naughty children afield to a gallows by a crossroad and seating them under the swinging corpse of a malefactor, deduces how easily they may come to this if they go on as they have been going. The authors of such monitory or cautionary tales understood but one form of development, the development of Original Sin. You stole a pin and proceeded, by fatal steps, to the penitentiary; you threw a stick at a pheasant, turned poacher, shot a gamekeeper and ended on the gallows. You were always Eric and it was always Little by Little with you. … Stay! memory preserves one gem from a Sunday school dialogue, one sharp-cut intaglio of childhood springing fully armed from the head of Satan:

===Chinese===
这一异象在我看来既可喜，又自有诗意的真实：我的意思是，它并非感伤之作。我们知道它理应是真的；在一个按我们对它的最好祝愿而善加安排的世界里，它本会*自然而然*成为真实。它道出了上了年岁的人天性的慈爱，对着孩子们天性的热切欢欣冥想低回。而我们的学校在阅读这件事上兢兢业业加以扼杀的，恰恰就是这种天性的热切欢欣。

在阅读这件事上——在儿童的阅读上——我们此刻正站在两条道路之间，或者说此刻正驻足于两条道路之间。我相信，做父母的已断然回归了那条正路，而善良的母亲们从来不曾完全离弃它。中间有过一段间隔——从上个世纪早年起，直到维多利亚女王在位中期及其后一小段——那时的儿童大抵是在一个假设之下养育成人的：天性本恶。孩子们或许崇拜父母，渴望同爸爸交成更好的朋友；可总有一个老亚当，一个蠢蠢欲动的邪灵；总有小鬼们成心捣乱，见他们的鬼！我自己就同极好的祖父母住在一起，有几年和地狱、和一只全视之眼过从颇密；直到我对两者厌倦透顶，此后对哪一个也再无半点需求。你们中间或许有人读过一部有趣的书，那部讨人喜欢的《费尔柴尔德一家》（The Fairchild Family）：书里费尔柴尔德先生领着他那几个淘气孩子到野外去，走到岔路口的一座绞架下，让他们在一个罪犯荡着的尸首底下坐定，然后演绎道：倘若照老样子走下去，他们要落到这一步是何等容易。这类训诫故事或警惕故事的作者们，只认得一种发育的形式，就是原罪的发育。你偷了一枚别针，便循着命定的台阶走向感化院；你朝一只雉鸡丢了根棍子，便做起偷猎营生，开枪打了猎场看守，末了上了绞架。你永远是那部《埃里克》（Eric），对你而言一切也总是「一点一点」（Little by Little）地沉沦。……且慢！记忆里还存着一颗主日学对话中的宝石，一枚刀法峻利的凹雕：一个童年形象全副披挂，从撒旦的头颅里一跃而出——

===Original===
> Q. Where hast thou been this Sabbath morning?
> A. I have been coursing of the squirrel.
> Q. Art not afraid so to desecrate the Lord’s Day with idle sport?
> A. By no means: for I should tell you that I am an Atheist.

I forget what happened to that boy: but doubtless it was, as it should have been, something drastic.

The spell of prohibition, of repression, lies so strong upon these authors that when they try to break away from it, to appeal to something better than fear in the child, and essay to amuse, they become merely silly. For an example in verse:

> If Human Beings only knew
>
> What sorrows little birds go through,
>
> I think that even boys
>
> Would never think it sport or fun
>
> To stand and fire a frightful gun
>
> For nothing but the noise.

===Chinese===
> 问：这个安息日的早晨，你到哪里去了？
> 答：我去追猎松鼠来着。
> 问：你拿无聊的嬉戏这样亵渎主日，难道不怕么？
> 答：毫不害怕：因为我该告诉你，我是个无神论者。

那孩子后来遭了什么，我记不得了；但无疑正如应有的下场，总归是些严厉手段。

禁止与压制的咒语把这些作者缚得这样紧，以致他们试图挣脱、试图向儿童心中唤起比恐惧更好的东西、试着逗趣的时候，便只剩下满纸傻气。举一首韵文为例：

> 倘若人类真个知道
>
> 小小的鸟儿历多少烦恼，
>
> 我想连那些男孩
>
> 也决不会觉得嬉戏有趣，
>
> 站着放一杆可怕的枪，
>
> 只为听那一声响。

===Original===
For another (instructional and quite a good *memoria technica* so far as it goes):

> William and Mary came next to the throne:
>
> When Mary died, there was William alone.

Now for a story of incident.—It comes from the book *Reading Without Tears*, that made my small sister weep. She did not weep over the story, because she did not claim to be an angel.

> Did you ever hear of the donkey that went into the sea with the little cart? … A lady drove the cart down to the beach. She had six children with her. Three little ones sat in the cart by her side. Three bigger girls ran before the cart. When they came to the beach the lady and the children got out.

Very good so far. It opens like the story of Nausicaa.3

===Chinese===
再举一例（这一例志在教诲，就其所及而论，倒是一部顶不错的助记宝鉴）：

> 威廉与玛丽接着登上王位：
>
> 玛丽一死，只剩威廉独位。

现在来一段情节故事。——它出自那本曾教我小妹妹哭泣的《读书不哭》。她并没有为这个故事哭，因为她不曾自命为天使。

> 你可曾听说过那头拉着小车下海的驴子？……一位太太赶着小车下到海滩。她带着六个孩子。三个小的坐在车里她身旁。三个大些的女孩在车前跑。到了海滩，太太和孩子们都下了车。

到这里都还不错。这个故事的开头活脱是瑙西卡（Nausicaa）的故事。3

===Original===
> The lady wished the donkey to bathe its legs in the sea, to make it strong and clean. But the donkey did not like to go near the sea. So the lady bound a brown shawl over its eyes, and she bade the big girls lead it close to the waves. Suddenly a big wave rushed to the land. The girls started back to avoid the wave, and they let go the donkey’s rein.
> The donkey was alarmed by the noise the girls made, and it went into the sea, not knowing where it was going because it was not able to see. The girls ran screaming to the lady, crying out, “The donkey is in the sea!”
> There it was, going further and further into the sea, till the cart was hidden by the billows. The donkey sank lower and lower every moment, till no part of it was seen but the ears; for the brown shawl was over its nose and mouth. Now the children began to bawl and to bellow! But no one halloed so loud as the little boy of four. His name was Merty. He feared that the donkey was drowned. …
> Two fishermen were in a boat far away. They said “We hear howls and shrieks on the shore. Perhaps a boy or girl is drowning. Let us go and save him.” So they rowed hard, and they soon came to the poor donkey, and saw its ears peeping out of the sea. The donkey was just going to sink when they lifted it up by the jaws, and seized the bridle and dragged it along. The children on the shore shouted aloud for joy. The donkey with the cart came safe to land. The poor creature was weak and dripping wet. The fishermen unbound its eyes, and said to the lady, “We cannot think how this thing came to be over its eyes.” The lady said she wished she had not bound up its eyes, and she gave the shillings in her purse to the fishermen who had saved her donkey.

===Chinese===
> 太太想让驴子在海里泡泡腿，好叫它又强壮又干净。可是驴子不愿意挨近海。于是太太把一条棕色披巾蒙在它眼睛上，吩咐大女孩们牵它挨近浪头。忽然一个大浪扑上岸来。女孩们慌忙躲浪，松开了驴子的缰绳。
> 女孩们的喊声惊了驴子，它便朝海里走去，什么也看不见，不知道自己要往哪里去。女孩们尖叫着奔到太太跟前，喊道：「驴子下海了！」
> 它就在那里，越走越深，直到小车被波涛吞没。驴子一分一秒地往下沉，到末了只剩两只耳朵露在水面上；因为那条棕色披巾罩住了它的鼻子和嘴。这会儿孩子们嚎啕大喊起来！可谁也没有那个四岁的小男孩喊得响。他叫梅蒂。他怕驴子淹死了。……
> 两个渔夫驾着小船，离得老远。他们说：「我们听见岸上又嚎又尖叫，说不定有男孩或女孩溺水了。我们去救他吧。」于是他们奋力划桨，不多时便来到那头可怜的驴子跟前，看见它的耳朵在海面探头。驴子眼看就要沉下去了，他们托住它的下巴把它抬起，抓住笼头把它拖了走。岸上的孩子们欢喜得高声喊叫。驴子拉着小车平安上岸。这可怜的畜生浑身湿透，软乏无力。渔夫解下它眼睛上的东西，对太太说：「我们实在想不出这东西怎么会蒙在它眼上。」太太说她悔不该蒙它的眼，把钱包里的先令都给了救她驴子的渔夫。

===Original===
Now every child knows that a donkey may change into a Fairy Prince: that is a truth of imagination. But to be polite and say nothing of the lady, every child knows that so donkey would be ass enough to behave as in this narrative. And the good parents who, throughout the later 18th century and the 19th, inflicted this stuff upon children, were sinning against the light. Perrault’s Fairy Tales, and Madame D’Aulnoy’s were to their hand in translations; *Le Cabinet des Fées*, which includes these and M. Galland’s *Arabian Nights* and many another collection of delectable stories, extends on my shelves to forty-one volumes (the last volume appeared during the fury of the French Revolution!). The brothers Grimm published the first volume of their immortal tales in 1812, the second in 1814. A capital selection from them, charmingly rendered, was edited by our Edgar Taylor in 1823; and drew from Sir Walter Scott a letter of which some sentences are worth our pondering.

He writes:

> There is also a sort of wild fairy interest in [these tales] which makes me think them fully better adapted to awaken the imagination and soften the heart of childhood than the good-boy stories which have been in later years composed for them. In the latter case their minds are, as it were, put into the stocks … and the moral always consists in good moral conduct being crowned with temporal success. Truth is, I would not give one tear shed over Little Red Riding Hood for all the benefit to be derived from a hundred histories of Jemmy Goodchild.

===Chinese===
然而每个孩子都知道，一头驴子可以变成一位仙王子：这是想象力的真理。可出于礼貌，那位太太姑置勿论——每个孩子也都知道，故事里这样的驴子要照这番情节行事，真真是一头十足的蠢驴了。而那些好父母——在 18 世纪晚期乃至整个 19 世纪把这类货色硬塞给孩子的——乃是明知故犯，亏负了光照。佩罗（Perrault）的童话、奥努瓦夫人（Madame D’Aulnoy）的童话，早有译本现成在手；收有这两家、又有加朗（Galland）先生《一千零一夜》（Arabian Nights）和许许多多别的可喜故事集的《童话宝库》（Le Cabinet des Fées），在我的书架上足足排到四十一卷（末一卷还在法国大革命最狂暴的日子里问世！）。格林（Grimm）兄弟那不朽的童话，第一卷 1812 年刊行，第二卷 1814 年刊行。从他们书中选出一部精萃、译得可喜的本子，由我们的埃德加·泰勒（Edgar Taylor）在 1823 年编成；并引出了沃尔特·司各特爵士（Sir Walter Scott）的一封信，其中有几句话值得我们细细咀嚼。

他写道：

> （这些故事里）另有一种野性的仙趣，使我觉得，就唤醒儿童的想象、软化儿童的心肠而论，它们比近些年编给孩子们的那些乖孩子故事要合用得多。在后一类故事里，孩子们的心智仿佛被戴上了木枷……而其寓意永远不外乎：品行端正，必得尘世的成功加冕。说实在话，我决不肯拿一滴为小红帽掉的眼泪，去换一百部「乖孩子杰米」故事里能够捞到的一切好处。

===Original===
Few nowadays, I doubt, remember Gammer Grethel. She has been ousted by completer, maybe far better, translations of the Grimms’ *Household Tales*. But turning back, the other day, to the old volume for the old sake’s sake (as we say in the West) I came on the Preface—no child troubles with a Preface—and on these wise words:

> Much might be urged against that too rigid and philosophic (we might rather say, unphilosophic) exclusion of works of fancy and fiction from the libraries of children which is advocated by some. Our imagination is surely as susceptible of improvement by exercise as our judgment or our memory.

And that admirable sentence, Gentlemen, is the real text of my discourse today. I lay no sentimental stress upon Wordsworth’s Ode and its doctrine that “Heaven lies about us in our infancy.” It was, as you know, a favourite doctrine with our Platonists of the 17th century: and critics who trace back the Ode “Intimations of Immortality” to Henry Vaughan’s

===Chinese===
如今，我猜没有几个人还记得《格雷特婆婆》（Gammer Grethel）了。她已被更完整、或许也远更高明的格林《家庭童话集》（Household Tales）译本取而代之。但前几天，我为了怀想的旧谊（在我们西部是这么说的）翻回那册旧书，撞见了它的序言——没有哪个孩子肯费神理会序言——还撞见了这样几句明达的话：

> 有人主张把幻想与虚构的作品逐出儿童的藏书，其说过于苛严，又自命哲学（我们倒不如说，全无哲学），对此尽可以举出许多话来驳难。我们的想象力无疑也同我们的判断力、我们的记忆力一样，可以靠操练而精进。

诸位先生，那句可敬佩的话，才是我今天这篇讲演真正的题旨。华兹华斯的《颂》及其「天堂环照着我们的婴年」之说，我并不想向感伤借力。你们知道，这原是我们 17 世纪柏拉图主义者心爱的学说；批评家们既把《永生的暗示》那首颂上溯到亨利·沃恩（Henry Vaughan）的

===Original===
> Happy those early days, when I
>
> Shined in my Angel-infancy.

might connect it with a dozen passages from authors of that century. Here is one from *Centuries of Meditations* by that poor Welsh parson, Thomas Traherne, whom I quoted to you the other day:

> Those pure and virgin apprehensions I had from the womb, and that divine light wherewith I was born are the best unto this day, wherein I can see the Universe. By the Gift of God they attended me into the world, and by His special favour I remember them till now. … Certainly Adam in Paradise had not more sweet and curious apprehensions of the world, than I when I was a child.

===Chinese===
> 幸福啊那些早岁，那时节
>
> 我在我天使的婴年里放光。

诗行，尽可以拿那个世纪作家笔下的十几段文字与之相连。这里是一段，出自那位可怜的威尔士穷牧师托马斯·特拉赫恩（Thomas Traherne）的《冥想诸世纪》（Centuries of Meditations）——前几天我向你们引过他的话：

> 我从母腹带来的那些纯净无瑕的领会，连同我生来便有的那道神性之光，直到今天依然是我观看宇宙的最好凭依。蒙上帝的恩赐，它们随我入世；蒙祂特殊的眷爱，我至今记得它们。……无疑，亚当在乐园里对世界的领会，也未必比我孩提时更甜美、更精微。

===Original===
And here is another from John Earle’s Character of “A Child” in his *Microcosmography*:

> His father hath writ him as his own little story, wherein he reads those days of his life that he cannot remember; and sighs to see what innocence he has out-liv’d.
> He is the Christian’s example, and the old man’s relapse: the one imitates his pureness, and the other falls into his simplicity. Could he put off his body with his little coat, he had got Eternity without a burden, and exchang’d but one Heaven for another.

===Chinese===
另一段出自约翰·厄尔（John Earle）《小宇宙志》（Microcosmography）中「一个孩子」的性格速写：

> 他的父亲把他写成一部自家的小小故事，他从中读到生命里那些自己记不得的日子，叹息着自己竟已把那般天真活过了头。
> 他是基督徒的典范，也是老人的退化：前者效法他的纯洁，后者跌入他的天真。倘若他能连同那件小衣衫一起脱去肉身，他便得着永生而无须背负重担，不过是从一个天堂换住另一个天堂。

===Original===
Bethinking me again of “the small apple-eating urchin whom we know,” I suspect an amiable fallacy in all this: I doubt if when he scales an apple-bearing tree which is neither his own nor his papa’s he does so under impulse of any conscious yearning back to Hierusalem, his happy home,

> Where trees for evermore bear fruit.

At any rate, I have an orchard, and he has put up many excuses, but never yet that he was recollecting Zion.

Still the doctrine holds affinity with the belief which I firmly hold and tried to explain to you with persuasion last term: that, boy or man, you and I, the microcosms, do—sensibly, half-sensibly, or insensibly—yearn, through what we feel to be best in us, to “join up” with the greater harmony; that by poetry or religion or whatnot we have that within us which craves to be drawn out, “e‑ducated,” and linked up.

===Chinese===
重新想起「那个我们熟悉的吃苹果的小顽童」，我疑心这一番议论里藏着一个可爱的谬见：我怀疑，当他攀上那棵既不属于他自己、也不属于他爸爸的苹果树时，心中可有什么自觉的渴念，向往耶路撒冷，他的快乐家乡——

> 那里树木永远结着果实。

无论如何，我自己就有一座果园，而他支过的托词不在少数，却从没有一回说他在追念锡安。

然而这一学说，同我坚信不疑、上学期曾竭力向你们阐明的一个信念，确有亲缘：那就是，无论身为孩童还是成人，你我这些小宇宙，总要凭着各自自觉为至善的东西，或分明、或半分明、或不分明地渴求与那更大的和谐「接合」；凭着诗，凭着宗教，或凭着别的什么，我们内里总有一样东西渴求被引出、被「e-ducated」（导引出来）、被连缀起来。

===Original===
Now the rule of the nursery in the last century rested on Original Sin, and consequently and quite logically tended not to educate, but to repress. There are no new fairytales of the days when your grandmothers wore crinolines—I know, for I have searched. Mothers and nurses taught the old ones; the Three Bears still found, one after another, that “somebody has been sleeping in my bed”; Fatima continued to call “Sister Anne, do you see anyone coming?” the Wolf to show her teeth under her nightcap and snarl out (O, great moment!) “All the better to eat you with, my dear.” But the Evangelicals held field. Those of our grandfathers and grandmothers who understood joy and must have had fairies for ministers—those of our grandmothers who played croquet through hoop with a bell and practised Cupid’s own sport archery—those of our grandfathers who wore jolly peg-top trousers and Dundreary whiskers, and built the Crystal Palace and drove to the Derby in green-veiled top-hats with Dutch dolls stuck about the brim—*tot circa unum caput tumultuantes deos*—and those splendid uncles who used to descend on the old school in a shower of gold—half-a-sovereign at the very least—all these should have trailed fairies with them in a cloud. But in practice the evangelical parent held the majority, put away all toys but Noah’s Ark on Sundays, and voted the fairies down.

===Chinese===
而在上个世纪，育婴室的规则既奠基于原罪，于是也就在逻辑上顺理成章地倾向于压制而非教育。你们的祖母们还穿裙撑的那些年月，不曾产生过任何新的童话——我知道，因为我搜寻过。母亲和保姆们教的还是老一套：三只熊依然一只接一只地发现「有人睡过我的床」；法蒂玛（Fatima）依然在喊「安娜姐姐，你可看见有人来了？」；狼依然隔着睡帽龇出牙齿，吼出（啊，多么了不起的一刻！）「这正好让我吃掉你呀，我的宝贝。」然而当道的是福音派。我们祖辈中那些懂得欢乐、想必有仙女在他们跟前当差的人——我们祖母中那些玩过带铃铛的槌球拱门、练过丘比特自家运动（射箭）的人——我们祖父中那些穿过快活的陀螺裤、蓄过邓德里式连鬓胡、盖起水晶宫、又戴着插满荷兰娃娃的绿纱大礼帽驱车赴德比赛马的人——*tot circa unum caput tumultuantes deos*（那么多神明围着一个脑袋喧腾）——还有那些气派非凡的叔伯，惯于一头金雨似地降临老学校——一撒至少半金镑——凡此种种，本该叫他们身后云霞里拖着一队仙子才是。可实际上，福音派父母占着多数，星期日收起诺亚方舟以外的一切玩具，投票把仙女否决了。

===Original===
I know not who converted the parents. It may have been that benefactor of Europe, Hans Christian Andersen, born at Odensee in Denmark in April 1805. He died, near Copenhagen, in 1875, having by a few months outlived his 70th birthday. I like to think that his genius, a continuing influence over a long generation, did more than anything else to convert the parents. The schools, always more royalist than the King, professionally bleak, professionally dull, professionally repressive rather than educative, held on to a tradition which, though it had to be on the sly, every intelligent mother and nurse had done her best to evade. The schools made a boy’s life penitential on a system. They discovered athletics, as a safety-valve for high spirits they could not cope with, and promptly made that safety-valve compulsory! They went on to make athletics a religion. Now athletics are not properly a religious exercise, and their meaning evaporates as soon as you enlist them in the service of repression. They are being used to do the exact opposite of that for which God meant them. Things are better now: but in those times how many a boy, having long looked forward to it, rejoiced in his last day at school?

I know surely enough what must be in your minds at this point: I am running up my head hard against the doctrine of Original Sin, against the doctrine that in dealing with a child you are dealing with a “fallen nature,” with a human soul “conceived in sin,” unregenerate except by repression; and therefore that repression and more repression *must* be the only logical way with your Original Sinners.

===Chinese===
我不知道究竟是谁把父母们说服了。或许是那位欧洲的恩人，汉斯·克里斯蒂安·安徒生（Hans Christian Andersen），1805 年 4 月生于丹麦的欧登塞（Odensee）。他 1875 年在哥本哈根近郊去世，比他的 70 岁寿辰多活了不过几个月。我愿意相信，他的天才作为一个绵延一代之久的影响力，在促使父母们回心转意上所做的事，比什么都多。学校呢，向来比国王更保王，把荒凉、沉闷、压制（而非启发）当作本行，死死抱住一种传统；而那种传统，每一位聪明的母亲和保姆尽管只得偷偷行事，却个个竭力规避过。学校把一个男孩的日子按部就班地办成了赎罪的苦修。他们发现了体育运动，拿它当应付不了的高昂精力的安全阀，随即又把这个安全阀定为必修！他们更进而把体育奉为宗教。可体育本来算不得宗教操练；你一旦把它征调去为压制服役，它的意义便立刻蒸发。人们正拿它去做上帝造它之意的恰正反面。如今情形好些了：但在那些年月，有多少男孩盼望了许久，直到离校的最后一天才欢天喜地？

我很清楚此刻你们心里必在转什么念头：我这是在拿脑袋硬撞原罪的学说，硬撞这样一条教义——你对付一个孩子，便是在对付一种「堕落的本性」，对付一个「于罪中受孕」、非压制不得重生的人类灵魂；因而对付你的原罪之子，压制、再压制*必然*是唯一合乎逻辑的路数。

===Original===
Well, then, I am. I have loved children all my life; studied them in the nursery, studied them for years—ten or twelve years intimately—in elementary schools. I know for a surety, if I have acquired any knowledge, that the child is a “child of God” rather than a “Child of wrath”; and here before you I proclaim that to connect in any child’s mind the Book of Joshua with the Gospels, to make its Jehovah identical in that young mind with the Father of Mercy of whom Jesus was the Son, to confuse, as we do in any school in this land between 9:30 and 9:45 a.m., that bloodthirsty tribal deity whom the Hohenzollern family invokes with the true God the Father, is a blasphemous usage, and a curse.

But let me get away to milder heresies. If you will concede for a moment that the better way with a child is to draw out, to *educate*, rather than to repress, what is in him, let us observe what he instinctively wants. Now first, of course, he wants to eat and drink, and to run about. When he passes beyond these merely animal desires to what we may call the instinct of growth in his soul, how does he proceed? I think Mr. Holmes, whom I have already quoted, very fairly sets out these desires as any grown-up person can perceive them. The child desires

===Chinese===
好，我正是在撞，而且明知道。我一辈子疼爱孩子；在育婴室里研究过他们，在小学里研究过他们多年——亲密地研究了十年上下。倘若我总算得了些知识，那么我可以断然知道：孩子是「上帝的孩子」，而不是「震怒之子」；此刻当着你们，我宣布：在任何孩子的心里把《约书亚记》同《福音书》连缀为一，使耶和华在那幼小的心灵里同那位慈悲之父——耶稣是祂的儿子——合为一人，像我国每一所学校在上午 9:30 到 9:45 之间所做的那样，把那位霍亨索伦（Hohenzollern）一家祈求的嗜血部族神同真正的圣父上帝搅作一团，是渎神的行径，是一种诅咒。

且容我退到温和些的异端。你们若肯姑且承认，对待孩子的上策是把他内在的东西引出来、加以*教育*，而不是压制，那么让我们看看他本能上想要什么。头一件自然是要吃要喝、要跑要跳。待他越过这些纯属兽性的欲望，进到我们不妨称作灵魂里生长之本能的境地，他又如何行事？我想，前面已经引用过的霍姆斯先生（Mr. Holmes），把这些愿望摆得相当公道，任何一个成年人都能据此辨认出来。孩子渴望——

===Original===
1. to talk and to listen;
2. to act (in the dramatic sense of the word);
3. to draw, paint and model;
4. to dance and sing;
5. to know the why of things;
6. to construct things.

===Chinese===
1. 说话与聆听；
2. 表演（取这个词的戏剧意义）；
3. 勾画、涂绘与塑形；
4. 舞蹈与歌唱；
5. 知晓事物何以如此；
6. 营造器物。

===Original===
Now I shall have something to say by and by on the amazing preponderance in this list of those instincts which Aristotle would have called “mimetic.” This morning I take only the least imitative of all, the desire to know the why of things.

Surely you know, taking only this, that the master-key admitting a child to all, or almost all, palaces of knowledge is his ability to *read*. When he has grasped that key of his mother-tongue he can with perseverance unlock all doors to all the avenues of knowledge. More—he has the passport to heavens unguessed.

You will perceive at once that what I mean here by “reading” is the capacity for silent reading, taking a book apart and mastering it; and you will bear in mind the wonder that I preached to you in a previous lecture—that great literature never condescends, that what yonder boy in a corner reads of a king is happening to *him*. Do you suppose that in an elementary school one child in ten reads thus? Listen to a wise ex-inspector, whose words I can corroborate of experience:

===Chinese===
这份清单里，那些亚里士多德会称之为「摹仿」的本能占着惊人的优势，对此我下文还有话说。今天上午，我只取其中最不摹仿的一项：想知道事物何以如此的渴望。

单取这一项，你们想必明白：让孩子得以走进一切——或几乎一切——知识宫殿的总钥匙，是他的*阅读*能力。他一旦握住母语这把钥匙，便能凭毅力打开通往一切知识大道的所有门户。还不止此——他更握有去往未曾想见的诸天的护照。

你们即刻会看出，我这里说的「阅读」，是指默读的能力——把一本书拆开来、把它吃透的能力；你们还会记得我在先前一讲里向你们宣讲过的那个奇迹：伟大的文学从不屈尊俯就；那边角落里那孩子读到的一个国王，他所读的事正发生在他自己身上。你们可曾想过，在一所小学里，十个孩子中有一个是这样阅读的吗？且听一位睿智的前督学怎么说——他的话，我可以拿经验来佐证：

===Original===
> The first thing that strikes the visitor who enters an ordinary elementary school while a reading lesson is in progress is that the children are not reading at all, in the accepted sense of the word. They are not reading to themselves, not studying, not mastering the contents of the book, not assimilating the mental and spiritual nutriment that it may be supposed to contain. They are standing up one by one and reading aloud to their teacher.

Ah! but I have seen far worse than that. I have visited and condemned rural schools where the practice was to stand a class up—say a class of thirty children—and make them read in unison: which meant, of course, that the front row chanted out the lesson while the back rows made inarticulate noises. I well remember one such exhibition, in a remote country school on the Cornish hills, and having my attention arrested midway by the face of a girl in the third row. She was a strikingly beautiful child, with that combination of bright auburn, almost flaming, hair with dark eyebrows, dark eyelashes, dark eyes, which of itself arrests your gaze, being so rare; and those eyes seemed to challenge me half scornfully and ask, “Are you really taken in by all this?” Well, I soon stopped the performance and required each child to read separately: whereupon it turned out that, in the upper standards of this school of seventy or eighty children, one only—this disdainful girl—could get through half a dozen easy sentences with credit. She read well and intelligently, being accustomed to read to herself, at home.

===Chinese===
> 一位来访者走进一所普通小学，正值阅读课进行之中，头一样叫他吃惊的事便是：孩子们根本不在阅读——照这个词公认的语义而言。他们不是读给自己，不是在自习，不是在掌握书中的内容，不是在消化那本书里想必含有的心智与精神的养分。他们是一个个站起来，朗读给老师听。

啊！可我见过比这糟得多的。我视察并申斥过多村学校，那里的惯例是叫全班起立——比方说三十个孩子一班——齐声朗读：这自然意味着，前排把课文唱了出来，后排只发出些含混不清的声响。有一场这样的表演我记忆犹新，地点在康沃尔（Cornwall）丘陵间一所偏远的乡村学校：进行到一半，我的注意力叫第三排一个女孩的脸庞攫住了。她是个出奇美丽的孩子，一头明亮的赤褐色、几乎像火焰般的头发，衬着深色的眉、深色的睫毛、深色的眼睛——这样罕见的组合本身就能攫住你的目光；而那双眼睛仿佛半带讥诮地向我发现挑战，问道：「这一切您也真信么？」于是我很快叫停了那场表演，要求每个孩子单独朗读：结果发现，在这所有七八十个孩子的学校里，高年级各班只有一个——就是这个满脸不屑的女孩——能够体体面面地读完五六句浅易的句子。她读得好，也读得懂，因为在家里她惯于自己读书。

===Original===
I daresay that this bad old method of block-reading is dead by this time.

Reading aloud and *separately* is excellent for several purposes. It tests capacity: it teaches correct pronunciation by practice, as well as the mastery of difficult words: it provides a good teacher with frequent opportunities of helping the child to understand what he reads.

But as his schooling proceeds he should be accustomed more and more to read to himself: for that, I repeat, is the master-key.

===Chinese===
我想，这种整班齐读的坏旧法子，到如今总该绝迹了。

分开来的朗读，为好几种目的都是极好的。它测验能力；它借练习教给正确的发音，也教给难词的攻克；它给好的教师提供了频频的机会，去帮助孩子懂得他所读的东西。

但随着学业推进，他应当越来越习惯于读给自己：因为我再说一遍，那才是总钥匙。

## Lecture IV Children’s Reading (II) / 第四讲 儿童的阅读（下）

===Original===
Wednesday, February 21, 1917

I

In our talk, Gentlemen, about Children’s Reading we left off upon a list, drawn up by Mr. Holmes in his book *What Is, and What Might Be*, of the things that, apart from physical nourishment and exercise, a child instinctively desires.
He desires
1. to talk and to listen;
2. to act (in the dramatic sense of the word);
3. to draw, paint and model;
4. to dance and sing;
5. to know the why of things;
6. to construct things.

===Chinese===
1917 年 2 月 21 日，星期三

I

诸位先生，在我们关于儿童的阅读的谈话里，上回是歇在一份清单上——那是霍姆斯（Holmes）先生在他的书《现实与可能》（What Is, and What Might Be）里开列的：除身体的滋养与锻炼之外，一个孩子本能地想要的东西。
他想要
1. 说话与倾听；
2. 表演（就这个词的戏剧意义而言）；
3. 素描、着色与塑形；
4. 跳舞与唱歌；
5. 知道万事的所以然；
6. 造出东西来。

===Original===
Let us scan through this catalogue briefly, in its order.

No. (1). *To talk and to listen*—Mr. Holmes calls this the “communicative instinct.” Every child wants to talk with those about him, or at any rate with his chosen ones—his parents, brothers, sisters, nurse, governess, gardener, boot-boy (if he possess these last)—with other children, even if his dear papa is poor: to tell them what he has been doing, seeing, feeling: and to listen to what they have to tell him.

Nos. (2), (3), (4). *To act*—our author calls this the “dramatic instinct”: *to draw, paint and model*—this the “artistic instinct”—*to dance and sing*—this the “musical instinct.” But obviously all these are what Aristotle would call “mimetic” instincts: “imitative” (in a sense I shall presently explain); even as No. (2)—acting—like No. (1)—talking and listening—comes of craving for sympathy. In fact, as we go on, you will see that these instincts overlap and are not strictly separable, though we separate them just now for convenience.

===Chinese===
让我们按着次序，把这份目录大略检视一遍。

第一项，「说话与倾听」——霍姆斯先生称之为「交往的本能」（communicative instinct）。每个孩子都想同他身边的人说话，至少要同他拣选的那几个人——父母、兄弟、姊妹、保姆、家庭女教师、园丁、擦靴童（倘若后几位他也有的话）——说说话；还想同别的孩子说话，哪怕他亲爱的爸爸是个穷人：要告诉他们他做了什么、看见了什么、感觉到了什么；也要听他们讲给他听的事。

第二、三、四项，「表演」——我们的作者称之为「戏剧的本能」（dramatic instinct）；「素描、着色与塑形」——这是「艺术的本能」（artistic instinct）；「跳舞与唱歌」——这是「音乐的本能」（musical instinct）。但显而易见，这些全是亚里士多德（Aristotle）会称作「摹仿的」（mimetic）本能——即「摹拟的」（imitative，这个字眼我随即解释）；正如第二项——表演——同第一项——说话与倾听——一样，都是出于对同情的渴求。其实往下讲你们就会看到，这些本能互相交叠，严格说来分不开；眼下我们分开来说，只是为了方便。

===Original===
No. (5). *To know the why of things*—the “inquisitive instinct.” This, being the one which gives most trouble to parents, parsons, governesses, conventional schoolmasters—to all grown-up persons who pretend to know what they don’t and are ashamed to tell what they do—is of course the most ruthlessly repressed.

> “The time is come,” the Infant said,
> “To talk of many things:
> Of babies, storks and cabbages
> And—

—having studied the Evangelists’ Window facing the family pew—

> And whether cows have wings.”

===Chinese===
第五项，「知道万事的所以然」——「好问的本能」（inquisitive instinct）。这一项给父母、牧师、家庭女教师、拘守成规的塾师添的麻烦最多——给一切明明不知道偏要装知道、知道了一点又羞于说出的成年人——所以自然是被压制得最狠的。

> 「时候到了，」那婴孩说，
> 「来谈许多的事情：
> 谈谈婴孩、鹳鸟和卷心菜
> 还有——

——他在端详过正对着自家长椅的那扇四福音书作者之窗之后——

> 母牛究竟有没有翅膀。」

===Original===
The answer, in my experience, is invariably stern, and “in the negative”: in tolerant moments compromising on “Wait, like a good boy, and see.”

But we singled out this instinct and discussed it in our last lecture.

No. (6). *To construct things*—the “constructive instinct.” I quote Mr. Holmes here:

> After analysis comes synthesis. The child pulls his toys to pieces in order that he may, if possible, reconstruct them. The ends that he sets before himself are those which Comte set before the human race—*savoir pour prévoir, afin de pouvoir: induire pour déduire, afin de construire.* The desire to make things, to build things up, to control ways and means, to master the resources of nature, to put his knowledge of her laws and facts to practical use, is strong in his soul. Give him a box of bricks, and he will spend hours in building and rebuilding houses, churches. … Set him on a sandy shore with a spade and a pail, and he will spend hours in constructing fortified castles with deep encircling moats.

Again obviously this constructive instinct overlaps with the imitative ones. Construction, for example, enters into the art of making mud-pies and has also been applied in the past to great poetry. If you don’t keep a sharp eye in directing this instinct, it may conceivably end in an *Othello* or in a *Divina Commedia*.

===Chinese===
据我的经验，回答总是疾言厉色，总是「断乎不可」；宽容的时刻则折中一句：「乖孩子，等着，等着瞧。」

不过这一本能，我们上一讲已经单独挑出来讨论过了。

第六项，「造出东西来」——「构造的本能」（constructive instinct）。这里我引霍姆斯先生的原话：

> 分析之后便是综合。孩子把玩具拆散，为的是——若有可能——再把它们装配起来。他给自己立下的目标，正是孔德（Comte）给人类立下的目标——*savoir pour prévoir, afin de pouvoir: induire pour déduire, afin de construire*（求知以为预见，预见以为行动；归纳以为演绎，演绎以为构造）。制作事物的欲望、营造事物的欲望、驾驭门径与手段的欲望、掌握自然的资源、把他关于自然律与自然事实的知识付诸实用的欲望，在他的灵魂里是强烈的。给他一匣积木，他会花上几个钟头把房屋、教堂造了又造。……给他一把铲子、一只桶，放到沙岸上，他会花上几个钟头修筑带深深环壕的要塞城堡。

同样显而易见，这构造的本能也与摹仿的本能交叠。譬如说，做泥饼的艺术里就有构造；而构造在过去也曾施之于伟大的诗。倘若你不留神看好、引导这本能，它没准会以一部《奥瑟罗》（Othello）或一部《神曲》（Divina Commedia）收场。

===Original===
II

Without preaching on any of the others, however, I take three of the six instincts scheduled by Mr. Holmes—the three which you will allow to be almost purely imitative.

They are:
- Acting,
- Drawing, painting, modelling,
- Dancing and singing.

Now let us turn to the very first page of Aristotle’s *Poetics*, and what do we read?

===Chinese===
II

不过，其余几项我不多说教，且从霍姆斯先生开列的六种本能里取出三种来——你们会承认，这三种几乎纯粹是摹仿性的。

它们是：
- 表演，
- 素描、着色与塑形，
- 跳舞与唱歌。

现在让我们翻开亚里士多德《诗学》的第一页，读到的是什么呢？

===Original===
> Epic poetry and tragedy, comedy also and dithyrambic poetry, and the greater part of the music of the flute and of the lyre, are all, in general, modes of imitation. …
> For as there are persons who represent a number of things by colours and drawings, and others vocally, so it is with the arts above mentioned. They all imitate by rhythm, language, harmony, singly or combined.

Even dancing (he goes on)

> imitates character, emotion and action, by rhythmical movement.

Now, having touched on mud-pies, let me say a few words upon these aesthetic imitative instincts of acting, dancing, singing before I follow Aristotle into his explanation of the origin of Poetry, which I think we may agree to be the highest subject of our Art of Reading and to hold promise of its highest reward.

Every wise mother sings or croons to her child and dances him on her knee. She does so by sure instinct, long before the small body can respond or his eyes—always blue at first and unfathomably aged—return her any answer. It lulls him into the long spells of sleep so necessary for his first growth. By and by, when he has found his legs, he begins to skip, and even before he has found articulate speech, to croon for himself. Pass a stage, and you find him importing speech, drama, dance, incantation, into his games with his playmates. Watch a cluster of children as they enact “Here We Go Gathering Nuts in May”—eloquent line: it is just what they are doing!—or “Here Come Three Dukes A-Riding,” or “Fetch a Pail of Water,” or “Sally, Sally Waters”:

> Sally, Sally Waters,
> Sitting in the sand,
> Rise, Sally—rise, Sally,
> For a young man.

===Chinese===
> 史诗和悲剧、喜剧和酒神颂，还有大部分笛乐与琴乐——这一切总的说来都是摹仿的方式。……
> 因为有些人用颜色与图画来摹拟许多事物，另一些人则用声音；上述诸艺也是这样。它们全都借节奏、语言、和声来摹仿，或单用，或合用。

连舞蹈也是（他接着说）

> 借有节奏的动作来摹拟性格、情感与行动。

泥饼的话题既然已经沾到，在我跟随亚里士多德进入他对诗之起源的解释之前——我想我们会同意，这是我们的「阅读的艺术」中最高的题目，并许诺着它最高的报偿——请容我先就表演、舞蹈、歌唱这几种审美的摹仿本能说几句话。

每一位聪明的母亲都会对孩子唱歌、低吟，把他抱在膝上颠动。她这样做是出于确凿的本能，远在那小小的身体能作出回应之前，远在他那双眼睛——初生时总是蓝的，深得不可思议地苍老——能回报她任何回答之前。歌声哄他入睡，进入初次生长最需要的漫长酣眠。不久，等他找到了自己的两条腿，便开始蹦跳；甚至还没找到清晰的语言，就先自个儿哼唱起来。再过一程，你会发现他把语言、戏剧、舞蹈、咒语统统搬进与游伴的游戏里。看一伙孩子扮演「我们来采五月的坚果」（Here We Go Gathering Nuts in May）——好一句传神的歌词：他们干的就是这事！——或「三位公爵骑马来了」（Here Come Three Dukes A-Riding），或「提一桶水来」（Fetch a Pail of Water），或「萨莉，萨莉·沃特斯」（Sally, Sally Waters）：

> 萨莉，萨莉·沃特斯，
> 坐在沙地上，
> 起来，萨莉——起来，萨莉，
> 为着一个年轻人。

===Original===
Suitor presented, accepted [I have noted, by the way, that this game is more popular with girls than with boys]; wedding ceremony hastily performed—so hastily, it were more descriptive to say “taken for granted”—within the circle; the dancers, who join hands and resume the measure, chanting

> Now you are married, we wish you joy—
> First a girl and then a boy

—the order, I suspect, dictated by exigencies of rhyme rather than of Eugenics, as Dryden confessed that a rhyme had often helped him to a thought. And yet I don’t know; for the incantation goes on to redress the balance in a way that looks scientific:

===Chinese===
求爱者呈上了，被接受了[顺带说一句，我留意到这游戏在女孩中间比在男孩中间更流行]；婚礼就在圆圈里匆匆举行——匆忙到与其说「举行」，不如说「视为当然」更传神；于是跳舞的人们手拉起手，重新踏起节拍，嘴里唱着：

> 如今你们成了亲，我们祝你们欢喜——
> 先是女孩，然后是男孩

——这先后次序，我疑心是韵脚的急需所指定的，倒不是优生学的指示；德莱顿（Dryden）就招认过，一个韵常常帮他救出一个思想。可我也说不准；因为这咒语接着唱下去，竟以一副近乎科学的架势来摆平此事：

===Original===
> Ten years after, son and daughter,
> And now—

[Practically!]

> And now, Miss Sally, come out of the water.

The players end by supplying the applause which, in these days of division of labour, is commonly left to the audience.

===Chinese===
> 十年之后，一儿一女，
> 而如今——

[说办就办！]

> 而如今，萨莉小姐，从水里出来吧。

末了，孩子们自己把掌声也供应上了——在这分工的时代，掌声通常是留给观众去做的。

===Original===
III

Well, there you have it all: acting, singing, dancing, choral movement—enlisted ancillary to the domestic drama: and, when you start collecting evidence of these imitative instincts blent in childhood the mass will soon amaze you and leave you no room to be surprised that many learned scholars, on the supposition that uncivilised man is a child more or less—and at least so much of child that one can argue through children’s practice to his—have found the historical origin of Poetry itself in these primitive performances: “communal poetry” as they call it. I propose to discuss with you (may be neat term) in a lecture not belonging to this “course” the likelihood that what we call specifically “the Ballad,” or “Ballad Poetry,” originated thus. Here is a wider question. Did all Poetry develop out of this, historically, as a process in time and in fact? These scholars (among whom I will instance one of the most learned—Dr. Gummere) hold that it did: and I may take a passage from Dr. Gummere’s *Beginnings of Poetry* (p. 95) to show you how they call in the practice of savage races to support their theory. The Botocudos of South America are—according to Dr. Paul Ehrenreich who has observed them4—an ungentlemanly tribe, “very low in the social scale.”

> The Botocudos are little better than a leaderless horde, and pay scant respect to their chieftain; they live only for their immediate bodily needs, and take small thought for the morrow, still less for the past. No traditions, no legends, are abroad to tell them of their forbears. They still use gestures to express feeling and ideas; while the number of words which imitate a given sound “is extraordinarily great.” An action or an object is named by imitating the sound peculiar to it; and sounds are doubled to express greater intensity. … To speak is *aõ*; to speak loudly or to sing, is *aõ-aõ*. And now for their aesthetic life, their song, dance, poetry, as described by this accurate observer. “On festal occasions the whole horde meets by night round the camp fire for a dance. Men and women alternating … form a circle; each dancer lays his arms about the necks of his two neighbours, and the entire ring begins to turn to the right or to the left, while all the dancers stamp strongly and in rhythm the foot that is advanced, and drag after it the other foot. Now with drooping heads they press closer and closer together; now they widen the circle. Throughout the dance resounds a monotonous song to which they stamp their feet. Often one can hear nothing but a continually repeated *kalanî aha!* … Again, however, short improvised songs, in which we are told the doings of the day, the reasons for rejoicing, what not, as ‘Good hunting,’ or ‘Now we have something to eat,’ or ‘Brandy is good.’”

===Chinese===
III

好，这下你们看到全副光景了：表演、歌唱、舞蹈、集体的舞步——全都征调来做了这出家庭戏剧的辅助。而当你们动手收集这些摹仿本能在童年交融为一的证据时，材料的繁多很快会使你们吃惊，使你们再没有余地惊讶于这一点：许多博学的学者——他们假设未开化的人差不多就是个孩子，至少孩子气多到可以从儿童的实践去推论他——竟然从这些原始的表演里找到了诗本身的历史起源：即他们所谓的「社群的诗」（communal poetry）。我打算在一场不属于本「课程」的讲演里，同你们讨论一下（这或许是个妥帖的字眼）：我们所特称的「歌谣」（Ballad）或「歌谣诗」（Ballad Poetry）是否起源于此。这是一个更宽的问题：全部的诗在历史上是否都由此发展而来，作为时间中、事实中的一个过程？这些学者（其中我要举出一位极渊博的——格默尔（Gummere）博士）认为是的。我可以从格默尔博士的《诗的开端》（Beginnings of Poetry）第 95 页引一段话，给你们看看他们怎样召来野蛮种族的习俗支持自己的理论。南美的博托库多人（Botocudos），据观察过他们⁴的保罗·埃伦赖希（Paul Ehrenreich）博士说，是个粗野不文的部族，「在社会等级上很低」。

> 博托库多人几乎不比一群没有首领的乌合之众好些，对他们的酋长也鲜有敬意；他们只为眼下的身体需要活着，不为明天操心，更不为过去。四下没有传说，没有传奇，讲给他们听他们的祖先。他们仍用姿势表达情感与观念；而摹拟某一特定声音的词，「数目多得惊人」。一个动作或一件东西，就靠摹拟它特有的声音来命名；声音的重叠则用来表达更强的强度。……「说」是 *aõ*；大声说或唱，便是 *aõ-aõ*。现在来看他们的审美生活——他们的歌、舞、诗，如这位精细的观察者所描述的：「逢到节庆，整个部落夜里围着营火聚拢来跳舞。男男女女相间……围成一个圆圈；每个舞者伸手搂住左右两邻的脖子，整圈人开始向右或向左转动，所有舞者以踏在前面的那只脚重重地、有节奏地踏地，另一只脚拖在后面。时而他们垂着头越挤越紧；时而又把圆圈放宽。整场舞都回荡着一支单调的歌，他们踏着歌的节拍。往往除了不断重复的一句 *kalanî aha!* 便什么也听不见。……不过有时也有即兴的短歌，把白天的事情、欢庆的缘由讲给你听，什么都有，诸如『打得着猎』，或『如今我们有东西吃了』，或『白兰地真是好』。」

===Original===
“As to the aesthetic value” of these South American utterances, Dr. Gummere asks in a footnote, “how far is it inferior to the sonorous commonplaces of our own verse—say ‘The Psalm of Life?’” I really cannot answer that question. Which do you prefer, Gentlemen?—“Life is real, life is earnest,” or “Now we have something to eat”? I must leave you to settle it with the Food Controller.

The Professor goes on:

> “Now and then, too, an individual begins a song, and is answered by the rest in chorus. … *They never sing without dancing, never dance without singing, and have but one word to express both song and dance.*”
> As the unprejudiced reader sees [Dr. Gummere proceeds] this clear and admirable account confirms the doctrine of early days revived with fresh ethnological evidence in the writings of Dr. Brown and of Adam Smith, that dance, poetry and song were once a single and inseparable function, and is in itself fatal to the idea of rhythmic prose, of solitary recitation, as foundations of poetry. … All poetry is communal, holding fast to the rhythm of consent as to the one sure fact.

===Chinese===
至于这些南美吟咏的「审美价值」，格默尔博士在一个脚注里问道，「比起我们自己的诗那类响亮的老生常谈——比如说《人生颂》（The Psalm of Life）——究竟差到多少呢？」这个问题我可实在答不上来。诸位先生，你们更喜欢哪一个？——是「人生是实在的，人生是认真的」，还是「如今我们有东西吃了」？这只好请你们同食粮管制官去解决了。

这位教授接着写道：

> 「也有时，一个人起头唱一支歌，其余的人便以合唱应和。……*他们从不唱歌而不跳舞，从不跳舞而不唱歌，并且只用一个词来表达歌与舞。*」
> 正如不抱成见的读者所看到的[格默尔接着写道]，这段清楚而可钦佩的记述，证实了一种早期学说——布朗（Brown）博士与亚当·斯密（Adam Smith）的著作以新鲜的人种学证据使之复兴——即舞蹈、诗与歌曾经是单一而不可分的功能；而这段记述本身就足以致「有节奏的散文」与「独人吟诵」这类诗之基础的说法于死命。……一切诗都是社群的，紧紧抓住「应和的节奏」，把它当作唯一可靠的事实。

===Original===
IV

Now I should tell you, Gentlemen, that I hold such utterances as this last—whatever you may think of the utterances of the Botocudos—to be exorbitant: that I distrust all attempts to build up (say) *Paradise Lost* historically from the yells and capers of recondite savages. “Life is real, life is earnest” may be no better aesthetically (I myself think it a little better) than “Now we have something to eat” “Brandy is good” may rival Pindar’s *Ἀριστον μὲν ὐδωρ*, and indeed puts what it contains of truth with more of finality, less of provocation (though Pindar at once follows up *Ἀριστον μὲν ὐδωρ* with exquisite poetry): but you cannot—truly you cannot—exhibit the steps which lead up from “Brandy is good” to such lines as

> Thus with the year
> Seasons return; but not to me returns
> Day, or the sweet approach of even or morn,
> Or sight of vernal bloom, or summer’s rose,
> Or flocks, or herds, or human face divine.

===Chinese===
IV

诸位先生，如今我该告诉你们：这最后一段话——且不管你们怎么看博托库多人的吟咏——依我看是说得过了头。我不信一切想把（比如说）《失乐园》从幽僻野蛮人的呼号与跳跃出发、历史地构建起来的企图。「人生是实在的，人生是认真的」，在审美上也许并不比「如今我们有东西吃了」高明（我自己觉得略高明一点）；「白兰地真是好」或可与品达（Pindar）的 *Ἀριστον μὲν ὐδωρ*（首善莫如水）一争短长，而且它把自身含有的那点真理说得更斩截，更少挑衅（虽然品达紧接着 *Ἀριστον μὲν ὐδωρ* 之后写出的便是精妙的诗行）：但你没法——真的没法——指出从「白兰地真是好」一路上升到这样的诗句的阶梯：

> 如此随着年岁
> 四季轮转；可再不回到我这里来的有
> 白昼，或黄昏或清晨的甜美来临，
> 或春花的容色，或夏日的玫瑰，
> 或羊群，或牛群，或人的神圣面孔。

===Original===
I bend over the learned page pensively, and I seem to see a Botocudo Professor—though not high “in the social scale,” they may have such things—visiting Cambridge on the last night of the Lent races and reporting of its inhabitants as follows:

> They pay scant heed to their chiefs: they live only for their immediate bodily needs, and take small thought for the morrow. On festal occasions the whole horde meets by night round the camp fire for a dance. Each dancer lays his arms about the necks of his two neighbours, stamping strongly with one foot and dragging the other after it. Now with drooping heads they press closer and closer together; now they widen the circle. Often one can hear nothing but a continually repeated *kalanî aha*, or again one hears short improvised songs in which we are told the doings of the day, the reasons for rejoicing, what not, as “Good hunting,” “Good old—” (naming a tribal God), or in former times “*Now* we shall be but a short while,” or “*Woemma!*” Now and then, too, an individual begins a song and is answered by the rest in chorus—such as
> The chorus twice repeats this and asseverates that they are following a custom common to the flotilla, the expeditionary force, and even their rude seats of learning.

And Dr. Gummere, or somebody else, comments: “As the unprejudiced reader will see, this clear and admirable account confirms our hypothesis that in communal celebration we have at once the origin and model of three poems, *The Faerie Queene*, *Paradise Lost* and *In Memoriam*, recorded as having been composed by members of this very tribe.”

===Chinese===
我沉思地俯在那博学的书页上，恍惚看见一位博托库多教授——虽说「社会等级」不高，他们兴许也有这号人物——在大斋期赛船会的最后一夜来访剑桥，对当地居民作出如下报道：

> 他们对首领鲜有敬意：只为眼下的身体需要活着，不为明天操心。逢到节庆，整个部落夜里围着营火聚拢来跳舞。每个舞者伸手搂住左右两邻的脖子，一只脚重重踏地，另一只脚拖在后面。时而垂着头越挤越紧；时而又把圆圈放宽。往往除了不断重复的一句 *kalanî aha* 便什么也听不见；有时又能听到即兴的短歌，把白天的事情、欢庆的缘由讲给你听，什么都有，诸如「打得着猎」「好个老——」（念出一位部族神明的名号），往日里则唱「*如今*我们只停留短短一会儿」，或「*翁玛！*」（*Woemma!*）。也有时，一个人起头唱一支歌，其余的人便以合唱应和——比如：
> 合唱把这支歌重复了两遍，并指天誓日地说，他们这样做是遵循船队、远征军、乃至他们那简陋学府通行的习俗。

而格默尔博士——或是别的什么人——评注道：「正如不抱成见的读者将看到的，这段清楚而可钦佩的记述证实了我们的假设：在社群的庆典里，我们同时拥有三首诗的起源与范本——《仙后》（The Faerie Queene）、《失乐园》与《悼念集》（In Memoriam）——据记载，三首诗的作者都是这个部族的成员。」

===Original===
Although we have been talking of instincts, we are not concerned here with the steps by which the child, or the savage, following an instinct attains to *write* poetry; but, more modestly, with the instinct by which the child *likes* it, and the way in which he can be best encouraged to read and improve this natural liking. Nor are we even concerned here to define Poetry. It suffices our present purpose to consider Poetry as the sort of thing the poets write.

But obviously if we find a philosopher discussing poetry without any reference to children, and independently basing it upon the very same imitative instincts which we have noted in children, we have some promise of being on the right track.

V

So I return to Aristotle. Aristotle (I shall in fairness say) does not anticipate Dr. Gummere, to contradict or refute him; he may even be held to support him incidentally. But he sticks to business, and this is what he says (*Poetics*, C. IV):

> Poetry in general seems to have sprung from two causes, and these natural causes. First the instinct to imitate is implanted in man from his childhood, and in this he differs from other animals, being the most imitative of them all. Man gets his first learning through imitation, and all men delight in seeing things imitated. This is clearly shown by experience. …
> To imitate, then, being instinctive in our nature, so too we have an instinct for harmony and rhythm, metre being manifestly a species of rhythm: and man, being born to these instincts and little by little improving them, out of his early improvisations created Poetry.

===Chinese===
不错，我们一直在谈本能；但这里我们要关心的，并不是孩子或野蛮人循着本能一步步达到*写*诗的那些阶梯，而是更谦逊一点：关心孩子*喜欢*诗的那种本能，以及怎样最能把鼓励给他，让他去读诗、去增进这天生的爱好。我们甚至也不打算在这里给诗下定义。就眼前的目的而言，把诗看作诗人们写出的那一类东西，也就够了。

但显然，倘若我们发现一位哲学家谈论诗，全然不提儿童，却又独立地把诗建立在我们在儿童身上注意到的同一批摹仿本能之上，那我们就有几分指望：路子没有走错。

V

于是我回到亚里士多德。说句公道话，亚里士多德并没有先于格默尔博士去反驳他、驳倒他；甚至可以说，他不经意间还支持了格默尔。但他坚守本题，下面就是他的话（《诗学》第四章）：

> 一般说来，诗的起源仿佛有两个原因，都属于自然的原因。其一，摹仿的本能自孩提时代便植根于人；人凭这一点异于其他动物，在万物之中最富于摹仿性。人最初的求知是通过摹仿来的，人人见了摹仿之物都感到快乐。经验清楚地证明了这一点。……
> 摹仿既是我们的天性，我们对和声与节奏的感觉同样出于天性——音律显然只是节奏的一种：人天生具有这些本能，又一点一点加以改进，于是从早先的即兴之作里创造出了诗。

===Original===
Combining these two instincts, with him, we arrive at *harmonious imitation*. Well and good. But what is it we imitate in poetry?—noble things or mean things? After considering this, putting mean things aside as unworthy, and voting for the nobler—which must at the same time be true, since without truth there can be no real nobility—Aristotle has to ask, “In what way true? True to ordinary life, with its observed defeats of the right by the wrong? or true, as again instinct tells good men it should be, *universally*?” So he arrives at his conclusion that a true thing is not necessarily truth of fact in a world where truth in fact is so often belied or made meaningless—not the record that Alcibiades went somewhere and suffered something—but truth to the Universal, the superior demand of our conscience. In such a way only we know that *The Tempest* or *Paradise Lost* or *The Ancient Mariner* or *Prometheus Unbound* can be truer than any police report. Yet we know that they are truer in essence, and in significance, since they appeal to eternal verities—since they imitate the Universal—whereas the police report chronicles (faithfully, as in duty bound, even usefully in its way) events which may, nay must, be significant somehow but cannot at best be better to us than phenomena, broken ends and shards.

===Chinese===
循着他的思路，把这两种本能合在一起，我们便得到「和谐的摹仿」（harmonious imitation）。很好。可是我们在诗里摹仿的是什么？——是高贵的事物，还是卑贱的事物？把这个问题想过之后，把卑贱之物当作配不上的东西撇开，投了高贵一方的票——而高贵必须同时是真实的，因为没有真实便没有真正的高贵——亚里士多德就非问不可了：「在哪重意义上真实？是吻合日常生活的真实么？在那日常生活中，我们亲眼看见对的被错的打败；还是那种真实——本能再一次告诉好人们，事情本该如此——*普遍的*真实？」于是他得出结论：在一个事实的真理屡遭掩盖、屡被弄得名存实亡的世界里，一件真实的东西未必就是事实的真相——不是「亚西比德（Alcibiades）到了某地、遭了某事」那样的记载——而是对普遍（the Universal）的真实，是我们良心更高的要求。唯有沿着这条路，我们才知道《暴风雨》（The Tempest）、《失乐园》、《古舟子咏》（The Ancient Mariner）或《解放了的普罗米修斯》（Prometheus Unbound）可以比任何警务报道更真实。而我们也确实知道，它们在本质、在意蕴上更真实，因为它们诉求的是永恒的真理——因为它们摹仿的是普遍——而警务报道记录下来的（忠实地，职分所在，甚至自有其用处）是那些或许、不，必然有几分意义的事件，但再好也不过是一些现象，一些断折的残端与碎片。

===Original===
VI

I return to the child. Clearly in obeying the instinct which I have tried to illustrate, he is searching to realise himself; and, as educators, we ought to help this effort—or, at least, not to hinder it.

Further, if we agree with Aristotle, in this searching to realise himself through imitation, what will the child most nobly and naturally imitate? He will imitate what Aristotle calls “the Universal,” the superior demand. And does not this bring us back to consent with what I have been preaching from the start in this course—that to realise ourselves in *What Is* not only in degree transcends mere knowledge and activity, *What Knows* and *What Does*, but transcends it in kind? It is not only what the child unconsciously longs for: it is that for which (in St. Paul’s words) “the whole creation groaneth and travaileth in pain together until now”; craving for this (I make you the admission) as emotionally, as the heart may be thrilled, the breast surge, the eyes swell with tears, at a note drawn from the violin: feeling that somewhere, beyond reach, we have a lost sister, and she speaks to our soul.

===Chinese===
VI

我回到孩子身上。显然，在顺从我方才竭力描写的那种本能时，他是在寻求实现他自己；而身为教育者，我们应当帮助这一努力——至少，不该去阻挠它。

再有，倘若我们与亚里士多德一致，那么孩子在这种借摹仿以实现自身的寻求里，最高贵、最自然地要摹仿的是什么？他会摹仿亚里士多德所谓的「普遍」，那更高的要求。这难道不又把我们带回到本课程开讲以来我一直在宣讲的道理上么？——在*实然*（What Is）之中实现我们自己，不仅在程度上、而且就在种类上超越了单纯的知识与活动，超越了*知者*（What Knows）与*行者*（What Does）。这不单是孩子无意识中渴望的东西；它正是（用圣保罗的话说）「一切受造之物一同叹息、劳苦，直到如今」所指向的东西；对它的渴慕（这一点我向你们承认）是动情的——正如从提琴上引出一个音符，心会震颤，胸会起伏，眼中会盈满泪水：觉得在某个地方，在够不着的地方，我们有一个失散的姊妹，而她正对我们的灵魂说话。

===Original===
VII

Who, that has been a child, has not felt this surprise of beauty, the revelation, the call of it?

> The sounding cataract
> Haunted me like a passion …

—yes, or a rainbow on the spray against a cliff; or a vista of lawns between descending woods; or a vision of fish moving in a pool under the hazel’s shadow? Who has not felt the small surcharged heart labouring with desire to express it?

I preach to you that the base of all Literature, of all Poetry, of all Theology, is one, and stands on one rock: *the very highest Universal Truth is something so simple that a child may understand it.* This, surely, was in Jesus’ mind when he said “I thank thee, O Father, Lord of Heaven and Earth, because thou hast hid these things from the wise and prudent, and hast revealed them unto babes.”

For as the Universe is one, so the individual human souls, that apprehend it, have no varying values intrinsically, but one equal value. They vary but in power to apprehend, and this may be more easily hindered than helped by the conceit begotten of finite knowledge. I shall even dare to quote of this Universal Truth, the words I once hardily put into the mouth of John Wesley concerning divine Love: “I see now that if God’s love reach up to every star and down to every poor soul on earth, it must be vastly simple; so simple that all dwellers on Earth may be assured of it—as all who have eyes may be assured of the planet shining yonder at the end of the street—and so vast that all bargaining is below it, and they may inherit it without considering their deserts.” I believe this to be strictly and equally true of the appeal which Poetry makes to each of us, child or man, in his degree. As Johnson said of Gray’s “Elegy,” it “abounds with images which find a mirror in every mind, and with sentiments to which every bosom returns an echo.” It exalts us through the best of us, by telling us something new yet not strange, something that we *recognise*, something that we too have known, or surmised, but had never the delivering speech to tell. “There is a pleasure in poetic pains,” says Wordsworth: but, Gentlemen, if you have never felt the travail, yet you have still to understand the bliss of deliverance.

===Chinese===
VII

凡是做过孩子的，谁不曾感受过美的这一惊异，美的这一启示、这一呼唤？

> 那轰响的瀑布
> 像一腔激情萦绕着我……

——是啊，或者水沫挂在崖壁上的一道虹；或下行林木之间草坪的一线远景；或榛树荫下一泓池水里游鱼的影象？谁不曾感到那颗小小的心胀得满满，苦苦地渴望把它说出来？

我向你们宣讲：一切文学、一切诗、一切神学的根基是一个，立在磐石上：*最高的普遍真理，是这样一种东西，它简单到一个孩子也能懂得。*耶稣说「父啊，天地的主，我感谢你！因为你将这些事向聪明通达人就藏起来，向婴孩就显出来」，他心里所怀的，必定就是这个。

因为宇宙是一个，所以领受宇宙的各个人的灵魂，本身并没有高下不等的价值，而是同等的、一个价值。所差的只在领受的能力；而有限的知识所生的自负，妨碍这能力易，助成这能力难。我甚至要斗胆把一句话用在这普遍真理身上——那是我曾经冒昧借约翰·卫斯理（John Wesley）之口论神圣之爱时说过的话：「如今我看明白了，倘若上帝的爱上达每一颗星辰、下达世上每一个可怜的灵魂，那它必定是极其单纯；单纯到世上一切居民都可以确知它——正如凡有眼的都可以确知街尾那头闪耀的行星——又浩大到一切讨价还价都在它之下，人们无须计较自己的功德便可以承受它。」我相信，这话同等地、分毫不差地适用于诗对我们每一个人的呼唤，无论孩子还是大人，各按其份。正如约翰逊（Johnson）论格雷（Gray）的《墓园挽歌》所说，它「满含着在每颗心里都找得到镜子的意象，满含着每副胸膛都会报以回声的情感」。它借我们身上最好的部分把我们举起来，告诉我们一些新而并不陌生的事情，一些我们*认得*的事情，一些我们也曾知道或揣度过、却始终没有那解脱的言语把它说出来的事情。「作诗的辛苦中自有乐趣，」华兹华斯（Wordsworth）说；可是诸位先生，你们倘若从不曾感到那临产的阵痛，便还有待领会分娩得救的至福。

===Original===
VIII

If, then, you consent with me thus far in theory, let us now drive at practice. You have (we will say) a class of thirty or forty in front of you. We will assume that they know *a—b*, *ab*, can at least spell out their words. You will choose a passage for them, and you will not (if you are wise) choose a passage from *Paradise Lost*: your knowledge telling you that *Paradise Lost* was written, late in his life, by a great virtuoso, and older men (of whom I, sad to say, am one) assuring you that to taste the Milton of *Paradise Lost* a man must have passed his thirtieth year. You take the early Milton: you read out this, for instance, from “L’Allegro”:

> Haste thee, Nymph, and bring with thee
> Jest and youthful Jollity,
> Quips, and Cranks, and wanton wiles,
> Nods and Becks, and wreathed Smiles
> Such as hang on Hebe’s cheek,
> And love to live in dimple sleek;
> Sport that wrinkled Care derides,
> And Laughter holding both his sides. …

===Chinese===
VIII

那么，倘若你们在理论上到这里都还赞同我，现在就让我们直趋实践。你们面前有（比方说）一班三四十个学生。我们假设他们认得 *a—b*、*ab*，至少能把单词一个个拼读出来。你们要替他们选一段文字，而你们不会（倘若你们聪明）从《失乐园》里选：你们的学问告诉你们，《失乐园》是一位伟大的大家晚年的手笔；而上了年纪的人（说来惭愧，我是其中之一）会向你们担保：要品出《失乐园》里那个弥尔顿（Milton）的滋味，一个人总得过了三十岁。你们取早年的弥尔顿：比如，把《快乐的人》（L’Allegro）里的这一段读给他们听：

> 来吧，女神，快些同来，
> 带着戏谑与青春的欢快，
> 带着俏皮话、怪念头、放浪的狡狯，
> 带着点头、招手、颦起的花环般的微笑，
> 像挂在赫柏（Hebe）的脸颊上那样，
> 爱住在光滑的酒窝里；
> 带着皱纹满面的「忧愁」所讥笑的嬉游，
> 带着「笑」，他两手捧住自己的两肋。……

===Original===
Go on: just read it to them. They won’t know who Hebe was, but you can tell them later. The metre is taking hold of them (in my experience the metre of “L’Allegro” can be relied upon to grip children) and anyway they can see “Laughter holding both his sides”: they recognise it as if they saw the picture. Go on steadily:

> Come, and trip it as ye go,
> On the light fantastick toe;
> And in thy right hand lead with thee
> The Mountain Nymph, sweet Liberty;
> And, if I give thee honour due,
> Mirth, admit me of thy crew—

Do not pause and explain what a Nymph is, or why Liberty is the “Mountain Nymph”! Go on reading: the Prince has always to break through briers to kiss the Sleeping Beauty awake. Go on with the incantation, calling him, persuading him, that he is the Prince and she is worth it. Go on reading—

> Mirth, admit me of thy crew,
> To live with her, and live with thee,
> In unreprovèd pleasures free;
> To hear the lark begin his flight,
> And singing startle the dull night,
> From his watch-towre in the skies,
> Till the dappled dawn doth rise.

===Chinese===
读下去：就只管读给他们听。他们不会知道赫柏是谁，但你可以留到以后再讲。格律正在把他们攫住（凭我的经验，《快乐的人》的格律稳可以抓住孩子们）；而且无论如何，他们看得见「笑，两手捧住他的两肋」：他们认得，就像亲眼见了那幅画。稳稳地读下去：

> 来，一路跳着舞步，
> 踮着轻盈灵巧的脚尖；
> 用你右手牵了
> 山间的女神，甜美的自由；
> 倘若我敬你应得的尊荣，
> 欢乐，收纳我进你的队伍——

不要停下来解释女神是什么，也不要解释自由为什么是「山间的女神」！只管读下去：王子总得披荆斩棘，才能吻醒睡美人。咒语接着念下去，呼唤他，劝说他：他正是那位王子，而她值得这一切。只管读下去——

> 欢乐，收纳我进你的队伍，
> 与她同住，也与你同住，
> 在无可指摘的欢愉里自在无碍；
> 去听云雀振翅起飞，
> 边飞边唱，把沉沉的黑夜
> 从它在天上的守望楼里惊起，
> 直到斑驳的黎明升起。

===Original===
At this point—still as you read without stopping to explain, the child certainly feels that he is being led to something. He knows the lark: but the lark’s “watch-towre”—he had never thought of that: and “the dappled dawn”—yes that’s just *it*, now he comes to think:

> Then to come, in spite of sorrow,
> And at my window bid good-morrow,
> Through the sweetbriar or the vine
> Or the twisted eglantine;
> While the cock with lively din
> Scatters the rear of Darkness thin;
> And to the stack, or the barn door,
> Stoutly struts his dames before:
> Oft listening how the hounds and horn
> Cheerily rouse the slumbering Morn,
> From the side of some hoar hill,
> Through the high wood echoing shrill:
> Sometime walking, not unseen,
> By hedgerow elms on hillocks green,
> Right against the eastern gate,
> Where the great sun begins his state,
> Robed in flames and amber light,
> The clouds in thousand liveries dight;
> While the ploughman, near at hand,
> Whistles o’er the furrow’d land,
> And the milkmaid singeth blithe,
> And the mower whets his sithe,
> And every shepherd tells his tale
> Under the hawthorn in the dale.

===Chinese===
到这里——你依旧读个不停、并不停下来解释——孩子确实感到自己正被引向什么。云雀他认得：可云雀的「守望楼」——他从来没有想到过；还有「斑驳的黎明」——对，正是*它*，如今他想起来了：

> 然后不顾愁苦，
> 来到我的窗前道声早安，
> 穿过香蔷薇，或葡萄藤，
> 或虬曲的多花蔷薇；
> 那时雄鸡啼声欢腾，
> 把「黑暗」的殿后扫得稀薄；
> 它昂首阔步，在草垛旁，在谷仓门前，
> 在它的母鸡们面前神气活现：
> 不时侧耳听猎犬与号角
> 怎样兴冲冲唤起酣睡的清晨，
> 从某座苍老的山侧，
> 穿过高高的树林，回声尖利：
> 有时漫步，并不隐匿行踪，
> 在绿丘上、篱边的榆树旁，
> 正对着那扇东方的门，
> 伟大的太阳在那里开始他的仪仗，
> 身披火焰与琥珀的光，
> 云彩穿上千种的号衣；
> 犁手就在近旁，
> 吹着口哨掠过犁沟纵横的田垄，
> 挤奶姑娘欢快地歌唱，
> 割草人磨着他的镰刀，
> 每个牧人清点着他的羊数，
> 在山谷的山楂树下。

===Original===
Don’t stop (I say) to explain that Hebe was (for once) the legitimate daughter of Zeus and, as such, had the privilege to draw wine for the gods. Don’t even stop, just yet, to explain who the gods were. Don’t discourse on amber, otherwise ambergris; don’t explain that “gris” in this connection doesn’t mean “grease”; don’t trace it through the Arabic into Noah’s Ark; don’t prove its electrical properties by tearing up paper into little bits and attracting them with the mouthpiece of your pipe rubbed on your sleeve. Don’t insist philologically that when every shepherd “tells his tale” he is not relating an anecdote but simply keeping tally of his flock.

Just go on reading, as well as you can; and be sure that when the children get the thrill of it, for which you wait, they will be asking more questions, and pertinent ones, than you are able to answer.

IX

This advice, to be sure, presupposes of the teacher himself some capacity of reading aloud, and reading aloud is not taught in our schools. In our elementary schools, in which few of the pupils contemplate being called to Holy Orders or to the Bar, it is practised, indeed, but seldom taught as an art. In our secondary and public schools it is neither taught nor practised: as I know to my cost—and you, to yours, Gentlemen, on whom I have had to practise.

===Chinese===
不要停下（我说）去解释赫柏是（这一回可是正经的）宙斯（Zeus）的嫡生女儿，因此有资格为众神斟酒。也不要连众神是谁都停下来解释。不要就琥珀大发议论，扯到龙涎香（ambergris）；不要解释「gris」在这里并不是「油脂」（grease）的意思；不要顺着阿拉伯语一路追到挪亚的方舟；不要把纸撕成小碎片，拿在袖子上擦过的烟斗嘴把它们吸起来，来证明它的电的性质。也不要在语文学上较真，硬说每个牧人「清点羊数」并不是在讲一桩轶事，不过是数一数他的羊罢了。

只管尽你所能读下去；你只管放心，等到孩子们得到你等候的那阵震颤，他们问出的问题会比你答得过来的更多，而且问得中肯。

IX

诚然，这个忠告预先假定教师本人多少有些朗读的能力，而朗读在我们学校里是没人教的。在我们的小学里——那里没几个学生打算日后受圣职或进律师界——朗读固然在操练，却很少有人当作一门艺术来教。在我们的中学与公学里，它既不教也不练：这一点我吃过苦头才知道——而诸位先生，你们也吃过苦头，因为我一直在拿你们操练。

===Original===
But let the teacher take courage. First let him read a passage “at the long breath”—as the French say—aloud, and persuasively as he can. Now and then he may pause to indicate some particular beauty, repeating the line before he proceeds. But he should be sparing of these interruptions. When Laughter, for example, is already “holding both his sides” it cannot be less than officious, a work of supererogation, to stop and hold them for him; and he who obeys the counsel of perfection will read straight to the end and then recur to particular beauties. Next let him put up a child to continue with the tale, and another and another, just as in a construing class. While the boy is reading, the teacher should *never* interrupt: he should wait, and return afterwards upon a line that has been slurred or wrongly emphasised. When the children have done reading he should invite questions on any point they have found puzzling: it is with the operation of poetry on *their* minds that his main business lies. Lastly, he may run back over significant points they have missed.

“And is that all the method?”—Yes, that is all the method. “So simple as that?”—Yes, even so simple as that, and (I claim) even so wise, seeing that it just lets the author—Chaucer or Shakespeare or Milton or Coleridge—have his own way with the young plant—just lets them drop “like the gentle rain from heaven,” and soak in.

> The moving Moon went up the sky,
> And nowhere did abide:
> Softly she was going up,
> And a star or two beside.

===Chinese===
但教师要有勇气。第一步，让他「一口气到底」——照法语的说法——把一段文章朗读出来，倾其所能，读得动人。偶尔他可以停一下，指出某处特殊的美，把那一句重复一遍再往下走。但这种打断务必少用。比如「笑」已经「两手捧住他的两肋」了，你再停下替他去捧，那就说多不少是越俎代庖、画蛇添足；肯奉行成全之道的人会一口气读到末尾，再回头去赏那些特美的句子。第二步，叫一个孩子接着往下读，再一个，再一个，就像在文法解析课上那样。孩子读书的时候，教师*切不可*打断：他应当等着，过后再回到那句念含糊了或重音读错了的诗行上来。孩子们读完了，他应当请他们把任何感到困惑的地方提出来问：诗在*他们*心灵上的作用，才是他主要的事务所在。最后，他可以把他们错过的重要关目再串讲一遍。

「这就是全部的方法了么？」——是的，这就是全部的方法。「就这么简单？」——是的，就这么简单，而且（我敢说）也就这么高明：因为它只是随作者——乔叟（Chaucer），或莎士比亚（Shakespeare），或弥尔顿，或柯勒律治（Coleridge）——拿他自己的方式去对待那株幼苗，只是任他们「像温柔的甘霖自天而降」，浸润进去。

> 移动的月亮升上天心，
> 哪里也不停留：
> 轻轻地她往上走，
> 旁边伴着一两颗星。

===Original===
Do you really want to chat about *that*? Cannot you trust it?

> The stars were dim, and thick the night,
> The steersman’s face by his lamp gleamed white;
> From the sails the dew did drip—
> Till clomb above the eastern bar
> The hornèd Moon, with one bright star
> Within the nether tip.

*Must* you tell them that for the Moon to hold a star anywhere within her circumference is an astronomical impossibility? Very well, then; tell it. But tell it afterwards, and put it away quietly. For the quality of Poetry is not strained. Let the rain soak; then use your hoe, and gently; and still trust Nature; by which, I again repeat to you, all spirit attracts all spirit as inevitably as all matter attracts all matter.

===Chinese===
你们当真还想就*这个*闲聊么？你们就不能信它么？

> 星星昏暗，夜色浓重，
> 舵手就着灯，脸照得惨白；
> 露水从帆上滴落——
> 直到长在东方云堤上空的
> 那弯角月，带着一颗亮星
> 含在它下角的弯环里，爬了上来。

你*非要*告诉他们，月亮的圆周之内任何地方含着一颗星，在天文学上是不可能的么？那好；就告诉他们。但要等到过后再讲，讲完就轻轻放下。因为诗的质地不是勉强的。让雨水浸透；然后动你的锄头，轻轻地；依旧信托自然；我再向你们说一遍：凭这自然，一切精神吸引一切精神，正如一切物质吸引一切物质，不可避免。

===Original===
“Strained.” I am glad that memory flew just here to the word of Portia’s: for it carries me on to a wise page of Dr. Corson’s, and a passage in which, protesting against the philologers who cram our children’s handbooks with irrelevant information that but obscures what Chaucer or Shakespeare *mean*, he breaks out in Chaucer’s own words:

> Thise cookes, how they stampe, and streyne, and grynde,
> And turnen substaunce into accident!

(Yes, and make the accident the substance!)—as he insists that the true subject of literary study is the author’s meaning; and the true method a surrender of the mind to that meaning, with what Wordsworth calls “a wise passiveness”:

> The eye—it cannot choose but see;
> We cannot bid the ear be still;
> Our bodies feel, where’er they be,
> Against or with our will.
> Nor less I deem that there are Powers
> Which of themselves our minds impress;
> That we can feed this mind of ours
> In a wise passiveness.
> Think you, “mid all this mighty sum
> Of things forever speaking,
> That nothing of itself will come,
> But we must still be seeking?”

===Chinese===
「勉强」——我很高兴，记忆恰好飞到此处，落在鲍西娅（Portia）的那句话上：因为它把我引到科尔森（Corson）博士智慧的一页，引到一段文字；在那里，他抗议那些语文学家——他们拿不相干的材料塞满我们的儿童读本，恰恰把乔叟或莎士比亚的*意思*给蒙住了——他忍不住借乔叟自己的话喊出来：

> 这些厨子，瞧他们舂啊，滤啊，磨啊，
> 把实体翻搅成了偶性！

（是啊，还要把偶性当成实体！）——他坚持认为，文学研究的真正对象是作者的意思；而真正的方法，是把心灵降服于那意思，用华兹华斯的话说，怀着「一种智慧的被动」：

> 眼睛——它不能不看；
> 我们不能叫耳朵安静；
> 我们的身子无论在哪里都觉得到，
> 由不得我们愿意不愿意。
>
> 我也同样相信，有一种力量
> 自行把我们的心灵打动；
> 我们可以在一种智慧的被动里
> 喂养自己这颗心。
>
> 你以为，「在万物滔滔不绝
> 言说着的这浩大总和里，
> 竟没有一样自己会来，
> 倒非要我们一味去寻？」

===Original===
X

I have been talking today about children; and find that most of the while I have been thinking, if but subconsciously, of poor children. Now, at the end, you may ask, “Why, lecturing here at Cambridge, is he preoccupied with poor children who leave school at fourteen and under, and thereafter read no poetry?” … Oh, yes! I know all about these children and the hopeless, wicked waste; these with a common living-room to read in, a father tired after his day’s work, and (for parental encouragement) just the two words “Get out!” A Scots domine writes in his log:

> I have discovered a girl with a sense of humour. I asked my qualifying class to draw a graph of the attendance at a village kirk. “And you must explain away any rise or fall,” I said.
> Margaret Steel had a huge drop one Sunday, and her explanation was “Special Collection for Missions.” Next Sunday the Congregation was abnormally large: Margaret wrote “Change of Minister.” … Poor Margaret! When she is fourteen, she will go out into the fields, and in three years she will be an ignorant country bumpkin.

===Chinese===
X

我今天一直在谈儿童；却发现大半时间里，我若明若暗地想着的，是穷人的孩子。到了末尾，你们或许要问：「在这儿剑桥讲学，他为什么一心惦着那些十四岁上下就离校、此后再不读诗的穷孩子？」……哦，不错！这些孩子的情形，还有那无可救药的、罪过的浪费，我全知道；他们只有一间合家共用的起居室可以读书，父亲做完工一身疲惫，而（论父母的鼓励）只有两个字：「出去！」一位苏格兰的乡塾教师（domine）在他的日志里写道：

> 我发现了一个有幽默感的女孩。我叫资格试班画一张乡村教堂礼拜人数的曲线图。「上升也好，下降也好，你们都得给个说法，」我说。
> 玛格丽特·斯蒂尔（Margaret Steel）有一个星期日跌了一大截，她的解释是「为传教特别募捐」。下一个星期日，会众异乎寻常地多：玛格丽特写「牧师换人」。……可怜的玛格丽特！她一到十四岁，就要下地干活去；不出三年，她就会变成一个无知的乡下土包子。

===Original===
And again:

> Robert Campbell (a favourite pupil) left the school today. He had reached the age-limit. … Truly it is like death: I stand by a new made grave, and I have no hope of a resurrection. Robert is dead.

Precisely because I have lived on close terms with this, and the wicked waste of it, I appeal to you who are so much more fortunate than this Robert or this Margaret and will have far more to say in the world, to think of them—how many they are. I am not sentimentalising. When an elementary schoolmaster spreads himself and tells me he looks upon every child entering his school as a potential Lord Chancellor, I answer that, as I expect, so I should hope, to die before seeing the world a Woolsack. Jack cannot ordinarily be as good as his master; if he were, he would be a great deal better. You have given Robert a vote, however, and soon you will have to give it to Margaret. Can you not give them also, in their short years at school, something to sustain their souls in the long Valley of Humiliation?

===Chinese===
还有一条：

> 罗伯特·坎贝尔（Robert Campbell），一个得意门生，今天离校了。他到了年限。……这真像死亡：我站在一座新坟旁边，看不到复活的指望。罗伯特死了。

正因为我同这一切厮守得近，深知其中罪过的浪费，我才向你们呼吁——你们比这个罗伯特、这个玛格丽特幸运得多，将来在世界上的分量也要大得多——请想一想他们，想一想他们有多少人。我并不是在感伤。当一位小学教师眉飞色舞地对我说，他把走进他学校的每个孩子都看作一位潜在的大法官时，我回答：我期望如此——也但愿如此——在世界变成一张羊毛座垫（Woolsack，大法官的席位）之前死去。杰克通常不会赶得上他的主人好；倘若赶得上，那他就要高明许多了。不过，你们已经给了罗伯特一张选票，不久也得把选票给玛格丽特。那么，能不能也在他们短短的在校年月里，给他们一样东西，好在漫长的屈辱谷里支撑他们的灵魂？

===Original===
Do you remember this passage in *The Pilgrim’s Progress*—as the pilgrims passed down that valley?

> Now as they were going along and talking, they espied a Boy feeding his Father’s Sheep. The Boy was in very mean Clothes, but of a very fresh and well-favoured Countenance, and as he sat by himself he Sung. Hark, said Mr. Greatheart, to what the Shepherd’s Boy saith.

Well, it was a very pretty song, about Contentment.

> He that is down need fear no fall
> He that is low, no Pride:
> He that is humble ever shall
> Have God to be his Guide.

But I care less for its subject than for the song. Though life condemn him to live it through in the Valley of Humiliation, I want to hear the Shepherd Boy singing.

===Chinese===
《天路历程》（The Pilgrim’s Progress）里这一段，你们还记得么？——朝圣的人们正走下那条谷。

> 他们正边走边谈的时候，望见一个男孩在牧放他父亲的羊。那孩子衣衫十分粗陋，面色却非常鲜润可爱；他独自坐着，唱起歌来。听哪，大心先生（Mr. Greatheart）说，听这牧羊的孩子说些什么。

是啊，那确是一支很好听的歌，唱的是知足。

> 卑微的不必怕跌倒
> 低微的不必怕骄傲：
> 谦卑的永远有
> 上帝作他的向导。

不过，歌的题目倒在其次，我在乎的是这支歌本身。哪怕生活判定他要一辈子在屈辱谷里把这人生从头走完，我也要听见那牧羊的孩子唱歌。

## Lecture V On Reading for Examinations / 第五讲 论应试阅读

===Original===
Wednesday, May 9, 1917

I

You, Gentlemen, who so far have followed with patience this course of lectures, advertised, maybe too ambitiously, as “On the Art of Reading,” will recall to your memory, when I challenge it across the intervals of Vacation, that three propositions have been pretty steadily held before you.
===Chinese===
1917 年 5 月 9 日，星期三

I

诸位先生，你们至今耐心随听这一系列讲演——它张榜的题目也许过于雄心勃勃，叫作《论阅读的艺术》（*On the Art of Reading*）——当我在假期的间隔之间向你们的记性发起挑战时，你们会想起：有三个命题已相当一贯地摆在你们面前。

===Original===
The *first*: (bear me out) that, man’s life being of the length it is, and his activities multifarious as they are, out of the mass of printed matter already loaded and still being shot upon this planet, he *must* make selection. There is no other way.

The *second*: that—the time and opportunity being so brief, the mass so enormous, and the selection therefore so difficult—he should select the books that are best for him, and take them *absolutely*, not frittering his time upon books written about and around the best: that—in their order, of course—the primary masterpieces shall come first, and the secondary second, and so on; and mere chat about any of them last of all.

My *third* proposition (perhaps more discutable) has been that, the human soul’s activities being separated, so far as we can separate them, into *What Does, What Knows, What Is*—to *be* such-and-such a man ranks higher than either *knowing* or *doing* this, that, or the other: that it transcends all man’s activity upon phenomena, even a Napoleon’s: all his housed store of knowledge, though it be a Casaubon’s or a Mark Pattison’s: that only by learning to *be* can we understand or reach, as we have an instinct to reach, to our right place in the scheme of things: and that, anyway, all the greatest literature commands this instinct. To be Hamlet—to feel yourself Hamlet—is more important than killing a king or even knowing all there is to be known about a text. Now most of us have been Hamlet, more or less: while few of us, I trust, have ever murdered a monarch: and still fewer, perhaps, can hope to know all that is to be known of the text of the play.
