But for value, Gentlemen, let us not rank these three achievements by order of their rarity. Shakespeare means us to feel—to *be*—Hamlet. That is all: and from the play it is the best we can get.
===Chinese===
第一个：（请诸位为我作证）人生短促如斯，人的活动又如此繁多，面对已经堆上、并且仍在向这颗星球倾泻的印刷品洪流，他必须有所拣择。别无他法。

第二个：光阴与机会如此短促，书海如此浩瀚，拣择因此如此艰难——所以他应当拣选对自己最好的书，而且要「全副」领受，不把光阴虚掷在那些谈论最好的书、围着最好的书打转的书上：也就是说——当然要依其等次——头等的杰作居首，次等的居次，以此类推；而关于其中任何一本的清谈，一律殿后。

我的第三个命题（或许更可争议）是：人的灵魂活动，就我们所能分者，可分为所行者（What Does）、所知者（What Knows）、所是者（What Is）——去「是」这样一个人物，其位次高于去「知」这知那、去「做」这做那：它超越人对现象的一切活动，哪怕是拿破仑（Napoleon）的活动；也超越他全部收贮于内的知识，哪怕那是卡索邦（Casaubon）或马克·帕蒂森（Mark Pattison）的知识：唯有学着去「是」，我们才能理解、才能企及——我们本有企及的本能——自己在万物格局中的正位；而无论如何，一切最伟大的文学都听命于这一本能。做哈姆雷特（Hamlet）——感到你自己就是哈姆雷特——比弑一个国王、甚至比通晓关于一部文本的一切可知之事更要紧。而我们多数人都多多少少做过哈姆雷特：至于弑君，我相信我们中间很少有人干过；而或许更少的人，敢指望通晓这部剧本文本的一切可知之事。但论及价值，诸位，我们不要按稀有程度来给这三样成就排座次。莎士比亚是要我们感受——是要我们「是」——哈姆雷特。仅此而已：而这出戏所能给的最好的东西，也尽在于此。

===Original===
II

Now in talking to you, last term, about children I had perforce to lay stress on the point that, with all this glut of literature, the mass of children in our commonwealth who leave school at fourteen go forth starving.

But you are happier. You are happier, not in having your selection of reading in English done for you at school (for you have in the public schools scarce any such help): but happier (1) because the time of learning is so largely prolonged, and (2) because this most difficult office of sorting out from the mass what you should read as most profitable has been tentatively performed for you by us older men for your relief. For example, those of you—“if any,” as the Regulations say—who will, a week or two hence, be sitting for Section A of the Medieval and Modern Languages Tripos, have been spared, all along, the laborious business of choosing what you should read or read with particular attention for the good of your souls. Is Chaucer your author? Then you will have read (or ought to have read) “The Parlement of Fowls,” the Prologue to *The Canterbury Tales*, “The Knight’s Tale,” “The Man of Law’s Tale,” “The Nun Priest’s Tale,” “The Doctor’s Tale,” “The Pardoner’s Tale” with its Prologue, “The Friar’s Tale.” You were not dissuaded from reading *Troilus*; you were not forbidden to read all the Canterbury Tales, even the naughtiest; but the works that I have mentioned have been “prescribed” for you. So, of Shakespeare, we do not discourage you (at all events, intentionally) from reading *Macbeth*, *Othello*, *As You Like It*, *The Tempest*, any play you wish. In other years we “set” each of these in its turn. But for this Year of Grace we insist upon *King John*, *The Merchant of Venice*, *King Henry IV, Part I*, *Much Ado About Nothing*, *Hamlet*, *King Lear*, “certain specified works”—and so on, with other courses of study. Why is this done? Be fair to us, Gentlemen. We do it not only to accommodate the burden to your backs, to avoid overtaxing one-and-a-half or two years of study; not merely to guide you that you do not dissipate your reading, that you shall—with us, at any rate—know where you are. We do it chiefly, and honestly—you likewise being honest—to give you each year, in each prescribed course, a sound nucleus of knowledge, out of which, later, your minds can reach to more. We are not, in the last instance, praiseworthy or blameworthy for your range. I think, perhaps, too little of a man’s *range* in his short while here between (say) nineteen and twenty-two. For anything I care, the kernel may be as small as you please. To plant it wholesome, for a while to tend it wholesome, then to show it the sky and that it is wide—not a hothouse, nor a brassy cupola over a man, but an atmosphere shining up league on league; to reach the moment of saying “All this now is yours, if you have the perseverance as I have taught you the power, *coelum nactus es, hoc exorna*”: this, even in our present Tripos, we endeavour to do.
===Chinese===
II

上学期同诸位谈儿童时，我不得不着力强调一点：尽管文学如此过剩，我们这个共同体里那大批十四岁离校的儿童，走出去时却是饿着肚子的。

但你们要幸运些。你们幸运，不在于你们英文阅读的拣选已由学校代劳（因为你们在公学里几乎得不到这类帮助）；而有幸于两点：（1）求学的岁月被大大延长；（2）从书海中分拣出何者当读、何者最有益——这最艰难的职分——已由我们这些年长者试着为你们代行，好让你们卸担。比如，你们中间那些——照学章的措辞，「如有此等人」——一两周后就要坐进中世纪与现代语言荣誉学位考试（Medieval and Modern Languages Tripos）甲组考场的，向来免去了那份劳神的差事：为自己灵魂的益处去挑拣该读什么、该格外着力读什么。乔叟（Chaucer）是你们的作家吗？那么你们就读过（或应当读过）《百鸟议会》（“The Parlement of Fowls”）、《坎特伯雷故事集》（*The Canterbury Tales*）总引、《骑士的故事》（“The Knight’s Tale”）、《律师的故事》（“The Man of Law’s Tale”）、《修女院教士的故事》（“The Nun Priest’s Tale”）、《医生的故事》（“The Doctor’s Tale”）、《赦罪僧的故事》（“The Pardoner’s Tale”）及其引子、《游乞僧的故事》（“The Friar’s Tale”）。没有人劝阻你们去读《特洛伊罗斯》（*Troilus*）；没有人禁止你们通读全部坎特伯雷故事，连顶顶不规矩的也不禁；但我列举的这些作品，是「指定」给你们修习的。莎士比亚也一样，我们并不（至少存心并不）劝阻你们去读《麦克白斯》（*Macbeth*）、《奥赛罗》（*Othello*）、《皆大欢喜》（*As You Like It*）、《暴风雨》（*The Tempest*），你们愿读什么剧都行。换别的年份，这些剧会轮番「指定」。但在今年这个恩典之年，我们坚持指定《约翰王》（*King John*）、《威尼斯商人》（*The Merchant of Venice*）、《亨利四世》上篇（*King Henry IV, Part I*）、《无事生非》（*Much Ado About Nothing*）、《哈姆雷特》（*Hamlet*）、《李尔王》（*King Lear*），以及「若干特定作品」——其他课程，依此类推。为什么要这样做？请公道待我们，诸位。我们这样做，不单是为了把担子配你们的脊背，免得一年半或两年的学业负担过重；也不只是为了引导你们，叫你们的阅读不至散漫无归，叫你们——至少跟着我们——知道自己身在何处。我们这样做，主要是——而且说得老实——你们也老实——为了每年、在每门指定课程里给你们一个扎实的知识内核，日后你们的心智能从这个内核伸展到更多。归根到底，你们见识的广狭，无可褒贬于我们。也许，一个人在此间从（比如说）十九岁到二十二岁这段短促时光里的「广度」（*range*），我原就看得太轻。随你们的便，那内核哪怕再小，我也不在乎。要紧的是把它种得茁壮，有片刻把它照料得茁壮，然后指给它看天空，指给它看天空之辽阔——不是温室，不是扣在人头顶的一口黄铜穹顶，而是迤逦数里、光华直上的大气；要走到能说出这句话的时刻：「这一切如今都归你，只要你有毅力，正如我已教会你能力——『你既生而有天，就把这天真装饰起来』（coelum nactus es, hoc exorna）」：即便在我们如今的荣誉学位考试里，我们也在竭力这样做。

===Original===
III

All very well. But, as Elizabeth Barrett Browning asked,

> Do ye hear the children weeping, O my brothers?

“Yes,” I hear you ingeminate; “but what about Examinations? We thank you, sirs, for thus relieving and guiding us: we acknowledge your excellent intentions. But in practice you hang up a bachelor’s gown and hood on a pole, and right under and just in front of it you set the examination-barrier. For this in practice we run during three years or so, and to this all the time you are exhorting, directing us—whether you mean it or not, though we suspect that you cannot help yourselves.” Yes; and, as labouring swimmers will turn their eyes even to a little boat in the offing, I hear you pant “This man at all events—always so insistent that good literature teaches *What Is* rather than *What Knows*—will bring word that we may float on our backs, bathe, enjoy these waters and be refreshed, instead of striving through them competitive for a goal. He *must* condemn literary examinations, nine-tenths of which treat Literature as matter of Knowledge merely.”
===Chinese===
III

都好得很。但是，正如伊丽莎白·巴雷特·勃朗宁（Elizabeth Barrett Browning）所问的：

> 弟兄们，你们可听见孩子们在哭泣？

「是的，」我听见你们一迭连声地说；「可是考试怎么办？先生们，多谢你们这样为我们卸担、为我们引路：你们的良苦用心，我们领情。但实际上，你们把学士袍和风帽挂上一根杆子，就在它正下方、正前方设下考试的栏栅。实际上，我们朝它奔跑了三年上下，而你们始终在为它对我们劝勉、指点——不管你们有没有这个意思，不过我们疑心你们也是身不由己。」是啊；正如力竭的泳者会把眼睛投向远处哪怕一只小船，我听见你们喘着气说：「这位先生无论如何——他一贯那么坚持说好的文学教的是所是者而非所知者——总会带话来说，我们可以仰面漂浮，戏水，享用这片水域而回复精神，不必为着一个目标在其中竞渡。他必定要谴责文学考试，那种考试十之八九把文学仅仅当作知识的事。」

===Original===
IV

I am sorry, Gentlemen: I cannot bring you so much of comfort as all that. I have a love of the past which, because it goes down to the roots, has sometimes been called Radicalism: I could never consent with Bacon’s gibe at antiquity as *pessimum augurium*, and Examinations have a very respectable antiquity. Indeed no university to my knowledge has ever been able in the long run to do without them: and although certain colleges—King’s College here, and New College at Oxford—for long persevered in the attempt, the result was not altogether happy, and in the end they have consigned with custom.

Of course universities have experimented with the *process*. Let me give you two or three ancient examples, which may help you to see (to vary Wordsworth) that though “the Form decays, the function never dies.”

(1) I begin with most ancient Bologna, famous for Civil Law. At Bologna the process of graduation—of admission to the *jus docendi*, “right to teach”—consisted of two parts, the Private Examination and the Public (*conventus*):
===Chinese===
IV

很抱歉，诸位：我带来的安慰，到不了那个份上。我对往昔怀有一种热爱，因为它往根柢里去（radix，『根』），有时竟得了个「激进主义」（Radicalism）的名号：我从来无法附和培根（Bacon）讥诮崇古为最凶之兆（pessimum augurium）的那句话；而考试实在有着一份相当可敬的古远来历。诚然，据我所知，没有哪所大学终局而论离得开它们：虽然某些学院——此地的国王学院（King’s College），以及牛津的新学院（New College）——曾长期坚持不设考试，结果却不算圆满，到头来还是随了旧例。

当然，大学们曾就这套「程序」做过种种实验。让我给你们两三个古老的例子，或许能帮你们看清（套用华兹华斯（Wordsworth）的一句话说，稍加变化）：「形式虽朽，功能不死。」

（1）我从最古老的博洛尼亚（Bologna）说起，它以民法著称。在博洛尼亚，毕业授业的程序——即获授「授教权」（jus docendi，「执教之权」）——分为两部分：私试与公试（conventus，公开会试）：

===Original===
> The private Examination was the real test of competence, the so-called public Examination being in practice a mere ceremony. Before admission to each of these tests the candidate was presented by the Consiliarius of his Nation to the Rector for permission to enter it, and swore that he had complied with all the statutable conditions, that he would give no more than the statutable fees or entertainments to the Rector himself, the Doctor, or his fellow-students, and that he would obey the Rector. Within a period of eight days before the Examination the candidate was presented by “his own” Doctor or by some other Doctor or by two Doctors to the Archdeacon, the presenting Doctor being required to have satisfied himself by private examination of his presentee’s fitness. Early on the morning of the Examination, after attending a Mass of the Holy Ghost, the candidate appeared before the assembled college and was assigned by one of the Doctors present two passages (*puncta*) in the Civil or Canon Law as the case might be. He then retired to his house to study the passages, in doing which it would appear that he had the assistance of the presenting Doctor. Later in the day the Doctors were summoned to the Cathedral, or some other public building, by the Archdeacon, who presided over but took no active part in the ensuing examination. The candidate was then introduced to the Archdeacon and Doctors by the presenting Doctor or Promotor as he was styled. The Prior of the college then administered a number of oaths in which the candidate promised respect to that body and solemnly renounced all the rights of which the college had succeeded in robbing all Doctors of other colleges not included in its ranks. The candidate then gave a lecture or exposition of the two prepared passages: after which he was examined upon them by two of the Doctors appointed by the college. Other Doctors might ask supplementary questions of Law (which they were required to swear that they had not previously communicated to the candidate) arising more indirectly out of the passages selected, or might suggest objections to the answers. With a tender regard for the feelings of their comrades at this “rigorous and tremendous Examination” (as they style it) the Statutes required the Examiner to treat the examinee as *his own son*.

But, knowing what we do of parental discipline in the Middle Ages, we need not take this to enjoin a weak excess of leniency.

> The Examination concluded, the votes of the Doctors present were taken by ballot and the candidate’s fate determined by the majority, the decision being announced by the Archdeacon.

(2) Let us pass to the great and famous University of Paris. At Paris
===Chinese===
> 私试才是对学力的真正检验，所谓公试在实践中不过是一场仪式。参加这两场测验之前，应试者须由本民族团（Nation）的顾问（Consiliarius）引荐于校长（Rector），求得入场之许，并宣誓：他已履行学章规定的全部条件；他给校长本人、诸位博士以及同学们的钱财或宴请，不会超过学章所定之数；他愿服从校长。考试前八日之内，应试者由「他本人的」博士，或由另一位博士，或由两位博士，引荐给副主教（Archdeacon）；引荐的博士须已通过私下考核，确信被荐者堪应试。考试当天一早，应试者先望一场圣灵弥撒，然后出现在齐集一堂的博士团面前，由在场的博士之一指定民法或教会法——视其所攻而定——中的两段（puncta）。他随即退回住处研读这两段；其间看来可得到引荐博士的指点。当天晚些时候，副主教把博士们召集到大教堂或其他公共建筑，由他主持而本人并不实际参与随后的考试。应试者由引荐博士——时称「推荐人」（Promotor）——引到副主教与博士们面前。随后博士团的会长（Prior）主持多项宣誓：应试者承诺敬重该团体，并郑重放弃一切权利——即该博士团设法从未列于本团的其他博士团的诸博士那里攫夺来的那些权利。应试者然后就备好的两段作一次讲授或阐发；随后由博士团指定的两位博士就这两段考问他。其他博士可就所选段落较为间接引出的问题提出法律补充问题（他们须宣誓未曾预先向应试者透露），或可对答词提出异议。学章出于对考生们在这场「严格而可畏的考试」（他们的说法）中的同僚情面的温柔体贴，规定主考须把受试者当作亲生儿子对待。

不过，我们既了解中世纪父辈管教的情形，就不必把这条规定理解成要求一种软弱无度的宽纵。

> 考试既毕，在场的博士以无记名投票表决，应试者的去取由多数定夺，结果由副主教宣布。

（2）让我们转到那所伟大而著名的巴黎大学。在巴黎

===Original===
> In 1275, if not earlier, a preliminary test (or “Responsions”) was instituted to ascertain the fitness of those who wanted to take part in the public performance. At these “Responsions” which took place in the December before the Lent in which the candidate was to determine, he had to dispute in Grammar and Logic with a Master. If this test was passed in a satisfactory manner, the candidate was admitted to the *Examen Baccalariandorum*, Examination for the Baccalaureate, which was conducted by a board of Examiners appointed by each Nation for its own candidates. The duty of the Examiners was twofold, firstly to ascertain by inspecting the *schedules* given by his Masters that the candidate had completed the necessary residence and attended Lectures in the prescribed subjects, and secondly to examine him in the contents of his books. If he passed this Examination, he was admitted to determine.

> Determination was a great day in the student’s university life. It retained much of its primitive character of a student’s festivity. It was not, it would seem, till the middle of the fifteenth century that the student’s Master was required to be officially present at it. The Speech-day of a public school if combined with considerably more than the license of the Oxford Encaenia or degree day here in May week would perhaps be the nearest modern equivalent of these medieval exhibitions of rising talent. Every effort was made to attract to the schools as large an audience as possible, not merely of Masters or fellow-students, but if possible of ecclesiastical dignitaries and other distinguished persons. The friends of a Determiner who was not successful in drawing a more distinguished audience, would run out into the streets and forcibly drag chance passersby into the school. Wine was provided at the Determiner’s expense in the schools: and the day ended in a feast [given in imitation of the Master’s Inception-banquets], even if dancing or torchlight processions were forborne in deference to authority.

I may add here in parenthesis that the thirstiness, always so remarkable in the medieval man whether it make him strange to you or help to ingratiate him as a human brother, seems to have followed him even into the Tripos. “It was not only after a university exercise,” says the historian (Rashdall, Vol. II, p. 687), “but during its progress that the need of refreshment was apt to be felt. … Many Statutes allude—some by way of prohibition, but not always—to the custom of providing wine for the Examiners or Temptator [good word] before, during, or after the Examination. At Heidelberg the Dean of the Faculty might order in drinks, the candidate not. At Leipzig the candidate is forbidden to treat [*facere propinam*] the Examiners *before* the Examination: which seems sound. At Vienna (medical school) he is required to spend a florin ‘*pro confectionibus*.’”
===Chinese===
> 至迟在 1275 年，便已设立一项预试（或称「预备考」〔Responsions〕），用以查明那些想参加公开答辩者是否合格。预试在四旬斋前的十二月举行——应试者预定于该四旬斋中「答辩」（determine）——届时他须与一位教师就文法与逻辑进行论辩。若测验以令人满意的方式通过，应试者即获准入「学士考试」（Examen Baccalariandorum），由各民族团为其本团应试者委任的主考团主持。主考之职有二：其一，查验他诸位教师出具的「簿册」（*schedules*），确证该生已修满必需的居留年限，并听习规定科目的授课；其二，考问他各部书的内容。此项考试既过，他便获准答辩。

> 「答辩日」（Determination）是学生大学生涯中的一个大日子。它保留了许多学生欢庆的原始性质。似乎直到 15 世纪中叶，才要求学生的导师正式到场。公学的演说日（Speech-day）——倘若再加上远过牛津「恩凯尼亚」庆典（Encaenia）、也远过此地五月周学位日的放纵——或许最接近这些中世纪新秀才具的公开展演。人们竭尽所能，要为学堂招来尽可能多的听众：不仅是教师们与同学们，若有可能，还要有教会显贵和其他名流。哪位答辩人若招不来更体面的听众，他的朋友们便跑上街去，硬把过路的闲人拖进学堂。酒由答辩人出资在学堂里备办：这一日以一场盛宴收场〔仿硕士「就任礼」的宴会〕，即便出于对当局的顾忌而免去了舞蹈或火把游行。

请容我在此插一句：中世纪人的口渴——那始终令人瞩目的特质，无论它使你觉得他古怪难解，还是使他作为人类的兄弟而博得你的亲近——似乎一直跟进了荣誉学位考试。「不只在大学仪式之后，」史家（拉什德尔〔Rashdall〕，第 II 卷，第 687 页）说，「就在仪式进行之中，也往往感到需要提神。……许多学章都提到给主考或『考问者』（Temptator〔好一个词〕）备酒的习俗——有的语带禁绝，但并非全都如此——时间或在考前，或考中，或考后。在海德堡，可以叫酒的是学院长，应试者则不可。在莱比锡，禁止应试者在考试『之前』宴请（facere propinam）主考：这条看来很在理。在维也纳（医学院），则要求应试者花一弗罗林『置办蜜饯』（pro confectionibus）。」

===Original===
V

Now when we come to England—that is, to Oxford and Cambridge, which ever had queer ways of their own—we find, strange to say, for centuries no evidence at all of any kind of examination. As for *competitive* examinations like the defunct Mathematical and Classical Triposes here—with Senior Wranglers, Wooden Spoons and what lay between—of all European universities, Louvain alone used the system and may have invented it. At Louvain the candidates for the Mastership were placed in three classes, in each of which the names were arranged in order of merit. The first class were styled *Rigorosi* (Honour-men), the second *Transibiles* (Pass-men), the third *Gratiosi* (Charity-passes); while a fourth class, not publicly announced, contained the names of those who could not be passed on any terms. “*Si autem (quod absit!)*,” says the Statute, “*aliqui inveniantur refutabiles, erant de quarto ordine.*” “These competitive examinations”—I proceed in the historian’s words—“contributed largely to raise Louvain to the high position as a place of learning and education which it retained before the universities were roused from their 15th century torpor by the revival of Learning.” Pope Adrian VI was one of its famous *Primuses*, and Jansen another. The college which produced a *Primus* enjoyed three days’ holiday, during which its bell was rung continuously day and night.

At Oxford and Cambridge (I repeat) we find in their early days no trace of any examination at all. To be sure—and as perhaps you know—the first archives of this university were burned in the “Town and Gown” riots of 1381 by the Townsmen, whose descendants Erasmus describes genially as “combining the utmost rusticity with the utmost malevolence.” But no student will doubt that Cambridge used pretty much the same system as Oxford, and the system was this:—When a candidate presented himself before the Chancellor for a License in Arts, he had to swear that he had heard certain books,5 and nine Regent Masters (besides his own Master, who presented him) were required to depose to their knowledge (*de scientia*) of his sufficiency: and five others to their credence (*de credulitate*), says the Statute. Only in the School of Theology was no room allowed to credulity: there all the Masters had to depose “of their knowledge,” and one black ball excluded.
===Chinese===
V

如今说到英格兰——也就是牛津与剑桥，这两处向来有自家古怪做派的地方——说来奇怪，一连几个世纪，我们找不到任何一种考试的痕迹。至于竞争性考试——比如此地已废的数学荣誉学位考试与古典文学荣誉学位考试那种，有首席数学优等生（Senior Wrangler）、「木勺」（Wooden Spoon，榜尾名次）以及介于其间的种种名次——全欧洲的大学里，唯独鲁汶（Louvain）用过这套制度，而且可能正是它发明的。在鲁汶，申请硕士学位的候选人被分作三等，每等中的名字依成绩高下排列。第一等称作「严格者」（Rigorosi，荣誉生），第二等「通过者」（Transibiles，及格生），第三等「人情通过者」（Gratiosi，恩典及格）；另有不公开宣布的第四等，收录无论怎样也无法放行的人名。学章说：「但若（天不容！）发现有不堪放行者（refutabiles），即归入第四等。」「这些竞争性考试」——我沿用史家的话——「大大促成了鲁汶作为学问与教育之地的崇高地位；这一地位它一直保持到各大学被学术复兴从 15 世纪的昏睡中唤醒之前。」教皇阿德里安六世（Pope Adrian VI）就是它著名的「头名」（Primus）之一，詹森（Jansen）是另一位。出了一位「头名」的学院享有三天假日，其间学院之钟昼夜连敲不息。

在牛津与剑桥（我再说一遍），我们在其早期找不到任何考试的痕迹。诚然——诸位或许知道——本校最早的一批档案，就在 1381 年「镇民与学袍」（Town and Gown）骚乱中被镇民付之一炬；伊拉斯谟（Erasmus）曾温和地形容这些镇民的后代，说他们「把极度的乡野粗鄙与极度的歹毒合于一身」。但没有哪个学生会怀疑，剑桥当年用的制度与牛津大体相同，而那制度是这样的：——候选人到校长（Chancellor）跟前申领文科执照（License in Arts）时，须宣誓听习过某些书籍⁵；还须有九位在任教师（Regent Masters）——连同引荐他的本人导师，后者不在其列——就其学力作「确知」（de scientia）之证；另有五人作「相信」（de credulitate）之证，学章如是说。唯独神学院不容「相信」容身：在那里，所有教师都须就「其所确知」作证，且一粒黑球即可否决。

===Original===
VI

Well, you may urge that this method has a good deal to be said for it. I will go some way to meet you too: but first you must pay me the compliment of supposing me a just man. Being a just man, and there also being presumed in me some acquaintance with English Literature—not indeed much—not necessarily much—but enough to distinguish good writing from bad or, at any rate, real writing from sham, and at least to have an inkling of what these poets and prose-writers were trying to do—why then I declare to you that, after two years’ reading with a man and talk with him about literature, I should have a far better sense of his industry, of his capacity, of his performance and (better) of his promise, than any examination is likely to yield me. In short I could sign him up for a first, second or third class, or as *refutabilis*, with more accuracy and confidence than I could derive from taking him as a stranger and pondering his three or four days’ performance in a Tripos. For some of the best men mature slowly: and some, if not most, of the best writers write slowly because they have a conscience; and the most original minds are just those for whom, in a *literary* examination, it is hardest to set a paper.

But the process (you will admit) might be invidious, might lend itself to misunderstanding, might conceivably even lead to reimposition of an oath forbidding the use of a knife or other sharp implement. And among colleges rivalry is not altogether unknown; and dons, if unlike other men in outward aspect, sometimes resemble them in frailty; and in short I am afraid we shall have to stick to the old system for a while longer. I am sorry, Gentlemen: but you see how it works.
===Chinese===
VI

好吧，你们或可主张：这套办法颇有可取。我也愿意让步几分：但首先，诸位得抬举我一下，假定我是个公正的人。我既是个公正的人，又（姑且假定）对英国文学略有所知——诚然不多——无须很多——但足以分辨文章的好坏，或至少分辨真文章与假文章，至少对这些诗人与散文家想要做什么有个依稀的领会——那么我向诸位宣布：与一个人同读两年、与他谈了两年文学之后，我对他的勤勉、他的才具、他的业绩，以及（更要紧的）他的前景，必有远胜任何考试所能给我的把握。总之，我可以在更准确、更有信心的前提下，签定他为一等、二等或三等，或签为「不堪放行者」（refutabilis），这比我把他当陌生人、揣摩他在荣誉学位考试中那三四天的表现，要可靠得多。因为有些最好的人成熟得慢：而有些作家——纵非多数——写得慢是因为他们有良心；而最具原创性的头脑，恰恰就是那些在「文学」考试中最难为其命题的人。

但这套做法（你们会承认）可能招人忌恨，可能引起误会，甚至可以想见会导致重新立誓、禁用刀剪之类的利器。而学院与学院之间的争竞也并非全然不为人知；学监们在外表上纵与常人不同，脆弱处却有时与常人相仿。总之，恐怕我们还得在旧制度上再支撑一阵。我很抱歉，诸位：但你们已经看得出它是怎么运作的了。

===Original===
VII

Yet—and I admit it—the main objection abides: that, while Literature deals with *What Is* rather than with *What Knows*, Examinations by their very nature test mere Knowledge rather than anything else: that in the hands of a second-rate examiner they tend to test knowledge alone, or what passes for knowledge: and that in the very run of this world most examiners will be second-rate men: which, if we remind ourselves that they receive the pay of fifth-rate ones is, after all, considerably better than we have a right to expect.

We are dealing, mind you, with *English* Literature—our own literature. In examining upon a foreign literature we can artfully lay our stress upon Knowledge and yet neither raise nor risk raising the fatal questions “What is it all *about*?” “What is it, and why is it *it*?”—since merely to translate literally a chorus of the *Agamemnon*, or an ode of Pindar’s, or a passage from Dante or Molière is a creditable performance; to translate either well is a considerable feat; and to translate either perfectly is what you can’t do, and the examiner knows you can’t do, and you know the examiner can’t do, and the examiner knows you know he can’t do. But when we come to a fine thing in our own language—to a stanza from Shelley’s “Adonais” for instance:

> He has outsoared the shadow of our night;
> Envy and calumny and hate and pain,
> And that unrest which men miscall delight,
> Can touch him not and torture not again;
> From the contagion of the world’s slow stain
> He is secure, and now can never mourn
> A heart grown cold, a head grown gray in vain;
> Nor, when the spirit’s self has ceased to burn,
> With sparkless ashes load an unlamented urn.
===Chinese===
VII

然而——我承认——主要的诘难依然成立：文学关涉的是所是者而非所知者，考试却按其本性只检验单纯的知识而别无其他；落到二流考官手里，它们便趋于只考知识，或考那些冒充知识的东西；而在人世的常规中，多数考官注定是二流人物——不过，只要想起他们领的是五流人物的薪水，这毕竟已大大好过我们有权期待的水准了。

请注意，我们打交道的是「英国」文学——我们自己的文学。考外国文学时，我们可以乖巧地把重心放在知识上，而既不必抬起、也不必冒险抬起那个致命的问题：「这一切究竟是『关于什么』的？」「它是什么，而为什么恰是『它』？」——因为逐字直译《阿伽门农》（*Agamemnon*）的一节歌队、品达（Pindar）的一首颂歌、但丁（Dante）或莫里哀（Molière）的一段文字，已是可称道的成绩；译得好是相当的本领；译得完美则是你做不到的事，考官知道你做不到，你也知道考官做不到，考官也知道你知道他做不到。可一旦碰到我们自己语言中的一件妙物——比如雪莱（Shelley）《阿多尼》（“Adonais”）中的一节：

> 他已飞越我们这黑夜的暗影；
> 嫉妒、诽谤、仇恨与苦痛，
> 还有被世人误唤作欢愉的躁动不宁，
> 再也触他不到，再也折磨不成；
> 世道缓慢侵蚀的恶浪毒氛，
> 他已安然躲过，如今永不会哀恸
> 一颗变冷的心，一头枉然斑白的鬓；
> 也不会，当灵魂自身燃尽，
> 把无火的死灰压上无人哀悼的瓮。

===Original===
what can you do with *that*? How can you examine on *that*? Well, yes, you can request the candidate, to “Write a short note on the word *calumny* above,” or ask “From what is it derived?” “What does he know of *Blackwood’s Magazine*?” “Can he quote any parallel allusion in Byron?” You can ask all that: but you are not getting within measurable distance of *it*. Your mind is not even moving on the right plane. Or let me turn back to some light and artless Elizabethan thing—say to the Oenone duet in Peele’s *Arraignment of Paris*:

> **Oenone.** Fair and fair and twice so fair,
> As fair as any may be:
> The fairest shepherd on our green,
> A love for any lady.

> **Paris.** Fair and fair and twice so fair,
> As fair as any may be:
> Thy love is fair for thee alone,
> And for no other lady.

> **Oenone.** My love is fair, my love is gay,
> As fresh as bin the flowers in May,
> And of my love my roundelay,
> My merry merry merry roundelay
> Concludes with Cupid’s curse:
> They that do change old love for new,
> Pray gods they change for worse. …

> My love can pipe, my love can sing,
> My love can many a pretty thing,
> And of his lovely praises ring
> My merry merry merry roundelays
> “Amen” to Cupid’s curse:
> They that do change old love for new
> Pray gods they change for worse.

> **Ambo.** Fair and fair and twice so fair,
> As fair as any may be:
> The fairest shepherd on our green,
> A love for any lady. …
===Chinese===
面对「那个」，你能怎么办？你怎么能拿「那个」来考试？好吧，是的，你可以要求考生「就上文 calumny（诽谤）一词写一则短注」，或问「此词源出何处？」「他对《布莱克伍德杂志》（*Blackwood’s Magazine*）知道些什么？」「他能引拜伦（Byron）诗中平行的典故吗？」这些你都可以问：但你离「那个」还远得没有影儿。你的心智甚至根本没有在正确的层面上运行。或者，让我折回某种轻盈质朴的伊丽莎白时代的小东西——比如皮尔（Peele）《巴黎的审判》（*The Arraignment of Paris*）里俄诺涅（Oenone）的那段二重唱：

> **俄诺涅.** 美又美，美上加美，
> 谁能有这般美貌：
> 我们草地上最美的牧人，
> 哪位小姐都值得他钟情。

> **帕里斯.** 美又美，美上加美，
> 谁能有这般美貌：
> 你的爱人只属你一人，
> 不为别的淑女倾心。

> **俄诺涅.** 我的爱人俊，我的爱人欢，
> 像五月的鲜花一样新鲜；
> 我为我的爱人轮唱小曲，
> 我欢欢喜喜、欢欢喜喜的小曲
> 终结于丘比特的咒语：
> 谁把旧爱换作新欢，
> 祈求诸神叫他越换越糟。……

> 我的爱人会吹笛，我的爱人会歌唱，
> 我的爱人会许许多多俏皮花样；
> 他可爱的美名四处鸣响，
> 我欢欢喜喜、欢欢喜喜的轮唱曲
> 为丘比特的咒语道一声「阿门」：
> 谁把旧爱换作新欢，
> 祈求诸神叫他越换越糟。

> **二重唱（Ambo）.** 美又美，美上加美，
> 谁能有这般美貌：
> 我们草地上最美的牧人，
> 哪位小姐都值得他钟情。……

===Original===
How can anyone examine on *that*? How can anyone solemnly explain, in a hurry, answering one of five or six questions selected from a three hours’ paper, just why and how that hits him? And yet, if it hit him not, he is lost. If even so simple a thing as that—a thing of silly sooth—do not hit him, he is all unfit to traffic with literature.

VIII

You see how delicate a business it is. Examination in Literature, being by its very nature so closely tied down to be a test of *Knowledge*, can hardly, save when used by genius, with care, be any final test of that which is better than Knowledge, of that which is the crown of all scholarship, of *understanding*.

But do not therefore lose heart, even in your reading for strict purposes of examination. Our talk is of reading. Let me fetch you some comfort from the sister and correlative, but harder, art of writing.
===Chinese===
谁能拿「那个」来考试？谁能仓促之间、从一个三小时试卷所出的五六道题里拣出一道来作答时，庄严地解释清楚「那个」究竟为什么、又如何击中了他？然而，若「那个」击不中他，他便完了。倘若连这样简单的东西——一件痴憨而真纯的小物事（silly sooth）——都击不中他，那他全然不配与文学打交道。

VIII

诸位看得出，这是件多么精微的事。文学考试按其本性被紧紧捆在「知识」的检验上，除非出自天才之手且加意为之，几乎不可能是对那优于知识的东西、对一切学问的冠冕——「理解」——的最终检验。

但不要因此灰心，即便你们在为严格的应试目的而阅读时。我们谈的是阅读。让我从姊妹的、相关的、却更难的艺术——写作——那里，给你们捎来一点安慰。

===Original===
I most potently believe that the very best writing, in verse or in prose, can only be produced in moments of high excitement, or rather (as I should put it) in those moments of still and solemn awe into which a noble excitement lifts a man. Let me speak only of prose, of which you may more cautiously allow this than of verse. I think of St. Paul’s glorious passage, as rendered in the Authorised Version, concluding the 15th chapter of his First Epistle to the Corinthians. First, as you know, comes the long, swaying, scholastic, somewhat sophisticated argument about the evidence of resurrection; about the corn, “that which thou sowest,” the vivification, the change in vivification, and the rest. All this, almost purely argumentative, should be read quietly, with none of the bravura which your prize reader lavishes on it. The argument works up quietly—at once tensely and sinuously, but very quietly—to conviction. Then comes the hush; and then the authoritative voice speaking out of it, awful and slow, “Behold, I show you a mystery” … and then, all the latent emotion of faith taking hold and lifting the man on its surge, “For the trumpet shall sound, and the dead shall be raised incorruptible” … and so, incorruption tolling down corruption, the trumpet smashes death underfoot in victory: until out of the midst of tumult, sounds the recall; sober, measured, claiming the purified heart back to discipline. “Therefore, my beloved brethren, be ye steadfast, unmoveable, always abounding in the work of the Lord, forasmuch as ye know that your labour is not in vain in the Lord.”

I think of that triumphant passage. I think of the sentences with which Isaak Walton ends his life of Donne. I think of the last pages of Motley’s *Dutch Republic*, with its eulogy on William the Silent so exquisitely closing:

> As long as he lived, he was the guiding-star of a whole brave nation, and when he died the little children cried in the streets.
===Chinese===
我极其坚定地相信：最好的诗或散文，只能在高亢激越的时刻写出，或者（照我的说法）在庄严静穆的敬畏时刻写出——高贵的激动把人举升到那样的时刻。让我只谈散文；比起诗来，诸位对散文承认这一点可以更从容些。我想起圣保罗（St. Paul）那段辉煌的文字——钦定版圣经（Authorised Version）所译《哥林多前书》（First Epistle to the Corinthians）第十五章的收尾。首先，诸位知道，是关于复活之证据的那段悠长、摇曳、经院气的、略带机巧的论证：关于那粒「你所种的」籽粒，关于赋生，关于赋生中的改变，等等。这些几乎纯属论辩的文字应当安静地读，不要带半点你们那些得奖朗诵者挥洒其上的炫技。论证安静地步步推进——既紧绷又蜿蜒，却极其安静地——推向信服。随后是静默；随后，那权威的声音自静默中响起，威严而迟缓：「看哪，我把一件奥秘告诉你们」……随后，信仰的全部潜藏情感攫住了他，把他举上它的浪头：「号筒要响，死人要复活成为不朽坏的」……就这样，不朽坏将朽坏一路敲落，号筒在得胜中把死亡踩碎于脚下：直到喧嚣的中途响起收兵号——清醒的、有分寸的，把炼净的心收回纪律。「所以，我亲爱的弟兄们，你们务要坚固，不可摇动，常常竭力多做主的工作，因为知道你们的劳苦在主里面不是徒然的。」

我想起那段凯旋的文字。我想起伊扎克·沃尔顿（Izaak Walton）为多恩（Donne）作传的收束之句。我想起莫特利（Motley）《荷兰共和国》（*Dutch Republic*）的最后几页，那篇对沉默者威廉（William the Silent）的颂词收束得何等精妙：

> 只要他活着，他便是整个勇敢民族的指路星；他死去时，街头的小儿为之啼哭。

===Original===
I think of two great prose passages in Thackeray’s *Esmond*; of Landor’s *Dream of Boccaccio* … and so on: and I am sure that, in prose or in verse, the best that man can utter flows from him either in moments of high mental excitement or in the hush of that *Altitudo* to which high excitement lifts him.

But, first now, observe how all these passages—and they are the first I call to mind—rise like crests on a large bulk of a wave—St. Paul’s on a labouring argument about immortality; Motley’s at the conclusion of a heavy task. Long campaigning brings the reward of Harry Esmond’s return to Castlewood, long intrigue of the author’s mind with his characters closes that febrile chapter in which Harry walks home to break the news of the death of the Duke of Hamilton—in the early morning through Kensington, where the newsboys are already shouting it:

> The world was going to its business again, although dukes lay dead and ladies mourned for them. … So day and night pass away, and tomorrow comes, and our place knows us not. Esmond thought of the courier now galloping on the north road to inform him, who was Earl of Arran yesterday, that he was Duke of Hamilton today, and of a thousand great schemes, hopes, ambitions, that were alive in the gallant heart, beating but a few hours since, and now in a little dust quiescent.
===Chinese===
我想起萨克雷（Thackeray）《埃斯蒙德》（*Esmond*）里两段伟大的散文；想起兰多（Landor）的《薄伽丘之梦》（*Dream of Boccaccio*）……等等；我确信，无论散文还是诗，人所能道出的最好的东西，要么从他精神高亢激越的时刻涌出，要么在那高亢激越把他举升到的「高处」（Altitudo）的静默中涌出。

但现在先请注意：所有这些段落——它们是我信手拈来的头几个——都像浪峰一样，隆起于一整大片浪体之上：圣保罗的隆起于关于不朽的一段艰苦论证，莫特利的隆起于一桩沉重劳作的收尾。漫长的征战换来哈里·埃斯蒙德（Harry Esmond）回到卡斯尔伍德（Castlewood）的报偿；作者的心灵与人物的长久纠结，逼出那热病似的一章：哈里清晨步行回家，穿过肯辛顿（Kensington），去报汉密尔顿公爵（Duke of Hamilton）的死讯——那里报童已经在叫卖了：

> 世人又各奔各的营生了，虽然公爵们僵卧于地下，贵妇们还在为他们哀悼。……白昼与黑夜就这样逝去，明天到来，我们的位置便不认得我们。埃斯蒙德想起此刻正沿北道疾驰的信使，他要报信给那个人——昨天还是阿兰伯爵，今天已是汉密尔顿公爵——想起那颗豪勇的心里千百样宏伟的图谋、希望与抱负：几小时前还在跳动，如今已在一小撮尘灰里安息。

===Original===
And on top of this let me assure you that in writing, or learning to write, solid daily practice is the prescription and “waiting upon inspiration” a lure. These crests only rise on the back of constant labour. Nine days, according to Homer, Leto travailed with Apollo: but he was Apollo, lord of Song. I *know* this to be true of ordinary talent: but, supposing you all to be geniuses, I am almost as sure that it holds of genius. Listen to this:

> Napoleon I used to say that battles were won by the sudden flashing of an idea through the brain of a commander at a certain critical instant. The capacity for generating this sudden electric spark was military genius. … Napoleon seems always to have counted upon it, always to have believed that when the critical moment arrived the wild confusion of the battlefield would be illuminated for him by that burst of sudden flame. But if Napoleon had been ignorant of the prosaic business of his profession, *to which he attended more closely than any other commander*, would these moments of supreme clearness have availed him, or would they have come to him at all?

My author thinks not: and I am sure he is right. So, in writing, only out of long preparation can come the truly triumphant flash: and I ask you to push this analogy further, into the business of reading, even of reading for examination. You learn to discipline yourselves, you acquire the art of marshalling, of concentrating, driving your knowledge upon a point: and—for you are young—that point is by no means the final point. Say that it is only an examination, and silly at that. Still you have been learning the art, you have been training yourself to be, for a better purpose, effective.
===Chinese===
在此之上，让我向诸位担保：在写作、或学习写作时，扎扎实实的日课才是处方，「坐等灵感光临」只是诱饵。这些浪峰只在持续劳作的背上隆起。据荷马（Homer）说，勒托（Leto）怀阿波罗（Apollo），阵痛了九天：但他终究是阿波罗，歌之神主。我「知道」这对寻常才具是真话：但纵然假定诸位个个都是天才，我也几乎同样肯定，这话于天才亦然。请听：

> 拿破仑一世常说，战役的胜利靠的是某一观念在某一危急的瞬间倏然闪过统帅的脑际。能产生这道倏然电火花的能力，便是军事天才。……拿破仑似乎始终把赌注押在它上面，始终相信：当危急时刻来临，战场上那一片狂乱的混沌，会由那道倏然而起的火光照亮。然而，倘若拿破仑对自己这一行的日常事务一无所知——「而他对这些事务的留意，比任何一位统帅都更紧」——这些至为澄明的时刻还能派上用场吗？它们根本还会降临到他身上吗？

我的作者以为不然：而我确信他是对的。同样，在写作中，唯有从长久的准备里，才可能迸出那真正凯旋的电光：我请诸位把这层类比再往前推一步，推到阅读的事务上——乃至推到为考试而阅读上。你们学会约束自己，习得整军、聚拢、把知识驱赶到一点上的技艺；而——因为你们年轻——那一点绝非终点。就算它只是一场考试，而且还是场傻气十足的考试。可你们毕竟学得了这门技艺，你们一直在训练自己——为了更好的目的——做到切实有效。

===Original===
IX

Yet, and when this has been granted, the crucial question abides and I must not shirk it—“you say that the highest literature deals with *What Is* rather than with *What Knows*. It is all very fine to assure us that testing our knowledge *about* Literature and *around* Literature, and on this side or that side of Literature, is healthy for us in some oblique way: but can you examiners examine, or can you not, on Literature in what you call its own and proper category of *What Is*?”

So I hear the question—the question which beats and has beaten, over and over again, good men trying to construct Schools of English in our universities.

With all sense of a responsibility, of a difficulty, that has lain on my mind for these five years, I answer, Gentlemen, “Yes, we ought: yes, we can: and yes, we will.”

But, for the achievement, we teachers must first know how to teach. When that is learned, Examination will come as a consequent, easy, almost trivial matter. I will, for example—having already allowed how *hard* it is to examine on literature—take the difficulty at its very extreme. I will select a piece of poetry, and the poet shall be Keats—on whom, if on anyone, is felt the temptation to write gush and loose aesthetic chatter. A pupil comes to read with me, and I open at the famous “Ode to a Grecian Urn.”
===Chinese===
IX

然而，纵使这一切得到承认，关键的问题依然在，我不可回避——「你说最高的文学关涉所是者而非所知者。你向我们保证：考我们『关于』文学的知识、『环着』文学的知识、在文学这一侧或那一侧的知识，以某种迂回的方式于我们有益——这些都好听得很：可你们这些考官究竟能不能——能不能——就文学本身、就你们所称它固有的正当范畴『所是者』——来考试？」

我便这样听见这个问题——这个问题正一再敲打、并且一直在敲打着我们大学里那些力图建立英文学院（School of English）的好人。

带着全部的责任感、全部的难处感——它们在我心头压了五年——我回答，诸位：「是的，我们应当：是的，我们能够：是的，我们必将做到。」

但要做成这件事，我们做教师的必须先学会怎样教。一旦学会，考试便会作为结果随之而来，轻易，几乎无足挂齿。举例说——我既已承认就文学出题何等之「难」——我且把这难处推到极处。我选一首诗，而诗人就定济慈（Keats）——在他身上，若说在谁身上，最容易诱人写下滥情与散漫的美学闲话。一个学生来同我共读，我便翻到那首著名的《希腊古瓮颂》（“Ode to a Grecian Urn”）。

===Original===
(1) We read it through together, perhaps twice; at the second attempt getting the emphasis right, and some, at any rate, of the modulations of voice. So we reach a working idea of the Ode and what Keats meant it to be.

(2) We then compare it with his other Odes, and observe that it is (a) regular in stanza form, (b) in spite of its outburst in the third stanza—“More happy love! more happy, happy love” etc.—much severer in tone than, e.g., the “Ode to a Nightingale” or the “Ode to Psyche,” (c) that the emotion is not luscious, but simple, (d) that this simplicity is Hellenic, so far as Keats can compass it, and (e) eminently well-suited to its subject, which is a carven urn, gracious but severe of outline; a moment of joy caught by the sculptor and arrested, for time to perpetuate; yet—and this is the point of the Ode—conveying a sense that innocent gaiety is not only its own excuse, but of human things one of the few eternal—and eternal just because it is joyous and fleeting.

(3) Then we go back and compare this kind of quiet immortal beauty with the passionate immortality hymned in the “Nightingale Ode”:

> Thou wast not born for death, immortal Bird!
> No hungry generations tread thee down …

with all the rest of that supreme stanza: from which (with some passages my reading supplies to illustrate the difference) we fall to contrasting the vibrating thrill of the “Nightingale” with the happy grace of the “Grecian Urn” and, allowing each to be appropriate, dispute for a while, perhaps, over the merits of classical calm and romantic thrill.
===Chinese===
（1）我们一起通读一遍，也许两遍；第二遍时把重音读准，至少也读出若干声调的抑扬转折。由此我们得到一个可用的观念：这首颂歌是什么，济慈要它成为什么。

（2）然后我们把它与其余几首颂歌对读，观察到：（a）它节式匀整；（b）尽管第三节有「更幸福的爱情！更幸福、更幸福的爱情」那样的迸发，其格调却比《夜莺颂》（“Ode to a Nightingale”）或《普绪克颂》（“Ode to Psyche”）严峻得多；（c）它的情感不腻甜，而是单纯；（d）这单纯是希腊气质的——就济慈所能企及者而言；（e）它与题材极为相称：那题材是一座雕成的古瓮，轮廓秀雅而严峻；是雕塑家擒住并定格的一个欢乐瞬间，留给时间去使之永存；然而——这正是全诗的命意——它传递出一种感受：无邪的欢愉不但是自身的理由，而且是人类事物中为数不多的永恒之一——而其永恒，恰恰因为它欢愉而倏忽。

（3）随后我们回过头，把这种静穆的永生之美，与《夜莺颂》所颂扬的那份炽烈的永生对勘：

> 你不是为死亡而生，不朽的鸟！
> 饥馁的世代踩不倒你……

连同那一节至高诗行的其余部分：由此（再加上我本人的阅读所提供来申明差别的若干段落），我们开始对照《夜莺》的震颤激越与《希腊古瓮》的欢愉秀雅；既承认各自得其所哉，或许还要争一争古典的静穆与浪漫的激越孰优孰劣。

===Original===
(4) From this we proceed to examine the Ode in detail line by line: which examination brings up a whole crowd of questions, such as:

(a) We have a thought enounced in the first stanza. Does the Ode go on to develop and amplify it, as an Ode should? Or does Pegasus come down again and again on the prints from which he took off? If he do this, and the action of the Ode be dead and unprogressive, is the defect covered by beauty of language? Can such defect ever be so covered?

(b) Lines 15 and 16 anticipate lines 21–24, which are saying the same thing and getting no forwarder.
===Chinese===
（4）由此我们进而逐行细察这首颂歌：这一细察牵出成堆的问题，比如：

（a）第一节申明了一个思想。颂歌接着是否像一首颂歌该做的那样，去展开它、扩大它？还是说，珀伽索斯（Pegasus）一次又一次落回它起跳的蹄印？倘若如此，而颂歌的行动又是死的、不前进的，这缺陷能否被语言之美遮住？这样的缺陷果真能被这样遮住吗？

（b）第 15、16 行预告了第 21 至 24 行的内容，后者重复着同一件事，并无寸进。

===Original===
(c) We come to the lines

> What little town by river or sea shore,
> Or mountain-built with peaceful citadel,
> Is emptied of this folk, this pious morn?

with the answering lines

> And, little town, thy streets for evermore
> Will silent be; and not a soul to tell
> Why thou art desolate, can e’er return.

and we note Sir Sidney Colvin’s suggestion that this breaks in upon an arrest of art as though it were an arrest on reality: and remember that he raised a somewhat similar question over “The Nightingale”; and comparing them, discuss truth of emotion against truth of reality.

===Chinese===
（c）我们读到这样的诗行：

> 这小小的城镇，临河或傍海，
> 或筑于山上，围着安谧的堡楼，
> 谁把它的人们清空，在这虔敬的清晨？

以及与之相续的诗行：

> 而小城啊，你的街道将永远
> 寂静；再没有一个灵魂，能回来
> 诉说你的荒凉。

我们注意到西德尼·科尔文爵士（Sir Sidney Colvin）的提法：说这里忽然闯进来一笔，把艺术的定格当成了现实的定格；并且记得他就《夜莺》也提出过略相类似的问题；两相对照，我们便讨论情感之真与现实之真的对峙。

===Original===
We come to the last stanza and lament “O Attic shape! Fair attitude” for its jingle: but note how the poet recovers himself and brings the whole to a grand close.

I have, even yet, mentioned but a few of the points. For one, I have omitted its most beautiful vowel-play, on which teacher and pupil can dwell and learn together. And heaven forbid that as a teacher I should *insist* even on half of those I have indicated. A teacher, as I hold, should watch for what his pupil divines of his own accord; but if, trafficking with works of inspiration, he have no gift to catch that inspiration nor power to pass it on, then I say “Heaven help him! but he has no valid right on earth to be in the business.”

And if a teacher have all these chances of teaching—mind you, of *accurate* teaching—supplied him by a single Ode of Keats, do you suppose we cannot set in an Examination paper one intelligent question upon it, in its own lawful category?
===Chinese===
我们读到末节，为「哦阿提卡的形！美的姿态！」那一声谐音的叮当而惋惜：但请注意诗人如何稳住自己，把全诗收束得堂而皇之。

到此为止，我所举的还只是寥寥数端。单举一项：我漏掉了它最美的元音回环，师生可以在那里流连，一同领会。但上天不容我这个做教师的，把我列举的这些硬要学生记到一半。依我看，教师应当留意学生自己悟到的东西；但倘若他同灵感之作打交道，却既无才具捕捉那灵感，又无能力传递它，那我就要说：「上天救他吧！但他在世上绝无正当的权利操此营生。」

而倘若一册济慈颂歌里的一首，就能给教师提供这么多教授的机会——请注意，是「精确」教授的机会——那你们还以为，我们不能在一份试卷里，就它所属的正当固有范畴，出一个有见识的问题吗？

===Original===
Gentlemen, with the most scrupulous tenderness for aged and even decrepit interests, we have been trying to liberate you from certain old bad superstitions and silently laying the stones of a new School of English, which we believe to be worthy even of Cambridge.

Our proposals are before the University. Should they be passed, still everything will depend on the loyalty of its teachers to the idea; and on that enthusiasm which I suppose to be the nurse of all studies and know to be the authentic cherishing nurse of ours. We may even have conceded too much to the letter, but we have built and built our trust on the spirit “which maketh alive.”
===Chinese===
诸位，我们本着对陈年乃至于衰朽利益的极其审慎的体恤，一直在设法把诸位从某些陈腐的恶迷信里解放出来，并默默为一所新的英文学院垒下基石——我们相信，这所学院即便配剑桥也不为过。

我们的提案已呈大学。即便通过，一切仍将系于其教师对这一理念的忠诚；系于那种热忱——我猜想它是一切学问的乳母，并且知道它是我们这门学问真正的养育乳母。我们或许甚至在「字句」上让步过多，但我们的信赖是建造、再建造在那「叫人活的」精神上。

## Lecture VI On a School of English / 第六讲 论英文学院

===Original===
Wednesday, October 17, 1917

I

It is now, Gentlemen, five years less a term since, feeling (as they say of other offenders) my position acutely, I had the honour of reading an Inaugural before this University and the impudence to loose, in the course of it, a light shaft against a phrase in the very Ordinance defining the duties of this Chair.

> "It shall be the duty of the professor," says the Ordinance, "to deliver courses of lectures on English Literature from the age of Chaucer onwards, and otherwise to promote, so far as may be in his power, the study in the University of the subject of English Literature."

That was the phrase at which I glanced—"the subject of English Literature"; and I propose that we start today, for reasons that will appear, by subjecting this subject to some examination.

===Chinese===
1917 年 10 月 17 日，星期三

I

诸位先生，距今将近五年——满五年还差一个学期——我（像人们对其他罪犯所说的那样）深觉处境尴尬，却在这所大学面前有幸宣读了一篇就职演讲（Inaugural），并且厚着脸皮，在演讲过程中，朝界定本讲座职责的那份条例（Ordinance）里的一个措辞，射出了一支轻飘飘的箭。

> 「教授的职责，」条例写道，「是讲授自乔叟（Chaucer）时代以降英国文学的课程系列，并在其力所能及的范围内，以其他方式促进本校对英国文学这一科目（subject）的研究。」

我当时瞥向的正是这个措辞——「英国文学这一科目」；我提议今天就从这里起步，把这门科目拿来审察一番——个中缘由随后自会分明。

===Original===
II

"The *Subject* of English Literature." Surely—for a start—there is no such thing; or rather, may we not say that everything is, has been or can be, a subject of English Literature? Man's loss of Paradise has been a subject of English Literature, and so has been a Copper Coinage in Ireland, and so has been Roast Sucking-pig, and so has been Holy Dying, and so has been Mr. Pepys's somewhat unholy living, and so have been Ecclesiastical Polity, The Grail, Angling for Chub, The Wealth of Nations, The Sublime and the Beautiful, The Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire, Prizefights, Grecian Urns, Modern Painters, Intimations of Immortality in early Childhood, Travels with a Donkey, Rural Rides and Rejected Addresses—*all* these have been subjects of English Literature: as have been human complots and intrigues as wide asunder as *Othello* and *The School for Scandal*; persons as different as Prometheus and Dr. Johnson, Imogen and Moll Flanders, Piers the Plowman and Mr. Pickwick; places as different as Utopia and Cranford, Laputa and Reading Gaol. *Epipsychidion* is literature: but so is *A Tale of a Tub*.

===Chinese===
II

「英国文学的*科目*。」可以断言——首先——根本不存在这样一种东西；或者毋宁说，我们何不这样讲：一切事物都是、都曾经是、或都可能是英国文学的科目？人类失去乐园曾是一个科目，爱尔兰铜币铸造曾是，烤乳猪曾是，《圣洁之死》曾是，佩普斯（Pepys）先生那不大圣洁的*平生*也曾是；教会政制（Ecclesiastical Polity）、圣杯、垂钓鲦鱼、《国富论》、《崇高与美》、《罗马帝国衰亡史》、职业拳赛、希腊古瓮、《近代画家》、童年时代的不朽暗示（Intimations of Immortality）、《驴背旅行》、《乡间骑行》、《落选的致辞》——*所有*这些都曾是英国文学的科目：还有人间种种密谋与纠葛，相去之远如《奥赛罗》（*Othello*）与《造谣学校》（*The School for Scandal*）；还有种种人物，悬殊如普罗米修斯（Prometheus）与约翰逊博士（Dr. Johnson）、伊摩琴（Imogen）与摩尔·弗兰德斯（Moll Flanders）、农夫皮尔斯（Piers the Plowman）与匹克威克先生（Mr. Pickwick）；还有种种地方，迥异如乌托邦（Utopia）与克兰福德（Cranford）、飞岛国（Laputa）与雷丁监狱（Reading Gaol）。《心之灵》（*Epipsychidion*）是文学：《一只桶的故事》（*A Tale of a Tub*）也是。

===Original===
Listen, for this is literature:

> If some king of the earth have so large an extent of dominion, in north, and south, so that he hath winter and summer together in his dominions, so large an extent east and west as that he hath day and night together in his dominions, much more hath God mercy and judgement together: He brought light out of darkness, not out of a lesser light; he can bring thy summer out of winter, though thou have no spring; though in the ways of fortune, or understanding, or conscience, thou have been benighted till now, wintered and frozen, clouded and eclipsed, damped and benumbed, smothered and stupefied till now, now God comes to thee, not as in the dawning of the day, not as in the bud of the spring, but as the sun at noon to illustrate all shadows, as the sheaves in harvest, to fill all penuries, all occasions invite his mercies, and all times are his seasons.6

But listen again, for this also is literature:

> A sweet disorder in the dress
> Kindles in clothes a wantonness:
> A lawn about the shoulders thrown
> Into a fine distraction:
> An erring lace, which here and there
> Enthrals the crimson stomacher:
> A cuff neglectful, and thereby
> Ribbons to flow confusedly:
> A winning wave, deserving note,
> In the tempestuous petticoat:
> A careless shoestring, in whose tie
> I see a wild civility:
> Do more bewitch me than when art
> Is too precise in every part.

Here again is literature:

> When I was a child, at seven years old, my friends on a holiday filled my pockets with coppers. I went directly to a shop where they sold toys for children; and being charmed with the sound of a whistle that I met by the way in the hands of another boy, I voluntarily offered him all my money for one. I then came home and went whistling all over the house, much pleased with my whistle but disturbing all the family. My brothers and sisters and cousins, understanding the bargain I had made, told me I had given four times as much for it as it was worth… The reflection gave me more chagrin than the whistle gave me pleasure.
> — —Benjamin Franklin

===Chinese===
请听，这是文学：

> 倘若地上某位君王疆域广袤，南北延伸，以致冬夏同在其版图之内；东西辽阔，以致昼夜同在其版图之内；那么，上帝把怜悯与审判同聚一身，就更不在话下：他使光出于黑暗，而非出于较小的光；他能叫你的夏天出于冬天，哪怕你没有春天；纵使在命运之途、悟性之途或良知之途上，你直到如今一直身处黑夜，严冬冻馁，阴云遮蔽，湿冷麻木，窒息昏迷直到如今——如今上帝临到你，不像天光破晓，不像春芽初绽，却像正午的太阳照亮一切阴影，像收割时的庄稼捆填满一切匮乏；一切际遇都在邀请他的怜悯，一切时令都是他的季节。6

请再听，这也是文学：

> 衣着上一点甜美的散漫，
> 在衣衫里燃起一段风流：
> 一方细麻纱漫过双肩，
> 荡出绝妙的凌乱：
> 一根系错了的饰带，这里那里
> 缠住了绯红的胸衣：
> 一只漫不经心的袖口，任那
> 缎带纷乱地飘垂：
> 一道动人的波浪，值得称道，
> 起伏在那狂暴的衬裙之中：
> 一根马虎的鞋带，从它的系结里
> 我看出一种狂野的礼度：
> 这些比技艺——
> 每一处都过分精确——更令我销魂。

这里又是文学：

> 我小时候，七岁那年，朋友们在假日里把我的口袋塞满了铜币。我径直去了一家卖儿童玩具的铺子；路上见到另一个男孩手里吹着一只哨子，那声音令我着迷，我便主动把所有的钱都给他换了一只。随后我回到家，一路吹着哨满屋子跑，对自己的哨子满意极了，却搅得全家不得安宁。我的兄弟姐妹和表亲们得知我做的这笔交易，告诉我我出的价钱是它价值的四倍……这番反省给我的懊恼，超过了哨子给我的快乐。
> ——本杰明·富兰克林（Benjamin Franklin）

===Original===
Of a bridal, this is literature:

> Open the temple gates unto my love,
> Open them wide that she may enter in!

But so also is Suckling's account of a wedding that begins

> I tell thee, Dick, where I have been.

This is literature:

> And a man shall be as an hiding place from the wind, and a covert from the tempest;
> As rivers of water in a dry place,
> As the shadow of a great rock in a weary land.

===Chinese===
写新娘的，这是文学：

> 为我所爱敞开庙宇之门，
> 把门敞开，好让她进来！

但萨克林（Suckling）记一场婚礼的诗也同样是文学，开头的句子是

> 迪克，我对你说，我方才去了哪里。

这是文学：

> 必有一人像避风所，像避暴雨的隐密处；
> 又像河流在干旱之地，
> 像大磐石的影子在疲乏之地。

===Original===
But so is this literature:

> One circle cannot touch another circle on the outside at more points than one.
> For, if it be possible, let the circle ACK touch the circle ABC at the points A, C. Join AC.
> Then because the two points A, C are in the circumference of the circle ACK the line which joins them falls within that circle.
> But the circle ACK is without the circle ABC. Therefore the straight line AC is without the circle ABC.
> But because the two points A, C are in the circumference of ABC therefore the straight line AC falls within that circle. *Which is absurd.*
> Therefore one circle cannot touch another on the outside at more points than one.

All thoughts, as well as all passions, all delights

> votum, timor, ira, voluptas—

whatsoever, in short, engages man's activity of soul or body, may be deemed the subject of literature and is transformed into literature by process of recording it in memorable speech. It is so, it has been so, and God forbid it should ever not be so!

===Chinese===
而这难道就不是文学吗：

> 一个圆与另一个圆外切，切点不能多于一个。
> 因为，倘若有此可能，设圆 ACK 与圆 ABC 相切于 A、C 两点。连接 AC。
> 于是，由于 A、C 两点在圆 ACK 的圆周上，连接这两点的线便落在该圆之内。
> 但圆 ACK 在圆 ABC 之外，故直线 AC 在圆 ABC 之外。
> 然而，由于 A、C 两点在圆 ABC 的圆周上，直线 AC 便落在该圆之内。*此为荒谬。*
> 所以一个圆与另一个圆外切，切点不能多于一个。

一切思想，正如一切激情、一切欢愉——

> *votum, timor, ira, voluptas*（愿望、恐惧、愤怒、欢愉）——

总之，凡能唤起人灵魂或身体之活动的，都可以视为文学的科目；而以令人难忘的言辞将它记录下来的过程，便把它转化成了文学。事实如此，历来如此，但愿上帝保佑它永远如此！

===Original===
III

Now this, put so, is (you will say) so extremely obvious that it must needs hide a fallacy or at best a quibble on a word. I shall try to show that it does not: that it directly opposes plain truth to a convention accepted by the Ordinance, and that the fallacy lies in that convention.

A convention may be defined as something which a number of men have agreed to accept in lieu of the truth and to pass off for the truth upon others: I was about to add, preferably when they can catch them young: but some recent travel in railway trains and listening to the kind of stuff men of mature years deliver straight out of newspapers for the products of their own digested thought have persuaded me that the ordinary man is as susceptible at fifty, sixty, or even seventy as at any earlier period of growth, and that the process of incubation is scarcely less rapid.

I am not, to be sure, concerned to deny that there may be conventions useful enough to society, serving it to maintain government, order, public and private decency, or the commerce on which it must needs rest to be a civilised society at all—commerce of food, commerce of clothing, and so on, up to commerce in knowledge and ideas. Government itself—any form of it—is a convention; marriage is a convention; money of course is a convention, and the alphabet itself I suppose to contain as many conventions as all the old Courts of Love and Laws of Chivalry put together, and our English alphabet one tremendous fallacy, that twenty-six letters, separately or in combination are capable of symbolising all the sounds produced by an Englishman's organs of speech, let alone the sounds he hears from foreigners, dogs, guns, steam-engines, motor-horns and other friends and enemies to whom we deny the franchise. Also of course it ignores the whole system of musical notes—another convention—which yet with many of the older bards could hardly be separated from the words they used, though now only the words survive and as literature.

===Chinese===
III

诸位会说，这番道理摆到这个份上，浅显得未免过分，背后必定藏着一个谬误，至多不过是抠字眼。我要试着说明并非如此：它是拿平白的真理，去正面对抗那份条例所认可的一套约定俗成，而谬误恰恰藏在那套约定里。

所谓约定俗成，不妨定义为：一群人商定接受某种东西来代替真理，再把它当作真理兜售给别人——我本想补一句：最好趁他们年幼、便于捉住的时候下手；但近来几次乘火车旅行，听到一批成年男子把报纸上的东西原样端出来，冒充自己消化的思想产物，这使我确信：寻常人在五十岁、六十岁甚至七十岁上，同他成长的任何早期阶段一样易于感染，而且孵化的过程一点也不见得慢。

诚然，我无意否认世上可能有对社会足够有用的约定：它帮助社会维持统治、秩序、公共与私人的体面，或维系那种文明社会赖以立足的商业往来——粮食的商业、衣着的商业，直至知识与观念的商业。统治本身——无论何种形式——是一种约定；婚姻是一种约定；钱币当然是一种约定；而字母表本身，我猜它所含的约定，比古老的爱情法庭与骑士法则加起来还多；何况我们的英文字母表还包含一个天大的谬误，即假定二十六个字母，无论单独使用还是组合使用，足以标写英国人发音器官发出的一切声音，更遑论他从外国人、狗、枪炮、蒸汽机、汽车喇叭以及其他被我们拒于门外、不给公民权的朋友与敌人那里听来的声音。当然，它还全然无视整套音乐记谱法——那又是一套约定——而在许多古代行吟诗人那里，这套记谱法同他们的歌词几乎无法分开，虽然如今传下来的只剩歌词，而且是以文学的身份传下来的。

===Original===
IV

But every convention has a fallacy somewhere at the root; whether it be useful and operative, as many a legal fiction is operative, for good; or senile, past service yet tyrannous by custom, and so pernicious; or merely foolish, as certain artistic conventions are traceable, when a Ruskin comes to judgment, back to nothing better than folly: and it becomes men of honest mind, in dealing with anything recognisable as a convention, to examine its accepted fallacy, whether it be well understood or ill understood; beneficent or pernicious or merely foolish or both foolish and pernicious: and this is often most handily done by tracing its history.

Now I shall assume that the framers of the Ordinance regulating the duties of this Chair knew well enough, of their own reading, that English Literature deals with a vast variety of subjects: and that, if any piece of writing miss to deal with its particular subject, so closely that theme and treatment can scarcely be separated, by so much will it be faulty as literature. Milton is fairly possessed with the story of Man's fall, Boswell possessed with Johnson, Shelley with hatred of tyranny in all its manifestations, Mill again with the idea of Liberty: and it is only because we had knowledge presented to us at an age when we thought more attentively of apples, that we still fail to recognise in Euclid and Dr. Todhunter two writers who are excellent because possessed with a passion for Geometry.

I infer, then, that the framers of the Ordinance, when they employed this phrase "the study of the subject of English Literature," knew well enough that no such thing existed in nature, but adopted the convention that English Literature could be separated somehow from its content and treated as a subject all by itself, for teaching purposes: and, for purposes of examination, could be yoked up with another subject called English Language, as other universities had yoked it.

===Chinese===
IV

但每一种约定，其根子某处总有一个谬误；它或是有用而起作用的——如许多法律拟制确实行之有效，且是为了好处；或是衰老的——早不中用了，却凭着积习依旧专横，因而有害；或是纯粹的愚蠢——当一位罗斯金（Ruskin）出来裁断时，某些艺术上的约定俗成，追根究底不过是愚蠢而已。因此，凡诚实的头脑，在处理任何可以认出的约定时，都应当审察它那被认可的谬误：无论人们对它理解得完善与否；它是有益、有害、还是纯粹愚蠢，抑或既愚蠢又有害；而做到这一点最便当的办法，往往是追溯它的历史。

那么我要假定：制定条例、规定本讲座职责的那些人，凭自己的阅读本该深知，英国文学的题材浩繁无比；而且，任何一篇文字，倘若未能紧扣它特殊的题材——紧扣到主题与处理几乎不可拆分——那么作为文学，它便按欠缺的程度而成为瑕疵。弥尔顿（Milton）被人类的堕落故事完全占有了；鲍斯韦尔（Boswell）被约翰逊占有了；雪莱（Shelley）被对一切形态的暴政的憎恨占有了；密尔（Mill）又被自由这个观念占有了；而我们至今没能认出欧几里得（Euclid）和托德亨特（Todhunter）博士是两位出色的作者——只因为他们对几何学怀有一种占有了他们的热情——原因无非是：这门知识呈送到我们面前时，我们正处在满心思忖苹果的年纪。

于是我推断：条例的制定者们使用「英国文学这一科目的研究」这个说法时，心里十分清楚自然界并不存在这么一种东西，却采纳了一套约定：为了教学的目的，可以把英国文学同它的内容用某种方式剥离开来，单独立为一门科目；而为了考试的目的，又可以把它同另一门叫做「英语语言」的科目套在一副轭上，别的大学就是这么套的。

===Original===
V

I believe the following to be a fair account of how these examinations in English Language and Literature came to pass, and how a certain kind of student came to pass these Examinations. At any rate since the small revolution has happened in my lifetime and most of it since I was able to observe, the account here is drawn from my own observation and may be checked and corrected by yours.

Thirty-five or forty years ago—say in the late seventies or early eighties—some preparatory schools, and others that taught older boys but ranked below the great public schools in repute, taught so much of English Literature as might be comprised, at a rough calculation, in two or three plays of Shakespeare, edited by Clark and Aldis Wright; a few of Bacon's Essays, Milton's early poems, Stopford Brooke's little primer, a book of extracts for committal to memory, with perhaps Chaucer's "Prologue" and a Speech of Burke. In the great public schools *no* English Literature was studied, save in those which had invented "Modern Sides," to prepare boys specially for Woolwich or Sandhurst or the Indian Civil Service; for entrance to which examinations were held on certain prescribed English Classics, and marks mainly given for acquaintance with the editors' notes.

In the universities, the study of English Classics was not officially recognised at all.

Let us not hastily suppose that this neglect of English rested wholly on unreason, or had nothing to say for itself. Teachers and tutors of the old Classical Education (as it was called) could plead as follows:

===Chinese===
V

我相信，下面这个叙述，大体公平地交代了英语语言与文学考试如何应运而生，某类学生又是如何应运而生地通过了这些考试。无论如何，这场小小的革命就发生在我的有生之年，其中大半发生在我能够亲眼观察之后，所以这里的叙述出自我的亲见，诸位可以拿你们自己的观察来核对与修正。

三十五或四十年前——姑且说在七十年代末、八十年代初——有些预备学校，还有些招收较大男孩、但声望低于大公学的学校，所教的英国文学，粗略估算，不出克拉克（Clark）与奥尔迪斯·赖特（Aldis Wright）编注的两三种莎士比亚戏剧、培根《随笔集》里的几篇、弥尔顿的早期诗作、斯托福德·布鲁克（Stopford Brooke）的小小入门书、一本供背诵的文选，或许再加乔叟的《总引》和伯克（Burke）的一篇演说。在大公学里，英国文学*根本无人研习*，除非那些发明了「近代班」（Modern Sides）的学校——专为报考伍尔威奇（Woolwich）、桑德赫斯特（Sandhurst）或印度文官系统（Indian Civil Service）的男孩而设；这些入学考试以若干指定的英国经典为范围，给分主要看学生对编者注释的熟悉程度。

而在大学里，英国经典的研究根本得不到官方的承认。

我们不要急着假定，这种对英语的忽视全然出于无理，或它自己毫无可辩之处。旧式古典教育（人们这样称呼它）的教师与导师，本可以如此申辩：

===Original===
> "In the first place," they would say, "English Literature is too *easy* a study. Our youth, at school or university, starts on his native classics with a liability which in any foreign language he has painfully to acquire. The voices that murmured around his cradle, the voice of his nurse, of his governess, of the parson on Sundays; the voices of village boys, stablemen, gamekeepers and farmers—friendly or unfriendly—of callers, acquaintances, of the children he met at Children's Parties; the voices that at the dinner-table poured politics or local gossip into the little pitcher with long ears—all these were English voices speaking in English: and all these were all the while insensibly leading him up the slope from the summit of which he can survey the promised land spread at his feet as a wide park; and he holds the key of the gates, to enter and take possession. Whereas," the old instructors would continue, "with the classics of any foreign language we take him at the foot of the steep ascent, spread a table before him (*mensa, mensa, mensam*…) and coax or drive him up with variations upon *amo*, 'I love' or *τίπτω*, 'I beat,' until he, too, reaches the summit and beholds the landscape:

Now so much of truth, Gentlemen, as this plea contains was admitted last term by your Senate, in separating the English Tripos, in which a certain linguistic familiarity may be not rashly presumed of the student, from the Foreign Language Triposes, divided into two parts, of which the first will more suspiciously test his capacity to construe the books he professes to have studied. I may return to this and to the alleged *easiness* of studies in a School of English. Let us proceed just now with the reasoned plea for neglect.

These admirable old schoolmasters and dons would have hesitated, maybe, to say flatly with Dogberry that "to write and read comes by nature… and for your writing and reading, let that appear when there is no need of such vanity." But in practice their system so worked, and in some of the public schools so works to this day. Let me tell you that just before the war an undergraduate came to me from the Sixth Form of one of the best reputed among these great schools. He wished to learn to write. He wished (poor fellow) to write me an essay, if I would set him a subject. He had never written an essay at school. "Indeed," said I, "and there is no reason why you should, if by 'essay' you mean some little treatise about 'Patriotism' or 'A Day in the Country.' I will choose you no such subject nor any other upon any book which you have never read. Tell me, what is your Tripos?" He said, "the History Tripos." "Then," said I, "since History provides quite a large number of themes, choose one and I will try to correct your treatment of it, without offence to your opinions or prejudice to your facts." "But," he confessed, "at So-and-so"—naming the great public school—"we never *wrote* out an account of anything, or set down our opinions on anything, to be corrected. We just construed and did sums." And when he brought me his first attempt, behold, it was so. He could not construct a simple sentence, let alone putting two sentences together; while, as for a paragraph, it lay beyond his farthest horizon. In short, here was an instance ready to hand for any cheap writer engaged to decry the old Classical Education.

===Chinese===
> 「『首先，』他们会说，『英国文学是一门太*容易*的学问。我们的青少年，无论在中学还是大学，上手本族经典时自带一份资历，而这份资历，换作任何一门外语，他都得费尽辛苦去挣得。围绕他摇篮低语的那些声音——保姆的声音、家庭女教师的声音、礼拜天牧师的声音；村里男孩、马夫、猎场看守和农夫的声音——友善的也罢，不友善的也罢——还有来访的客人、熟人的声音，他在儿童晚会上遇到的孩子们的声音；那些在餐桌上把政治或本地闲话，倒进那只长耳朵小罐里的声音——这些都是用英语说话的英语声音：而所有这些声音，始终都在不知不觉地领着他爬坡，从坡顶他可以把那片应许之地一览无余，它像一座开阔的猎园铺展在他脚下；而大门的钥匙就握在他手里，只消进去占取就行。可是，』老派的教师们接着说，『换作任何一门外语的经典，我们是从陡坡脚下接手他的，先在他面前摆一张桌子（*mensa, mensa, mensam*——桌子，桌子，桌子……），再拿 *amo*（「我爱」）或 *τίπτω*（「我打」）的各种变化形式，连哄带赶地把他往上撵，直到他也登临绝顶，饱览风景：』

诸位先生，这一申辩里所含的那点真理，上一学期已得到你们评议会（Senate）的首肯：评议会把剑桥英文荣誉学位考试（English Tripos）——在这里，对学生的语言熟练程度，尽可以不作冒失的假定——同外语荣誉学位考试（Triposes）分了家；后者分为两部分，其第一部分将以更挑剔的眼光，考查学生解读他自称研读过的那些书籍的能力如何。关于这一点，以及关于人家指责英文学院（School of English）课程*容易*的问题，我或许还会回头再谈。眼下，让我们继续听那条经过斟酌的、为忽视英语所作的申辩。

这些令人敬佩的老校长和导师们，或许会迟疑，不肯像道格贝里（Dogberry）那样直截了当地说「读和写是天生就会的……至于你的读和写，且等用不着这般虚荣的时候，再拿出来见人吧」。但在实际操作中，他们的体制就是这么运转的，而且在一些公学里，直到今天依然这么运转。让我告诉诸位：就在大战前夕，有一名本科生来找我，他出自这些大公学里声誉最好的学校之一的六年级（Sixth Form）。他想学写作。他想（可怜的人）给我写一篇作文，只要我肯给他出个题目。他在学校从未写过作文。『哦，』我说，『那也怪不着你，倘若你说的「作文」是指一篇论「爱国精神」或「乡间一日」的小论文的话。这样的题目我不会替你出，任何你没有读过的书我也不会出题。告诉我，你考的是什么 Tripos？』他说：『历史 Tripos。』『那么，』我说，『历史提供的题目相当多，你自己挑一个，我来试着批改你的论述，绝不冒犯你的观点，也绝不损害你的事实。』『可是，』他招认道，『在某某公学』——他说出了那所大公学的名字——『我们从来不曾就任何事情*写出*一份说明，也从不把我们对任何事情的意见写下来交人批改。我们只是做句法解析和算术。』而当他把第一次习作拿来给我时，瞧吧，果然如此。他连一个简单的句子都造不出来，更不用说把两个句子连在一起；至于段落，那是他地平线以外的东西。总之，随便哪个受雇来贬低旧式古典教育的下等写手，都能信手拈来拿这个例子用。

===Original===
What would the old schoolmasters plead in excuse? Why this, as I suggest—"You cite an extreme instance. But, while granting English Literature to be great, we would point out that an overwhelming majority of our best writers have modelled their prose and verse upon the Greek and Roman classics, either directly or through tradition. Now we have our own language *gratis*, so to speak. Let us spend our pains, then, in acquiring Latin and Greek, and the tradition. So shall we most intimately enjoy our own authors; and so, if we wish to write, we shall have at hand the clues they followed, the models they used."

Now I have as you know, Gentlemen, a certain sympathy with this plea, or with a part of it: nor can so much of truth as its argument contains be silenced by a "What about Shakespeare?" or a "What about Bunyan?" or a "What about Burns?" I believe our imaginary pleader for the Classics could put up a stout defence upon any of those names. To choose the forlornest hope of the three, I can hear him demonstrating, to his own satisfaction if not to yours, that Bunyan took his style straight out of the Authorised Version of our Bible; which is to say that he took it from the styles of forty-seven scholars, *plus* Tyndale's, *plus* Coverdale's, *plus* Cranmer's—the scholarship of fifty scholars expressed and blended.

But, as a theory, the strict classical argument gives itself away, as well by its intolerance as by its obvious distrust of the genius of our own wonderful language. I have in these five years, and from this place, Gentlemen, counselled you to seek back ever to those Mediterranean sources which are the wellheads of our civilisation: but always (I hope) on the understanding that you use them with a large liberty. They are effete for us unless we add and mingle freely the juice of our own natural *genius*.

===Chinese===
那么老派校长们会拿什么来开脱呢？哦，是这么一条，如我提示的——『你们举的是极端例子。不过，姑且承认英国文学伟大，我们要指出：我们最优秀的作家，其散文与韵文有压倒性的多数是取法希腊罗马经典的，或直接取法，或经由传统。而我们的母语，可以说是不费分文就有的。那么，让我们把功夫花在掌握拉丁文、希腊文及其传统上。这样，我们便能最贴己地领略自家作家；这样，倘若我们想写作，手边也自会有人家走过的线索、人家用过的范本。』

诸位先生，你们知道，我对这条申辩、或对它的一部分，是怀有几分同情的：它的论证里所含的那点真理，也不是一句『那莎士比亚怎么算？』或一句『那班扬（Bunyan）怎么算？』或一句『那彭斯（Burns）怎么算？』就能堵住嘴的。我相信，我们设想中那位为古典辩护的先生，拿这三个名字中的任何一个，都能打出一场顽强的防御战。挑三者中最没指望的一个来说吧，我可以听见他论证——论证到他自己心满意足，倘若未必说服得了你们——班扬的文笔是直接取自我们圣经的钦定版圣经（詹姆士王钦定本，AV）的；这也就是说，他取法于四十七位学者的文笔，*外加*廷代尔（Tyndale）的，*外加*科弗代尔（Coverdale）的，*外加*克兰麦（Cranmer）的——五十位学者的学识得到了表达与交融。

但作为一种理论，这种严格的古典论调是不攻自破的：既败在它的不宽容，也败在它明显不信任我们自己这门奇妙语言的*天才*。这五年里，诸位先生，我一直在这个讲台上劝你们不断回溯那些地中海的源头——我们文明的泉眼所在；但始终（我希望）是基于这样的理解：你们使用它们时要带着充分的自由。倘若我们不自如地掺入并调和自己天生的*天才*之汁，它们对我们便是衰竭无用的。

===Original===
And in practice the strict classical theory, with its implied contempt of English, has been disastrous: disastrous not only with the ordinary man—as with my Sixth Form boy who could not put two sentences together, and had read no English authors; but disastrous even to highly eminent scholars. Listen, pray, to this passage from one of them, Frederick Paley, who condescended (Heaven knows why) to turn the majestic verse of Pindar into English Prose—

From the VIII Isthmian:

> And now that we are returned from great sorrows, let us not fall into a dearth of victories, nor foster griefs; but as we have ceased from our tiresome troubles, we will publicly indulge in a sweet roundelay.

===Chinese===
而在实际操作中，这种暗含着对英语轻蔑的严格古典理论，为害之烈，罄竹难书：受害的不止寻常人——比如我那位六年级男孩，连两个句子都连不到一起，也没读过任何英国作家；受害的甚至还有极负盛名的学者。请听一听其中一位——弗雷德里克·佩利（Frederick Paley）——笔下的一段文字吧：他屈尊（天晓得为什么）把品达（Pindar）庄严的诗行改写成了英文散文——

《地峡颂》第八首（Isthmian VIII）：

> 如今我们已从巨大的悲怆中归来，就让我们不要陷入胜利的荒年，也不要养育哀伤；既然我们已从恼人的烦劳中歇息下来，就让我们当众纵情于一支甜美的小回旋曲吧。

===Original===
From the IV Pythian:

> It had been divinely predicted to Pelias, that he should die by the doughty sons of Aeolus and an alarming oracle had come to his wary mind, delivered at the central point of tree-clad mother-earth, "that he must by all means hold in great caution the man with one shoe, when he shall have come from a homestead on the hills."
> And he accordingly came in due time, armed with two spears, a magnificent man. The dress he wore was of a double kind, the national costume of the Magnesians.… Nor as yet had the glossy clusters of his hair been clipped away, but dangled brightly adown his back.
> Forward he went at once and took his stand among the people.… Him then they failed to recognise: but some of the reverent-minded went so far as to say, "Surely this cannot be Apollo!"

It needs no comment, I think. Surely *this* cannot be Apollo!

===Chinese===
《皮托颂》第四首（Pythian IV）：

> 有神谕向珀利阿斯（Pelias）预示，他必死于埃俄罗斯（Aeolus）那几个勇敢儿子之手；一道可怖的神谕传到他那颗警觉的心里，是在林木覆盖的大地之母的中心点传下的：『那独鞋之人，你千万要加意提防，当他从山间的田庄而来。』
> 于是他如期而至，手持两支长矛，一个威仪堂堂的人物。他的穿着是双重的，马格涅西亚人（Magnesians）的民族服装。……他那油亮的发卷尚未剪除，而是在他背后明亮地垂荡着。
> 他立刻上前，站到人群中间。……他们竟没能认出他来；但几位心存虔敬的人竟这样说：『这莫非不是阿波罗（Apollo）！』

我想，这无须任何评论了。这莫非*不是*阿波罗！

===Original===
Frederick Paley flourished—if the word be not exorbitant for so demure a writer—in the middle of the last century (he was born in the year of Waterloo and died in the year after Queen Victoria's first jubilee). Well, in that period there grew up a race of pioneers who saw that English Literature—that proud park and rolling estate—lay a tangled, neglected wilderness for its inheritors, and set themselves bravely to clear broad ways through it. Furnivall and Skeat, Aldis Wright, Clark, Grosart, Arber, Earle, Hales, Morris, Ellis and the rest—who can rehearse these names now but in deepest respect? Oh, believe me, Gentlemen! they were wonderful fighters in a cause that at first seemed hopeless. If I presume to speak of foibles today, you will understand that I do so because, lightly though I may talk to you at times, I have a real sense of the responsibilities of this Chair. I worship great learning, which they had: I loathe flippant detraction of what is great; I have usually a heart for men-against-odds and the unpopular cause. But these very valiant fighters had, one and all, some very obvious foibles: and because, in the hour of success, these foibles came to infect the whole teaching of English in this country, and to infect it fatally for many years, I shall dare to point them out.

VI

(a) To begin with, then, these valiant fighters, intent on pushing their cause to the front, kept no sense of proportion. All their geese were swans, and *Beowulf* a second *Iliad*. I think it scarcely too much to say that, of these men, all so staunch in fighting for the claims of English Literature, not one (with the exception of Dr. Hales) appears to have had any critical judgment whatever, apart from the rhyme, verse and inflectional tests on which they bestowed their truly priceless industry. Criticism, as Sainte-Beuve, Matthew Arnold or Pater understood and practised it, they merely misprized.

===Chinese===
弗雷德里克·佩利成名——倘若「成名」二字对这样一位拘谨的作者不算僭越——于上个世纪中叶（他生于滑铁卢之战的那一年，死于维多利亚女王首次在位庆典之后的第二年）。嗯，就在那个年代，长起来了一辈开路人：他们看出英国文学——那片骄傲的猎园、那片绵延的庄园——对它的继承者们来说竟是一片纠缠丛杂、无人照管的荒野，于是勇敢地着手在其中开劈宽阔的道路。弗尼瓦尔（Furnivall）与斯基特（Skeat）、奥尔迪斯·赖特（Aldis Wright）、克拉克（Clark）、格罗萨特（Grosart）、阿伯（Arber）、厄尔（Earle）、黑尔斯（Hales）、莫里斯（Morris）、埃利斯（Ellis）以及其余诸人——如今有谁能复诵这些名字而不怀着最深的敬意？哦，相信我，诸位先生！他们是为一项起初看似毫无希望的事业奋战过的出色斗士。倘若我今天斗胆谈论他们的弱点，你们会理解，我这样做是因为：尽管我有时同你们说话轻快，我对本讲座的责任却怀着真切的意识。我崇敬渊博的学问——他们正有这份学问；我厌恶对伟大事物的轻薄贬损；对于以寡敌众的人与不得人心的事业，我素来心有戚戚。但这些英勇的斗士们，无一例外，都有一些显而易见的弱点；而由于在成功之时，这些弱点蔓延开来，感染了全国的英语教学，并且为害多年、病入膏肓，我不揣冒昧，要把它指出来。

VI

（a）那么首先，这些英勇的斗士一心要把他们的事业推到前台，却毫无比例感。他们的鹅无一不是天鹅，《贝奥武甫》（*Beowulf*）成了第二部《伊利亚特》。我想这样说几乎不算过分：这些人个个为英国文学的权利奋战不懈，却没有一个（黑尔斯博士（Dr. Hales）除外）看上去具备任何一点批评判断力——除了他们倾注了真正无价辛劳的那些韵脚、格律与屈折形式的检验之外。批评——圣伯夫（Sainte-Beuve）、马修·阿诺德（Matthew Arnold）或佩特（Pater）所理解并实践的那种批评——他们只是错看了。

===Original===
(b) I think it was of true scholarly desire to vindicate English Literature from the charge of being "too easy," that—as their studies advanced—they laid more and more stress on Middle-English and Old English writings than on what our nations of England and Scotland have written since they learned to write. I dare to think also that we may attribute to this dread of "easiness" their practice of cumbering simple texts with philological notes; on which, rather than on the text, we unhappy students were carefully examined. For an example supplied to Dr. Corson—I take those three lines of Cowper's *Task* (Book I, 86–88):

> Thus first necessity invented stools,
> Convenience next suggested elbow-chairs,
> And luxury th' accomplish'd **Sofa** last.

Now in these three lines the word "accomplish'd" is the only one that needs even the smallest explanation. "But," says Dr. Corson, "in two different editions of *The Task* in my library, prepared for the use of the young, no explanation is given of it, but in both the Arabic origin of 'sofa' is given. In one the question is asked what other words in English have been derived from the Arabic." ("Abracadabra" would be my little contribution.)

===Chinese===
（b）我想，正是出于要把英国文学从『太容易』的指控中解救出来的真正学术热望，他们才随着研究的推进，把愈来愈重的砝码压在中古英语与古英语的文献上，而轻看了英格兰与苏格兰两个民族自学会书写以来所写下的一切。我也斗胆认为，他们那种给平易的文本塞满语文学注释的做法，同样可以归因于这种对『容易』的恐惧；而我们这些倒霉的学生，被仔细考查的正是这些注释，而不是文本本身。科森博士（Dr. Corson）曾举过一个例子——我且取柯珀（Cowper）《任务》（*Task*）中那三行诗（第一卷，86–88 行）：

> 于是先是「必需」发明了板凳，
> 接着「便利」提议了扶手椅，
> 奢华末了成就了那张尽善尽美的**沙发**。

在这三行诗里，唯一需要哪怕最细微解释的词，就是「尽善尽美的」（accomplish'd）这个词。『可是，』科森博士说，『在我的藏书中，有两种为学生使用而编的《任务》版本，对它没有任何解释，倒是两种版本都交代了「沙发」（sofa）的阿拉伯语词源。其中一种还提问：英语里还有哪些词是从阿拉伯语派生来的。』（「阿布拉卡达布拉」（Abracadabra）便算我的一点小小贡献。）

===Original===
(c) These valiant fighters—having to extol what Europe had, wrongly enough, forgotten to count among valuable things—turned aggressively provincial, parted their beards in the Anglo-Saxon fashion; composed long sentences painfully innocent of any word not derivable from Anglo-Saxon, sentences in which the "impenetrability of matter" became the "un-go-throughsomeness of stuff" (but that may have happened in a parody), and in general comported themselves like the Anglo-Saxons they claimed for their forbears; rightly enough for anything anyone cared, but wrongly enough for the rest of us who had no yearning toward that kinship and went on spelling Alfred with an *A*.

(d) They were—I suppose through opposition—extremely irascible men; like farmers. Urbanity was the last note in their gamut, the City—*urbs quam dicunt Romam*—the last of places in their ken. There was no engaging them in dialectic, an Athenian art which they frankly despised. If you happened to disagree with them, their answer was a sturdy Anglo-Saxon brick. If you politely asked your way to Puddlehampton, and to be directed to Puddlehampton's main objects of interest, the answer you would get (see "Notes and Queries" *passim*) would be, "Who is this that comes out of Nowhere, enquiring for Puddlehampton, unacquainted with Stubbs? Is it possible at this time of day that the world can contain anyone ignorant of the published Transactions of the Wiltshire Walking Club, Vol. III, p. 159—'Puddlehampton, its Rise and Decline, with a note on Vespasian?'"

(e) These pioneers—pushing the importance of English, but occupied more and more with origins and with bad authors, simply could not see the vital truth; that English Literature is a continuing thing, ten times more alive today than it was in the times they studied and belauded. The last word upon them is that not a man of them could write prose in the language they thrust on our study. To them, far more than to the old classical scholars, English was a shut book: a large book, but closed and clasped, material to heighten a desk for schoolmasters and schoolmistresses.

===Chinese===
（c）这些英勇的斗士——既然必须颂扬一件欧洲错忘了算进珍贵事物之列的东西——便变得咄咄逼人地乡土气起来：他们按盎格鲁-撒克逊的式样梳理胡须；他们写出的长句，痛苦地清白无瑕，不含任何不能从盎格鲁-撒克逊派生的词——在这样的句子里，「物质的不可穿透性」（impenetrability of matter）变成了「物什的穿不过去性」（un-go-throughsomeness of stuff）（不过这一例或许出自滑稽仿作）；总而言之，他们的举止做派，处处效仿他们认作祖宗的盎格鲁-撒克逊人。这事随他去买账与否都算得上理直气壮，只是在我们其余这些人看来错得可以：我们对那门亲缘并无向往，照旧把阿尔弗雷德（Alfred）的名字以 *A* 开头来拼写。

（d）他们——我想是被对立面逼出来的——是些极易动怒的人；像庄稼汉。温文尔雅是他们音阶里最末的一个音；城市——*urbs quam dicunt Romam*（他们唤作罗马的那座城）——是他们见识里最末的一个地方。同他们进行辩证讨论是不可能的，那门雅典人的技艺，他们坦率地加以鄙夷。倘若你碰巧与他们意见不合，他们的回答便是一块结实的盎格鲁-撒克逊砖头。倘若你有礼有节地向他们问路，要去帕德尔汉普顿（Puddlehampton），请他们指点帕德尔汉普顿的主要名胜，你得到的回答（参见《札记与问询》（*Notes and Queries*）*各处*）会是：『这是哪儿钻出来的无名之辈，打听帕德尔汉普顿，竟不知有斯塔布斯（Stubbs）？都什么年头了，世上居然还有人不知道《威尔特郡徒步俱乐部汇刊》第三卷第 159 页——《帕德尔汉普顿的兴衰，附韦帕芗（Vespasian）考》？』

（e）这些开路人——一面鼓吹英语的重要，一面却把心思愈来愈多地用在源头考据与二三流作者身上——根本看不见那条性命攸关的真理：英国文学是一件绵延不断的东西，它今日的生机十倍于他们研习并盛赞的那个时代。对他们盖棺定论的一句话是：他们当中没有一个人能用他们硬塞给我们研习的那门语言写出散文。对他们而言——远比对老派古典学者为甚——英语是一本合上的书：一部大书，却紧闭加锁，只配做垫高校长先生与女校长们书桌的材料。

===Original===
But schoolmasters and schoolmistresses, like chickens and curses, come home to roost. Once set up your plea for a Tripos of English Language and Literature on the lower plea that it will provide for what *they* call a "felt want," and sooner or later you give English Language and Literature into *their* hands, and then you get the fallacy full-flowered into a convention. English Literature henceforth is a "subject," divorced from life: and what they have made of it, let a thousand handbooks and so-called histories attest. But this world is not a wilderness of classrooms. English Language? They cannot write it, at all events. They do not (so far as I can discover) try to write it. They talk and write about it; how the poor deceased thing outgrew infantile ailments, how it was operated on for umlaut, how it parted with its vermiform appendix and its inflections one by one, and lost its vowel endings in muted e's.

> And they went and told the sexton,
> And the sexton toll'd the bell.

But when it comes to *writing*; to keeping bright the noble weapon of English, testing its poise and edge, feeling the grip, handing it to their pupils with the word, "Here is the sword of your fathers, that has cloven dragons. So use it, that we who have kept it bright may be proud of you, and of our pains, and of its continuing valiance":—why, as I say, they do not even *try*. Our unprofessional forefathers, when they put pen to paper, did attempt English prose, and not seldom achieved it. But take up any elaborate History of English Literature and read, and, as you read, ask yourselves, "How can one of the rarest delights of life be converted into *this*? What has happened to merry Chaucer, rare Ben Jonson, gay Steele and Prior, to Goldsmith, Jane Austen, Charles Lamb?"

===Chinese===
然而校长先生和女校长们，像鸡群与诅咒一样，终究是要回巢的。你一旦为英语语言与文学的荣誉学位考试（Tripos）张目，用的却是那种低一级的理由——说它能满足*他们*所谓的一种「被感到的需要」（felt want）——那么或迟或早，你就会把英语语言与文学交到*他们*手上，到那时，这个谬误便花事全盛，长成了一套约定俗成。英国文学从此是一门「科目」，与生活割断了关系：他们把它弄成了什么样子，有一千种手册和所谓文学史可以作证。可这个世界并不是一片教室的荒野。英语语言？至少他们是写不了的。（据我所能发现）他们也不试着去写。他们只是围着它空谈、下笔空论：说这可怜的亡故之物如何熬过了幼年的病痛，如何为元音变换（umlaut）挨了一刀，如何把阑尾和屈折变化一件件割舍，把词尾元音丢在不发音的 e 里。

> 他们走去告诉了教堂司事，
> 司事便敲响了丧钟。

可是轮到*写作*的时候——轮到把英语这件高贵的兵器擦拭锃亮、检验它的平衡与锋刃、握一握它的柄、再交到学生手里说一句「这是你们父辈的剑，曾劈开过恶龙。用它吧，好让我们这些把它擦亮的人，能为你们、为我们的辛劳、也为它不衰的勇武而自豪」的时候——哼，正如我说的，他们连*试*都不试。我们那些不以教学为业的先辈，提笔落到纸上时，是真去尝试英文散文的，而且常常写成了。可你随手拿起任何一部精工细作的英国文学史来读，一边读一边问问自己：「人生最珍稀的乐事之一，怎么可能被弄成*这副*样子？快乐的乔叟、罕见的本·琼森（Ben Jonson）、欢快的斯梯尔（Steele）与普赖尔（Prior）、哥尔德斯密斯（Goldsmith）、简·奥斯汀（Jane Austen）、查尔斯·兰姆（Charles Lamb），都上哪儿去了？」

===Original===
> All, all are gone, the old familiar faces!

gone into the professional stockpot! And the next news is that these cooks, of whom Chaucer wrote prophetically

> These cookes, how they stampe, and streyne, and grynde,
> And turnen substaunce into accident!

have formed themselves into professional Associations to protect "the study of the subject of English Literature" and bark off any intruder who would teach in another way than theirs.

===Chinese===
> 全都不见了，那些熟悉的老面孔！

——都进了行业的汤锅！接着传来的消息是：这班厨子——乔叟当年有先见之明，写过他们——

> 这班厨子，如何捣啊、筛啊、碾啊、磨啊，
> 把实体翻搅成了偶性！

已经结成了一些职业协会，来保卫「英国文学这一科目的研究」，把任何想用别的路数教书的闯入者吠走。

===Original===
VII

But I say to you that Literature is not, and should not be, the preserve of any priesthood. To write English, so as to make Literature, may be *hard*. But English Literature is *not* a mystery, *not* a Professors' Kitchen.

And the trouble lies, not in the harm professionising does to schoolmasters and schoolmistresses, but in the harm it does "in widest commonalty spread" among men and women who, as Literature was written for them, addressed to them, ought to find in it, all their lives through, a retirement from mean occupations, a well of refreshment, sustainment in the daily drudgery of life, solace in calamity, an inmate by the hearth, ever sociable, never intrusive—to be sought and found, to be found and dropped at will:

> Men, when their affairs require,
> Must themselves at whiles retire;
> Sometimes hunt, and sometimes hawk,
> And not ever sit and talk—

to be dropped at will and left without any answering growl of moroseness; to be consulted again at will and found friendly.

===Chinese===
VII

但我要对你们说：文学不是、也不该是任何祭司阶层的禁脔。写英语而写成文学，或许*很难*。但英国文学*不是*什么秘教玄义，*不是*教授们的厨房。

症结并不在于职业化加诸校长先生和女校长们的伤害，而在于它加诸「至广的共众」（in widest commonalty spread）的伤害——那是男男女女，文学既是为他们写的、也是向他们说话的，他们本该终生在文学中找到一处从卑琐营生中退憩的所在、一泓提神的清泉、日常苦役中的支撑、灾祸中的慰藉、炉边一位常客——永远可亲，从不扰人——可以随心寻求而寻见，随心寻见而搁下：

> 人当事务有所驱策，
> 有时也须自作退憩；
> 时而狩猎，时而放鹰，
> 断不能终日坐着闲谈——

可以随兴搁下，而它决不回敬一声乖戾的低吼；又可以随时重新请教，而发现它依旧友善。

===Original===
For this is the trouble of *professionising* Literature. We exile it from the business of life, in which it would ever be at our shoulder, to befriend us. Listen, for example, to an extract from a letter written, a couple of weeks ago, by somebody in the Charity Commission:

> Sir,
> With reference to previous correspondence in this matter, I am to say that in all the circumstances of this case the Commissioners are of the opinion that it would be desirable that a public enquiry in connection with the Charity should be held in the locality.

And the man—very likely an educated man—having written *that*, very likely went home and read Chaucer, Dante, or Shakespeare, or Burke for pleasure! That is what happens when you treat literature as a "subject," separable from life and daily practice.

===Chinese===
这正是把文学*职业化*的祸害：我们把它从生活的事务中放逐出去，而在生活的事务里，它本该永远伴随我们的肩头，做我们的朋友。譬如，请听几周前慈善委员会（Charity Commission）某人写的一封信里的摘句：

> 先生：
> 关于本案先前的往来函件，谨此奉告：就本案的全部情况而论，委员会诸公认为，就该项慈善事业于当地举行一次公开调查一事，当属可取。

而写下*这个*的人——多半还是个受过教育的人——写完之后，多半便回家去，读乔叟、但丁（Dante）、莎士比亚或伯克，权作消遣！这就是你把文学当作一门「科目」、当作可与生活和日常实践相剥离之物时，所发生的事情。

===Original===
VIII
