I declare to you that Literature was *not* written for schoolmasters, nor for schoolmistresses. I would not exchange it for a wilderness of schoolmasters. It should be delivered from them, who, with their silly ablauts and "tendencies," can themselves neither read nor write. For the proof? Having the world's quintessential store of mirth and sharp sorrow, wit, humour, comfort, farce, comedy, tragedy, satire; the glories of our birth and state, piled all at their elbows, only one man of the crowd—and he M. Jusserand, a Frenchman—has contrived to draw out of the mass one interesting well-written history of the "subject."

===Chinese===
VIII

我向你们宣告：文学*不是*为校长先生们写的，也不是为女校长们写的。拿一片校长先生的荒野来换它，我也不换。文学应当从他们手里被解救出来——他们满口可笑的元音变换（ablaut）与「趋势」，自己却既不会读，也不会写。要证据吗？世界的欢笑与剧痛、机智、幽默、宽慰、闹剧、喜剧、悲剧、讽刺之至精储藏，我们生与身份的荣光，统统堆在他们手肘边上；然而这一大群人里，只有一位——还是个法国人，朱瑟朗（M. Jusserand）——设法从这堆材料里，为这门「科目」淘洗出一部有趣而文笔像样的历史。

===Original===
IX

Is there, then, no better way? Yes there is a better way: for the French have it, with their language and literature. In France, as Matthew Arnold noted, a generation ago, the ordinary journeyman work of literature is done far better and more conscientiously than with us. In France a man feels it almost a personal stain, an unpatriotic *lâche*, to write even on a police-order anything so derogatory to the tradition of his language as our Cabinet Ministers read out as answers to our House of Commons. I am told that many a Maire in a small provincial town in N.E. France, even when overwhelmed—*accablé*—with the sufferings of his town-folk, has truly felt the iron enter into his soul on being forced to sign a document written out for him in the invaders' French.

Cannot we treat our noble inheritance of literature and language as scrupulously, and with as high a sense of their appertaining to our national honour, as a Frenchman cherishes *his* language, *his* literature? Cannot we study to leave our inheritance—as the old Athenian put it temperately, "not worse but a little better than we found it"?

I think we can, and should. I shall close today, Gentlemen, with the most modest of perorations. In my first lecture before you, in January 1913, I quoted to you the artist in *Don Quixote* who, being asked what animal he was painting, answered diffidently "That is as it may turn out."

===Chinese===
IX

那么，难道就没有更好的路了吗？更好的路是有的：法国人便有，用在他们的语言与文学上。在法国，正如马修·阿诺德一代人之前注意到的，文学的那些寻常帮工活计，做得远比我们这里出色而尽责。在法国，一个人即便奉警察局之命而落笔，只要写出一点有损自己语言传统的东西——就如今日内阁大臣们拿来在下议院宣读作答的那种东西——他便会觉得那几乎是自己身上的一块污渍，一件不爱国怯阵的 *lâche*（失职）。我听说，法国东北部小城的好些市长（Maire），即便已被同城同胞的苦难压得不胜其负——*accablé*——在被迫签署一份用入侵者的法语替他起草好的文件时，也真切地感到铁烙进了灵魂。

我们就不能像法国人珍爱*他的*语言、*他的*文学那样，一丝不苟地、并且怀着同样高的自觉——认定文学与语言系于我们民族的荣誉——来对待我们这份高贵的遗产么？我们就不能用心传好这份遗产——如那位老雅典人节制地说过的：「不比我们受用时更坏，而要稍稍更好一点」——么？

我想我们能，也应该。诸位先生，今天我要用最谦卑的一段结语来收场。1913 年 1 月，我初次向你们演讲时，曾向你们引述过《堂吉诃德》（*Don Quixote*）里那位画家：有人问他画的是什么兽，他怯生生地答道：「那要看到画成才知道。」

===Original===
The teaching of our language and literature is, after all, a new thing and still experimental. The main tenets of those who, aware of this, have worked on the scheme for a School of English in Cambridge, the scheme recently passed by your Senate and henceforth to be in operation, are three:—

*The first.* That literature cannot be divorced from life: that (for example) you cannot understand Chaucer aright, unless you have the background, unless you know the kind of men for whom Chaucer wrote and the kind of men whom he made speak; that is the *national* side with which all our literature is concerned.

*The second.* Literature being so personal a thing, you cannot understand it until you have some personal understanding of the men who wrote it. Donne is Donne; Swift, Swift; Pope, Pope; Johnson, Johnson; Goldsmith, Goldsmith; Charles Lamb, Charles Lamb; Carlyle, Carlyle. Until you have grasped those men, as men, you cannot grasp their writings. That is the *personal* side of literary study, and as necessary as the other.

*The third.* That the writing and speaking of English is a living art, to be practised and (if it may be) improved. That what these great men have done is to hand us a grand patrimony; that they lived to support us through the trial we are now enduring, and to carry us through to great days to come. So shall our sons, now fighting in France, have a language ready for the land they shall recreate and repeople.

===Chinese===
我们的语言与文学的教学，终究是新起的事物，仍在试验之中。意识到这一点而着手筹划剑桥英文学院的人们——方案新近已由你们的评议会通过，即将付诸实施——其主要信条有三：—

*其一*。文学不能与生活相剥离：譬如，你不可能正确地理解乔叟，除非你拥有那份背景，知道乔叟为何等人写作、又让何等人开口说话；这是我们全部文学所关涉的*民族*的一面。

*其二*。文学既是如此切己的东西，你对写它的人没有一点切己的了解，便无法理解它。多恩（Donne）就是多恩；斯威夫特（Swift）就是斯威夫特；蒲柏（Pope）就是蒲柏；约翰逊（Johnson）就是约翰逊；哥尔德斯密斯（Goldsmith）就是哥尔德斯密斯；查尔斯·兰姆（Charles Lamb）就是查尔斯·兰姆；卡莱尔（Carlyle）就是卡莱尔。不曾把这些人物当作人来把握，你便把握不了他们的作品。这是文学研究的*个人*的一面，与另一面同样必要。

*其三*。英语的写作与言说是一门活着的艺术，须加修习，并（若可能）加以改进。那些伟大人物的功业，是把一份宏大的祖业交到我们手上；他们生当其世，为的是支撑我们度过此刻正承受的试炼，并把我们一路带到将来的伟大岁月。如此，我们此刻正在法兰西作战的儿子们，便有一种语言准备停当，留给他们将要重建、将要重新聚居的国土。

## Lecture VII The Value of Greek and Latin in English Literature / 第七讲 希腊拉丁文在英国文学中的价值

===Original===
Wednesday, February 6, 1918

I

I have promised you, Gentlemen, for today some observations on *The Value of Greek and Latin in English Literature*: a mild, academic title, a camouflage title, so to say; calculated to shelter us for a while from the vigilance of those hot-eyed reformers who, had I advertised *The Value of Greek and Latin in English Life* might even now be swooping from all quarters of the sky on a suggestion that these dry bones yet were flesh: for the eyes I dread are not only red and angry, but naturally microscopic—and that indeed, if they only knew it, is their malady. Yet "surely" groaned patient Job, "there *is* a path which the vulture's eye hath not seen!"

You, at any rate, know by this time that wherever these lectures assert literature they assert life, perhaps even too passionately, allowing neither the fact of death nor the possibility of divorce.

===Chinese===
1918 年 2 月 6 日，星期三

I

诸位先生，我今天答应过，要就《希腊拉丁文在英国文学中的价值》（*The Value of Greek and Latin in English Literature*）谈几点看法：一个温和的、学院气的题目，不妨说是个保护色的题目；打定了主意，要让我们在这班目光灼灼的改革家的警觉之下躲避一时——倘若我登出的广告是《希腊拉丁文在英国生活中的价值》，那么只消有人暗示这些枯骨犹是血肉，他们此刻兴许早已从天空的四面八方猛扑下来；因为我惧怕的那双眼睛，不但又红又怒，还天生带着显微镜——而说实在的，倘若他们自己明白，这恰是他们的病症。然而坚忍的约伯呻吟过：「诚然*有*一条路，是鸷鸟的眼睛未曾见过的！」

你们至少到此刻总该知道：这几场讲演凡断言文学处，便断言生活，或许还断言得过于热烈，既不容死亡的事实，也不容离异的假想。

===Original===
II

But let us begin with the first word, "Value"—"The *Value* of Greek and Latin in English Literature." What do I mean by "Value"? Well, I use it, generally, in the sense of "worth"; but with a particular meaning, or shade of meaning, too. And, this particular meaning is not the particular meaning intended (as I suppose) by men of commerce who, on news of a friend's death, fall a-musing and continue musing until the fire kindles, and they ask "What did So-and-so die worth?" or sometimes, more wisely than they know, "What did poor old So-and-so die worth?" or again, more colloquially, "What did So-and-so 'cut up' for?" Neither is it that which more disinterested economists used to teach; men never (I fear me) loved, but anyhow lost awhile, who for my green unknowing youth, at Thebes or Athens—growing older I tend to forget which is, or was, which—defined the Value of a thing as its "purchasing power" which the market translates into "price." For—to borrow a phrase which I happened on, the other day, with delight, in the Introduction to a translation of Lucian—there may be forms of education less paying than the commercial and yet better worth paying for; nay, above payment or computation in price.7

No: the particular meaning I use today is that which artists use when they talk of painting or of music. To see things, near or far, in their true perspective and proportions; to judge them through distance; and fetching them back, to reproduce them in art so proportioned comparatively, so rightly adjusted, that they combine to make a particular and just perspective: that is to give things their true *Values*.

===Chinese===
II

不过，让我们从第一个词说起：「价值」——「希腊拉丁文在英国文学中的*价值*」。我说的「价值」是什么意思？嗯，我一般是取它「worth」（值当）的意思，却也带着一层特殊的意义，或者说一层特殊的意味。这层特殊的意义，并不是（我猜想）买卖人心里的那一种——他们听得朋友的死讯，便沉入冥想，冥想个不停，直到炉火亮起，于是问道：「某某死时值多少？」或者问得更聪明些而自己不知：「可怜的老某某死时值多少？」或者问得更口语些：「某某『变卖』得几个钱？」也不是从前那些更无私的经济学家所教的那一种——那些先生（恕我直言）从未讨人喜爱，好在也已湮没了一阵子；当年正是他们，给身在底比斯或雅典、懵懂无知的青春时代的我下了定义——年岁一大，我渐渐记不清哪个是哪个，或曾经哪个是哪个——说一物的价值就是它的「购买力」，市场又把购买力译作「价格」。因为——容我借用前两天在一部琉善（Lucian）译本的导论里欣然邂逅的一句话——世上或有种种教育，获利不及商业，却更值得为之付钱；甚且高出付钱之上，非价格所能计量。7

不：我今天所用的特殊含义，是艺术家谈论绘画、谈论音乐时的那一种。把事物——无论远近——在其真实的透视与比例之中看出；隔着距离去判断它们；再把它们收回，用这样相对成比例、这样调停得宜的手法在艺术里复现出来，使它们合成一种特定而正确的透视：这才算给了事物它们真正的*价值*。

===Original===
Suppose yourself reclining on a bank on a clear day, looking up into the sky and watching the ascent of a skylark while you listen to his song. That is a posture in which several poets of repute have placed themselves from time to time: so we need not be ashamed of it. Well, you see the atmosphere reaching up and up, mile upon mile. There are no milestones planted there. But, wave on wave perceptible, the atmosphere stretches up through indeterminate distances; and according as your painter of the sky can translate these distances, he gives his sky what is called *Value*.

You listen to the skylark's note rising, spiral by spiral, on "the very jet of earth":

> As up he wings the spiral stair,
> A song of light, and pierces air
> With fountain ardour, fountain play,
> To reach the shining tops of day:

and you long for the musical gift to follow up and up the delicate degrees of distance and thread the notes back as the bird ascending drops them—on a thread, as it were, of graduated beads, half music and half dew:

===Chinese===
请设想自己在晴日里斜倚在土坡上，仰望天空，看着一只云雀盘旋而上，一面听它的歌。有好几位有名的诗人不时也摆出这个姿势：所以我们不必为此害臊。喏，你看见大气一层层向上延伸，一哩又一哩。那里没有埋设里程碑。但大气一波接一波依稀可辨，向不确定的远方伸展上去；你这位天空的画家能把这距离传达几分，他便给了他的天空几分所谓*价值*。

你听那云雀的歌音，一圈螺旋又一圈螺旋，顺着「大地迸涌的正中一点」向上升去：

> 它振翅飞上那螺旋的天梯，
> 一曲光的歌，刺透空气，
> 带着泉涌的热忱，泉涌的欢跃，
> 要直上白昼闪耀的绝顶：

你便渴望自己也有音乐的天赋，好沿着距离那精微的层级一路向上追寻，把那鸟儿一面上升一面撒落的音符重新穿引回来——像穿在一根线上，一根由渐次递变的珠子串成的线，一半是音乐，一半是露水：

===Original===
> That was the chirp of Ariel
> You heard, as overhead it flew,
> The farther going more to dwell
> And wing our green to wed our blue;
> But whether note of joy, or knell,
> Not his own Father-singer knew;
> Nor yet can any mortal tell,
> Save only how it shivers through;
> The breast of us a sounded shell,
> The blood of us a lighted dew.

Well in music, in painting, this graduating which gives right proportion and, with proportion, a sense of distance, of atmosphere, is called *Value*. Let us, for a minute or two, assay this particular meaning of Value upon life and literature, and first upon life, or, rather upon one not negligible facet of life.

===Chinese===
> 那是精灵爱丽儿的啁啾，
> 你方才听见，它从头顶飞过，
> 越飞越远，歌喉越见流连，
> 载我们的绿，去与我们的蓝成婚；
> 可那究竟是欢歌，还是丧钟，
> 连他那唱过父亲的歌者自己也不知情；
> 凡人更没有一个说得上来，
> 只知道它怎样寒颤着穿透我们；
> 我们的胸膛成了一枚鸣响的贝壳，
> 我们的血液成了一点发亮的露。

好，在音乐里、在绘画里，这种给出恰当比例——并随比例而来给出距离感、大气感——的分级法，就叫做*价值*。让我们花上一两分钟，试把这个特殊含义的「价值」用在生活与文学上；先用在生活上，或者毋宁说，用在生活上一个不可忽视的侧面。

===Original===
I suppose that if an ordinary man of my age were asked which has better helped him to bear the burs of life—religion or a sense of humour—he would, were he quite honest, be gravelled for an answer. Now the best part of a sense of humour, as you know without my telling you, consists in a sense of proportion; a habit, abiding and prompt at command, of seeing all human affairs in their just perspective, so that its happy possessor at once perceives anything odd or distorted or overblown to be an excrescence, a protuberance, a swelling, literally a *humour*: and the function of Thalia, the Comic Spirit, as you may read in Meredith's *Essay on Comedy*, is just to prick these humours. I will but refer you to Meredith's *Essay*, and here cite you the words of an old schoolmaster:

> It would seem to be characteristic of the same mind to appreciate the beauty of ideas in just proportion and harmonious relation to each other, and the absurdity of the same ideas when distorted or brought into incongruous juxtaposition. The exercise of this sense of humour … compels the mind to form a picture to itself, accompanied by pleasurable emotion; and what is this but setting the imagination to work, though in topsy-turvy fashion? Nay, in such a case, imagination plays a double part, since it is only by instantaneous comparison with ideal fitness and proportion that it can grasp at full force the grotesqueness of their contraries.8

===Chinese===
我想，倘若有人问一个我这般年纪的寻常人：宗教还是幽默感，哪一个更好地帮他忍受了生活的芒刺？——他若十分诚实，多半会被问住。你们不必我告诉也知道，幽默感最好的部分在于比例感；在于一种经久不渝、随唤随应的习惯：把一切人间事务放在正确的透视里去看，于是这位幸运的持有者一见任何古怪、扭曲、虚张之物，立刻认出那是一株赘疣、一个隆起、一处肿胀——字面意义上正是一个 *humour*（体液；古人兼指脾性与身上的肿凸）；而喜剧之神塔利亚（Thalia）的职能，正如你们可以在梅瑞狄斯（Meredith）的《论喜剧》（Essay on Comedy）里读到的，恰恰就是来刺破这些 humour。梅瑞狄斯那篇论文我且只提一笔，这里却要引一位老教师的话：

> 似乎是同一种心灵的特质：它既能赏识种种观念在恰如其分的比例中、在彼此和谐的关系里的美，也能赏识这同一些观念遭到扭曲、或被安放在不和谐的并置之中时的荒诞。这种幽默感的运用……迫使心灵为自己构出一幅图景，并伴着愉悦的情感；这除了是叫想象力开工——纵然是颠三倒四地开工——又是什么？不仅如此，在这种情形里，想象力还一身兼二职：唯有拿它同理想的合宜与比例作刹那间的比较，它才能全力把握住其反面的怪诞。8

===Original===
Let us play with an example for one moment. A child sees such an excrescence, such an offence upon proportion, in an immoderately long nose. He is apt to call attention to it on the visage of a visitor: it intrigues him in Perrault's "Prince Charming" and many a fairy tale: it amuses him in Lear's *Book of Nonsense*:

> There was an old man with a Nose,
> Who said "If you choose to suppose
> That my nose is too long
> You are certainly wrong"—

This old man he detects as lacking sense of proportion, sense of humour. Pass from the child to the workingman as we know him. A few weeks ago, a lady—featured, as to nose, on the side of excess—was addressing a North Country audience on the Economic Position of Women after the War. Said she, "There won't be men to go round." Said a voice "Eh, but they'll *have to*, Miss!" Pass from this rudimentary criticism to high talent employed on the same subject, and you get *Cyrano de Bergerac*. Pass to genius, to Milton, and you find the elephant amusing Adam and Eve in Paradise, and doing his best:

===Chinese===
让我们拿一个例子把玩片刻。孩子在一根长得过分的鼻子上，看见了这样一株赘疣、这样一桩对比例的冒犯。他很容易在来访客人的脸上把众人的视线引到它身上；在佩罗（Perrault）的童话「迷人王子」和许许多多童话里，那鼻子叫他着迷；在利尔（Edward Lear）的《荒诞书》（Book of Nonsense）里，那鼻子又逗他发笑：

> 有位老人长着一只鼻子，
> 他说：「你若偏要猜想
> 我的鼻子太长，
> 你就一定想错了」——

这位老人，孩子看得出是缺乏比例感、缺乏幽默感的。再从孩子转到我们熟悉的做工的人。几个星期前，一位太太——单讲鼻子，生在过剩的那一侧——正在向北方的一批听众讲《战后妇女的经济地位》。她说：「往后男人要不够分的了。」有个声音答道：「嗐，那也得*凑合*分呀，小姐！」从这种萌芽状态的批评，转到用在同一题目上的高手，便得到《大鼻子情圣》（Cyrano de Bergerac）。再转到天才，转到弥尔顿（Milton），你便看见大象在乐园里逗亚当与夏娃欢喜，而且尽了它的全力：

===Original===
> the unwieldy elephant,
> To make them mirth, used all his might, and wreathed
> His lithe proboscis.

Milton, like the elephant, jokes with difficulty, but he, too, is using all his might.

===Chinese===
> 那笨重的大象，
> 要逗他们欢喜，使尽了全力，卷起
> 它柔软的长鼻。

弥尔顿和那头大象一样，开玩笑很吃力；但他同样在使尽全力。

===Original===
III

I have illustrated, crudely enough, how a sense of things in their right values will help us on one side of our dealings with life. But truly it helps us on every side. This was what Plato meant when he said that a philosopher must see things as they relatively are within his horizon—*ὀ συνοπτικὸς διαλεκτικός*. And for this it was that an English poet praised Sophocles as one

> Who saw life steadily, and saw it whole.

And this of course is what Dean Inge meant when, the other day, in a volume of *Cambridge Essays on Education*, he reminded us, for a sensible commonplace, that "The wise man is he who knows the relative values of things."

===Chinese===
III

上面我相当粗糙地演示了，对事物正当价值的感知，会在我们应付生活的某一个方面帮我们的忙。可它实实在在处处帮忙。柏拉图说，哲学家必须在其眼界之内、就事物相对的样子去看事物——*ὀ συνοπτικὸς διαλεκτικός*（希腊文：总揽全局的辩证家）——就是这个意思。也是为了这个，一位英国诗人称赞索福克勒斯（Sophocles），说他是那样一个人：

> 他把人生看得沉稳，也看得全整。

这也自然正是英奇（Inge）院长的意思：前几天，他在《剑桥教育论文集》（Cambridge Essays on Education）一部文集里，以一句通达的老生常谈提醒我们：「智者，就是懂得事物相对价值的人。」

===Original===
IV

Applying this to literature, I note, but shall not insist here on the fact—though fact it is—that the Greek and Roman "classical" writers (as we call them) laid more stress than has ever been laid among the subsequent tribes of men upon the desirability of getting all things into proportion, of seeing all life on a scale of relative values. And the reason I shall not insist on this is simply that better men have saved me the trouble.

I propose this morning to discuss the value of the classics to students of English literature from, as the modern phrase goes, a slightly different angle.

===Chinese===
IV

把这一点应用到文学上：希腊罗马的「古典」作家（我们这样称呼他们），比后来各族人类都更强调把万物纳入比例、把全部生活放在相对价值的天平上看的可贵——这是个事实；我注意到它，却不打算在这里坚持发挥。我之所以不坚持，道理很简单：更高明的人已经替我省了这个麻烦。

今天上午，我打算——借用眼下时髦的说法——换一个稍微不同的角度，来同你们讨论古典著作对英国文学学生的价值。

===Original===
Reclining and looking up into that sky which is not too grandiose an image for our own English Literature, you would certainly not wish, Gentlemen, to see it as what it is not—as a cloth painted on the flat. No more than you would choose the sky overarching your life to be a close, hard, copper vault, would you choose this literature of ours to resemble such a prison. I say nothing, for the moment, of the thrill of comparing ours with other constellations—of such a thrill as Blanco White's famous sonnet imagines in Adam's soul when the first night descended on Eden and

> Hesperus with the host of heaven came,
> And lo! Creation widen'd in man's view.
> Who could have thought such darkness lay conceal'd
> Within thy beams, O sun! …

No: I simply picture you as desiring to realise *our own* literature, its depths and values, mile above mile deeper and deeper shining, with perchance a glimpse of a city celestial beyond, or at whiles, on a ladder of values, of the angels—the messengers—climbing and returning.

===Chinese===
斜倚着，仰望那片天空——拿它来比拟我们自己的英国文学，还不算太夸张——诸位先生，你们当然不愿把它看成它所不是的东西：一幅平涂出来的画布。正如你们不愿笼罩你们一生的天空是一个密不透风、又硬又冷的铜穹顶，你们也不愿我们的这番文学像这样一座监狱。把它同别的星座相比所生的那种战栗，我此刻且不说——不说布兰科·怀特（Blanco White）那首有名的十四行诗在亚当灵魂里想象的那种战栗：当第一个黑夜降临伊甸园，——

> 赫斯珀罗斯（Hesperus）偕诸天的星宿来到，
> 看哪！创造在人的眼界里展宽了。
> 谁能想得到这样的黑暗竟潜藏在
> 你的光束里，哦，太阳！……

不：我只是把你们想成渴望着认识*我们自己的*文学，认识它的深处与价值，一哩高过一哩、愈往深处愈亮地闪耀，或许还能瞥见远处一座天上的城；或者时时，循着一架价值的梯子，望见天使——使者们——攀上又折回。

===Original===
V

Well, now, I put it to you that without mental breeding, without at least some sense of ancestry, an Englishman can hardly have this perception of value, this vision. I put to you what I posited in an earlier course of lectures, quoting Bagehot, that while a knowledge of Greek and Latin is not necessary to a writer of English, he should at least have a firm conviction that those two languages existed. I refer you to a long passage which, in one of those lectures, I quoted from Cardinal Newman to the effect that for the last 3,000 years the Western World has been evolving a human society, having its bond *in a common civilisation*—a society to which (let me add, by way of footnote) Prussia today is firmly, though with great difficulty, being tamed. There are, and have been, other civilisations in the world—the Chinese, for instance; a huge civilisation, stationary, morose, to us unattractive; "but *this* civilisation," says Newman, "together with the society which is its creation and its home, is so distinctive and luminous in its character, so imperial in its extent, so imposing in its duration, and so utterly without rival upon the face of the earth, that the association may fitly assume for itself the title of 'Human Society,' and its civilisation the abstract term 'Civilisation.' "

He goes on:

> Looking, then, at the countries which surround the Mediterranean Sea as a whole, I see them to be, from time, the seat of an association of intellect and mind as deserves to be called the Intellect and Mind of the Human Kind.

===Chinese===
V

好，现在我把它向你们摆出来：一个英国人，若没有精神上的门第教养，若至少没有一点关于祖先来历的意识，便很难有这种对价值的感知，很难有这种异象。我把先前那几场讲演里立过的论点再向你们申说一遍——那里引了白芝浩（Bagehot）的话：希腊文与拉丁文的知识，对一个用英文写作的人并非必需；但他至少应当坚信不移：这两种语言存在过。纽曼（Newman）枢机有一长段文字，我在那几场讲演里引过，大意是说：近 3,000 年来，西方世界一直在孕育一个人类社会，其纽带在于*一种共同的文明*——（容我以脚注的方式添一句）今日的普鲁士正被稳步地、虽然万分艰难地，驯服到这个社会里来。世上另有一些、从前也曾有过别的文明——比如中华文明：庞大、停滞、阴郁，在我们看来缺乏魅力；「但唯独*这个*文明，」纽曼说，「连同那作为它的创造与家园的社会，品格如此独特而光耀，幅员如此帝王气象，历时如此令人肃然，在地球的表面上又如此全然无可匹敌，这个联合体尽可以名正言顺地自称『人类社会』，它的文明也尽可以领受『文明』这个抽象名词。」

他接着说：

> 那么，把环地中海的诸国合起来看，我看见它们自古便是一个理智与心灵的联合体的所在地——这个联合体配得上称作人类的理智与心灵。

===Original===
But I must refer you to his famous book *The Idea of a University* to read at length how Newman, in that sinuous, sinewy, Platonic style of his, works it out—the spread, through Rome, even to our shores, of the civilisation which began in Palestine and Greece.

VI

I would press the point more rudely upon you, and more particularly, than does Newman. And first, for Latin—

I waive that Rome occupied and dominated this island during 400 years. Let that be as though it had never been. For a further 1,000 years and more Latin remained the common speech of educated men throughout Europe: the "Universal Language." Greek had been smothered by the Turk. Through all that time—through the most of what we call Modern History, Latin reigned everywhere. Is this a fact to be ignored by any of you who would value "values"?

===Chinese===
但我不得不请你们去翻他那部有名的《大学的理念》（The Idea of a University），细读纽曼如何用他那曲折而遒劲的柏拉图式文笔，把这件事铺陈出来——那始自巴勒斯坦与希腊的文明，如何经由罗马一路播散，甚至播到了我们这两处海岸。

VI

这一点，我要比纽曼更粗率、也更切近地向你们敲打下去。先说拉丁文——

罗马占领并君临过本岛 400 年之久，这一节我姑且搁下，就当它不曾发生。此后又是 1,000 余年，拉丁文始终是全欧洲受教育者的共同语言：那「万国通行语」。希腊文则早被土耳其人闷死。整整那段时期——在我们所谓现代史的大部分岁月里——拉丁文四处君临。你们之中凡想在「价值」上有些眼力的，能把这个事实一笔抹杀吗？

===Original===
Here are a few particulars, by way of illustration. More wrote his *Utopia*, Bacon wrote all the bulk of his philosophical work, in Latin; Newton wrote his *Principia* in Latin. Keble's Lectures on Poetry (if their worth and the name of Keble may together save me from bathos) were delivered in Latin. Our Vice-Chancellor, our Public Orator still talk Latin, securing for it what attention they can: nor have

> The bigots of this iron time
> *Yet* call'd their harmless art a crime.

But there is a better reason why you should endeavour to understand the value of Latin in our literature; a filial reason. Our fathers built their great English prose, as they built their oratory, upon the Latin model. Donne used it to construct his mighty fugues: Burke to discipline his luxuriance. Says Cowper, it were

> Praise enough for any private man,
> That Chatham's language was his mother tongue,
> And Wolfe's great name compatriot with his own.

===Chinese===
这里举几件细目作个例证。莫尔（More）写他的《乌托邦》（Utopia），培根（Bacon）写他哲学著作的绝大部分，用的是拉丁文；牛顿（Newton）写他的《原理》（Principia），用的是拉丁文。凯布尔（Keble）的《诗学讲演》（但愿那讲演的价值连同凯布尔之名，能一道救我不致坠入突降之弊）也是用拉丁文讲授的。我们的副校长、我们的公共演说人至今还在讲拉丁文，能替它挣得几分注意便挣几分；而且

> 这个铁血时代的顽固派
> *尚*未把他们那无害的技艺唤作罪愆。

但还有一个更好的理由，要你们去设法懂得拉丁文在我们文学中的价值；一个为子孙的理由。我们的父辈建造他们伟大的英文散文，一如建造他们的演说，皆取范于拉丁文。多恩（Donne）靠它构筑他雄浑的赋格；伯克（Burke）靠它整饬他恣肆的繁茂。柯珀（Cowper）说，一个人只要

> 得到这样的赞美便已足够：
> 查塔姆（Chatham）的语言是他的母语，
> 沃尔夫（Wolfe）的大名与他的名字同为国人。

===Original===
Well then, here is a specimen of Chatham's language: from his speech, Romanly severe, denouncing the Government of the day for employing Red Indians in the American War of Independence. He is addressing the House of Lords:

> I call upon that right reverend bench, those holy ministers of the Gospel, and pious pastors of our Church—I conjure them to join in the holy work, and vindicate the religion of their God. I appeal to the wisdom and the law of this learned bench to defend and support the justice of their country. I call upon the bishops to interpose the unsullied sanctity of their lawn; upon the learned judges to interpose the purity of their ermine, to save us from this pollution. I call upon the honour of your lordships to reverence the dignity of your ancestors, and to maintain your own. I call upon the spirit and humanity of my country to vindicate the national character. I invoke the genius of the Constitution. From the tapestry that adorns these walls the immortal ancestor of this noble lord [Lord Suffolk] frowns with indignation at the disgrace of his country. In vain he led your victorious fleet: against the boasted Armada of Spain; in vain he defended and established the honour, the liberties, the religion—the *Protestant religion*—of this country, against the arbitrary cruelties of Popery and the Inquisition, if these more than Popish cruelties and inquisitorial practices are let loose among us—to turn forth into our settlements, among our ancient connections, friends, and relations, the merciless cannibal, thirsting for the blood of man, woman, and child! to send forth the infidel savage—against whom? against your Protestant brethren; to lay waste their country, to desolate their dwellings, and extirpate their race and name, with these horrible hellhounds of savage war!—hellhounds, I say, of savage war! Spain armed herself with bloodhounds to extirpate the wretched natives of America, and we improve on the inhuman example even of Spanish cruelty; we turn loose these savage hellhounds against our brethren and countrymen in America, of the same language, laws, liberties, and religion, endeared to us by every tie that should sanctify humanity. …

===Chinese===
好，那么这里便是查塔姆语言的一份样本：出自他一篇罗马人般严峻的演说，谴责当时的政府在美国独立战争中雇用印第安人（Red Indians）作战。他是向贵族院致辞：

> 我吁请那尊敬的主教席，那些福音的圣洁执事、我们教会的虔敬牧者——我恳求他们来加入这神圣的工作，为他们的上帝的宗教辩护。我吁请这博学席位上的智慧与法律，来捍卫、支持他们国家的正义。我吁请主教们以他们法衣未被玷污的神圣出面干预；吁请博学的法官们以他们白鼬袍的纯洁出面干预，把我们从这种污染中救出来。我吁请诸位勋爵的荣誉，去尊崇你们先人的尊严，也维护你们自己的尊严。我吁请我国的道义精神与人道，来为国民品格申辩。我祈唤宪法的守护神。在这些墙壁所悬的织锦上，这位勋爵大人[萨福克勋爵（Lord Suffolk）]的不朽先人，正为国耻愤然蹙额。他曾经率领你们凯旋的舰队，迎战西班牙人夸耀的无敌舰队，那是徒然的；他曾经捍卫并确立了这个国家的荣誉、自由、宗教——*新教*的宗教——以抵御教皇制与宗教法庭的专横酷虐，那也是徒然的，倘若这些比教皇更甚的酷虐、比宗教法庭更甚的手段竟被放纵到我们中间来——把那残忍的食人生番赶进我们的垦殖地，赶到我们旧日的盟好、朋友和亲眷中间——他们渴望着男人、女人、孩子的血！把那不信神的野人放出去——去对付谁？去对付你们新教的弟兄；去糟蹋他们的国土，去毁坏他们的家园，用这些野蛮战争的可怕地狱犬，去斩尽他们的种族、灭绝他们的名号！——我说，野蛮战争的地狱犬！西班牙曾武装嗜血犬去剿灭美洲的不幸土著，我们竟连西班牙残酷的不人道榜样也要加以改进；我们把野蛮的地狱犬放去咬我们的弟兄同胞——他们同我们语言相同、法律相同、自由相同、宗教相同，本有每一条应当圣化人性的纽带把我们联在一起。……

===Original===
> My lords, I am old and weak, and at present unable to say more; but my feelings and indignation were too strong to have said less. I could not have slept this night in my bed, nor reposed my head on my pillow, without giving this vent to my eternal abhorrence of such preposterous and enormous principles.

That was Chatham. For Wolfe—he, as you know, was ever reading the classics even on campaign: as Burke again carried always a Virgil in his pocket. *Abeunt studia in mores.* Moreover can we separate Chatham's Roman morality from Chatham's language in the passage I have just read? No: we cannot. No one, being evil, can speak good things with that weight; "*for out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaketh.*" We English (says Wordsworth)

> We must be free or die, who speak the tongue
> That Shakespeare spake. …

===Chinese===
> 诸位勋爵，我老了，衰弱了，此刻说不出更多的话；可我的情感与义愤太强烈，容不得我说得更少。今晚我无法在自己床上安眠，无法把头安放在枕上，倘若不把我对这些悖逆而穷凶极恶的原则的永恒憎恶，这样一泄而出。

那就是查塔姆。至于沃尔夫——你们知道，他即使在出征作战时也总是读着古典：伯克呢，也总是口袋里揣着一册维吉尔（Virgil）。*Abeunt studia in mores.*（拉丁文：研习化入品性。）况且，在我方才读过的那段文字里，我们能把查塔姆的罗马式道义同查塔姆的语言分开吗？不能，分不开。一个人若居心邪恶，就说不出带着那种分量的话；「*因为心里所充满的，口里就说出来。*」我们英国人（华兹华斯如是说）——

> 我们这说着莎士比亚说过的话舌的，
> 必须自由，否则便死。……

===Original===
You may criticise Chatham's style as too consciously Ciceronian. But has ever a Parliamentary style been invented which conveys a nobler gravity of emotion? "Buskined"?—yes: but the style of a man. "Mannered"?—yes, but in the grand manner. "Conscious"?—yes, but of what? Conscious of the dignity a great man owes to himself, and to the assembly he addresses. He conceives that assembly as "the British Senate"; and, assuming, he communicates that high conception. The Lords feel that they are listening as Senators, since it is only thus a Senate should be addressed, as nothing less than a Senate should be addressed thus.

Let me read you a second passage; of *written* prose:

> Laodameia died; Helen died; Leda, the beloved of Jupiter, went before. It is better to repose in the earth betimes than to sit up late; better, than to cling pertinaciously to what we feel crumbling under us, and to protract an inevitable fall. We may enjoy the present while we are insensible of infirmity and decay: but the present, like a note in music, is nothing but as it appertains to what is past and what is to come. There are no fields of amaranth on this aide of the grave; there are no voices, O Rhodopè! that are not soon mute, however tuneful; there is no name, with whatever emphasis of passionate love repeated, of which the echo is not faint at last.9

===Chinese===
你们尽可以批评查塔姆的风格过于有意识地模仿西塞罗。可曾发明过哪一种议会风格，能传达更庄严的情感之重？「厚底靴气」？——是的：可这是一个人的风格。「矫饰」？——是的，却是宏大手笔中的矫饰。「自觉」？——是的，可自觉的是什么？自觉到一位伟人欠自己、也欠他所面对的集会的那份尊严。他把那个集会设想为「不列颠元老院」；他既这样设想，也就把这崇高的设想传递了过去。勋爵们感到自己是以元老的身份在聆听；因为唯有对元老院才该这样说话，唯有不止是一个寻常集会的元老院，才配受这样一番话。

让我再读给你们第二段文字；一段*笔之于书*的散文：

> 拉俄达弥亚（Laodameia）死了；海伦死了；勒达——朱庇特所爱的人——先她们而去。及早安息于泥土之中，胜过迟迟不卧；胜过死死攀住我们感到正在脚底下碎裂的东西，把一场不可避免的坠落拖延下去。趁我们尚未觉出衰颓与朽坏，尽可以享受现在；可是现在，像音乐里的一个音符，唯有连着已逝者与将来者，才算得数。坟墓这一边没有不死草的田野；没有不会很快喑哑的声音，哦，罗多珀（Rhodopè）！任它多么动听；没有这样一个名字——纵然以热烈爱情所能有的全部重音反复念诵——它的回声到末了不再微弱。9

===Original===
Latin—all Latin—down to its exquisite falling close! And I say to you, Gentlemen, that passages such as these deserve what Joubert claimed of national monuments, *Ce sont les crampons qui unissent une génération à une autre. Conservez ce qu'ont vu vos pères*, "These are the clamps that knit one generation to another. Cherish those things on which your fathers' eyes have looked."

*Abeunt studia in mores.*

If, years ago, there had lacked anything to sharpen my suspicion of those fork-bearded professors who derived our prose from the stucco of Anglo-Saxon prose, it would have been their foolish deliberate practice of composing whole pages of English prose without using one word derivative from Latin or Greek. Esau, when he sold his birthright, had the excuse of being famished. These pedants, with a full board, sought frenetically to give it away—board and birthright. "*So when this corruptible shall have put on incorruption, and this mortal shall have put on immortality*"—almost, I say, these men had deserved to have a kind of speech more to their taste read over their coffins.

===Chinese===
拉丁文——通体是拉丁文——直到它那声精致的下行收束！而我对你们说，诸位先生：这样的段落，配享儒贝尔（Joubert）为民族纪念物所要求的那句话——*Ce sont les crampons qui unissent une génération à une autre. Conservez ce qu'ont vu vos pères*——「这些是把一代人与另一代人箍结起来的铁扣。珍藏你们父辈的眼睛看过的事物。」

*Abeunt studia in mores.*（拉丁文：研习化入品性。）

倘若多年前，还缺点什么来磨利我对那班分叉胡须的教授们的疑心——那班把我们的散文从盎格鲁—撒克逊散文的墙灰里推衍出来的教授——那缺点的东西，就是他们那愚蠢而存心的做法：接连写上整页整页的英文散文，不用一个源自拉丁文或希腊文的词。以扫（Esau）卖掉长子名分时，还推说自己饿得发昏。这些学究则饱食终日，却发狂似的要把它白送出去——连饭带长子名分一齐送人。「*这必朽坏的既变成不朽坏的，这必死的既变成不死的*」——我说，这几位先生简直配得上，叫人在他们棺木前诵读一篇更对口味的话语。

===Original===
VII

What, in the next place, can I say of Greek, save that, as Latin gave our fathers the model of prose, Greek was the source of it all, the goddess and genius of the wellhead? And, casting about to illustrate, as well as may be, what I mean by this, I hit on a minor dialogue of Plato, the "Phaedrus," and choose you a short passage in Edward FitzGerald's rendering:

> When Socrates and Phaedrus have discoursed away the noonday under the plane trees by the Ilissus, they rise to depart toward the city. But Socrates (pointing perhaps to some images of Pan and other sylvan deities) says it is not decent to leave their haunts without praying to them, and he prays:
> "O auspicious Pan, and ye other deities of this place, grant to me to become beautiful *inwardly*, and that all my outward goods may prosper my inner soul. Grant that I may esteem wisdom the only riches, and that I may have so much gold as temperance can handsomely carry.
> "Have we yet aught else to pray for, Phaedrus? For myself I seem to have prayed enough."
> *Phaedrus*: "Pray as much for me also: for friends have all in common."
> *Socrates*: "Even so be it. Let us depart."

===Chinese===
VII

其次，关于希腊文，除了一句——拉丁文给了我们父辈散文的范型，而希腊文是这一切的源头，是泉眼处的女神与精灵——我还能说些什么呢？左寻右想，要尽量说清我这意思，我碰上了柏拉图一篇小小的对话，《斐德罗篇》（Phaedrus），从里面给你们选出一小段，用爱德华·菲茨杰拉德（Edward FitzGerald）的译文：

> 苏格拉底与斐德罗（Phaedrus）在伊利索斯河畔的悬铃木下把正午的时光谈论尽了，起身要回城里去。苏格拉底（也许指着潘神（Pan）和别的林间神祇的几尊像）说，不先祷告就离开神明的居所，是失礼的，于是他祷告：
> 「哦，吉利的潘神，还有这地方的诸神，请恩准我在*内里*变得美好，也恩准我一切身外所有都能裨益我内在的灵魂。请恩准我把智慧看作唯一的财富，也恩准我拥有的金子不多不少，恰是节制能体面携带的分量。
> 「斐德罗，我们还有别的可祷告的吗？就我自己说，我像是已经祷告够了。」
> *斐德罗*：「也请替我这样祷告：因为朋友之间一切共有。」
> *苏格拉底*：「那就这样吧。我们走吧。」

===Original===
To this paternoster of Socrates, reported more than four centuries before Christ taught the Lord's Prayer, let me add an attempted translation of the lines that close Homer's hymn to the Delian Apollo. Imagine the old blind poet on the beach chanting to the islanders the glorious boast of the little island—how it of all lands had harboured Leto in her difficult travail; how she gave birth to the Sun God; how the immortal child, as the attendant goddesses touched his lips with ambrosia, burst his swaddling bands and stood up, sudden, a god erect:

> But he, the Sun-God, did no sooner taste
> That food divine than every swaddling band
> Burst strand by strand,
> And burst the belt above his panting waist—
> All hanging loose
> About him as he stood and gave command:
> "Fetch me my lyre, fetch me my curving bow!
> And, taught by these, shall know
> All men, through me, the unfaltering will of Zeus!"
> So spake the unshorn God, the Archer bold,
> And turn'd to tread the ways of Earth so wide;
> While they, all they, had marvel to behold
> How Delos broke in gold
> Beneath his feet, as on a mountainside
> Sudden, in Spring, a tree is glorified
> And canopied with blossoms manifold.
> But he went swinging with a careless stride,
> Proud, in his new artillery bedight,
> Up rocky Cynthus, and the isles descried—
> All his, and their inhabitants—for wide,
> Wide as he roam'd, ran these in rivalry
> To build him temples in many groves:
> And these be his, and all the isles he loves,
> And every foreland height,
> And every river hurrying to the sea.
> But chief in thee,
> Delos, as first it was, is his delight.
> Where the long-robed Ionians, each with mate
> And children, pious to his altar throng,
> And, decent, celebrate
> His birth with boxing-match and dance and song:
> So that a stranger, happening them among,
> Would deem that these Ionians have no date,
> Being ageless, all so met;
> And he should gaze
> And marvel at their ways,
> Health, wealth, the comely face
> On man and woman—envying their estate—
> And yet
> *You* shall he least be able to forget,
> You maids of Delos, dear ones, as ye raise
> The hymn to Phoebus, Leto, Artemis,
> In triune praise,
> Then slide your song back upon ancient days
> And men whose very name forgotten is,
> And women who have lived and gone their ways:
> And make them live agen,
> Charming the tribes of men,
> Whose speech ye mock with pretty mimicries
> So true
> They almost woo
> The hearer to believe he's singing too!
> Speed me, Apollo: speed me, Artemis!
> And you, my dears, farewell! Remember me
> Hereafter if, from any land that is,
> Some traveller question ye—
> "Maidens, who was the sweetest man of speech
> Fared hither, ever chanted on this beach?"
> I you beseech
> Make answer to him, civilly—
> "Sir, he was just a blind man, and his home
> In rocky Chios. But his songs were best,
> And shall be ever in the days to come."
> Say that: and as I quest
> In fair wall'd cities far, I'll tell them there
> (They'll list, for 'twill be true)
> Of Delos and of you.
> But chief and evermore my song shall be
> Of Prince Apollo, lord of Archery.
> God of the Silver Bow, whom Leto bare—
> Leto, the lovely-tress'd.

===Chinese===
在这篇苏格拉底的主祷文——它比基督教导主祷文早出四个多世纪——之后，让我再添上《得洛斯（Delos）的阿波罗颂》收尾诸行的一次试译，那颂歌出自荷马。请设想那年老的盲诗人立在海滩上，向岛民们咏唱这小小岛屿的光荣自诩——在万国之中，唯独它在勒托（Leto）艰难分娩的时辰收留了她；她怎样诞下太阳神；那不朽的孩子，当侍奉的众女神以仙馔轻触他的双唇，他怎样挣断襁褓，霍然而起，一位神明挺然屹立：

> 但他，太阳神，刚一尝到
> 那神圣的食物，每一条襁褓的布带
> 便一缕一缕地绽断，
> 束在他喘息的腰上的腰带也进裂——
> 全都松松地垂落
> 在他四周，他站立着发号施令：
> 「把我的琴取来，把我那弯弓取来！
> 有这两样施教，全人类
> 都将经由我，知道宙斯毫不动摇的旨意！」
> 长发未剪的神如此说，那勇武的箭手，
> 便转身踏上宽广的大地的路；
> 而他们，他们所有的人，都惊奇地看见
> 得洛斯在他脚下
> 迸裂成一片金黄，就像在山腰
> 春天里一棵树骤然荣耀起来，
> 披上重重叠叠的花的华盖。
> 可他迈着从容的步子摇摇地走了，
> 满心骄傲，佩着他崭新的兵器行头，
> 登上嵯峨的辛索斯山（Cynthus），望见了群岛——
> 全是他的，连同岛上的居民——因为无论
> 他游历到多远，各岛都在争相
> 在一座座丛林里为他建立庙宇：
> 这些都归他，还有他所爱的诸岛，
> 每一处海角的峻岭，
> 每一条奔流向海的河川。
> 但最主要的，是你，
> 得洛斯，一如当初，是他的欢悦所在。
> 在那里，束长袍的伊奥尼亚人（Ionians），各带着
> 妻小，虔敬地聚到他的祭坛前，
> 端端正正地庆祝
> 他的诞辰，用拳赛、用舞蹈、用歌：
> 以致一个异乡客，偶然来到他们中间，
> 会以为这些伊奥尼亚人不曾有过年纪，
> 人人不老，才聚得这样齐整；
> 他且要凝望，
> 惊叹于他们的种种：
> 健康、财富、那好看的容颜，
> 在男人与女人身上——艳羡他们的境遇——
> 然而
> 他最最不能忘怀的，将偏偏是*你*，
> 得洛斯的少女们，亲爱的人们，当你们唱起
> 颂扬福玻斯（Phoebus）、勒托、阿尔忒弥斯（Artemis）的
> 三重赞美诗，
> 然后又把歌滑回太古的岁月，
> 滑回那些连名字都被遗忘的男人，
> 和那些活过、走完各自路途的女人：
> 而让他们重新活转，
> 以甜美迷醉人类的部族，
> 你们用可爱的摹拟惟妙惟肖地学他们的言语，
> 学得那样真切，
> 几乎要诱得
> 听的人也相信自己正在歌唱！
> 为我催程吧，阿波罗：为我催程，阿尔忒弥斯！
> 还有你们，亲爱的，别了！记住我，
> 日后倘若，从世上任何一方土地，
> 有过客向你们问起——
> 「少女们，来到此地的人里，谁的言辞最甜美，
> 谁曾在这海滩上放声歌唱？」
> 我恳求你们
> 客客气气地回答他——
> 「先生，他只是个盲人，家住
> 岩石嶙峋的基俄斯（Chios）。但他的歌最好，
> 往后的日子也永远是最好。」
> 就这样说吧：而当我远游四方，
> 在筑着美好城垣的远方城市里，我会在那里讲起
> （他们会听的，因为这是真话）
> 得洛斯，和你们。
> 但首要地、永远地，我的歌将唱
> 王者阿波罗，箭术的君主，
> 银弓之神，勒托所生——
> 勒托，发辫可爱的勒托。

===Original===
Did time permit, I might quote you a chorus of Aeschylus, a passage from Thucydides or from Aristotle, to illustrate Gibbon's saying that the Greek language "gave a soul to the objects of sense, and a body to the abstractions of metaphysics." But there it is, and it has haunted our literature; at first filtering through Latin, at length breaking from Constantinople in flood and led to us, to Oxford and Cambridge, by Erasmus, by Grocyn:

> Thee, that lord of splendid lore
> Orient from old Hellas' shore.

To have a sense of Greek, too, is to own a corrective of taste. I quote another old schoolmaster here—a dead friend, Sidney Irwin:

===Chinese===
倘若时间允许，我原可以引给你们埃斯库罗斯（Aeschylus）的一支歌队、修昔底德（Thucydides）或亚里士多德的一段文字，来印证吉本（Gibbon）那句名言：希腊语「给感官的对象以灵魂，给形而上学的抽象以身体」。可它就在这里，它一直萦绕着我们的文学；起初透过拉丁文渗滤过来，到头来从君士坦丁堡破堤而出，滚滚而来，由伊拉斯谟（Erasmus）、由格罗辛（Grocyn）引到了我们这里，引到牛津与剑桥：

> 你啊，那辉煌典籍的主宰，
> 自古老的希腊海岸如旭日东升。

对希腊文有一点感觉，也便是持有一味矫正趣味的良药。我在这里再引一位老教师的话——一位亡友，西德尼·欧文（Sidney Irwin）：

===Original===
> What the Greeks disliked was extravagance, caprice, boastfulness, and display of all kinds. … The Greeks *hated* all monsters. The quaint phrase in the *Odyssey* about the Queen of the Laestrygones—"She was tall as a mountain, and they hated her"—would have seemed to them most reasonable. …

> To read Greek is to have a perpetual witness to the virtue of pruning—of condensing—a perpetual protest against all that crowds, and swells, and weakens the writer's purpose. To forget this is but to "confound our skill in covetousness." We cannot all be writers … but we all wish to have good taste, and good taste is born of a generous caution about letting oneself go. I say *generous*, for caution is seldom generous—but it is a generous mood which is in no haste to assert itself. To consider the thing, the time, the place, the person, and to take yourself and your own feelings *only fifth* is to be armour-proof against bad taste.

===Chinese===
> 希腊人所不喜欢的，是夸张、任性、自夸，以及一切炫示。……希腊人*恨*一切怪物。《奥德赛》里关于莱斯特律戈涅斯人（Laestrygones）的女王那句古怪的话——「她高得像一座山，他们恨她」——在他们看来一定最合情理。……

> 读希腊文，便是有一位常驻的见证人，见证剪裁——凝缩——之德；便是一场对一切堆砌、虚胀、削弱作者本意之物的永恒抗议。忘记这一点，不过是「把我们的本领混淆于贪多」。我们不可能人人成为作家……但我们人人都愿有好的趣味，而好的趣味生于一种宽宏的谨慎，关于放任自己这件事的谨慎。我说*宽宏*，因为谨慎很少是宽宏的——但那是一种不急于自我主张的宽宏气度。掂量事情、时候、场合、人物，而把自己和自己的情感*只放在第五位*，便是防备坏趣味的刀枪不入之身。

===Original===
VIII

They tell us that Greek is going, here. Well, I hold no brief for compulsory Greek; and I shall say but one word on it. I put it, rather idly, to a vote in a Cambridge Combination Room, the other day, and was amazed to find how the votes were divided. The men of science were by no means unanimous. They owned that there was much to be said even for compulsory Greek, if only Greek had been intelligently taught. And with that, of course, I agree: for to learn Greek is, after all, a baptism into a noble cult. The Romans knew *that*. I believe that, even yet, if the schools would rebuild their instruction in Greek so as to make it interesting, as it ought to be, from the first, we should oust those birds who croak and chatter upon the walls of our old universities. I find the following in FitzGerald's *Polonius*:

> An old ruinous tower which had harboured innumerable jackdaws, sparrows, and bats, was at length repaired. When the masons left it, the jackdaws, sparrows, and bats came back in search of their old dwellings. But these were all filled up. "Of what use now is this great building?" said they, "come let us forsake this useless stone-heap."

And the beauty of this little apologue is that you can read it either way.

===Chinese===
VIII

有人告诉我们，希腊文在这里正在衰亡。嗯，我不为强制希腊文当辩护人；关于这一点我只说一个字。前几天，我在剑桥的一间教师联谊室里把它相当随便地付诸表决，惊愕地发现票数竟是那样分的。科学家们也绝非一致。他们承认，即便强制希腊文，也大有一番道理可说——只要希腊文曾被聪明地教过。这一点我当然同意：因为学希腊文，说到底，是受洗加入一种高贵的崇奉。罗马人是懂得*这一层*的。我相信，即便到了今天，只要学校肯把希腊文教学重新建造起来，使它从头起就像本该的那样有趣，我们就能把那些在我们老大学的墙头呱呱啼叫、聒噪不休的鸟儿请出去。我在菲茨杰拉德的《波洛尼乌斯》（Polonius）里找到下面这则故事：

> 有一座残破的古塔，里面栖着数不清的白嘴鸦、麻雀和蝙蝠，末了终于得到修缮。泥瓦匠走后，白嘴鸦、麻雀和蝙蝠回来找它们旧日的窝。可那些窝全被填平了。「这幢大房子如今还有什么用处？」它们说，「来吧，我们舍弃这堆无用的石头吧。」

而这则小小寓言的美妙之处，正在于你怎么读它都行。

===Original===
IX

But, although a student of English Literature be ignorant of Greek and Latin as languages, may he not have Greek and Latin literature widely opened to him by intelligent translations? The question has often been asked, but I ask it again. May not *some* translations open a door to him by which he can see them through an atmosphere, and in that atmosphere the authentic ancient gods walking: so that returning upon English literature he may recognise them there, too, walking and talking in a garden of values? The highest poetical speech of any one language defies, in my belief, translation into any other. But Herodotus loses little, and North is every whit as good as Plutarch.

===Chinese===
IX

话虽如此——虽然一个英国文学的学生，就语言而论可以不识希腊文拉丁文，可难道聪明的译本就不能把希腊拉丁文学向他大大敞开吗？这个问题问过许多遍，但我今天再问一遍。难道不会有一些译本，为他开出一扇门，使他得以隔着一层大气看见它们，看见那大气里货真价实的古代诸神在行走；使他在回到英国文学里时，也认出他们在那里行走、交谈，走在一座价值之园里？任何一种语言里最高的诗歌语言，依我看，是无法译成任何别种语言的。但希罗多德（Herodotus）损失很小，而诺斯（North）的译笔丝毫不逊于普鲁塔克（Plutarch）本人。

===Original===
> Sigh no more, ladies; ladies, sigh no more!
> Men were deceivers ever;
> One foot in sea and one on shore,
> To one thing constant never

Suppose that rendered thus:

> I enjoin upon the adult female population (*γυναῑκες*), not once but twice, that there be from this time forward, a total cessation of sighing. The male is, and has been, constantly addicted to inconstancy, treading the ocean and the mainland respectively with alternate feet.

That, more or less, is what Paley did upon Euripides, and how would you like it if a modern Greek did it upon Shakespeare? None the less I remember that my own first awed surmise of what Greek might mean came from a translated story of Herodotus—the story of Cleobis and Biton—at the tail of an old grammar-book, before I had learnt the Greek alphabet; and I am sure that the instinct of the old translators was sound; that somehow (as Wordsworth says somewhere) the present must be balanced on the wings of the past and the future, and that as you stretch out the one you stretch out the other to strength.

===Chinese===
> 别再叹息吧，女士们；女士们，别再叹息！
> 男人们从来是骗子；
> 一脚踏在海里，一脚踏在岸上，
> 从不曾对一件事守常不变。

假如把它这样改译出来：

> 我责令成年女性人口（希腊文：γυναῑκες〔妇女〕），非止一次而是两次：自今以后，一切叹息须全面停止。男性之流行事，向来一贯沉溺于不守常之道，以两脚轮番分别踏于海洋与大陆之上。

佩利（Paley）之对待欧里庇得斯（Euripides），多少便是这么做的；倘若一个现代希腊人这样对待莎士比亚，你们意下如何？话虽如此，我却记得，我自己头一回敬畏地揣想希腊文可能意味着什么，来自希罗多德一篇译成英文的故事——克勒俄比斯与比顿（Cleobis and Biton）的故事——附在一本旧语法书的卷尾，那时我还没学过希腊字母；我确信老一辈译者的本能是健全的；不知怎么（如华兹华斯在某处所说），现在必须由过去与未来两翼来平衡，而当你把一翼伸展出去，另一翼也随之伸向力量。

===Original===
X

There is no derogation of new things in this plea I make specially to you who may be candidates in our School of English. You may remember my reading to you in a previous lecture that liberal poem of Cory's invoking the spirit of "dear divine Comatas," that

> Two minds shall flow together, the English and the Greek.

===Chinese===
X

我这一番恳请，丝毫没有贬抑新东西的意思；我是专门说给你们这些可能在我们的英文学院（School of English）里做候选人的人听的。你们也许记得，先前一讲里我给你们读过柯里（Cory）那首慷慨的诗，它唤来「亲爱的神圣的科玛塔斯（Comatas）」的精灵，说：

> 两股心灵将要汇流，英国的和希腊的。

===Original===
Well, I would have your minds, as you read our literature, reach back to that Dorian shepherd through an atmosphere—his made ours—as through veils, each veil unfolding a value. So you will recognise how, from Chaucer down, our literature has panted after the Mediterranean water-brooks. So through an atmosphere you will link (let me say) Collins's "Ode to Evening," or Matthew Arnold's "Strayed Reveller" up to the "Pervigilium Veneris," Mr. Sturge Moore's "Sicilian Vine-Dresser" up to Theocritus, Pericles' funeral oration down to Lincoln's over the dead at Gettysburg. And as I read you just now some part of an English oration in the Latin manner, so I will conclude with some stanzas in the Greek manner. They are by Landor—a proud promise by a young writer, hopeful as I could wish any young learner here to be. The title—

===Chinese===
好，我愿你们的头脑在阅读我们的文学时，隔着一层大气——他的大气已化作我们的大气——一层层帷幔般，回溯到那位多利安牧人那里，每一层帷幔展开一种价值。这样你们便会认出：从乔叟（Chaucer）以降，我们的文学如何一直渴慕着地中海的水溪。这样你们便会隔着一层大气，把（容我举出）柯林斯（Collins）的《黄昏颂》（Ode to Evening）、或马修·阿诺德（Matthew Arnold）的《浪游者》（Strayed Reveller）上溯到《维纳斯守夜歌》（Pervigilium Veneris）；把斯特奇·穆尔（Sturge Moore）先生的《西西里剪葡萄藤人》（Sicilian Vine-Dresser）上溯到忒奥克里托斯（Theocritus）；把伯里克利（Pericles）的葬礼演说下接到林肯（Lincoln）在葛底斯堡（Gettysburg）悼亡阵亡将士的演说。方才我给你们读过一段拉丁风味的英文演说，此刻我便要用希腊风味的一些诗节来作结。诗是兰多（Landor）作的——一位青年作家骄傲的许诺；那希望的气象，正像我希望在座任何年轻学子都会有的。诗题是——

===Original===
> Tanagra! think not I forget
> Thy beautifully storied streets;
> Be sure my memory bathes yet
> In clear Thermodon, and yet greets
> The blithe and liberal shepherd-boy,
> Whose sunny bosom swells with joy
> When we accept his matted rushes
> Upheav'd with sylvan fruit; away he bounds, and blushes.
> A gift I promise: one I see
> Which thou with transport wilt receive,
> The only proper gift for thee,
> Of which no mortal shall bereave
> In later times thy mouldering walls,
> Until the last old turret falls;
> A crown, a crown from Athens won,
> A crown no god can wear, beside Latona's son.
> There may be cities who refuse
> To their own child the honours due,
> And look ungently on the Muse;
> But ever shall those cities rue
> The dry, unyielding, niggard breast,
> Offering no nourishment, no rest,
> To that young head which soon shall rise
> Disdainfully, in might and glory, to the skies.
> Sweetly where cavern'd Dirce flows
> Do white-arm'd maidens chaunt my lay,
> Flapping the while with laurel-rose
> The honey-gathering tribes away;
> And sweetly, sweetly Attic tongues
> Lisp your Corinna's early songs;
> To her with feet more graceful come
> The verses that have dwelt in kindred breasts at home.
> O let thy children lean aslant
> Against the tender mother's knee,
> And gaze into her face, and want
> To know what magic there can be
> In words that urge some eyes to dance,
> While others as in holy trance
> Look up to heaven: be such my praise!
> Why linger? I must haste, or lose the Delphic bays.

===Chinese===
> 塔纳格拉（Tanagra）！莫以为我忘记
> 你那美丽典丽的街衢；
> 请确信我的记忆犹自沐浴在
> 清澈的特尔摩冬（Thermodon）里，犹自向
> 那欢快而大方的牧童致意——
> 他向阳的胸膛为欢喜而鼓动，
> 当我们收下他那缠结的灯芯草，
> 堆着林间的果实；他一蹦老远，红着脸跑了。
> 我许下一件礼物：我看见一件，
> 你将满心欢畅收下的礼物：
> 唯一配得上你的礼物，
> 后世无人能把它从你残剥的城墙夺走，
> 直到最末一座老塔楼倾塌；
> 一顶冠冕，一顶从雅典赢得的冠冕，
> 一顶冠冕，除拉托娜（Latona）之子外，
> 没有神明戴得了它。
> 或有城池，竟拒绝
> 把应得的荣誉给自己亲生的孩子，
> 以无情的冷眼看待缪斯；
> 但那些城池终将悔恨
> 自己那干涸、僵硬、吝啬的胸膛：
> 它不曾供养，也不曾安顿
> 那颗年轻的头颅——它很快便要
> 傲然直上云霄，挟着伟力与荣光。
> 在岩穴流出清泉的狄尔刻（Dirce）水边，
> 白臂的少女们甜美地咏唱我的歌，
> 一面挥动月桂与玫瑰，
> 把采蜜的族群驱散；
> 又甜美地、甜美地，阿提卡（Attica）的口舌
> 嗫嚅着念诵你们的科林娜（Corinna）早年的歌；
> 而那些曾栖居在家乡同胞胸臆里的诗章，
> 迈着更优雅的步履向她走来。
> 哦，让你的孩子斜斜地偎着
> 温柔的母亲的膝头，
> 凝望她的脸，一心想知道
> 那话音里能有什么样的魔法——
> 它催得一些眼睛翩然起舞，
> 又让另一些眼睛如在神圣的迷狂里
> 仰望上天：让这样的赞美归我！
> 还迟疑什么？我必须赶路，否则要误了德尔斐的月桂。

## Lecture VIII On Reading the Bible (I) / 第八讲 论圣经的阅读（上）

===Original===
Wednesday, March 6, 1918

I

“*Read not to Contradict and Confute,*" says Bacon of Studies in general: and you may be the better disposed, Gentlemen, to forgive my choice of subject today if in my first sentence I rule *that* way of reading the Bible completely out of court. You may say at once that, the Bible being so full of doctrine as it is, and such a storehouse for exegesis as it has been, this is more easily said than profitably done. You may grant me that the Scriptures in our Authorised Version are part and parcel of English Literature (and more than part and parcel); you may grant that a professor of English Literature has therefore a claim, if not an obligation, to speak of them in that Version; you may—having granted my incessant refusal to disconnect our national literature from our national life, or to view them as disconnected—accept the conclusion which plainly flows from it; that no teacher of English can pardonably neglect what is at once the most majestic thing in our literature and by all odds the most spiritually living thing we inherit; in our courts at once superb monument and superabundant fountain of life; and yet you may discount beforehand what he must attempt.

===Chinese===
1918 年 3 月 6 日，星期三

I

「*读书时不可存心诘难作者*，」培根（Bacon）论读书的总纲里这样说：诸位先生，倘若我在第一句话里，就把*那样*一种读圣经的路数彻底逐出庭外，你们或许更乐意原谅我今天所选的题目。你们立刻会说：圣经既像它现在这样充满教义，又向来是这样一座释义学的宝库，这话说来容易，行之有益则难。你们或许会承认：我们钦定版圣经（詹姆士王钦定本，AV）里的圣典是英国文学的一部分（而且远不止是一部分）；你们或许会承认：因此之故，一位英国文学教授便有权利、纵使不是有义务，以那个译本谈论它们；你们或许——既然已认可我始终拒绝把我们的民族文学同我们的民族生活割裂开来、或把它们看作割裂的两物——接受由此显然引出的结论：任何教英文的人，都不可原谅地忽视那件同时是我们文学中最庄严、又是我们继承物中最具属灵生命的东西；在我们的院宇中，它既是宏丽的丰碑，又是漫溢不息的生命之泉；然而，对他必须尝试的事，你们或许仍要预先打个折扣。

===Original===
For (say you) if he attempt the doctrine, he goes straight down to buffeted waters so broad that only stout theologians can win to shore; if, on the other hand, he ignore doctrine, the play is *Hamlet* with the Prince of Denmark left out. He reduces our Bible to "mere literature," to something "belletristic," pretty, an artifice, a flimsy, a gutted thing.

===Chinese===
因为（你们会说）他若去碰教义，便一头扎进风波拍击的浩渺水域，唯有强悍的神学家才能泅到岸边；他若反过来置教义于不顾，那出戏便是抽掉了丹麦王子的《哈姆雷特》（*Hamlet*）。他把我们的圣经贬成了「单纯的文学」（mere literature），贬成了某种「纯文笔的东西」（belletristic）——漂亮，小巧，一件人工造物，单薄脆劣，一件掏空了内脏的躯壳。

===Original===
II

Now of all ways of dealing with literature that happens to be the way we should least admire. By that way we disassociate literature from life; "what they said" from the men who said it and meant it, not seldom at the risk of their lives. My pupils will bear witness in their memories that when we talk together concerning poetry, for example, by "poetry" we mean "that which the poets wrote," or (if you like) "the stuff the poets wrote"; and their intelligence tells them, of course, that anyone who in the simple proposition "Poets wrote Poetry" connects an object with a subject by a verb does not, at any rate, intend to sunder what he has just been at pains, however slight, to join together: he may at least have the credit, whether he be right or wrong, of asserting his subject and his object to be interdependent. Take a particular proposition—John Milton wrote a poem called *Paradise Lost*. You will hardly contest the truth of that: but what does it mean? Milton wrote the story of the Fall of Man: he told it in some thousands of lines of decasyllabic verse unrhymed; he measured these lines out with exquisite cadences. The object of our simple sentence includes all these, and this much beside: that he wrote the total poem and made it what it is. Nor can that object be fully understood—literature being, ever and always, so personal a thing—until we understand the subject, John Milton—what manner of man he was, and how on earth, being such a man, he contrived to do it. We shall never *quite* know that: but it is important we should get as near as we can.

===Chinese===
II

而在一切对待文学的路数里，碰巧这偏偏是我们最不该称许的一种。循那条路，我们把文学同生活割裂开来；把「他们说了什么」同那些说了这话、并且当真作此想的人割裂开来——而那些人说这话，常常是冒着性命的危险。我的学生们凭记忆可以作证：譬如当我们一同谈论诗歌时，所谓「诗歌」，我们指的是「诗人们写下的东西」，或者（随你们怎么讲）「诗人们写的那玩意」；而他们的智力自然告诉他们：任何一个在「诗人写诗」这么一个简单命题里、用动词把宾语同主语连起来的人，无论如何都不会存心去拆散他方才好不容易——无论多么轻省——连在一起的东西：无论他对错，至少可以记他一功，因为他断言了他的主语与宾语相互依存。且看一个具体的命题——约翰·弥尔顿（John Milton）写了一首题为《失乐园》（*Paradise Lost*）的诗。这句话的真假你们几乎不会争辩：可它究竟是什么意思？弥尔顿写了人类堕落的故事：他用几千行不押韵的十音节诗行把它讲了出来；他以精妙的抑扬顿挫将这些诗行一一配量停当。我们这个简单命题的宾语，把这些统统包括在内，此外还包括这一层：是他写出了整部诗，并使它成为它现在的样子。而这个宾语——文学无论何时何地都是如此切己的东西——要待我们理解了主语约翰·弥尔顿，才能得到完全的理解：他是何等样的人，而这样的人究竟是怎么居然做成了这件事。这一点我们永远无法*全然*知晓：但重要的是，我们应当尽量走近它。

===Original===
Of the Bible this is yet more evident, it being a translation. Isaiah did not write the cadences of his prophecies, as we ordinary men of this country know them: Christ did not speak the cadences of the Parables or of the Sermon on the Mount, as we know them. These have been supplied by the translators. By all means let us study them and learn to delight in them; but Christ did not suffer for his cadences, still less for the cadences invented by Englishmen almost 1,600 years later; and Englishmen who went to the stake did not die for these cadences. They were Lollards and Reformers who lived too soon to have heard them; they were Catholics of the "old profession" who had either never heard or, having heard, abhorred them. These men were cheerful to die for the *meaning* of the Word and for its *authorship*—because it was spoken by Christ.

===Chinese===
对于圣经，这一点尤为显明，因为圣经是一部译作。以赛亚（Isaiah）并没有写下他那些预言的韵调——像我们本国的寻常人所得知的那种韵调：基督并没有说出比喻或登山宝训的韵调——像我们所知道的那种韵调。这些韵调是译经的人供给的。我们尽管去研习它们、学会以它们为乐；但基督不是为他的韵调受难的，更不是为了差不多 1,600 年之后才由英国人发明出来的韵调；而走上火刑柱的英国人，也不是为这些韵调死的。他们是为时太早、来不及听见过这些韵调的罗拉德派（Lollards）与改教家（Reformers）；他们又是「旧教门」的天主教徒，对那些韵调或者从未听闻，或者听过了便深恶痛绝。这些人欢欢喜喜地去死，是为了圣言的*意义*，为了它的*作者归属*（authorship）——因为它出自基督之口。

===Original===
III

There is in fact, Gentlemen, no such thing as "mere literature." Pedants have coined that contemptuous term to express a figmentary concept of their own imagination or—to be more accurate, an hallucination of wrath—having about as much likeness to a *vera causa* as had the doll which (if you remember) Maggie Tulliver used to beat in the garret whenever, poor child, the world went wrong with her somehow. The thoughts, actions and passions of men became literature by the simple but difficult process of being recorded in memorable speech; but in that process neither the real thing recorded nor the author is evacuated. "Belles lettres," "Fine Art" are odious terms, for which no clean-thinking man has any use. There is no such thing in the world as *belles lettres*; if there were, it would deserve the name. As for *Fine Art*, the late Professor Butcher bequeathed to us a translation of Aristotle's *Poetics* with some admirable appendixes—the whole entitled *Aristotle's Theory of Poetry and Fine Art*. Aristotle never in his life had a theory of Fine Art as distinct from other art: nor (I wager) can you find in his discovered works a word for any such thing. Now if Aristotle had a concept of "fine" art as distinguished from other art, he was man enough to find a name for it. His omission to do anything of the sort speaks for itself.

So you should beware of any teacher who would treat the Bible or any part of it as "fine writing," mere literature.

===Chinese===
III

事实上，诸位先生，根本不存在什么「单纯的文学」。学究们造出这个轻蔑的词，来表达一种他们自己想象中虚构出来的概念——说得更准确些，一场怒气的幻觉——它同任何*真实原因*（*vera causa*）的相像之处，大概就跟那具玩偶一样：你们想必记得，可怜的孩子玛吉·塔利弗（Maggie Tulliver）每逢这世界不知怎么跟她过不去，就跑到顶楼里去捶打那具玩偶。人的思想、行动与激情，经由一个简单却艰难的过程——被记录在令人难忘的言辞之中——而成为文学；但在这一过程中，无论被记录的真实事物，还是作者本人，都没有被掏空出去。「纯文学」（Belles lettres）、「美术」（Fine Art）都是可憎的名词，头脑清醒的人对它们毫无用处。世上并不存在*纯文学*（belles lettres）这东西；倘若它存在，它才配得上这个名号。至于*美术*（Fine Art），已故的布彻教授（Professor Butcher）遗赠给我们一部亚里士多德（Aristotle）《诗学》的译本，附有几篇令人赞赏的附录——全书题作《亚里士多德的诗学与美术理论》（*Aristotle's Theory of Poetry and Fine Art*）。亚里士多德终其一生，从不曾有过一种有别于其他艺术的「美术」理论：而且（我敢打赌）你在他已发现的著作里，也找不出一个词来称呼任何这样的东西。倘若亚里士多德当真抱有以一种有别于其他艺术的「美的」艺术的概念，以他的丈夫气概，总该给它寻出一个名字。他从未做任何这一类的事，这件事本身就够说明问题了。

所以，凡是想把圣经、或圣经的任何部分当作「美文」、当作单纯的文学来对待的教师，你们都当提防。

===Original===
IV

Let me, having said this, at once enter a caveat, a qualification. Although men do not go to the stake for the cadences, the phrases of our Authorised Version, it remains true that these cadences, these phrases, have for three hundred years exercised a most powerful effect upon their emotions. They do so by association of ideas by the accreted memories of our race enwrapping connotation around a word, a name—say the name "Jerusalem," or the name "Zion":

> And they that wasted us required of us mirth, saying,
> Sing us one of the songs of Zion.
> How shall we sing the Lord's song in a strange land? If I forget thee, O Jerusalem, let my right hand forget her cunning.

===Chinese===
IV

话虽如此，请容我立刻补进一项保留，一项限定。虽然人们并不为钦定版圣经的韵调、词句走上火刑柱，但这些韵调、这些词句三百年来对人的情感发生着极为强力的作用，这却依然属实。它们所以如此，靠的是观念的联想，靠我们这个种族积聚起来的记忆，把内涵裹缠到一个词、一个名字上——譬如「耶路撒冷」（Jerusalem）这个名字，或者「锡安」（Zion）这个名字：

> 掳掠我们的要我们欢笑，说：
> 给我们唱一首锡安的歌吧。
> 我们怎能在外邦唱耶和华的歌呢？耶路撒冷啊，我若忘记你，情愿我的右手忘记技巧。

===Original===
It must be known to you, Gentlemen, that these words can affect men to tears who never connect them in thought with the actual geographical Jerusalem; who connect it in thought merely with a quite different native home from which they are exiles. Here and there some one man may feel a similar emotion over Landor's

> Tanagra, think not I forget. …

But the word Jerusalem will strike twenty men twentyfold more poignantly: for to each it names the city familiar in spirit to his parents when they knelt, and to their fathers before them: not only the city which was his nursery and yet lay just beyond the landscape seen from its window; its connotation includes not only what the word "Rome" has meant, and ever must mean, to thousands on thousands setting eyes for the first time on *The City*: but it holds, too, some hint of the New Jerusalem, the city of twelve gates before the vision of which St. John fell prone:

===Chinese===
诸位先生，你们一定知道：这些词句能感动人流泪，而这样的人在思想上从不把它们同地理上那个真实的耶路撒冷相连；他们在思想上把它连向的，只是一个全然不同的故乡——他们正是从那里被放逐出来的。此地彼地，偶然会有一人为兰多（Landor）的诗句动起同样的感情：

> 塔纳格拉，请别以为我忘了你。……

但「耶路撒冷」这个词，会以二十倍的痛切刺中二十个人：因为在每个人那里，它都指着那座城——他父母跪祷时在精神上与它相熟，他们以前的列祖也是如此：这不只是一座做过他育婴室的城——而这座城却又恰恰躺在他从那扇窗望出去的风景的更远处；它的内涵不仅包括「罗马」这个词，对千千万万初次目睹*那座城*（The City）的人所曾经意味、并且永远必须意味的一切；它还藏着一点新耶路撒冷的暗示——那座有十二座门的城，圣约翰（St. John）一见它的异象便俯伏在地：

===Original===
> Ah, my sweet home, Hierusalem,
> Would God I were in thee!
> Thy Gardens and thy gallant walks
> Continually are green:
> There grows such sweet and pleasant flowers
> As nowhere else are seen.
> Quite through the streets with pleasant sound
> The flood of Life doth flow;
> Upon whose banks on every side
> The wood of Life doth grow. …
> Our Lady sings "Magnificat"
> With tones surpassing sweet:
> And all the virgins bear their part,
> Sitting about her feet.
> Hierusalem, my happy home,
> Would God I were in thee!
> Would God my woes were at an end,
> Thy joys that I might see!

===Chinese===
> 哦，我甜美的家园，耶路撒冷，
> 但愿我身在其中！
> 你的园囿与华美的径道
> 四时常青：
> 那里长着如此甜美悦目的花，
> 别处再也无处得见。
> 一道生命的洪流，
> 挟着悦耳之声流过条条街道；
> 在它两岸的每一边，
> 生长着生命之树。……
> 圣母咏唱「尊主颂」（Magnificat），
> 音调无比甜美：
> 众童贞女一同应和，
> 坐在她的脚边。
> 耶路撒冷，我快乐的家园，
> 但愿我身在其中！
> 但愿我的忧愁止息，
> 好叫我得见你的欢乐！

===Original===
You cannot (I say) get away from these connotations accreted through your own memories and your fathers'; as neither can you be sure of getting free of any great literature in any tongue, once it has been written. Let me quote you a passage from Cardinal Newman (he is addressing the undergraduates of the Catholic University of Dublin):

> How real a creation, how sui generis, is the style of Shakespeare, or of the Protestant Bible and Prayer Book, or of Swift, or of Pope, or of Gibbon, or of Johnson!

(I pause to mark how just this man can be to his great enemies. Pope was a Roman Catholic, you will remember; but Gibbon was an infidel.)

> Even were the subject-matter without meaning, though in truth the style cannot really be abstracted from the sense, still the style would, on *that* supposition, remain as perfect and original a work as Euclid's *Elements* or a symphony of Beethoven.
> And, like music, it has seized upon the public mind: and the literature of England is no longer a mere letter, printed in books and shut up in libraries, but it is a living voice, which has gone forth in its expressions and its sentiments into the world of men, which daily thrills upon our ears and syllables our thoughts, which speaks to us through our correspondents and dictates when we put pen to paper. Whether we will or no, the phraseology of Shakespeare, of the Protestant formularies, of Milton, of Pope, of Johnson's *Table-Talk*, and of Walter Scott, have become a portion of the vernacular tongue, the household words, of which perhaps we little guess the origin, and the very idioms of our familiar conversation. … So tyrannous is the literature of a nation; it is too much for us. We cannot destroy or reverse it. … We cannot make it over again. It is a great work of man, when it is no work of God's. … We cannot undo the past. English Literature will ever *have been* Protestant.

===Chinese===
你们（我说）躲不开这些经由你们自己的记忆和你们先辈的记忆积聚起来的内涵；正如任何一种语言的伟大文学，一旦写了出来，你们也未必保证能摆脱它。请让我给你们引一段纽曼枢机（Cardinal Newman）的话（他是在对都柏林天主教大学（Catholic University of Dublin）的本科生讲话）：

> 是何等真实的一种创造，何等自成一格（sui generis）——莎士比亚的文体，或新教圣经与《公祷书》的文体，或斯威夫特（Swift）的、蒲柏（Pope）的、吉本（Gibbon）的、约翰逊（Johnson）的文体！

（我停一停，请大家注意：此人对他的大敌们能够何等公正。你们记得，蒲柏是罗马天主教徒；吉本却是个不信教的人。）

> 纵使题材毫无意义——虽然文体实际上无法从意义中真正抽离——在*那个*假定之下，这文体仍会是一件同欧几里得的《几何原本》或贝多芬的一部交响曲一样完美、一样独创的作品。
> 而且，如同音乐，它已攫住了公众的心灵：英格兰的文学不再是一个印在书页里、锁闭在图书馆中的死字母，而是一个活着的声音，它带着自己的表达与情感，走入了人间世界；它每日在我们耳畔震响，把我们的思想吐成音节；它藉着写信的人对我们说话，在我们提笔时口授我们。无论我们愿意与否，莎士比亚的措辞、新教信条的措辞、弥尔顿的、蒲柏的、约翰逊《席间闲谈》（*Table-Talk*）的、瓦尔特·司各特（Walter Scott）的措辞，已然成为本土语言的一部分，成为家常的字眼——我们或许很少猜到它们的来历——成为我们日常谈话最地道的习语。……一个民族的文学，其专横竟至于此；它对于我们未免太强大了。我们不能摧毁它，不能颠倒它。……我们无法把它重新造一遍。它纵然不是上帝的功业，也是人的伟大功业。……我们不能撤销过去。英国文学将永远*曾是*新教的。
===Original===
V

I am speaking, then, to hearers who would read not to contradict and confute; who have an inherited sense of the English Bible; and who have, even as I, a store of associated ideas, to be evoked by any chance phrase from it; beyond this, it may be, nothing that can be called scholarship by any stretch of the term.

Very well, then: my first piece of advice *on reading the Bible* is that you do it.

I have, of course, no reason at all to suppose or suggest that any member of this present audience omits to do it. But some general observations are permitted to an occupant of this Chair: and, speaking generally, and as one not constitutionally disposed to lamentation (in the book we are discussing, for example, I find Jeremiah the contributor least to my mind), I do believe that the young read the Bible less, and enjoy it less—probably read it less, because they enjoy it less—than their fathers did.

===Chinese===
V

那么，我对之说话的听者，是这样一些人：他们读书不求诘难与驳斥；他们对英文圣经怀着一种承袭而来的感应；而且同我一样，储有一仓的联想观念，圣经里随便哪句短语都能唤起它们；除此之外，或许再没有哪怕极力伸张这个词、也称得上「学问」的东西了。

那就好办了：我*论读圣经*的第一条忠告就是：去读它。

当然，我毫无理由设想或暗示：今天在座的任何一位听者会忽略了这件事。但坐在这把讲座上的人，总容许说几句概括之论；而且，泛泛而谈，又作为一个天性不惯于哀叹的人（譬如在我们正在讨论的这本书里，我发现耶利米（Jeremiah）就是诸位执笔者中最不合我意的一位），我确实相信：如今的年轻人读圣经读得比他们的父辈少，从中得的乐趣也少——多半正因为乐趣少了，才读得少了。

===Original===
The Education Act of 1870, often in these days too sweepingly denounced, did a vast deal of good along with no small amount of definite harm. At the head of the harmful effects must (I think) be set its discouragement of Bible reading; and this chiefly through its encouraging parents to believe that they could henceforth hand over the training of their children to the State, lock, stock and barrel. You all remember the picture in Burns of "The Cotter's Saturday Night":

> The chearfu' supper done, wi' serious face,
> They, round the ingle, form a circle wide;
> The sire turns o'er, wi' patriarchal grace,
> The big ha'-Bible, ance his father's pride.
> His bonnet rev'rently is laid aside,
> His lyart haffets wearing thin and bare;
> Those strains that once did sweet in Zion glide,
> He wales a portion with judicious care,
> And "Let us worship God!" he says, with solemn air.

===Chinese===
1870 年的《教育法》，如今常常受到过于扫荡式的抨击，其实它做成了大量好事，也带来了绝不算小的确凿祸害。诸祸害之中，我想应当放在头一条的，是它挫抑了圣经的阅读；而它之所以如此，主要因为它鼓励父母们相信：从此以后，他们可以把管教儿女的事整个儿地——连锅带灶——移交给国家。你们都记得彭斯（Burns）《佃农的星期六夜》（"The Cotter's Saturday Night"）里那幅图画：

> 欢乐的晚餐既毕，他们面色肃然，
> 围着炉火，坐成宽宽的一圈；
> 老家长带着族长的仪度，
> 翻开那部大本的家府圣经——当年他父亲的骄傲。
> 他虔敬地把便帽搁在一边，
> 露出花白的、日渐稀疏的鬓发；
> 那曾在锡安甜美流泻的诗篇，
> 他审慎地拣出一段，
> 「让我们敬拜上帝！」他庄容说道。

===Original===
But you know that the sire bred on the tradition of 1870, and now growing grey, does nothing of that sort on a Saturday night: that, Saturday being tub-night, he inclines rather to order the children into the back-kitchen to get washed; that on Sunday morning, having seen them off to a place of worship, he inclines to sit down and read, in place of the Bible, his Sunday newspaper: that in the afternoon he again shunts them off to Sunday-school. Now—to speak first of the children—it is good for them to be tubbed on Saturday night; good for them also, I dare say, to attend Sunday-school on the following afternoon; but not good in so far as they miss to hear the Bible read by their parents and

> Pure religion breathing household laws.

"Pure religion"?—Well perhaps that begs the question: and I dare say Burns' cotter when he waled "a portion with judicious care," waled it as often as not—perhaps oftener than not—to contradict and confute; that often he contradicted and confuted very crudely, very ignorantly. But we may call it simple religion anyhow, sincere religion, parental religion, household religion: and for a certainty no "lessons" in day-school or Sunday-school have, for tingeing a child's mind, an effect comparable with that of a religion pervading the child's home, present at bedside and board:—

> Here a little child I stand,
> Heaving up my either hand;
> Cold as paddocks the they be,
> Here I lift them up to Thee;
> For a benison to fall
> On our meat and on us all. Amen.

—permeating the house, subtly instilled by the very accent of his father's and his mother's speech. For the grown man… I happen to come from a part of England10 where men, in all my days, have been curiously concerned with religion and are yet so concerned; so much that you can scarce take up a local paper and turn to the correspondence column but you will find some heated controversy raging over Free Will and Predestination, the Validity of Holy Orders, Original Sin, Redemption of the many or the few:

> Go it Justice, go it Mercy!
> Go it Douglas, go it Percy!

===Chinese===
可你们知道，在 1870 年的传统里养大的那位家长，如今头发渐白，星期六晚上是全不做这等事的：星期六是洗澡的夜晚，他宁肯把孩子们支使到后灶间去洗刷；星期天上午，看着他们打发去了敬拜的处所之后，他宁肯坐下来，撇下圣经，去读他的星期日报纸；到了下午，他又把他们拨去主日学校。现在——先说孩子们——星期六晚上洗个澡，对他们有好处；我敢说，次日下午上主日学校，对他们也有好处；但凡他们因此错过了听父母诵读圣经，错过了那

> 吐纳着家室法则的纯洁宗教

，便不算好了。「纯洁的宗教」？——嗯，这话或许是预支了论点：我敢说，彭斯笔下那位佃农，当他「审慎地拣出一段」的时候，拣出来的那一段，多半——也许多半不止——正是为了诘难与驳斥；他常常诘难得很粗率，驳斥得很无知。但无论如何，我们可以称它为朴素的宗教、真诚的宗教、为父母的宗教、家室的宗教：而且可以断言，无论日校或主日学校里的任何「功课」，要在孩子的心上染色，其功效都比不上一种弥漫于孩子家中的宗教——它在床头，也在饭桌：—

> 我站在这里，一个小孩子，
> 双手一齐向上举起；
> 两手像蛙掌一样冰凉，
> 我把它们向祢举起；
> 求赐福降下，
> 落在我们的饮食和我们众人身上。阿们。

——它弥漫全宅，凭着父亲与母亲说话的那种语调本身，悄悄地渗灌进去。至于成年人……我恰好出身于英格兰的一个地方10，那里的人在我有生之日一直出奇地关心宗教，如今依然如此；以至于你几乎拿不起一份地方报纸、翻不到读者来信栏，而总会看见某场火热的论战正在鏖战，争的是自由意志与预定论、圣职之有效性、原罪、多数人抑或少数人得蒙救赎：

> 加油，正义！加油，怜悯！
> 加油，道格拉斯！加油，珀西！

===Original===
But the contestants do not write in the language their fathers used. They seem to have lost the vocabulary, and to have picked up, in place of it, the jargon of the Yellow Press, which does not tend to clear definition on points of theology. The mass of all this controversial stuff is no more absurd, no more frantic, than it used to be: but in language it has lost its dignity with its homeliness. It has lost the colouring of the Scriptures, the intonation of the Scriptures, the Scriptural *habit*.

If I turn from it to a passage in Bunyan, I am conversing with a man who, though he has read few other books, has imbibed and soaked the Authorised Version into his fibres so that he cannot speak but Biblically. Listen to this:

> As to the situation of this town, it lieth just between the two worlds, and the first founder, and builder of it, so far as by the best, and most authentic records I can gather, was one Shaddai; and he built it for his own delight. He made it the mirror, and glory of all that he made, even the Top-piece beyond anything else that he did in that country: yea, so goodly a town was Mansoul, when first built, that it is said by some, the Gods at the setting up thereof, came down to see it, and sang for joy. …
> The wall of the town was well built, yea so fast and firm was it knit and compact together, that had it not been for the townsmen themselves, they could not have been shaken, or broken forever.

Or take this:

> Now as they were going along and talking, they espied a Boy feeding his Father's Sheep. The Boy was in very mean Clothes, but of a very fresh and well-favoured Countenance, and as he sat by himself he Sung. … Then said their Guide, Do you hear him? I will dare to say, that this Boy lives a merrier Life, and wears more of that Herb called Heart's-ease in his Bosom, than he that is clad in Silk and Velvet.

I choose ordinary passages, not solemn ones in which Bunyan is consciously scriptural. But you cannot miss the accent.

===Chinese===
可是论战的双方，不再用他们父辈的语言来写了。他们似乎丢失了那套词汇，拾来顶替的，是黄色报刊的黑话，而那种黑话在神学问题上从来不趋向明晰的界定。这一大堆论战材料，就其总量而言，并不比从前更荒唐、更狂乱：但在语言上，它把体面连着家常气一并丢掉了。它丢掉了圣经的色泽、圣经的声调，丢掉了圣经的*习气*。

倘若我从它转向班扬（Bunyan）的一段文字，我便是在同这样一个人交谈：他虽然没有读过几本别的书，却把钦定版圣经吸收浸泡进了自己的纤维里，以至于他一开口便只能是圣经腔。请听这一段：

> 至于这座城的坐落，它恰在两个世界之间；照我所能搜罗到的最好、最可信的记载，它的首位奠基者与建造者是一位名叫沙代（Shaddai）的；他为自己建造了它，好自得其乐。他使它成为他一切造物的明镜与荣耀，是他在那个国度里所做的一切之上的压卷之作：诚然，灵魂城（Mansoul）初建之时是如此美好的一座城，据有些人说，诸神在它落成之际曾下来观看，并且欢呼歌唱。……
> 这城的城墙建造得极好，它结合得那样牢固紧凑，倘若不是城里的居民自己动手，谁也不能摇撼它、拆毁它，直到永远。

再听这一段：

> 且说他们正边走边谈，瞥见一个男孩在牧放他父亲的羊。那孩子衣衫十分粗陋，面貌却极清朗俊秀；他独自坐着，一面唱歌。……他们的向导便说：你们听见他了吗？我敢说，这孩子过得比那身穿绫罗绸缎的人更快活，胸中佩着更多那味名叫「心安草」（Heart's-ease）的香草。

我选的是寻常段落，不是班扬有意识地仿效圣经的那些庄严段落。但那个腔调你们绝不会听漏。

===Original===
That is Bunyan, of course; and I am far from saying that the labouring men among whom I grew up, at the fishery or in the hayfield, talked with Bunyan's magic. But I do assert that they had something of the accent; enough to be *like*, in a child's mind, the fishermen and labourers among whom Christ found his first disciples. They had the large simplicity of speech, the cadence, the accent. But let me turn to Ireland, where, though not directly derived from our English Bible, a similar scriptural accent survives among the peasantry and is, I hope, ineradicable. I choose two sentences from a book of "Memories" recently written by the survivor of the two ladies who together wrote the incomparable Irish R.M. The first was uttered by a small cultivator who was asked why his potato-crop had failed:

> "I couldn't hardly say" was the answer. "Whatever it was, God spurned them in a boggy place."

Is that not the accent of Isaiah?

> He will surely violently turn and toss thee like a ball into a large country.

The other is the benediction bestowed upon the late Miss Violet Martin by a beggar-woman in Skibbereen:

> Sure ye're always laughing! That ye may laugh in the sight of the Glory of Heaven!

===Chinese===
这自然是班扬；我远不是说，我从小在其中长大的那些劳作者——渔场上的、干草田里的——说话带有班扬的魔力。但我的确断言：他们有几分那个腔调；在孩子的心目中，那已足以使他们*像*基督从中寻得首批门徒的那些渔夫与劳工了。他们有言语的阔大的简朴，有那韵律，那腔调。再让我转向爱尔兰：那里虽然没有直接从我们的英文圣经衍承，一种类似的圣经腔调却在农夫中间存活着，而且——我希望——是无法根除的。我从一本新近写成的《回忆录》（Memories）里拣出两句话；其作者是合写那部无与伦比的《爱尔兰皇家裁判官》（Irish R.M.）的两位女士中尚存人世的一位。头一句出自一个小佃户之口，有人问他土豆的收成为何失了收：

> 「这我几乎说不上来，」他答道。「不管它是怎么回事，上帝把它们撇弃在了一片烂泥地里。」

这难道不是以赛亚的腔调吗？

> 他必定猛烈地将你滚转抛掷，如同抛球一般，抛入宽阔之地。

另一句，是斯基伯林（Skibbereen）的一位行乞妇人祝给已故的维奥莱特·马丁（Violet Martin）小姐的福：

> 可不是，你们总是在笑！愿你们在天上荣耀的面前也笑得成！

===Original===
VI

But one now sees, or seems to see, that we children did, in our time, read the Bible a great deal, if perforce we were taught to read it in sundry bad ways: of which perhaps the worst was that our elders hammered in all the books, all the parts of it, as equally inspired and therefore equivalent. Of course this meant among other things that they hammered it all in literally: but let us not sentimentalise over that. It really did no child any harm to believe that the universe was created in a working week of six days, and that God sat down and looked at it on Sunday, and behold it was very good. A week is quite a long while to a child, yet a definite division rounding off a square job. The bath-taps at home usually, for some unexplained reason, went wrong during the weekend: the plumber came in on Monday and carried out his tools on Saturday at midday. These little analogies really do (I believe) help the infant mind, and not at all to its later detriment. Nor shall I ask you to sentimentalise overmuch upon the harm done to a child by teaching him that the bloodthirsty jealous Jehovah of the Book of Joshua is as venerable (being one and the same unalterably, "with whom is no variableness, neither shadow of turning") as the Father "the same Lord, whose property is always to have mercy," revealed to us in the Gospel, invoked for us at the Eucharist. I do most seriously hold it to be fatal if we grow up and are fossilised in any such belief. (Where have we better proof than in the invocations which the family of the Hohenzollerns have been putting up, any time since August 1914—and for years before—to this bloody identification of the Christian man's God with Joshua's?) My simple advice is that you not only read the Bible early but read it again and again: and if on the third or fifth reading it leave you just where the first left you—if you still get from it no historical sense of a race *developing* its concept of God—well then, the point of the advice is lost, and there is no more to be said. But over this business of teaching the Book of Joshua to children I am in some doubt. A few years ago an Education Committee, of which I happened to be Chairman, sent ministers of religion about, two by two, to test the religious instruction given in elementary schools. Of the two who worked around my immediate neighbourhood, one was a young priest of the Church of England, a medievalist with an ardent passion for ritual; the other a gentle Congregational minister, a mere holy and humble man of heart. They became great friends in the course of these expeditions, and they brought back this report—"It is positively wicked to let these children grow up being taught that there is no difference in value between Joshua and St. Matthew: that the God of the Lord's Prayer is the same who commanded the massacre of Ai." Well, perhaps it is. Seeing how bloodthirsty old men can be in these days, one is tempted to think that they can hardly be caught too young and taught decency, if not mansuetude. But I do not remember, as a child, feeling any horror about it, or any difficulty in reconciling the two concepts. Children *are* a bit bloodthirsty, and I observe that two volumes of the late Captain Mayne Reid—*The Rifle Rangers*, and *The Scalp Hunters*—have just found their way into The World's Classics and are advertised alongside of Ruskin's *Sesame and Lilies* and the *De Imitatione Christi*. I leave you to think this out; adding but this for a suggestion: that as the Hebrew outgrew his primitive tribal beliefs, so the bettering mind of man casts off the old clouts of primitive doctrine, he being in fact better than his religion. You have all heard preachers trying to show that Jacob was a better fellow than Esau somehow. You have all, I hope, rejected every such explanation. Esau was a gentleman: Jacob was not. The instinct of a young man meets that wall, and there is no passing it. Later, the mind of the youth perceives that the writer of Jacob's history has a tribal mind and supposes throughout that for the advancement of his tribe many things are permissible and even admirable which a later and urbaner mind rejects as detestably sharp practice. And the story of Jacob becomes the more valuable to us historically as we realise what a hero he is to the bland chronicler.

===Chinese===
VI

不过人们如今看出——或者似乎看出——我们这些孩子当年确实读了大量的圣经，尽管我们不得不以种种糟糕的方式受教去读它：其中最糟的一条，或许是长辈们把全书的每一卷、每一部分，当作同样蒙了默示、因而彼此等价的东西，硬灌给我们。当然，这首先意味着他们是按字面把这一切硬灌进来的：但在这上头我们且不多愁善感。相信宇宙是在一个六天的工作周里造成的，上帝在礼拜天坐下来端详它、并且看哪，这很好——这对任何孩子实在并无害处。一个星期，对一个孩子来说是相当长的时间，却又是一个明确的区划，把一件方方正正的活计收了尾。家里的浴缸龙头，通常总在周末出毛病，缘故无人说得出：管子匠星期一进门，星期六中午才收起家伙走人。这些小小的类比，确实（我相信）有助于幼小的心智，而且丝毫无损于它日后的成长。我也不会请你们为另一桩事过分地流连感伤——教孩子相信《约书亚记》里那位嗜血善妒的耶和华，同那位圣父一样可敬（因为二者不折不扣是同一位、永不改变，「在他并没有改变，也没有转动的影儿」）——就是福音书向我们显明、圣餐礼中为我们呼求的那位「同一的主，他的本性常是怜悯」的圣父。但我的确至为严肃地认为：倘若我们长大成人，并且在任何这样一种信仰里僵化成化石，那便是致命的。（我们还有比这更好的证据吗——自 1914 年 8 月以来，并且此前许多年，霍亨索伦家族（the Hohenzollerns）一直在向这种把基督徒之人的上帝与约书亚的上帝所作的血腥等同献上呼告？）我的浅白忠告是：你们不仅要在早年读圣经，还要一读再读；倘若读到第三遍、第五遍，它仍把你留在第一遍所留之处——倘若你从它仍得不出一种历史感，即一个民族如何*逐步发展*出它的上帝概念——那么这条忠告便失去了意义，无须再多说什么。但在这个把《约书亚记》教给孩子的问题上，我颇有些疑虑。几年前，有一个教育委员会——我碰巧是它的主席——派宗教界人士两个两个分头出去，抽查各地小学的宗教教学。在我住处邻近一带工作的那两位，一位是英国国教的年轻司铎，醉心中古，热恋礼仪；另一位是温良的公理会牧师，一个单纯圣洁、心存谦卑的人。他们在这些考察的旅程中结成了莫逆之交，带回的报告是——「任凭这些孩子在『约书亚与圣马太在价值上并无分别』、『主祷文的上帝就是那位下令屠戮艾城（Ai）的上帝』这样的教导底下长大，实在是件十足的恶事。」嗯，或许是吧。眼见如今的老人们能够何等嗜血，人不禁要想：趁着年幼捉住他们，教以正派——纵使教不成温驯——无论多早也不为过。但我不记得自己小时候对此感到过什么恐怖，也不觉得调和这两个概念有什么困难。孩子*确实*有点嗜血；而且我注意到，已故的梅恩·里德（Mayne Reid）上尉的两卷书——《来福枪游骑兵》（*The Rifle Rangers*）与《猎取头皮者》（*The Scalp Hunters*）——刚刚入选了《世界经典丛书》（The World's Classics），同罗斯金（Ruskin）的《芝麻与百合》（*Sesame and Lilies*）以及《效法基督》（*De Imitatione Christi*）并排登着广告。这件事就留给你们自己去想；我只补一句提示：正如希伯来人长过了他原始的部落信仰，人类那日进于善的心智也蜕下了原始教义的旧襁褓——他这个人，实际上比他的宗教更好。你们都听见过传道人设法证明：雅各不知怎么总归是个比以扫更好的人。我希望你们把这类解释一概拒绝了。以扫是个君子：雅各不是。年轻人的直觉撞上那堵墙，便无路可通。再往后，青年的心智便会察觉：写雅各生平的那位作者，怀着一副部落的心肠，自始至终认定，为了本部族的兴旺，许多事不但可行，甚至可嘉——而一个较晚、也较有城市教养的心智，却把这些事当作可憎的狡诈伎俩加以拒斥。而当我们认识到，对那位四平八稳的编年史家来说雅各是怎样的一位英雄，雅各的故事在历史上对我们便愈发显得可贵。
===Original===
VII

But of another thing, Gentlemen, I am certain: that we were badly taught in that these books, while preached to us as equivalent, were kept in separate compartments. We were taught the books of Kings and Chronicles as history. The prophets were the Prophets, inspired men predicting the future which they only did by chance, as every inspired man does. Isaiah was never put into relation with his time at all; which means everything to our understanding of Isaiah, whether of Jerusalem or of Babylon. We ploughed through Kings and Chronicles, and made out lists of rulers, with dates and capital events. Isaiah was all fine writing about nothing at all, and historically we were concerned with him only to verify some far-fetched reference to the Messiah in this or that Evangelist. But there is not, never has been, really fine literature—like Isaiah—composed about nothing at all: and in the mere matter of prognostication I doubt if such experts as Zadkiel and Old Moore have anything to fear from any School of Writing we can build up in Cambridge. But if we had only been taught to read Isaiah concurrently with the Books of the Kings, what a fire it would have kindled among the dry bones of our studies!

> Then said the Lord unto Isaiah, Go forth now to meet Ahaz, thou, and Shear-jashub thy son, at the end of the conduit of the upper pool in the highway of the fuller's field.

Scholars, of course, know the political significance of that famous meeting. But if we had only known it; if we had only been taught what Assyria was—with its successive monarchs Tiglath-pileser, Shalmaneser, Sargon, Sennacherib; and why Syria and Israel and Egypt were trying to cajole or force Judah into alliance; what a difference (I say) this passage would have meant to us!

===Chinese===
VII

但另一件事，诸位先生，我却是确知的：我们受教不善于此——这些书卷，一面被讲成彼此等价，一面却被分藏在互不相通的隔仓里。《列王纪》与《历代志》是当历史教给我们的。先知就是「先知」——蒙了默示、预言将来的人，而他们之所以预言将来，也只是碰运气，正如一切蒙默示之人所做的那样。以赛亚从来不曾被放到与他的时代的关系中去；而这对我们理解以赛亚——无论耶路撒冷的以赛亚，还是巴比伦的以赛亚——正意味着一切。我们犁田一般犁过《列王纪》与《历代志》，开出一张张统治者的名录，附着年代与重大事件。以赛亚则全是关于「空无一事」的美文；在历史上我们与他发生干系，只是为了核实这一位或那一位福音书作者书里某处关于弥赛亚（Messiah）的牵强附会的指涉。可是，从来不曾有过——从来没有过——真正优美的文学，像以赛亚那样的文学——是关于「空无一事」而写成的：至于预卜吉凶这桩本事，我怀疑扎德基尔（Zadkiel）与老摩尔（Old Moore）这样的行家，会害怕我们在剑桥所能建起的任何写作学院。可倘若当年有人教我们把以赛亚同《列王纪》对照着同时来读，那会在我们功课的枯骨中间点燃起怎样一场大火啊！

> 耶和华对以赛亚说：现在你去，你和你的儿子施亚雅述（Shear-jashub），到上池的水沟尽头、漂布者田间的大路上，迎接亚哈斯。

学者们当然知道那次著名会见的政治意义。可倘若我们当年也知道；倘若有人教过我们亚述（Assyria）是什么——它一脉相承的君王提革拉毗列色（Tiglath-pileser）、撒缦以色（Shalmaneser）、撒珥根（Sargon）、西拿基立（Sennacherib）；又教过我们叙利亚、以色列与埃及为何千方百计要哄诱或强迫犹大入盟——那么（我说）这一段经文于我们该是何等不同的意义！

===Original===
VIII

I daresay, after all, that the best way is not to bother a boy too early and overmuch with history; that the best way is to let him ramp at first through the Scriptures even as he might through *The Arabian Nights*: to let him take the books as they come, merely indicating, for instance, that Job is a great poem, the Psalms great lyrics, the story of Ruth a lovely idyll, the Song of Songs the perfection of an Eastern love-poem. Well and what then? He will certainly get less of "The Cotter's Saturday Night" into it, and certainly more of the truth of the East.
