There he will feel the whole splendid barbaric story for himself: the flocks of Abraham and Laban: the trek of Jacob's sons to Egypt for corn: the figures of Rebekah at the well, Ruth at the gleaning, and Rispah beneath the gibbet: Sisera bowing in weariness: Saul—great Saul—by the tent-prop with the jewels in his turban:

> All its lordly male-sapphires, and rubies courageous at heart.

Or consider—to choose one or two pictures out of the tremendous procession—consider Michal, Saul's royal daughter: how first she is given in marriage to David to be a snare for him; how loving him she saves his life, letting him down from the window and dressing up an image on the bed in his place: how, later, she is handed over to another husband Phaltiel, how David demands her back, and she goes:

> And her husband [Phaltiel] went with her along weeping behind her to Bahurim. Then said Abner unto him, Go, return. And he returned.

Or, still later, how the revulsion takes her, Saul's daughter, as she sees David capering home before the ark, and how her affection had done with this emotional man of the ruddy countenance, so prone to weep in his bed:

> And as the ark of the Lord came into the city of David, Michal Saul's daughter—

Mark the three words—

> Michal Saul's daughter looked through a window, and saw King David leaping and dancing before the Lord; and she despised him in her heart.

The whole story goes into about ten lines. Your psychological novelist nowadays, given the wit to invent it, would make it cover 500 pages at least.

===Chinese===
VIII

说到底，我倒敢说，最好的办法是不让一个男孩过早、过多地为历史所搅扰；最好的办法是让他起初就在圣经里纵情驰骋，如同他在《一千零一夜》（*The Arabian Nights*）里驰骋那样：让他按着书卷来了一部便读一部，只须略作指点，譬如：《约伯记》（Job）是一首伟大的诗，《诗篇》是伟大的抒情诗，路得（Ruth）的故事是一支可爱的田园牧歌，《雅歌》是东方情诗的极致。好，那又怎样？他从中带进的那点《佃农的星期六夜》自然要少些，而带进的东方的真实却肯定要多些。在那里，他将亲身感受那整个辉煌而野蛮的故事：亚伯拉罕（Abraham）与拉班（Laban）的羊群；雅各的众子为籴粮下埃及的跋涉；井旁的利百加（Rebekah）、拾穗时的路得、绞架底下的利斯巴（Rispah）的身形；疲乏中躬身的西西拉（Sisera）；扫罗——伟大的扫罗——倚着帐棚的橛子，头巾上缀着宝石：

> 它那些华贵的雄性蓝宝石，还有一颗颗勇气在胸的红宝石。

或者看看——从那浩浩荡荡的行列里拣出一两幅画面——看看米甲（Michal），扫罗王的女儿：她起初如何被许配给大卫（David），好叫他陷进网罗；她如何因爱他而救他的性命，把他从窗口缒下去，又把一尊神像打扮好摆在床上顶替他：后来她如何被转交给另一个丈夫帕铁（Phaltiel），大卫如何讨她还回，而她去了：

> 她的丈夫［帕铁］跟着她，一面走一面哭，直送到巴户琳（Bahurim）。押尼珥（Abner）对他说：你回去吧。他就回去了。

或者，再往后，扫罗的这个女儿，看见大卫在约柜前雀跃着进城，心中如何起了反感；她的一腔情意，如何同这个红光满面、动辄伏在床上哭泣的易感之人两讫了：

> 耶和华的约柜进了大卫城的时候，米甲，扫罗的女儿——

留意这三个词——

> 扫罗的女儿米甲从窗户里观看，见大卫王在耶和华面前踊跃跳舞，心里就轻视他。

整个故事不过十行上下便写完了。如今你们的心理小说家，倘若真有虚构出它来的才具，少说也要铺陈上 500 页。

===Original===
Or take the end of David in the first two chapters of the First Book of Kings, with its tale of Oriental intrigues, plots, treacheries, murderings in the depths of the horrible palace wherein the old man is dying. Or read of Solomon and his ships and his builders, and see his Temple growing (as Heber put it) like a tall palm, with no sound of hammers. Or read again the end of Queen Athaliah:

> And when Athaliah heard the noise of the guard and of the people, she came to the people into the temple of the Lord.—And when she looked, behold, the king stood by a pillar, as the manner was, and the princes and the trumpeters by the king, and all the people of the land rejoiced, and blew with trumpets: And Athaliah rent her clothes, and cried Treason, Treason.—But Jehoiada the priest commanded the captains of the hundreds, the officers of the host, and said unto them, Have her forth without the ranges. …
> —And they laid hands on her; and she went by the way by the which the horses came into the king's house: and there was she slain.

Let a youngster read this, I say, just as it is written; and how the true East—sound, scent, form, colour—pours into the narrative!—cymbals and trumpets, leagues of sand, caravans trailing through the heat, priest and soldiery and kings going up between them to the altar; blood at the foot of the steps, blood everywhere, smell of blood mingled with spices, sandalwood, dung of camels!

Yes, but how—if you will permit the word—how the *enjoyment* of it as magnificent literature might be enhanced by a scholar who would condescend to whisper, of his knowledge, the magical word here or there, to the child as he reads! For an instance:

===Chinese===
或者读《列王纪上》头两章里大卫的结局，那里面讲的是东方的钩心斗角、密谋、叛卖，在那座阴森可怖的宫殿深处、在那位垂死老人的榻前发生的种种杀戮。或者读所罗门（Solomon）和他的船队、他的匠人，看他的圣殿（如希伯（Heber）所说）像一株高高的棕榈生长起来，听不见一点锤凿之声。或者再读亚他利雅（Athaliah）王后的结局：

> 亚他利雅听见护卫兵和民的声音，就到民那里，进耶和华的殿——她一看，见王照例站在柱旁，首领和吹号的人侍立在王左右，国中的众民都欢乐吹号；亚他利雅就撕裂衣服，喊着说：反了！反了！——祭司耶何耶大（Jehoiada）吩咐统管军兵的百夫长说：把她赶出班外。……
> ——众人就拿住她；她走到马匹进王宫的那条路上，就在那里把她杀了。

我说，让一个少年人照着字面所写的来读这一段；看那真正的东方——声音、气味、形状、色彩——如何涌进叙事！——铙钹与号角，绵延数里的沙海，顶着酷热迤逦而行的商队，祭司、兵卒与君王从其间拾级而上、走向祭坛；台阶脚下有血，遍地是血，血的气味混着香料、檀香木、骆驼粪的气味！

是啊，可是——倘若你们容许我用这个词——把它当作辉煌的文学来*享受*，这份享受，倘若有一位学者肯屈尊俯就，在孩子读着的时候，把他学问里的那句魔语在这里或那里低声耳语一句，又该被增添多少啊！举个例子：

===Original===
No child—no grown man with any sense of poetry—can deny his ear to the Forty-fifth Psalm; the one that begins "My heart is inditing a good matter," and plunges into a hymn of royal nuptials. First (you remember) the singing-men, the sons of Korah, lift their chant to the bridegroom, the King:

> Gird thy sword upon thy thigh, O most mighty … And in thy majesty ride prosperously.

Or as we hear it in the Book of Common Prayer:

> Good luck have thou with thine honour …
> —because of the word of truth, of meekness, and righteousness; and thy right hand shall teach thee terrible things. …
> All thy garments smell of myrrh, aloes, and cassia: out of the ivory palaces, whereby they have made thee glad.

Anon they turn to the Bride:

> Hearken, O daughter, and consider, and incline thine ear; forget also thine own people, and thy father's house. …
> The King's daughter is all glorious within: her clothing is of wrought gold.
> She shall be brought unto the king in raiment of needlework: the virgins that be her fellows shall bear her company. And the daughter of Tyre shall be there with a gift. Instead of thy fathers shall be thy children, whom thou mayest make princes in all the earth.

For whom (wonders the young reader, spellbound by this), for what happy bride and bridegroom was this glorious chant raised? Now suppose that, just here, he has a scholar ready to tell him what is likeliest true—that the bridegroom was Ahab—that the bride, the daughter of Sidon, was no other than Jezebel, and became what Jezebel now is—with what an awe of surmise would two other passages of the history toll on his ear?

===Chinese===
任何一个孩子——任何一个稍具诗感的成年人——都无法对《诗篇》第四十五篇掩上耳朵；就是那篇以「我心里涌出美事」开篇、随即一头扎进王家婚颂的篇章。起初（你们记得），歌唱的人、可拉（Korah）的后裔，把颂歌扬向新郎，那位君王：

> 大能者啊，愿你腰间佩刀……愿你在威严中乘驾而往，无往不利。

或者，照我们在《公祷书》里听到的：

> 愿福祉与你的尊荣同在……
> ——为着真理之言、温柔与公义而前行；你的右手必指教你可畏之事。……
> 你一切的衣服都散发着没药、沉香与肉桂的香气：从象牙宫中——它们使你欢喜。

随即他们转向新娘：

> 女子啊，你要听，要想，要侧耳而听！不要记念你的民和你的父家。……
> 王女在宫里极其荣华，她的衣服是金线绣的。
> 她要穿着锦绣的衣服被引到王前，陪伴她的众童女要随行左右。推罗的女儿也必临在，带着礼物。你的子孙要接续你的列祖，你要立他们在全地作王。

为了谁（年轻的读者为之目眩神迷，不免纳闷），为了怎样一对幸福的新娘新郎，才扬起这支辉煌的颂歌？好，请设想：恰在此处，有一位学者随侍在旁，可以告诉他什么是最近情理的实情——新郎便是亚哈（Ahab）——新娘，那西顿（Sidon）的女儿，不是别人，正是耶洗别（Jezebel），而她后来便成了今日所谓耶洗别的那个耶洗别——那么，历史中另外两段文字，又将带着怎样一种惊惧的悬想，在他耳边悠悠鸣响呢？

===Original===
> And one washed the chariot in the pool of Samaria; and the dogs licked up his blood. …
> And when he [Jehu] was come in, he did eat and drink, and said, Go, see now this cursed woman, and bury her: for she is a king's daughter.
> And they went to bury her: but they found no more of her than the skull, and the feet, and the palms of her hands.
> Wherefore they came again, and told him. And he said, This is the word of the Lord, which he spake by his servant Elijah the Tishbite, saying, In the portion of Jezreel shall dogs eat the flesh of Jezebel … so that [men] shall not say, This is Jezebel.

In another lecture, Gentlemen, I propose to take up the argument and attempt to bring it to this point. "How can we, having this incomparable work, *necessary* for study by all who would write English, bring it within the ambit of the English Tripos and yet avoid offending the experts?"

===Chinese===
> 有人在撒玛利亚的池子里洗那辆战车；狗来舔了他的血。……
> 他［耶户］进去，吃了喝了，便说：你们现在去看看那个受咒诅的妇人，把她葬了；因为她原是王的女儿。
> 他们就去葬她，却只寻得她的头骨、双脚和两只手掌。
> 他们回来告诉了他。他说：这正是耶和华的话，是他藉他仆人提斯比人以利亚（Elijah the Tishbite）所说的，说：在耶斯列（Jezreel）的田间，狗必吃耶洗别的肉……以致人不再说：这是耶洗别。

诸位先生，在另一讲里，我打算重拾这条论证，并尝试把它推进到这样一点：「我们既有这部无与伦比的作品——凡想写英文的人都*必须*研读的作品——怎样才能把它纳入剑桥英文荣誉学位考试（English Tripos）的范围之内，而又不致开罪各位专家呢？」

## Lecture IX On Reading the Bible (II) / 第九讲 论圣经的阅读（中）

===Original===
Wednesday, April 24, 1918

I

We left off last term, Gentlemen, upon a note of protest. We wondered why it should be that our English Version of the Bible lies under the ban of schoolmasters, Boards of Studies, and all who devise courses of reading and examinations in English Literature: that among our "prescribed books" we find Chaucer's "Prologue," we find *Hamlet*, we find *Paradise Lost*, we find Pope's "Essay on Man," again and again, but the Book of Job never; *The Vicar of Wakefield* and Gray's "Elegy" often, but Ruth or Isaiah, Ecclesiasticus or Wisdom never.

I propose this morning:
1. to enquire into the reasons for this, so far as I can guess and interpret them;
2. to deal with such reasons as we can discover or surmise;
3. to suggest today, some simple first aid: and in another lecture, taking for experiment a single book from the Authorised Version, some practical ways of including it in the ambit of our new English Tripos. This will compel me to be definite: and as definite proposals invite definite objections, by this method we are likeliest to know where we are, and if the reform we seek be realisable or illusory.

===Chinese===
1918 年 4 月 24 日，星期三

I

诸位先生，上学期我们是在一个抗议的音调上停下来的。我们纳闷：为什么我们的英文版圣经竟受着教师们、学科委员会（Boards of Studies）以及一切设计英国文学阅读书目与考试之人的禁制：在我们开列的「指定书目」里，乔叟（Chaucer）的「总引」一遍又一遍地出现，《哈姆雷特》（*Hamlet*）出现，《失乐园》（*Paradise Lost*）出现，蒲柏（Pope）的《人论》（"Essay on Man"）出现，一遍又一遍，《约伯记》却从来不出现；《威克菲尔德的牧师》（*The Vicar of Wakefield*）与格雷（Gray）的「挽歌」（"Elegy"）屡见不鲜，《路得记》（Ruth）、《以赛亚书》（Isaiah）、《德训篇》（Ecclesiasticus）或《智慧篇》（Wisdom）却从来不。

我今天提议：
1. 尽我所能揣摩并解释个中缘由，对这些缘由作一番探究；
2. 处理我们所能发现或推测出的这些缘由；
3. 今天先提出一些简单的急救办法；在另一讲里，则从钦定版圣经（詹姆士王钦定本，AV）中取出单独一卷书来作试验，提出若干切实的办法，把它纳入我们新的剑桥英文荣誉学位考试（English Tripos）的范围。这将迫使我说得明确；而明确的提议招致明确的反对，靠这个办法，我们最有望知道自己身处何地，我们所寻求的改革究竟可以实现，还是一场虚幻。

===Original===
II

I shall ask you then, first, to assent with me, that the Authorised Version of the Holy Bible is, as a literary achievement, one of the greatest in our language; nay, with the possible exception of the complete works of Shakespeare, the very greatest. You will certainly not deny this.

As little, or less, will you deny that more deeply than any other book—more deeply even than all the writings of Shakespeare—far more deeply—it has influenced our literature. Here let me repeat a short passage from a former lecture of mine (May 15, 1913, five years ago). I had quoted some few glorious sentences such as:

===Chinese===
II

那么首先，我要请你们与我一同认定：钦定版圣经作为一项文学成就，在我们语言里是最伟大的成就之一；不，除莎士比亚（Shakespeare）的全集或可例外，它就是最伟大的。这一点你们断然不会否认。

你们同样不会否认——或者说更不会否认：它对我们文学的影响，比任何别的书都深——比莎士比亚的全部著述还深——深得多。这里，容我重引我从前一讲（1913 年 5 月 15 日，五年以前）里的一小段。我当时引过几句光辉的句子，如：

===Original===
> Thine eyes shall see the king in his beauty: they shall behold the land that is very far off.
> And a man shall be as an hiding-place from the wind, and a covert from the tempest; as rivers of water in a dry place, as the shadow of a great rock in a weary land. …
> So when this corruptible shall have put on incorruption, and this mortal shall have put on immortality …

and having quoted these I went on:

> When a nation has achieved this manner of diction, these rhythms for its dearest beliefs, a literature is surely established. … Wyclif, Tyndale, Coverdale and others before the forty-seven had wrought. The Authorised Version, setting a seal on all, set a seal on our national style. … It has cadences homely and sublime, yet so harmonises them that the voice is always one. Simple men—holy and humble men of heart like Isaak Walton and Bunyan—have their lips touched and speak to the homelier tune. Proud men, scholars—Milton, Sir Thomas Browne—practise the rolling Latin sentence; but upon the rhythms of our Bible they, too, fall back—"The great mutations of the world are acted, or time may be too short for our designs." "Acquaint thyself with the Choragium of the stars." "There is nothing immortal but immortality." The precise man Addison cannot excel one parable in brevity or in heavenly clarity: the two parts of Johnson's antithesis come to no more than this "Our Lord has gone up to the sound of a trump; with the sound of a trump our Lord has gone up." The Bible controls its enemy Gibbon as surely as it haunts the curious music of a light sentence of Thackeray's. It is in everything we see, hear, feel, because it is in us, in our blood.

===Chinese===
> 你的眼必见王的华美，他们必望见辽远之地。
> 必有一人像避风所，可以躲避狂风；像避暴雨的覆庇，像干旱之地的河流，像疲乏之地大磐石的影子。……
> 这必朽坏的既变成不朽坏的，这必死的既变成不死的……

引过这几句，我当时接着说：

> 当一个民族为它最珍视的信念获得了这样一种措辞方式、这样一些节奏，一种文学便确然确立起来。……威克里夫（Wyclif）、廷代尔（Tyndale）、科弗代尔（Coverdale）以及四十七位译者之前的诸位，都早已耕耘过了。钦定版圣经既为这一切盖上印，也就为我们的民族文风盖了印。……它的抑扬既家常又崇高，却把二者调和得如此妥帖，声音始终如一。朴质的人——心地圣洁而谦卑的人，像伊扎克·沃尔顿（Isaak Walton）与班扬（Bunyan）——唇上受了点化，说着那更家常的调子。骄傲的人、学者——弥尔顿（Milton）、托马斯·布朗爵士（Sir Thomas Browne）——操演滔滔的拉丁句式；可连他们也回落到我们圣经的节奏上——「世界的种种大变故都在上演，不然便是时间容不下我们的图谋。」「去结识众星的领唱班吧。」「除了不朽本身，没有什么是不朽的。」精确的艾迪生（Addison），论简洁、论属天的明晰，超不过任何一篇比喻：约翰逊（Johnson）那对偶的两支，归结起来不过是这一句——「我们的主随号角之声升上去了；随着号角之声，我们的主升上去了。」圣经辖制着它的敌人吉本（Gibbon），一如它萦绕在萨克雷（Thackeray）一句轻巧句子的奇妙音乐里。凡我们看见、听见、感到的一切里都有它，因为它在我们里面，在我们的血里。

===Original===
If that be true, or less than gravely overstated: if the English Bible hold this unique place in our literature; if it be at once a monument, an example and (best of all) a well of English undefiled, no stagnant water, but quick, running, curative, refreshing, vivifying; may we not agree, Gentlemen, to require the weightiest reason why our instructors should continue to hedge in the temple and pipe the fountain off in professional conduits, forbidding it to irrigate freely our ground of study?

It is done so complacently that I do not remember to have met one single argument put up in defence of it; and so I am reduced to guesswork. What can be the justifying reason for an embargo on the face of it so silly and arbitrary, if not senseless?

III

Does it reside perchance in some primitive instinct of taboo; of a superstition of fetish-worship fencing off sacred things as unmentionable, and reinforced by the bad Puritan notion that holy things are by no means to be enjoyed?

===Chinese===
倘若这话是真的，或还不算过分夸大：倘若英文圣经在我们的文学里果真占着这个独一无二的位置；倘若它同时是一座纪念碑、一个典范，并且（最好的是）一泓未曾搀杂的英文清泉，不是死水，而是活水、流水，能医治、能清凉、能赋生活力的水；那么诸位先生，我们难道不该一致要求，请我们的教育者们拿出最重大的理由，说明他们为什么还继续把圣殿圈禁起来、用专门的管道把泉水引开，不许它自由灌溉我们研学的园地？

这件事做得这样心安理得，以致我不记得曾经遇见过哪怕一条为它辩护的论据；于是我只落得靠臆测。一道表面上如此愚蠢、如此专断——倘不说全然无理——的禁令，它能拿出来的正当理由会是什么呢？

III

它会不会栖身于某种原始的禁忌本能；栖身于一种拜物教式的迷信——把圣物围起来当作不可言说之物——又得到一种恶劣的清教观念的助长，以为圣物断乎不可享用？

===Original===
If so, I begin by referring you to the Greeks and their attitude towards the Homeric poems. We, of course, hold the Old Testament more sacred than Homer. But I very much doubt if it be more sacred to us than the *Iliad* and the *Odyssey* were to an old Athenian, in his day. To the Greeks—and to forget this is the fruitfullest source of error in dealing with the Tragedians or even with Aristophanes—to the Greeks, their religion, such as it was, mattered enormously. They built their Theatre upon it, as we most certainly do not; which means that it had sunk into their daily life and permeated their enjoyment of it, as our religion certainly does not affect *our* life to enhance it as amusing or pleasurable. We go to Church on Sunday, and write it off as an observance; but if eager to be happy with a free heart, we close early and steal a few hours from the working-day. We antagonise religion and enjoyment, worship and holiday. Nature being too strong for any convention of ours, courtship has asserted itself as permissible on the Sabbath, if not as a Sabbatical institution.

Now the Greeks were just as much slaves to the letter of their Homer as any Auld Licht Elder to the letter of St. Paul. No one will accuse Plato of being overfriendly to poetry. Yet I believe you will find in Plato some 150 direct citations from Homer, not to speak of allusions scattered broadcast through the dialogues, often as texts for long argument. Of these citations and allusions an inordinate number seem to us laboriously trivial—that is to say, unless we put ourselves into the Hellenic mind. On the other hand Plato uses others to enforce or illustrate his profoundest doctrines. For an instance, in "Phaedo" (§ 96) Socrates is arguing that the soul cannot be one with the harmony of the bodily affections, being herself the master-player who commands the strings:

===Chinese===
若是如此，那让我先请你们去看希腊人，看他们对待荷马（Homer）诗篇的态度。我们当然把《旧约》看得比荷马更神圣。可我十分怀疑，它之于我们，是否比《伊利亚特》与《奥德赛》之于当日一位老雅典人更神圣。对希腊人——忘记这一点，便是我们研究悲剧家、甚至研究阿里斯托芬（Aristophanes）时最易滋生的谬误之源——对希腊人来说，他们的宗教，尽管是那样的宗教，却关系重大。他们把剧场盖在这宗教之上，我们断然不这样做；这意味着那宗教已沉入他们的日常生活，浸透了他们对日常生活的享用，而我们的宗教确乎不曾这样影响*我们的*生活，不曾使它作为乐事或快事而增色。我们礼拜天上教堂，把它当作一项例行的遵守销账了事；可若一心想放开怀抱快活一场，我们便早早打烊，从工作日里偷出几个钟头来。我们把宗教与享乐对立起来，把敬拜与假日对立起来。天性比我们任何的习俗都强大，求爱遂自行申张，宣布自己在安息日也可以行之——纵然还算不上一项安息日的建制。

而希腊人之拘守他们荷马的字句，同任何一位旧光派长老（Auld Licht Elder）之拘守圣保罗的字句，一般无二。没有人会指控柏拉图（Plato）对诗歌过分友好。可我相信你们会在柏拉图书里找到约 150 处直引荷马的句子，还不算散布在诸对话篇里俯拾皆是的暗引——它们常用作长篇论证的经文。这些直引与暗引之中，数量出奇之多的一部分在我们看来是费力而不关痛痒的——也就是说，除非我们把自己放进希腊人的心灵里去。另一方面，柏拉图也拿另一些引文来加强或阐明他最深的学说。举个例子：在《斐多篇》（Phaedo）§ 96，苏格拉底（Socrates）正论证灵魂不可能与身体情感的那个和谐是同一个东西，因为她本人正是号令琴弦的主奏者：

===Original===
> —almost always [he says] opposing and coercing them in all sorts of ways throughout life, sometimes more violently with the pains of medicine and gymnastic; then again more gently;—threatening, and also reprimanding the desires, passions, fears, as if talking to a thing which is not herself; as Homer in the Odyssey represents Odysseus doing in the words
> Do you think [asks Socrates] that Homer wrote this under the idea that the soul is a harmony capable of being led by the affections of the body, and not rather of a nature which should lead and master them—herself a far diviner thing than any harmony?

A Greek, then, will use Homer—*his* Bible—minutely on niceties of conduct or broadly on first principles of philosophy or religion. But equally, since it is poetry all the time to him, he will take—or to instance particular writers, Aristotle and the late Greek, Longinus will take—a single hexameter to illustrate a minute trick of style or turn of phrase, as equally he will choose a long passage or the whole *Iliad*, the whole *Odyssey*, to illustrate a grand rule of poetic construction, a first principle of aesthetics. For an example—"Herein," says Aristotle, starting to show that an Epic poem must have Unity of Subject—"Herein, to repeat what we have said before, we have a further proof of Homer's superiority to the rest. He did not attempt to deal even with the Trojan War in its entirety, though it was a whole story with a definite beginning, middle and end—feeling apparently that it was too long a story to be taken in at one view or else over-complicated by variety of incidents." And as Aristotle takes the *Iliad*—*his* Bible—to illustrate a grand rule of poetical construction, so the late writer of his tradition—Longinus—will use it to exhibit the core and essence of poetical sublimity; as in his famous ninth chapter, of which Gibbon wrote:

===Chinese===
> ——[他说]几乎在一生之中，总在以种种方式抗拒它们、强制它们，有时较猛烈，用医药与体操的痛苦；时而又较温和；——威吓着，也申斥着种种欲望、激情、恐惧，仿佛在同一个不是她自身的东西说话；正如荷马在《奥德赛》里描写奥德修斯（Odysseus）所做的事，他的话是——
> 「你们以为，[苏格拉底问道]荷马写下这一段，是心想灵魂是一种和谐，可以被身体的情感牵着走吗？还是心想它的本性倒是应当引导并制伏它们——她自身远比任何和谐更为神圣？」

于是，一个希腊人会精微地把荷马——*他的*圣经——用在行为细目上，也会宽泛地用在哲学或宗教的第一原理上。但同样地，既然那在他始终是诗，他就会——具体说，亚里士多德（Aristotle）与晚期希腊人朗吉努斯（Longinus）就会——取一单个六音步诗行，来说明一种风格的细技或一个措辞的转折；也同样会选取一段长文，或整部《伊利亚特》、整部《奥德赛》，来说明诗艺建构的一条大法则、美学的一条第一原理。举个例子——亚里士多德说，他要开始证明一部史诗必须有主题的统一：「在此，重复我们前面说过的话，我们得到了进一步的证据，证明荷马之高出于其余众人。他甚至不曾试图处理特洛伊战争的全貌，虽然那是一个有确定的开端、中段与结尾的完整故事——显然是他感到这个故事太长，无法一目尽收，要不然就是被事件的纷繁弄得过分复杂。」亚里士多德取《伊利亚特》——*他的*圣经——说明诗艺建构的一条大法则；他那传统的晚期作者朗吉努斯，也照样会用它来展示诗之崇高的核心与本质——例如在他那著名的第九章里；关于这一章，吉本写道：

===Original===
> The ninth chapter … [of the *ΠΕΡΙΨΠΣΟΥΣ* or *De Sublimitate* of Longinus] is one of the finest monuments of antiquity. Till now, I was acquainted only with two ways of criticising a beautiful passage: the one, to show, by an exact anatomy of it, the distinct beauties of it, and whence they sprung; the other, an idle exclamation, or a general encomium, which leaves nothing behind it. Longinus has shown me that there is a third. He tells me his own feelings upon reading it; and tells them with so much energy, that he communicates them. I almost doubt which is more sublime, Homer's Battle of the Gods, or Longinus's Apostrophe to Terentianus upon it.

Well, let me quote you, in translation, a sentence or two from this chapter, which produced upon Gibbon such an effect as almost to anticipate Walter Pater's famous definition, "To feel the virtue of the poet, of the painter, to disengage it, to set it forth—these are the three stages of the critic's duty."

> "Elsewhere," says Longinus, "I have written as follows: *Sublimity is the echo of a great soul.*"

===Chinese===
> 第九章……〔朗吉努斯的《论崇高》（*ΠΕΡΙΨΠΣΟΥΣ*，即 *De Sublimitate*）〕是古代最精美的纪念物之一。直到如今，我所知道的批评一段美文的方式只有两种：一种是给它作一番精确的解剖，指出它各处的美、指出它们从何而来；另一种是空洞的惊叹，或一阵笼统的颂词，过后什么也不留下。朗吉努斯让我看到还有第三种。他向我讲述他自己读这段文字时的感受；而且讲得那样有力量，竟把感受传递给了我。我几乎说不准，荷马的诸神之战与朗吉努斯就此致特伦提安努斯（Terentianus）的那段呼告，究竟哪个更崇高。

好，让我从这一章里用译文引给你们一两句；这一章在吉本身上产生的效果，几乎预告了瓦尔特·佩特（Walter Pater）那个著名的定义：「感受到诗人、画家的那种德性，把它离析出来，把它陈列出来——这是批评家职责的三个阶段。」

> 朗吉努斯说：「在别处，我曾写下这样一句话：*崇高是伟大灵魂的回声。*」
===Original===
"Sublimity is the echo of a great soul."—It was worth repeating too—was it not?

> For it is not possible that men with mean and servile ideas and aims prevailing throughout their lives should produce anything that is admirable and worthy of immortality. Great accents we expect to fall from the lips of those whose thoughts are deep and grave. … Hear how magnificently Homer speaks of the higher powers: "As far as a man seeth with his eyes into the haze of distance as he sitteth upon a cliff of outlook and gazeth over the wine-dark sea, even so far at a bound leap the neighing horses of the Gods."

"He makes" [says Longinus] "the vastness of the world the measure of their leap." Then, after a criticism of the Battle of the Gods (too long to be quoted here) he goes on:

===Chinese===
「崇高是伟大灵魂的回声。」——这话也值得一再重复——不是吗？

> 终生怀着卑贱奴役的观念与目标的人，不可能产出任何令人赞叹、配得不朽的东西。我们期待伟大的声调，从思想深沉庄重者的唇间落下。……请听荷马谈起上界的神明，何等气概：「人坐在眺望的崖头，用眼睛极望远方的迷蒙、凝视那酒暗色的大海，能望见多远，众神嘶鸣的马匹一跃便有多远。」

朗吉努斯说：他「以世界的浩瀚，作为他们一跃的尺度」。随后，在评论过诸神之战（文字太长，此处不引）之后，他接着说：

===Original===
> Much superior to the passages respecting the Battle of the Gods are those which represent the divine nature as it really is—pure and great and undefiled; for example, what is said of Poseidon.

Then how does Longinus conclude? Why, very strangely—very strangely indeed, whether you take the treatise to be by that Longinus, the Rhetorician and Zenobia's adviser, whom the Emperor Aurelian put to death, or prefer to believe it the work of an unknown hand in the first century. The treatise goes on:

> Similarly, the legislator of the Jews [Moses], no ordinary man, having formed and expressed a worthy conception of the might of the Godhead, writes at the very beginning of his Laws, "God said"—What? "Let there be light, and there was light."

===Chinese===
> 远比写诸神之战的那些段落高明的，是另一些段落：它们表现神性的本来面目——纯洁、伟大、无玷；例如关于波塞冬（Poseidon）的那一段话。

那么朗吉努斯怎样收尾呢？唔，非常奇特——实在非常奇特：无论你们把这篇论著当作那位朗吉努斯——修辞学家、芝诺比娅（Zenobia）的谋臣、被奥勒良皇帝（Emperor Aurelian）处死的那一位——所作，还是宁愿相信它出自第一世纪某位无名之士的手笔。论著接着说：

> 同样，犹太人的立法者[摩西（Moses）]，一位非常之人，对神权之能形成了一个配得上的观念并把它表达出来，在他律法的最开头写道：「神说」——说了什么？「要有光，就有了光。」

===Original===
IV

So here, Gentlemen, you have Plato, Aristotle, Longinus—all Greeks of separate states—men of eminence all three, and two of surpassing eminence, all three and each in his time and turn treating Homer reverently as Holy Writ and yet enjoying it liberally as poetry. For indeed the true Greek mind had no thought to separate poetry from religion, as to the true Greek mind reverence and liberty to enjoy, with the liberty of mind that helps to enjoy, were all tributes to the same divine thing. They had no professionals, no puritans, to hedge it off with a taboo: and so when the last and least of the three, Longinus, comes to *our* Holy Writ—the sublime poetry in which Christendom reads its God—his open mind at once recognises it as poetry and as sublime. "God said, Let there be light: and there was light." If Longinus could treat this as sublime poetry, why cannot we, who have translated and made it ours?

===Chinese===
IV

所以，诸位先生，你们这里有柏拉图、亚里士多德、朗吉努斯——都是分属不同城邦的希腊人——三位都是杰出人物，其中两位超群拔萃；三个人、每个人，都在他自己的时代、轮到他的时候，怀着敬意把荷马当作圣言对待，却又作为诗歌纵情享用。因为真正的希腊心灵压根没有想到把诗与宗教分开：在真正的希腊心灵看来，虔敬与享用的自由，连同助人享用的心灵的自由，都是献给同一样神圣之物的贡赋。他们没有把持专业的人，没有清教徒，用一道禁忌把圣物圈起来：于是，当三人中最后也最末的一位，朗吉努斯，来到*我们的*圣言——基督教世界借以诵读其上帝的那崇高诗歌——他敞开的心灵立刻认出它既是诗，又是崇高的。「神说，要有光：就有了光。」朗吉努斯尚且能把这句话当作崇高的诗来对待，我们这些已经把它译过来、使它成为自己之物的人，为什么不能？

===Original===
V

Are we forbidden on the ground that our Bible is directly inspired? Well, inspiration, as Sir William Davenant observed and rather wittily proved, in his Preface to *Gondibert*, "is a dangerous term." It is dangerous mainly because it is a relative term, a term of degrees. You may say definitely of some things that the writer was inspired, as you may certify a certain man to be mad—that is, so thoroughly and convincingly mad that you can order him under restraint. But quite a number of us are (as they say in my part of the world) "not exactly," and one or two of us here and there at moments may have a touch even of inspiration. So of the Bible itself: I suppose that few nowadays would contend it to be all inspired *equally*. "No" you may say, "not all equally: but all of it *directly*, as no other book is."

To that I might answer, "How do you *know* that direct inspiration ceased with the Revelation of St. John the Divine, and closed the book? It may be: but how do you know, and what authority have you to say that Wordsworth's 'Tintern Abbey,' for example, or Browning's great Invocation of Love was not directly inspired? Certainly the men who wrote them were rapt above themselves: and, if not directly, why indirectly, and how?"

===Chinese===
V

我们受禁制，是不是因为我们的圣经是直接受灵感而写的？唔，「灵感」，正如威廉·达文南特爵士（Sir William Davenant）在《贡迪伯特》（*Gondibert*）序言里指出并相当机智地证明过的，「是个危险的词。」它危险，主要因为它是个相对的词，一个有程度可分的词。对有些东西，你可以断然说作者是受了灵感的，正如你可以证明某个人疯了——疯得那样彻底、那样叫人信服，以致你可以下令对他加以约束。可我们中间颇有几个人（照我们那一带的说法）是「不很确切」的；此处彼处也总有一两个人，在某个时刻或许还沾过一点灵感的边。圣经本身也是如此：我想，如今很少有人会主张它是*均等地*全部受感而写的。「不错，」你们可以说，「不是均等：但它全部是*直接*的，没有别的书是这样。」

对此我可以反问：「你们*怎么知道*，直接的灵感止于圣约翰的《启示录》，就此合上了书？或许是如此：可你们怎么知道？你们又凭什么权威说，譬如华兹华斯（Wordsworth）的《丁登寺》（'Tintern Abbey'）、或布朗宁（Browning）那首伟大的爱的祈愿，不是直接受感而写？写下它们的人确然是狂喜忘我、超乎自身之上的：那么，若不是直接的，为什么倒是间接的？又是怎样间接的？」

===Original===
But I pause on the edge of a morass, and spring back to firmer ground. Our Bible, as we have it, is a translation, made by forty-seven men and published in the year 1611. The original—and I am still on firm ground because I am quoting now from *The Cambridge History of English Literature*—"either proceeds from divine inspiration, as some will have it, or, according to others, is the fruit of the religious genius of the Hebrew race. From either point of view the authors are highly gifted individuals" (!)—

> highly gifted individuals, who, notwithstanding their diversities, and the progressiveness observable in their representations of the nature of God, are wonderfully consistent in the main tenor of their writings, and serve, in general, for mutual confirmation and illustration. In some cases, this may be due to the revision of earlier productions by later writers, which has thus brought more primitive conceptions into a degree of conformity with maturer and profounder views; but, even in such cases, the earlier conception often lends itself, without wrenching, to the deeper interpretation and the completer exposition. The Bible is not distinctively an intellectual achievement.

===Chinese===
可我在泥沼的边上停住脚，一跃跳回更坚实的地面。我们手头的圣经是一部译本，由四十七人译成，于 1611 年刊行。至于原作——我仍然站在坚实的地面上，因为我此刻是在引《剑桥英国文学史》（*The Cambridge History of English Literature*）——「它要么如某些人所主张，出自神圣的灵感，要么照另一些人的说法，是希伯来种族之宗教天才的果实。无论从哪个观点看，作者们都是天资极高的个人」（！）——

> 天资极高的个人：尽管彼此各异，尽管在他们关于上帝本性的表述中看得出渐进的痕迹，其著作的主导基调却出奇地一致，大体上可以互相印证、互相阐明。在有些情形，这或可归因于后来作者对较早作品的修订，修订由此把更原始的观念纳入了与更成熟、更深刻的见解某种程度的一致；但即便在这种情形，较早的观念往往也无需强扭，就经得起更深的解释与更完备的阐发。圣经并不是一项特别的心智成就。

===Original===
In all earnest I protest that to write about the Bible in such a fashion is to demonstrate inferentially that it has never quickened you with its glow; that, whatever your learning, you have missed what the unlearned Bunyan, for example, so admirably caught—the true *wit* of the book. The writer, to be sure, is dealing with the originals. Let us more humbly sit at the feet of the translators. "Highly gifted individuals," or no, the sort of thing the translators wrote was, "And God said, Let there be light," "A sower went forth to sow," "The Kingdom of Heaven is like unto leaven, which a woman took," "The wages of sin is death," "The trumpet shall sound," "Jesus wept," "Death is swallowed up in victory."

Let me quote you for better encouragement, as well as for relief, a passage from Matthew Arnold on the Authorised Version:

> The effect of Hebrew poetry can be preserved and transferred in a foreign language as the effect of other great poetry cannot. The effect of Homer, the effect of Dante, is and must be in great measure lost in a translation, because their poetry is a poetry of metre, or of rhyme, or both; and the effect of these is not really transferable. A man may make a good English poem with the matter and thoughts of Homer and Dante, may even try to reproduce their metre, or rhyme: but the metre and rhyme will be in truth his own, and the effect will be his, not the effect of Homer or Dante. Isaiah's, on the other hand, is a poetry, as is well known, of parallelism; it depends not on metre and rhyme, but on a balance of thought, conveyed by a corresponding balance of sentence; and the effect of this can be transferred to another language. … Hebrew poetry has in addition the effect of assonance and other effects which cannot perhaps be transferred; but its main effect, its effect of parallelism of thought and sentence, can.

I take this from the preface to his little volume in which Arnold confesses that his "paramount object is to get Isaiah enjoyed."

===Chinese===
我要郑重声明：用这样一种笔调来写圣经，等于用推论证明它从未用它那点热力把你点活；等于说，无论你学问多大，你错过了那不学无术的班扬——举例说——如此绝妙地把握住的东西：这本书真正的*机锋*（wit）。诚然，那位作者处理的是原文。让我们更谦卑地坐到译者们的脚前。「天资极高的个人」也好，不是也好，译者们笔下写出的那类句子是：「神说，要有光」，「有一个撒种的出去撒种」，「天国好像面酵，有妇人拿来」，「罪的工价乃是死」，「号筒要吹响」，「耶稣哭了」，「死被得胜吞灭」。

为了给大家添些鼓舞，也为了换换空气，容我从马修·阿诺德（Matthew Arnold）论钦定版圣经的文字里引一段：

> 希伯来诗歌的效果，可以在外国语里保存并传递过去，而其他伟大诗歌的效果不能。荷马的效果、但丁（Dante）的效果，在翻译中已经并且必定大半失掉，因为他们的诗是格律的诗，或押韵的诗，或两者兼是；而这些东西的效果实在无法传递。一个人可以用荷马与但丁的材料和思想写出一首好的英文诗，甚至可以试着再现他们的格律或韵脚：但那格律与韵脚事实上是他自己的，效果也是他的，而不是荷马或但丁的效果。另一方面，大家都知道，以赛亚的诗是对偶平行体的诗；它不靠格律与韵脚，而靠思想的平衡，由相应的句式平衡传达出来；这种效果可以传递到另一种语言里去。……希伯来诗歌此外还有谐音的效果与其他也许无法传递的效果；但它主要的效果，即思想与句式平行对偶的效果，是可以传递的。

这一段我取自阿诺德那本小书的序言；他在序言里自陈，他的「头等目标是让人享受《以赛亚书》」。

===Original===
VI

Sundry men of letters besides Matthew Arnold have pleaded for a literary study of the Bible, and specially of our English Version, that we may thereby enhance our enjoyment of the work itself and, through this, enjoyment and understanding of the rest of English Literature, from 1611 down. Specially among these pleaders let me mention Mr. F. B. Money-Coutts (now Lord Latymer) and a Cambridge man, Dr. R. G. Moulton, now Professor of Literary Theory and Interpretation in the University of Chicago. Of both these writers I shall have something to say. But first and generally, if you ask me why all their pleas have not yet prevailed, I will give you my own answer—the fault as usual lies in ourselves—in our own tameness and incuriosity.

There is no real trouble with the taboo set up by professionals and puritans, if we have the courage to walk past it as Christian walked between the lions; no real tyranny we could not overthrow, if it were worth while, with a push; no need at all for us to "wreathe our sword in myrtle boughs." What tyranny exists has grown up through the quite well-meaning labours of quite well-meaning men: and, as I started this lecture by saying, I have never heard any serious reason given why we should not include portions of the English Bible in our English Tripos, if we choose.

===Chinese===
VI

马修·阿诺德之外，还有好些文人学士为圣经的文学研究呼吁过，尤其是为我们的英文译本呼吁过，盼我们由此增进对这部书本身的享受，并借这享受，增进对 1611 年以来英国文学其余部分的享受与理解。在这些呼吁者之中，我要特别举出弗·比·曼尼-库茨先生（F. B. Money-Coutts，即今日的拉蒂默勋爵〔Lord Latymer〕），以及一位剑桥出身的莫尔顿博士（Dr. R. G. Moulton）——现任芝加哥大学文学理论与阐释教授。这两位作者，我都要说上几句。但先说一句总的：如果你们问我，为什么这一切呼吁至今未能奏效，我给你们我自己的回答——过错照例在我们自己身上——在我们自己的驯顺与不好奇。

专业人士与清教徒立起的那道禁忌，其实并不真有多了不起，只要我们有胆量从它旁边走过去，一如《天路历程》里的基督徒（Christian）走过狮群中间；也没有什么真正的暴政是我们不能一推而倒的——只要值得一推；我们更用不着「用桃金娘的枝条缠裹我们的剑」。现有的暴政，是靠相当善良的人相当善良的操劳长起来的：而且，正如我这讲开头所说，我从来没听人给出过任何像样的理由，说明我们何以不该——倘若我们愿意挑选——把英文圣经的若干篇章纳入我们的剑桥英文荣誉学位考试。
===Original===
> Nos te,
 Nos facimus, Scriptura, deam.

Then why don't we choose?

To answer this, we must (I suggest) seek somewhat further back. The Bible—that is to say the body of the old Hebrew Literature clothed for us in English—comes to us in our childhood. But how does it come?

===Chinese===
> *Nos te,*
> *Nos facimus, Scriptura, deam.*（拉丁文：是我们，是我们把你造作了女神，圣经啊。）

那么，我们为什么不挑选呢？

要回答这一点，我们（依我看）必须往更远处追寻。圣经——也就是说，那部古老的希伯来文学，为我们披上了英文的衣裳——是在我们的童年时代来到我们这里的。可它是怎样来的呢？

===Original===
Let me, amplifying a hint from Dr. Moulton, ask you to imagine a volume including the great books of our own literature all bound together in some such order as this: *Paradise Lost*, Darwin's *Descent of Man*, *The Anglo-Saxon Chronicle*, Walter Map, Mill *On Liberty*, Hooker's *Ecclesiastical Polity*, *The Annual Register*, Froissart, Adam Smith's *Wealth of Nations*, *Domesday Book*, *Le Morte d'Arthur*, Campbell's *Lives of the Lord Chancellors*, Boswell's *Johnson*, Barbour's *The Bruce*, Hakluyt's *Voyages*, Clarendon, Macaulay, the plays of Shakespeare, Shelley's *Prometheus Unbound*, *The Faerie Queene*, Palgrave's Golden Treasury, Bacon's Essays, Swinburne's *Poems and Ballads*, FitzGerald's *Omar Khayyàm*, Wordsworth, Browning, *Sartor Resartus*, Burton's *Anatomy of Melancholy*, Burke's *Letters on a Regicide Peace*, *Ossian*, *Piers Plowman*, Burke's *Thoughts on the Present Discontents*, Quarles, Newman's *Apologia*, Donne's Sermons, Ruskin, Blake, *The Deserted Village*, Manfred, Blair's *Grave*, *The Complaint of Deor*, Bailey's *Festus*, Thompson's *Hound of Heaven*.

Will you next imagine that in this volume most of the author's names are lost; that, of the few that survive, a number have found their way into wrong places; that Ruskin for example is credited with *Sartor Resartus*, that "Laus Veneris" and "Dolores" are ascribed to Queen Elizabeth, *The Anatomy of Melancholy* to Charles II; and that, as for the titles, these were never invented by the authors, but by a Committee?

Will you still go on to imagine that all the poetry is printed as prose; while all the long paragraphs of prose are broken up into short verses, so that they resemble the little passages set out for parsing or analysis in an examination paper?

===Chinese===
容我引申莫尔顿博士的一点提示，请你们设想有一部合集，把我们自己文学的各大名著悉数装订在一起，次序大致如下：《失乐园》（*Paradise Lost*）、达尔文（Darwin）的《人类的由来》（*Descent of Man*）、《盎格鲁—撒克逊编年史》（*The Anglo-Saxon Chronicle*）、沃尔特·马普（Walter Map）、密尔（Mill）的《论自由》（*On Liberty*）、胡克（Hooker）的《教会政制》（*Ecclesiastical Polity*）、《年度纪事》（*The Annual Register*）、弗鲁瓦萨尔（Froissart）、亚当·斯密（Adam Smith）的《国富论》（*Wealth of Nations*）、《末日审判书》（*Domesday Book*）、《亚瑟王之死》（*Le Morte d'Arthur*）、坎贝尔（Campbell）的《大法官列传》（*Lives of the Lord Chancellors*）、鲍斯韦尔（Boswell）的《约翰逊传》（*Johnson*）、巴布尔（Barbour）的《布鲁斯》（*The Bruce*）、哈克卢特（Hakluyt）的《航海记》（*Voyages*）、克拉伦登（Clarendon）、麦考莱（Macaulay）、莎士比亚的诸剧作、雪莱（Shelley）的《解放了的普罗米修斯》（*Prometheus Unbound*）、《仙后》（*The Faerie Queene*）、帕尔格雷夫（Palgrave）的《英诗金库》（Golden Treasury）、培根（Bacon）的《随笔集》（Essays）、斯温伯恩（Swinburne）的《诗歌与谣曲》（*Poems and Ballads*）、菲茨杰拉德（FitzGerald）的《鲁拜集》（*Omar Khayyàm*）、华兹华斯（Wordsworth）、布朗宁（Browning）、《旧衣新裁》（*Sartor Resartus*）、伯顿（Burton）的《忧郁的解剖》（*Anatomy of Melancholy*）、伯克（Burke）的《论弑君和平书简》（*Letters on a Regicide Peace*）、《莪相》（*Ossian*）、《农夫皮尔斯》（*Piers Plowman*）、伯克的《论当前不满的缘由》（*Thoughts on the Present Discontents*）、夸尔斯（Quarles）、纽曼（Newman）的《自辩》（*Apologia*）、多恩（Donne）的《布道文》（Sermons）、罗斯金（Ruskin）、布莱克（Blake）、《荒村》（*The Deserted Village*）、《曼弗雷德》（*Manfred*）、布莱尔（Blair）的《坟墓》（*Grave*）、《德奥尔的怨歌》（*The Complaint of Deor*）、贝利（Bailey）的《菲斯特斯》（*Festus*）、汤普森（Thompson）的《天堂猎犬》（*Hound of Heaven*）。

接下来请再设想：在这部合集里，大多数作者的名字已经失落；幸存的少数名字里，又有一批跑错了位置——例如《旧衣新裁》记在了罗斯金名下，「维纳斯的礼赞」（Laus Veneris）与「多洛雷斯」（Dolores）被归于伊丽莎白女王（Queen Elizabeth）名下，《忧郁的解剖》则归到查理二世（Charles II）头上；至于那些书名，那就从来不是作者自己起的，而是一家委员会替他们起出来的。

还请你们继续设想：全部诗歌都排成了散文的版式；而散文的长段落反倒被切成了短短的小节，看上去像考卷里为作语法解析或章句分析而摘出的小段。

===Original===
This device, as you know, was first invented by the exiled translators who published the Geneva Bible (as it is called) in 1557; and for pulpit use, for handiness of reference, for "waling a portion," it has its obvious advantages: but it is, after all and at the best, a very primitive device: and, for my part, I consider it the deadliest invention of all for robbing the book of outward resemblance to literature and converting it to the aspect of a gazetteer—a *biblion a-biblion*, as Charles Lamb puts it.

Have we done? By no means. Having effected all this, let us pepper the result over with italics and numerals, print it in double columns, with a marginal gutter on either side, each gutter pouring down an inky flow of references and cross references. Then, and not till then, is the outward disguise complete—so far as you are concerned. It remains only then to appoint it to be read in Churches, and oblige the child to get selected portions of it by heart on Sundays. But you are yet to imagine that the authors themselves have taken a hand in the game: that the later ones suppose all the earlier ones to have been predicting all the time in a nebulous fashion what they themselves have to tell, and indeed to have written mainly with that object: so that Macaulay and Adam Smith, for example, constantly interrupt the thread of their discourse to affirm that what they tell us must be right because Walter Map or the author of *Piers Plowman* foretold it ages before.

===Chinese===
你们知道，这个办法是 1557 年刊行所谓《日内瓦圣经》（Geneva Bible）的那批流亡译者头一个想出来的；用作讲坛之本、便于查考、便于「挑出一段经文」，它自有明显的便利：但说到底，它顶多是一种非常原始的办法；而在我个人看来，一切使这本书在外观上失去文学模样、把它变成一部地名词典模样的发明之中，数它最为致命——用查尔斯·兰姆（Charles Lamb）的话说，是 *biblion a-biblion*（希腊文：书而非书）。

我们做完了吗？远远没有。办妥了这一切，再让我们往成品上遍撒斜体字与数字，把它印成双栏，两侧各留一条边栏的沟槽，每条沟槽里都倾泻着墨黑的参引与互参。到这时——而且要到这时——外在的伪装才算齐备——至少就你们而论。剩下的只消规定它在各教堂里诵读，再责令孩子在礼拜天背诵选定的段落。可你们还要再设想一步：作者们自己也下场参与了这场赌局：晚出的那些作者认定，早出的那些一直在云山雾罩地预言着他们自己要讲的东西，而且写下那些文字主要就是为了这个目的：于是比如说麦考莱与亚当·斯密，会不断打断自己话头的线索，声明他们告诉我们的必定不错，因为沃尔特·马普或《农夫皮尔斯》的作者在多少世代之前早已预言在先。

===Original===
Now a grown man—that is to say, a comparatively unimpressionable man—that is again to say, a man past the age when to enjoy the Bible is priceless—has probably found out somehow that the word prophet does not (in spite of vulgar usage) mean "a man who predicts." He has experienced too many prophets of that kind—especially since 1914—and he respects Isaiah too much to rank Isaiah among them. He has been in love, belike; he has read the Song of Solomon: he very much doubts if, on the evidence, Solomon was the kind of lover to have written that Song, and he is quite certain that when the lover sings to his beloved:

> Thy two breasts are like two young roes that are twins. Thy neck is as a tower of ivory; thine eyes like the fishpools in Heshbon, by the gate of Bath-rabbim.

—he knows, I say, that this is not a description of the Church and her graces, as the chapter-heading audaciously asserts. But he is lazy; too lazy even to commend the Revised Version for striking Solomon out of the Bible, calling the poem The Song of Songs, omitting the absurd chapter-headings, and printing the poetry as poetry ought to be printed. The old-fashioned arrangement was good enough for him. Or he goes to church on Christmas Day and listens to a first lesson, of which the old translators made nonsense, and, in two passages at least, stark nonsense. But, again, the old nonsense is good enough for him; soothing in fact. He is not even quite sure that the Bible, looking like any other book, ought to be put in the hands of the young.

===Chinese===
再说一个成年男子——也就是说，一个比较不易受感动的人——再换句话说，一个已经过了「享受圣经乃无价之宝」那个年纪的人——多半已不知从哪里打听到：「先知」（prophet）这个词（尽管流俗用法如此）并不等于「预告将来的人」。那一类「预言家」他领教得太多了——1914 年以来尤其如此——而他太敬重以赛亚，不肯把以赛亚排进那类人里面去。他多半恋爱过；他读过《雅歌》：凭文本的证据，他大惑不解所罗门（Solomon）究竟是不是写得出那首歌的那种情人；而且他十分确定，当那情人向他的爱人歌唱——

> 你的两乳好像一对双生的小鹿；你的颈项如象牙台；你的眼如希实本（Heshbon）的水池，在巴特·拉宾（Bath-rabbim）门旁。

——我说，他知道这并不是对教会及其诸般恩典的描写，虽然章前标题大言不惭地如此断言。可他懒；懒到连称赞一句修订版圣经（RV）都不肯——那版本把所罗门的名字从圣经里划去，把这首诗题为《雅歌》（The Song of Songs），删去了荒唐的章前标题，把诗按诗应当有的样子排印。老式的编排对他来说尽够好了。或者，他在圣诞节上教堂，听一篇「第一读经」（first lesson），那篇经文被老译者们弄得文理不通，其中至少有两处十足不通。但老一套的不通对他也尽够好了；说实话还挺安神。他甚至不太确定：一本看起来跟别的书没两样的圣经，究竟该不该放到年轻人手里。

===Original===
In all this I think he is wrong. I am sure he is wrong if our contention be right, that the English Bible should be studied by us all for its poetry and its wonderful language as well as for its religion—the religion and the poetry being in fact inseparable. For then, in Euripides' phrase, we should clothe the Bible in a dress through which its beauty might best shine.

VII

If you ask me How? I answer—first begging you to bear in mind that we are planning the form of the book for our purpose, and that other forms will be used for other purposes—that we should start with the simplest alterations, such as these:

1. The books should be rearranged in their right order, so far as this can be ascertained (and much of it has been ascertained). I am told, and I can well believe, that this would at a stroke clear away a mass of confusion in strictly Biblical criticism. But that is not my business. I know that it would immensely help our *literary* study.
2. I should print the prose continuously, as prose is ordinarily and properly printed: and the poetry in verse lines, as poetry is ordinarily and properly printed. And I should print each on a page of one column, with none but the necessary notes and references, and these so arranged that they did not tease and distract the eye.
3. This arrangement should be kept, whether for the Tripos we prescribe a book in the Authorised text or in the Revised. As a rule, perhaps—or as a rule for some years to come—we shall probably rely on the Authorised Version: but for some books (and I instance Job) we should undoubtedly prefer the Revised.
4. With the verse we should, I hold, go farther even than the Revisers. As you know, much of the poetry in the Bible, especially of such as was meant for music, is composed in stanzaic form, or in strophe and antistrophe, with prelude and conclusion, sometimes with a choral refrain. We should print these, I contend, in their proper form, just as we should print an English poem in its proper form.

I shall conclude today with a striking instance of this, with four strophes from the 107th Psalm, taking leave to use at will the Authorised, the Revised and the Coverdale Versions. Each strophe, you will note, has a double refrain. As Dr. Moulton points out, the one puts up a cry for help, the other an ejaculation of praise after the help has come. Each refrain has a sequel verse, which appropriately changes the motive and sets that of the next stanza:

===Chinese===
凡此种种，我认为他错了。倘若我们的主张不错——英文圣经应当被我们大家研读，既为它的宗教，也为它的诗与它奇妙的语言——而宗教与诗实际上密不可分——那么我确信他错了。因为到那时，用欧里庇得斯（Euripides）的话说，我们就该给圣经穿上一件衣裳，让它的美透过这衣裳照得最亮。

VII

如果你们问：怎样做？我回答——先请你们记住：我们是在为我们自己的目的规划这部书的形制，别的目的自会用别的形制——我们应当从最简单的改动做起，比如下面这些：

1. 各卷书应当按它们正确的次序重排，凡考得出的都照次序排（其中大部分已经考定）。有人告诉我——我也可以十分相信——这一下子就能廓清严格圣经考证里的一大团混乱。但那不是我的分内事。我知道的是：它会极大地帮助我们的*文学*研究。
2. 散文应当连排，像散文通常而且应当排印的那样：诗则分行排，像诗通常而且应当排印的那样。而且两类都应单栏排在一页上，只留必要的注与参引，并且安排得不让它们烦扰眼睛、分人神。
3. 无论我们为剑桥英文荣誉学位考试指定的是钦定版经文的某一卷，还是修订版的，这一排版形制都应保持。大体说来——或者说作为今后若干年内的大体规则——我们多半会依重钦定版圣经：但对某些书卷（我举《约伯记》为例），我们无疑宁可取修订版。
4. 在诗体上，我以为我们甚至应当走得比修订家们更远。你们知道，圣经里的诗，尤其原本配乐的那些，大多按诗节的形式写成，或按正旋（strophe）与反旋（antistrophe）写成，有序曲与结语，有时还有一支合唱的叠句。我主张，这些应当按它们本来的形式排印，正如我们排印一首英文诗要按它本来的形式。

今天收尾，我要用一个醒目的实例来说明这一点：取《诗篇》第 107 篇的四段正旋歌，并自作主张，随意取用钦定版、修订版与科弗代尔译本。你们会注意到，每一段正旋歌都有一个双叠句。诚如莫尔顿博士指出的：一个叠句发出呼救之声，另一个则在救助来到之后发出赞美的呼喊。每个叠句都带一节后续的诗行，恰当地转换动机，并为下一诗节定下基调：

## Lecture X On Reading the Bible (III) / 第十讲 论圣经的阅读（下）

===Original===
Monday, May 6, 1918

I

===Chinese===
1918 年 5 月 6 日，星期一

I

===Original===
My task today, Gentlemen, is mainly practical: to choose a particular book of Scripture and show (if I can) not only that it deserves to be enjoyed, in its English rendering, as a literary masterpiece, because it abides in that dress, an indisputable classic for us, as surely as if it had first been composed in English; but that it can, for purposes of study, serve the purpose of any true literary school of English as readily, and as usefully, as the Prologue to *The Canterbury Tales* or *Hamlet* or *Paradise Lost*. I shall choose The Book of Job for several reasons, presently to be given; but beg you to understand that, while taking it for a striking illustration, I use it but to illustrate; that what may be done with Job may, in degree, be done with Ruth, with Esther, with the Psalms, The Song of Songs, Ecclesiastes; with Isaiah of Jerusalem, Ezekiel, sundry of the prophets; even with St. Luke’s Gospel or St. Paul’s letters to the Churches.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，我今天的工作主要是实际的：选定圣经中的某一卷书，来表明（倘若我能的话）它不仅配得上以其英文的形态当作文学杰作来欣赏——因为它既久居于那一衣装之中，对我们便是一位无可争议的经典，就像它当初就是用英文写成的一样确凿；而且表明，就研习而言，它能够像《坎特伯雷故事》（The Canterbury Tales）总引、《哈姆雷特》（Hamlet）或《失乐园》（Paradise Lost）一样便捷、一样有用地为任何一所真正的英文学院（School of English）效力。我将选《约伯记》，理由有数端，随即奉告；但请诸位明白：我拿它作一个醒目的例证，却只是拿它作例证；凡能施之于《约伯记》的，在相当的程度上也能施之于《路得记》、《以斯帖记》、《诗篇》、《雅歌》、《传道书》；施之于耶路撒冷的以赛亚、《以西结书》与先知中的若干卷；甚至施之于《路加福音》，或保罗致各教会的书信。

===Original===
My first reason, then, for choosing Job has already been given. It is the most striking illustration to be found. Many of the Psalms touch perfection as lyrical strains: of the ecstasy of passion in love I suppose The Song of Songs to express the very last word. There are chapters of Isaiah that snatch the very soul and ravish it aloft. In no literature known to me are short stories told with such sweet austerity of art as in the Gospel parables—I can even imagine a high and learned artist in words, after rejecting them as divine on many grounds, surrendering in the end to their divine artistry. But for high seriousness combined with architectonic treatment on a great scale; for sublimity of conception, working malleably within a structure which is simple, severe, complete, having a beginning, a middle and an end; for diction never less than adequate, constantly right and therefore not seldom superb, as theme, thought and utterance soar up together and make one miracle, I can name no single book of the Bible to compare with Job.

===Chinese===
那么，我选《约伯记》的第一个理由已经给出了：它是所能找到的最醒目的例证。《诗篇》中有许多首作为抒情曲调几近完美；论爱情中激情的狂喜，我想《雅歌》说尽了最后的言语。《以赛亚书》中有若干章能把灵魂一把攫住，携之高飞。就我所知，没有任何文学讲述短篇故事时，能像福音书的比喻那样，带着如此甜美而克制的艺术——我甚至可以设想一位高超而有学问的驭字大师，虽已从许多方面否认它们出自神意，终究拜服于它们那神圣的艺术手腕。但论到高贵的严肃与大格局的建筑式经营相结合；论到意念的崇高，在一个单纯、严整、完整——有开端、有中段、有结尾——的结构之内婉转地运化；论到措辞从不失于不足、恒常精确、因而时常臻于壮美，当主题、思想与言语一同翱翔而上、合成一个奇迹——我能指名与之比肩的圣经书卷，《约伯记》而外别无他卷。

===Original===
My second reason is that the poem, being brief, compendious and quite simple in structure, can be handily expounded; Job is what Milton precisely called it, “a brief model.” And my third reason (which I must not hide) is that two writers whom I mentioned in my last lecture Lord Latymer and Professor R. G. Moulton—have already done this for me. A man who drives at practice must use the tools other men have made, so he use them with due acknowledgment; and this acknowledgment I pay by referring you to Book II of Lord Latymer’s *The Poet’s Charter*, and to the analysis of Job with which Professor Moulton introduces his *Literary Study of the Bible*.

===Chinese===
我的第二个理由是：这首诗篇幅简短、内容扼要、结构极为单纯，可以便当地加以阐说；《约伯记》正是弥尔顿（Milton）所称的「一个简短的范型」（a brief model）。我的第三个理由（这可不能隐瞒）是：我在上一讲中提到过的两位作者——拉蒂默勋爵（Lord Latymer）与莫尔顿（R. G. Moulton）教授——已经先替我做了这件事。凡以实践为目标的人，必须使用别人造好的工具，只要他用得郑重、说明来历；这份郑重的说明，我就以请你们参阅拉蒂默勋爵《诗人的宪章》（The Poet’s Charter）第二编，以及莫尔顿教授在《圣经的文学研究》（Literary Study of the Bible）开篇对《约伯记》所作的分析来偿付。

===Original===
II

But I have a fourth reason, out of which I might make an apparent fifth by presenting it to you in two different ways. Those elders of you who have followed certain earlier lectures “On the Art of Writing” may remember that they set very little store upon metre as a dividing line between poetry and prose, and no store at all upon rhyme. I am tempted today to go farther, and to maintain that, the larger, the sublimer, your subject is, the more impertinent rhyme becomes to it: and that this impertinence increases in a sort of geometrical progression as you advance from monosyllabic to dissyllabic and on to trisyllabic rhyme. Let me put this by a series of examples.

===Chinese===
II

但我还有第四个理由；若换一种方式向你们呈报，我还可以从中做出一个表面上的第五个理由来。你们当中年资较深的几位，如果一直追随我先前题为「论写作的艺术」（On the Art of Writing）的若干讲演，也许还记得，那些讲演很少把音律（metre）当作诗与散文的分界线看待，对韵脚更是全不理会。今天我忍不住要走得更远，主张说：你们的题目越大、越崇高，韵脚就越发与它不相干；并且这不相干的程度，随着你从单音节韵进到双音节韵、再到三音节韵，按一种几何级数递增。让我用一连串例子来说明。

===Original===
We start with no rhyme at all:

> Hail, holy Light, offspring of Heaven first born!
> Or of the Eternal coeternal beam
> May I express thee unblamed? since God is light,
> And never but in unapproached light
> Dwelt from eternity.

===Chinese===
我们从完全没有韵的开始：

> 你好，神圣的光，上天首生的后裔！
> 或与永恒同其永恒的光焰，
> 我能否无愧地将你言说？既然上帝是光，
> 且从来不曾离开那不可逼近的光，
> 自永恒居住。

===Original===
We feel of this, as we feel of a great passage in *Hamlet* or *Lear*, that here is verse at once capable of the highest sublimity and capable of sustaining its theme, of lifting and lowering it at will, with endless resource in the slide and pause of the caesura, to carry it on and on. We feel it to be adequate, too, for quite plain straightforward narrative, as in this passage from *Balder Dead*:

> But from the hill of Lidskialf Odin rose,
> The throne, from which his eye surveys the world;
> And mounted Sleipner, and in darkness rode
> To Asgard. And the stars came out in heaven,
> High over Asgard, to light home the King.
> But fiercely Odin gallop’d, moved in heart;
> And swift to Asgard, to the gate, he came.
> And terribly the hoofs of Sleipner rang
> Along the flinty floor of Asgard streets,
> And the Gods trembled on their golden beds—
> Hearing the wrathful Father coming home—
> For dread, for like a whirlwind, Odin came.
> And to Valhalla’s gate he rode, and left
> Sleipner; and Sleipner went to his own stall:
> And in Valhalla Odin laid him down.

===Chinese===
我们读这一段，正如读《李尔王》（Lear）里某一伟大段落时的感受：这里的诗句既能达到最崇高的境域，又能撑住它的主题，随意把它举起、放下，在诗行休止的滑转与停顿中有无穷的手段，把这主题一程又一程地传送下去。我们也觉得它胜任于十分平直的叙事，比如《巴尔德之死》（Balder Dead）里的这一段：

> 奥丁（Odin）从利斯基亚夫（Lidskialf）的山丘起身，
> 离开那从上头他可纵观世界的宝座；
> 跨上斯莱普尼尔（Sleipner），在黑暗中驰往
> 阿斯加德（Asgard）。星辰在天上显现，
> 高悬在阿斯加德之上，为君王照路还家。
> 但奥丁怒马狂驰，心绪激荡；
> 迅疾来到阿斯加德，来到城门。
> 斯莱普尼尔的蹄声轰然可怖，
> 沿着阿斯加德街道的燧石地面滚过，
> 众神在他们的金榻上战栗——
> 听见怒气冲冲的父神还家——
> 因为可畏——奥丁来时如一阵旋风。
> 他驰到瓦尔哈拉（Valhalla）门前，留下
> 斯莱普尼尔；斯莱普尼尔回到自己的厩中：
> 奥丁在瓦尔哈拉里躺下。

===Original===
Now of rhyme he were a fool who, with Lycidas, or Gray’s “Elegy,” or certain choruses of *Prometheus Unbound*, or page after page of Victor Hugo in his mind, should assert it to be in itself inimical, or a hindrance, or even less than a help, to sublimity; or who, with Dante in his mind, should assert it to be, in itself, any bar to continuous and sustained sublimity. But languages differ vastly in their wealth of rhyme, and differ out of any proportion to their wealth in words: English for instance being infinitely richer than Italian in vocabulary, yet almost ridiculously poorer in dissyllabic, or feminine rhymes. Speaking generally, I should say that in proportion to its wonderful vocabulary, English is poor even in single rhymes; that the words “love,” “truth,” “God,” for example, have lists of possible congeners so limited that the mind, hearing the word “love,” runs forward to match it with “dove” or “above” or even with “move”: and this gives it a sense of arrest, of listening, of check, of waiting, which alike impedes the flow of Pope in imitating Homer, and of Spenser in essaying a sublime and continuous story of his own. It does well enough to carry Chaucer over any gap with a “forsooth as I you say” or “forsooth as I you tell”: but it does so at a total cost of the sublime. And this (I think) was really at the back of Milton’s mind when in the preface to *Paradise Lost* he championed blank verse against “the jingling sound of like endings.”

===Chinese===
至于韵——谁若心里记着《利西达斯》（Lycidas）、格雷（Gray）的《墓园挽歌》、《解放了的普罗米修斯》里的某些合唱曲、或维克多·雨果（Victor Hugo）一页又一页的诗，却断言韵本身就与崇高为敌、就是障碍、甚至连助益都算不上，谁便是愚人；谁若心里记着但丁（Dante），却断言韵本身对连绵持续的崇高竟是一重拦阻，也同样是愚人。但各语言在韵的财富上相差悬殊，其悬殊程度与它们词汇的多寡全不相称：比如英语的词汇远比意大利语丰富，双音节韵——即阴韵——却贫乏得近乎可笑。大略说来，我要讲：与其奇妙的词汇相比，英语连单音节韵也是贫乏的；比如「爱」、「真理」、「上帝」这些词，可与之押韵的候选词名单短得可怜，以致心灵一听「爱」这个字，就抢着往前去找「鸽」（dove）、「在上」（above）、甚至「移动」（move）来相配：这给它一种停驻之感、侧耳之感、受阻之感、等候之感，既妨碍蒲柏（Pope）摹仿荷马（Homer）时的流畅，也妨碍斯宾塞（Spenser）试写一篇自己的崇高而绵延的故事。用「真的，正像我告诉你们的」这类话头载着乔叟（Chaucer）跨过任何一段空隙，倒也够用：但代价是把崇高整个儿搭了进去。我想，这正是弥尔顿心里真正盘算的事：他在《失乐园》序言里为素体诗辩护，反对「同音结尾的叮当之声」（the jingling sound of like endings）。

===Original===
But when we pass from single rhymes to double, of which Dante had an inexhaustible store, we find the English poet almost a pauper; so nearly a pauper that he has to achieve each new rhyme by a trick—which tricking is fatal to rapture, alike in the poet and the hearer. Let me instance a poem which, planned for sublimity, keeps tumbling flat upon earth through the inherent fault of the machine—I mean Myers’s “St. Paul”—a poem which, finely conceived, pondered, worked and reworked upon in edition after edition, was from the first condemned (to my mind) by the technical bar of dissyllabic rhyme which the poet unhappily chose. I take one of its most deeply felt passages—that of St. Paul protesting against his conversion being taken for instantaneous, wholly accounted for by the miraculous vision related in the Acts of the Apostles:

===Chinese===
可是从单音节韵进到双音节韵——但丁的双音节韵取之不尽——我们就发现英国诗人几乎是个穷汉；穷到每要得一新韵，都须耍一个花招，而这耍弄对出神入迷是致命的，无论在诗人还是听者身上都是如此。让我举一首诗为例：它本为崇高而设，却因机器本身的固有毛病，一路扑倒在平地上——我指的是迈尔斯（Myers）的《圣保罗》（St. Paul）——那首诗立意精美，反复推敲，一版再版地加工，可（依我之见）从第一天起就被诗人不幸选中的双音节韵这道技术栅栏判了刑。我取其中感受最深切的一段——圣保罗抗议人们把他的悔改当作顷刻间完成、全由《使徒行传》所载那次异象神迹解释净尽的一段：

===Original===
> Let no man think that sudden in a minute
> All is accomplished and the work is done;—
> Though with thine earliest dawn thou shouldst begin it
> Scarce were it ended in thy setting sun.
> Oh the regret, the struggle and the failing!
> Oh the days desolate and useless years!
> Vows in the night, so fierce and unavailing!
> Stings of my shame and passion of my tears!
> How have I seen in Araby Orion,
> Seen without seeing, till he set again,
> Known the night-noise and thunder of the lion,
> Silence and sounds of the prodigious plain!
> How have I knelt with arms of my aspiring
> Lifted all night in irresponsive air,
> Dazed and amazed with overmuch desiring,
> Blank with the utter agony of prayer!
> “What,” ye will say, “and thou who at Damascus
> Sawest the splendour, answeredst the Voice;
> So hast thou suffered and canst dare to ask us,
> Paul of the Romans, bidding us rejoice?”

===Chinese===
> 莫让任何人以为，顷刻之间
> 一切功成，事工便算完毕；——
> 纵使你在破晓时分便已着手，
> 日头西沉时也难得完工。
> 哦那悔恨，那挣扎与跌倒！
> 哦那些荒芜无用的年日！
> 夜半的誓愿，何等激烈而徒劳！
> 我羞惭的刺痛，我泪水的激情！
> 我曾怎样在阿拉伯旷野仰望猎户座，
> 视而不见，直到它再度西沉，
> 听惯了夜半狮吼的喧腾，
> 广漠平原上那沉寂与声响！
> 我曾怎样以渴慕的双臂
> 整夜伸向不应答的空中，
> 因渴望太多而眩惑迷惘，
> 在祷告的至痛里一片空白！
> 「怎么，」你们要说，「而你——你在
> 大马士革见过那辉煌、应答过那声音的——
> 竟受过这些苦难，还敢开口要求我们，
> 罗马的保罗，吩咐我们欢喜？」

===Original===
You cannot say I have instanced a passage anything short of fine. But do you not feel that a man who is searching for a rhyme to Damascus has not really the time to cry “Abba, father”? Is not your own rapture interrupted by some wonder “How will he bring it off”? And when he has searched and contrived to “ask us,” are we responsive to the ecstasy? Has he not—if I may employ an Oriental trope for once—let in the chill breath of cleverness upon the garden of beatitude? No man can be clever and ecstatic at the same moment.12

===Chinese===
你们不能说，我举的例证有哪一处算不上精美。可是你们不觉得么？一个人正忙着给「大马士革」（Damascus）找韵的时候，实在抽不出工夫喊「阿爸，父啊」。你自己的出神入迷，岂不被一个疑念打断：「看他怎样收场」？而当他搜求再三、总算凑出「要求我们」的时候，我们还应和得了那狂喜么？他岂不是——容我用一回东方的譬喻——把聪明的一口寒气放进了至福的花园？无人能同时又聪明，又出神。12

===Original===
As for triple rhymes—rhymes of the comedian who had a lot o’ news with many curious facts about the square on the hypotenuse, or the cassiowary who ate the missionary on the plains of Timbuktu, with Bible, prayerbook, hymnbook too—they are for the facetious, and removed, as far as geometrical progression can remove them, from any *Paradise Lost* or *Regained*.

===Chinese===
至于三音节韵——那位「有一大堆消息、连斜边上正方形的许多奇妙事实也齐备」的滑稽家所用的韵，或那只「在廷巴克图平原上把传教士吃掉、连圣经、祈祷书、赞美诗集也一齐吞下」的食火鸡所用的韵——那是留给打诨的；离任何一部《失乐园》或《复乐园》，远得像几何级数所能移开的那样远。

===Original===
It may sound a genuine note, now and then:

> Alas! for the rarity
> Of Christian charity
> Under the sun!
> Oh, it was pitiful!
> Near a whole city full,
> Home she had none!

===Chinese===
三音节韵偶尔也能发出一声真的调子：

> 唉！可怜在太阳底下，
> 基督徒的仁爱
> 竟这般稀罕！
> 啊，好不凄惶！
> 满城熙熙攘攘，
> 她竟无家可还！

===Original===
But not often: and, I think, never but in lyric.

===Chinese===
但不常有；而且我想，除了抒情短诗，从不如此。

===Original===
III

So much, then, for rhyme. We will approach the question of metre, helped or unhelped by rhyme, in another way; and a way yet more practical.

===Chinese===
III

韵就说到这里。至于音律的问题——有韵扶助也罢，无韵扶助也罢——我们要换一条路子来考察；一条更为切实的路子。

===Original===
When Milton (determined to write a grand epic) was casting about for his subject, he had a mind for some while to attempt the story of Job. You may find evidence for this in a MS. preserved here in Trinity College Library.

You will find printed evidence in a passage of his *Reason of Church Government*:

> Time serves not now, [he writes,] and perhaps I might seem too profuse to give any certain account of what the mind at home, in the spacious circuits of her musing, hath liberty to propose to herself, though of highest hope and hardest attempting; whether that epic form whereof the two poems of Homer, and those other two of Virgil and Tasso, are a diffuse, and the book of Job a brief model …

===Chinese===
弥尔顿决意要写一部宏大的史诗、物色题材的时候，曾有一阵子打算试写约伯的故事。你们在本地三一学院（Trinity College）图书馆保存的一份手稿里，可以找到这方面的证据。

印行的证据，则可在他的《论教会政府的理由》（Reason of Church Government）里一段文字中找到：

> 如今时机未许，[他写道，]我若确然讲出这颗心在自家境内、在沉思的广阔回环里有自由向自己提出的那些东西——希望虽最高、尝试虽最难——或许要嫌我太不知分寸：譬如那种史诗形式，荷马的两首诗和维吉尔（Virgil）、塔索（Tasso）的另两首是它的铺张的范本，而《约伯记》是它的简短的范本……

===Original===
Again, we know Job to have been one of the three stories meditated by Shelley as themes for great lyrical dramas, the other two being the madness of Tasso and *Prometheus Unbound*. Shelley never abandoned this idea of a lyrical drama on Job; and if Milton abandoned the idea of an epic, there are passages in *Paradise Lost* as there are passages in *Prometheus Unbound* that might well have been written for this other story. Take the lines

===Chinese===
再者，我们知道，约伯的故事也曾是雪莱（Shelley）拟以三大抒情戏剧为题材的三个故事之一，另两个是塔索的疯狂与《解放了的普罗米修斯》（Prometheus Unbound）。雪莱从未放弃写一部约伯抒情戏剧的念头；而弥尔顿即使放弃了写史诗的念头，《失乐园》里也有些段落，一如《解放了的普罗米修斯》里也有些段落，倒像是专为这另一个故事写的。且看这几行：

===Original===
> Why am I mock’d with death, and lengthen’d out
> To deathless pain? How gladly would I meet
> Mortality my sentence, and be earth
> Insensible! how glad would lay me down
> As in my mother’s lap! There I should rest
> And sleep secure; …

===Chinese===
> 为什么我竟被死亡嘲弄，被拖延着
> 去受不死之痛？我多么乐意迎候
> 我的判词——死，化作无知无觉的尘土！
> 我多么乐意躺下身来，
> 像躺在母亲的膝上！在那里我将安息，
> 安然睡去；……

===Original===
What is this, as Lord Latymer asks, but an echo of Job’s words?—

> For now should I have lien down and been quiet;
> I should have slept; then had I been at rest:
> With kings and counsellers of the earth,
> Which built desolate places for themselves …
> There the wicked cease from troubling;
> And there the weary be at rest.

===Chinese===
这不正如拉蒂默勋爵所问的，正是约伯话语的回声么？——

> 我原可以躺下，得享安静；
> 我原可以睡了，那时便得安息：
> 可以同地上那些
> 为自己修造荒场的君王谋士在一起……
> 在那里，恶人止息搅扰；
> 在那里，困乏人得享安息。

===Original===
There is no need for me to point out how exactly, though from two nearly opposite angles, the story of Job would hit the philosophy of Milton and the philosophy of Shelley to the very heart. What is the story of the afflicted patriarch but a direct challenge to a protestant like Milton (I use the word in its strict sense) to justify the ways of God to man? It is the very purpose, in sum, of the Book of Job, as it is the very purpose, in sum, of *Paradise Lost*: and since both poems can only work out the justification by long argumentative speeches, both poems lamentably fail as real solutions of the difficulty. To this I shall recur, and here merely observe that *qui s’ excuse s’ accuse*: a God who can only explain himself by the help of long-winded scolding, or of long-winded advocacy, though he employ an archangel for advocate, has given away the half of his case by the implicit admission that there are two sides to the question. And when we have put aside the poetical ineptitude of a Creator driven to apology, it remains that to Shelley the Jehovah who, for a sort of wager, allowed Satan to torture Job merely for the game of testing him, would be no better than any other tyrant; would be a miscreant Creator, abominable as the Zeus of the *Prometheus Unbound*.

===Chinese===
约伯的故事虽从两个几乎相反的角度出发，却能不偏不倚、直中弥尔顿与雪莱各自的哲学心窝——这一点无须我来指点。这受难族长的故事，除了一场直接向弥尔顿那样一位抗议宗信徒（protestant，我用的是这个词的严格意义）发出的挑战——要他为上帝待人之道作辩白——又是什么呢？一言以蔽之，这正是《约伯记》的主旨，也正是一言以蔽之《失乐园》的主旨：而既然两首诗都只能靠长篇的论辩演说来完成这番辩白，两首诗作为这道难题的真实解答，便都令人痛心地失败了。这一点我后文还要回头来讲；这里只消指出：*qui s’ excuse s’ accuse*（辩解即是认罪）：一位只能靠冗长的斥责或冗长的讼词来解释自己的上帝，哪怕请一位天使长来当辩护人，也已在默认这道问题有两个面向之间，先输掉了自己案子的另一半。而当我们把「造物主被迫道歉」这一诗艺上的拙劣搁置一边，剩下的事实便是：在雪莱眼里，那位为了一场赌赛，容许撒但（Satan）折磨约伯、只为考验他取乐的耶和华（Jehovah），与任何别的暴君并无二致；将是一个恶迹昭彰的造物主，同《解放了的普罗米修斯》里的宙斯（Zeus）一样可憎。

===Original===
Now you may urge that Milton and Shelley dropped Job for hero because both felt him to be a merely static figure: and that the one chose Satan, the rebel angel, the other chose Prometheus the rebel Titan, because both are active rebels, and as epic and drama require action, each of these heroes makes the thing move; that Satan and Prometheus are not passive sufferers like Job but souls as quick and fiery as Byron’s Lucifer:

> Souls who dare use their immortality—
> Souls who dare look the Omnipotent tyrant in
> His everlasting face, and tell him that
> His evil is not good.

===Chinese===
如今你们或许要辩说：弥尔顿与雪莱把约伯撤下、另择主角，是因为两人都觉得他不过是个静态的人物：一个拣了撒但——反叛的天使，另一个拣了普罗米修斯（Prometheus）——反叛的泰坦（Titan），因为两者都是行动中的反叛者；史诗与戏剧既需要行动，这两位主角各各都能叫事情动起来；说撒但与普罗米修斯不像约伯那样是受动的受难者，而是同拜伦（Byron）笔下的路西法（Lucifer）一样快捷、火烈的灵魂：

> 那些敢于运用自己不朽的灵魂——
> 敢于直视全能的暴君
> 那万世不易的面孔，告诉他说：
> 他的恶并非善。

===Original===
Very well, urge this: urge it with all your might. All the while you will be doing just what I desire you to do, using Job alongside *Prometheus Unbound* and *Paradise Lost* as a comparative work of literature.

But, if you ask me for my own opinion why Milton and Shelley dropped their intention to make poems on the Book of Job, it is that they no sooner tackled it than they found it to be a magnificent poem already, and a poem on which, with all their genius, they found themselves unable to improve.

===Chinese===
很好，就这样辩下去：使出全力去辩。你们这样做的时候，恰恰在做我盼望你们做的事：把《约伯记》同《解放了的普罗米修斯》和《失乐园》并列，当作一部比较文学的著作来用。

但是，倘若你们问我自己的意见——弥尔顿与雪莱为何打消了以《约伯记》为题材作诗的念头——那便是因为：他们刚一上手，就发现这已经是一首恢弘的诗，一首凭他们全部的天才也无可改进的诗。

===Original===
I want you to realise a thing most simple, demonstrable by five minutes of practice, yet so confused by conventional notions of what poetry is that I dare say it to be equally demonstrable that Milton and Shelley discovered it only by experiment. Does this appear to you a bold thing to say of so tremendous an artist as Milton? Well, of course it would be cruel to quote in proof his paraphrases of Psalms 114 and 136: to set against the Authorised Version’s

===Chinese===
我要你们认识一件极简单的事，五分钟的实践便足以证明；可它被关于「诗是什么」的流俗之见搅得如此混乱，我敢说同样可以证明：弥尔顿与雪莱只是靠实验才发现它的。这话用在弥尔顿这样气象浩大的艺术家身上，你们听着可嫌武断？嗯，自然，引出他的《诗篇》第 114、136 篇拟作来作证据，未免残忍：拿它们去同钦定版圣经（詹姆士王钦定本，AV）的

such pomposity as

verses yet further weakened by the late Sir William Baker for *Hymns Ancient and Modern*.

===Chinese===
这些诗句后来又经已故的威廉·贝克爵士（Sir William Baker）为《古今圣诗集》（Hymns Ancient and Modern）改写，愈发疲弱。

===Original===
It were cruel, I say, to condemn these attempts as little above those of Sternhold and Hopkins, or even of those of Tate and Brady: for Milton made them at fifteen years old, and he who afterwards consecrated his youth to poetry soon learned to know better. And yet, bearing in mind the passages in *Paradise Lost* and *Paradise Regained* which paraphrase the Scriptural narrative, I cannot forbear the suspicion that, though as an artist he had the instinct to feel it, he never quite won to *knowing* the simple fact that the thing had already been done and surpassingly well done: he, who did so much to liberate poetry from rhyme—he—even he who in the grand choruses of *Samson Agonistes* did so much to liberate it from strict metre never quite realised, being hag-ridden by the fetish that rides between two panniers, the sacred and the profane, that this translation of Job already belongs to the category of poetry, *is* poetry, already above metre, and in rhythm far on its way to the insurpassable. If rhyme be allowed to that greatest of arts, if metre, is not rhythm above both for her service? Hear in a sentence how this poem uplifts the rhythm of the Vulgate:

===Chinese===
话说回来，要把这些习作贬作不过略高于斯特恩霍尔德与霍普金斯（Sternhold and Hopkins），或甚至略高于塔特与布雷迪（Tate and Brady）的手笔，毕竟是残忍的：因为弥尔顿作这些诗时才十五岁；而他后来既将青春奉献给了诗，不久便学会了高明一些。然而，想着《失乐园》与《复乐园》（Paradise Regained）里那些改写圣经叙事的段落，我按捺不住一个疑心：作为艺术家，他固然有本能感觉到它，却始终不曾完全*知道*这样一个简单的事实——这件事早已有人做过了，而且做得超凡入圣：他，那个为把诗从韵里解放出来出了偌大力的人——他——甚至那个在《力士参孙》（Samson Agonistes）的宏伟合唱里为把诗从严格音律中解放出来出了偌大力的人，也始终不曾真正醒悟——他被那个骑跨在圣俗两驮之间的妖祟驾驭着——这部《约伯记》译本早已归入诗的类目，*就*是诗，早已超乎音律之上，在节奏上远远走在通往无可企及的大路上。倘若韵可以为那最伟大的艺术所用，音律也可以，那么，服事她的节奏岂不高于两者？且听一句，看这首诗怎样把拉丁通俗译本（Vulgate）的节奏提举起来：

===Original===
> Ecce, Deus magnus vincens scientiam nostram; numerus annorum ejus inestimabilis!

===Chinese===
> *Ecce, Deus magnus vincens scientiam nostram; numerus annorum ejus inestimabilis!*（看哪，上帝至大，超乎我们的知识；他年岁之数，无可估量！）

===Original===
But hear, in a longer passage, how our English rhythm swings and sways to the Hebrew parallels:

> Surely there is a mine for silver,
> And a place for gold which they refine.
> Iron is taken out of the earth,
> And brass is molten out of the stone.
> *Man* setteth an end to darkness,
> And searcheth out to the furthest bound
> The stones of thick darkness and of the shadow of death.
> He breaketh open a shaft away from where men sojourn;
> They are forgotten of the foot *that passeth by*;
> They hang afar from men, they swing to and fro.
> As for the earth, out of it cometh bread:
> And underneath it is turned up as it were by fire.
> The atones thereof are the place of sapphires,
> And it hath dust of gold.
> That path no bird of prey knoweth,
> Neither hath the falcon’s eye seen it:
> The proud beasts have not trodden it,
> Nor hath the fierce lion passed thereby.

===Chinese===
可再听长长的一段，看我们的英文节奏怎样随希伯来文的平行句摆荡摇曳：

> 白银自有矿穴，
> 炼金自有地方。
> 铁从地里挖出，
> 铜从石中熔化。
> *人*为黑暗定界限，
> 查究幽暗阴翳的石头，
> 直到极处。
> 他在无人居住之处凿开矿道；
> 它们早被*过路的脚*忘记；
> 它们远悬在人身之外，摇来荡去。
> 至于地，能出粮食：
> 地内好像被火翻起来。
> 地中的石头是蓝宝石之处，
> 并且有金沙。
> 那条路，鸷鸟不知道，
> 鹰眼也未曾见过：
> 狂傲的野兽未曾行过，
> 猛烈的狮子也未曾经过。

===Original===
> He putteth forth his hand upon the flinty rock;
> He overturneth the mountains by the roots.
> He cutteth out channels among the rocks;
> And his eye seeth every precious thing.
> He bindeth the streams that they trickle not;
> And the thing that is hid bringeth he forth to light.
> But where shall wisdom be found?
> And where is the place of understanding?
> Man knoweth not the price thereof;
> Neither is it found in the land of the living.
> The deep saith, It is not in me:
> And the sea saith, It is not with me.

===Chinese===
> 他伸手凿开坚石；
> 倾覆山根。
> 他在磐石中凿出水道；
> 他的眼看见各样宝物。
> 他封闭水流，不得滴沥；
> 把隐藏的物带到光下来。
> 然而，智慧有何处可寻？
> 聪明之处在哪里呢？
> 智慧的价值无人知道；
> 在活人之地也无处可寻。
> 深渊说：不在我内：
> 沧海说：不在我中。

===Original===
> It cannot be gotten for gold,
> Neither shall silver be weighed for the price thereof.
> It cannot be valued with the gold of Ophir,
> With the precious onyx, or the sapphire.
> Gold and glass cannot equal it:
> Neither shall the exchange thereof be jewels of fine gold.
> No mention shall be made of coral or of crystal:
> Yea, the price of wisdom is above rubies.
> The topaz of Ethiopia shall not equal it,
> Neither shall it be valued with pure gold.
> Whence then cometh wisdom?
> And where is the place of understanding?
> Seeing it is hid from the eyes of all living,
> And kept close from the fowls of the air.

===Chinese===
> 智慧非用黄金可得，
> 也不能平白银为它的价值。
> 俄斐（Ophir）的金不足与较量，
> 贵重的红玛瑙与蓝宝石，也不足与之较量。
> 黄金和玻璃不足与比较：
> 精金的器皿不足与兑换。
> 珊瑚、水晶都不足论：
> 是的，智慧的价值胜过红宝石。
> 古实的黄玉不足与比较，
> 精金也不足与较量。
> 这样，智慧从何处来呢？
> 聪明之处在哪里呢？
> 是向一切活人的眼目隐藏，
> 向空中的飞鸟掩蔽。

===Original===
> Destruction and Death say,
> We have heard a rumour thereof with our ears.
> God understandeth the way thereof,
> And he knoweth the place thereof.
> For he looketh to the ends of the earth,
> And seeth under the whole heaven;
> To make a weight for the wind;
> Yea, he meteth out the waters by measure.
> When he made a decree for the rain,
> And a way for the lightning of the thunder:
> Then did he see it, and declare it;
> He established it, yea, and searched it out.
> And unto man he said,
> Behold, the fear of the Lord, *that* is wisdom;
> And to depart from evil is understanding.

===Chinese===
> 灭没和死亡说：
> 我们亲耳风闻过它的名声。
> 神明白智慧的道路，
> 他晓得智慧的所在。
> 因他鉴察直到地极，
> 遍观普天之下；
> 要为风定轻重；
> 又度量诸水。
> 他为雨定命令，
> 为雷电的闪光定道路：
> 那时他看见智慧，而且述说；
> 他坚定它，也查究它。
> 他对人说：
> 看哪，敬畏主，*那*才是智慧；
> 远离恶便是聪明。

===Original===
Is that poetry? Surely it is poetry. Can you improve it with the embellishments of rhyme and strict scansion? Well, sundry bold men have tried, and I will choose, for your judgment, the rendering of a part of the above passage by one who is by no means the worst of them—a hardy anonymous Scotsman. His version was published at Falkirk in 1869:

> His hand on the rock the adventurer puts,
> And mountains entire overturns by the roots;
> New rivers in rocks are enchased by his might,
> And everything precious revealed to his sight;
> The floods from o’er-flowing he bindeth at will,
> And the thing that is hid bringeth forth by his skill.
> But where real wisdom is found can he show?
> Or the place understanding inhabiteth? No!
> Men know not the value, the price of this gem;
> ’Tis not found in the land of the living with them.
> It is not in me, saith the depth; and the sea
> With the voice of an echo, repeats, Not in me.

===Chinese===
这是诗么？当然是诗。你能用韵和严格音步的装饰把它改得更好么？嗯，好些胆大的先生试过，我且从中拣一位绝不最坏的——一个无名的、胆气十足的苏格兰人——所译的上述段落的一部分，请你们品评。他的译本 1869 年刊行于福尔柯克（Falkirk）：

> 冒险的人把手按在岩石上，
> 把整座大山连根翻倒；
> 新的河川靠他的大力凿进石中，
> 一切珍贵之物显在他眼前；
> 他随意捆住那泛滥的洪流，
> 靠他的技巧把隐藏之物带出。
> 可是真智慧寻在何处，他能指明么？
> 聪明居住的地方在哪里？不能！
> 人不知道这宝石的价值、这价格；
> 在活人之地他们寻它不着。
> 不在我内，深渊说；大海
> 用回声的声音重复说，不在我内。

===Original===
(I have a suspicion somehow that what the sea really answered, in its northern vernacular, was “Me either.”)

> Whence then cometh wisdom? And where is the place
> Understanding hath chosen, since this is the case? …

===Chinese===
（我不知怎的总疑心，大海用它那北方土话真正回答的，其实是「我也不是。」）

> 这样，智慧从何处来呢？既然如此，
> 聪明所拣选的地方又在哪里？……

===Original===
Enough! This not only shows how that other rendering can be spoilt even to the point of burlesque by an attempt, on preconceived notions, to embellish it with metre and rhyme, but it also hints that parallel verse will actually resent and abhor such embellishment even by the most skilled hand. Yet, I repeat, our version of Job is poetry undeniable. What follows?

===Chinese===
够了！这不只表明：先抱着成见，硬要用音律与韵脚去装点那另一个译本，会把它糟蹋到近乎打诨的地步；它还暗示：平行体的诗句对这种装点——哪怕出自最高明的手——实在会憎恨、会深恶。然而我再说一遍，我们这个《约伯记》译本是无可否认的诗。那么，跟着推出的是什么？

===Original===
Why, it follows that in the course of studying it as literature we have found experimentally settled for us—and on the side of freedom—a dispute in which scores of eminent critics have taken sides: a dispute revived but yesterday (if we omit the blank and devastated days of this War) by the writers and apostles of *vers libres*. “Can there be poetry without metre?” “Is free verse a true poetic form?” Why, our Book of Job being poetry, unmistakable poetry, of course there can, to be sure it is. These apostles are butting at an open door. Nothing remains for them but to go and write *vers libres* as fine as those of Job in our English translation. Or suppose even that they write as well as M. Paul Fort, they will yet be writing ancestrally, not as innovators but as renewers. Nothing is done in literature by arguing whether or not this or that be possible or permissible. The only way to prove it possible or permissible is to go and do it: and then you are lucky indeed if some ancient writer has not forestalled you.

===Chinese===
咳，跟着推出的是：在把它当文学来研读的过程中，我们已经靠实验替自己解决了一场论争——而且是解决在自由一边；几十位名批评家都曾在这场论争里各执一端：这场论争（若不计这场大战那些空白而破碎的日子）就在昨天还被自由诗（vers libres）的作者们与使徒们重新点燃。「没有音律能有诗么？」「自由诗是一种真正的诗体么？」咳，我们的《约伯记》既然是诗，是不容误认的诗，那么当然能有，它当然是。这些使徒是在往一扇开着的门上撞。他们剩下的只有一件事好做：去写出同我们英文译本《约伯记》里那些自由诗一样好的自由诗来。退一步说，就算他们写得同保罗·福尔（Paul Fort）先生一样好，他们也仍旧是在写祖传的东西——不是革新家，而是更新者。在文学里，争辩这个或那个可能不可能、许可不许可，什么也成就不了。证明它可能、许可的唯一法子，就是动手去做：而做的时候，倘若竟没有哪位古代作家抢在你前头，你就算是造化了。

===Original===
IV

Now for another question (much argued, you will remember, a few years ago) “Is there—can there be—such a thing as a Static Theatre, a Static Drama?”

===Chinese===
IV

现在是另一个问题（你们记得，几年前曾争得热闹）：「有没有——能不能有——一种静态剧场、静态戏剧（Static Drama）这样的东西？」

===Original===
Most of you (I daresay) remember M. Maeterlinck’s definition of this and his demand for it. To summarise him roughly, he contends that the old drama—the traditional, the conventional drama—lives by action; that, in Aristotle’s phrase, it represents men doing, πράττοντας, and resolves itself into a struggle of human wills—whether against the gods, as in ancient tragedy, or against one another, as in modern. M. Maeterlinck tells us—

===Chinese===
你们大多数（我敢说）还记得梅特林克（Maeterlinck）先生对它的界定和他对它的吁求。粗略概括他的意思：他主张旧戏——传统的、因袭的戏剧——靠行动而活；用亚里士多德（Aristotle）的话说，它摹写行事中的人（πράττοντας），归结为人类意志的斗争——或是与神明斗，如古代悲剧；或是人与人相斗，如近代戏剧。梅特林克先生告诉我们——

===Original===
> There is a tragic element in the life of every day that is far more real, far more penetrating, far more akin to the true self that is in us, than is the tragedy that lies in great adventure. … It goes beyond the determined struggle of man against man, and desire against desire; it goes beyond the eternal conflict of duty and passion. Its province is rather to reveal to us how truly wonderful is the mere act of living, and to throw light upon the existence of the soul, self-contained in the midst of ever-restless immensities; to hush the discourse of reason and sentiment, so that above the tumult may be heard the solemn uninterrupted whisperings of man and his destiny.
> To the tragic author [he goes on, later], as to the mediocre painter who still lingers over historical pictures, it is only the violence of the anecdote that appeals, and in his representation thereof does the entire interest of his work consist. … Indeed when I go to a theatre, I feel as though I were spending a few hours with my ancestors, who conceived life as though it were something that was primitive, arid and brutal. … I am shown a deceived husband killing his wife, a woman poisoning her lover, a son avenging his father, a father slaughtering his children, murdered kings, ravished virgins, imprisoned citizens—in a word all the sublimity of tradition, but alas how superficial and material! Blood, surface-tears and death! What can I learn from creatures who have but one fixed idea, who have no time to live, for that there is a rival, a mistress, whom it behoves them to put to death?

===Chinese===
> 日常生活里自有一种悲剧因素，比起大惊险故事里包藏的悲剧来，它更真实，更深切，更贴近我们里面的那个真我。……它超出人对人、欲望对欲望的既定斗争；它超出责任与激情那永恒的冲突。它的职分，毋宁是向我们启示：单单「活着」这一桩行为，就何其真实地奇妙；是照亮灵魂的存在——那在永不止息的浩渺之中自足自处的魂；是让理性与情感的言谈静下来，好教喧嚣之上，可以听见人与他的命运之间那庄严而不曾间断的低语。
> 对于悲剧作者[他后来接着说]，一如对还在历史画前流连的平庸画家，打动他的只是轶事的暴力，而他的作品的全部趣味，也就全在摹写这轶事上。……真的，每逢走进剧院，我便觉得自己在同几位祖先消磨几个钟头——他们把人生设想成某种原始、干瘪而残忍的东西。……人们演给我看：被欺的丈夫杀死妻子，女人毒杀情人，儿子为父复仇，父亲屠戮儿女，被弑的君王，被劫的处女，被囚的公民——一句话，传统的一切崇高，可叹多么浮面、多么物质！血，浮面的泪，和死亡！从这些生灵身上，我能学到什么？他们只有一个固着的念头，无暇生活，只因有一个情敌、一个情妇，非置于死地不可？

===Original===
M. Maeterlinck does not (he says) know if the Static Drama of his craving be impossible. He inclines to think—instancing some Greek tragedies such as *Prometheus* and *Choephori*—that it already exists. But may we not, out of the East—the slow, the stationary East—fetch an instance more convincing?

===Chinese===
他（据他自己说）不知道他所渴求的静态戏剧是否为不可能之物。他倾向于认为——举出《普罗米修斯》（Prometheus）、《奠酒人》（Choephori）等几部希腊悲剧为例——它已经存在。但我们何妨从东方——那迟缓的、凝定的东方——取来一个更有说服力的例证呢？

===Original===
V

The Drama of Job opens with a Prologue in the mouth of a Narrator.

There was a man in the land of Uz, named Job; upright, God-fearing, of great substance in sheep, cattle and oxen; blest also with seven sons and three daughters. After telling of their family life, how wholesome it is, and pious, and happy—

The Prologue passes to a Council held in Heaven. The Lord sits there, and the sons of God present themselves each from his province. Enters Satan (whom we had better call the Adversary) from his sphere of inspection, the Earth, and reports. The Lord specially questions him concerning Job, pattern of men. The Adversary demurs. “Doth Job fear God for nought? Hast thou not set a hedge about his prosperity? But put forth thy hand and touch all that he hath, and he will renounce thee to thy face.” The Lord gives leave for this trial to be made (you will recall the opening of *Everyman*):

===Chinese===
V

《约伯记》这出戏，以一段出自叙述者（Narrator）之口的序幕开场。

乌斯（Uz）地有一个人，名叫约伯；为人正直，敬畏上帝，羊群、牛畜、牲口极多；又蒙赐福，有七个儿子、三个女儿。叙述者讲过这一家的生活——何等康健、何等虔敬、何等快乐——

序幕便转入天上召开的一次会议。耶和华坐在那里，神的众子各从自己的职分辖地前来朝见。撒但——我们最好称他为「那对手」（the Adversary）——从他的巡察之区，就是地上，进来，呈报事务。耶和华特别向他问起约伯——人中的典范。那对手表示异议：「约伯敬畏神，岂是无故呢？你不是圈上篱笆围护他的家业么？你且伸手，毁他一切所有的，他必当面弃掉你。」耶和华准许这场试炼施行（你们当记得《凡人》（Everyman）的开场）：
===Original===
So, in the midst of his wealth, a messenger came to Job and says—

> The oxen were plowing,
> and the asses feeding beside them:
> and the Sabeans fell upon them,
> and took them away;
> yea, they have slain the servants with the edge of the sword;
> and I only am escaped alone to tell thee.

===Chinese===
于是，正当他财富盈门之中，一个报信的来到约伯跟前，说——

> 牛正耕地，
> 驴在旁边吃草：
> 示巴人（Sabeans）闯来，
> 把它们掳去；
> 并且用刀杀了仆人；
> 惟有我一人逃脱，来报信给你。

===Original===
> While he was yet speaking, there came also another, and said,
> The fire of God is fallen from heaven,
> and hath burned up the sheep, and the servants,
> and consumed them;
> and I only am escaped alone to tell thee.

===Chinese===
> 他还说话的时候，又有人来说：
> 神从天上降下火来，
> 将群羊和仆人都烧了，
> 烧灭净尽；
> 惟有我一人逃脱，来报信给你。

===Original===
> While he was yet speaking, there came also another, and said,
> The Chaldeans made three bands,
> and fell upon the camels,
> and have taken them away,
> yea, and slain the servants with the edge of the sword;
> and I only am escaped alone to tell thee.

===Chinese===
> 他还说话的时候，又有人来说：
> 迦勒底人（Chaldeans）分作三队，
> 闯来扑向骆驼，
> 把它们掳去；
> 并且用刀杀了仆人；
> 惟有我一人逃脱，来报信给你。

===Original===
> While he was yet speaking, there came also another, and said,
> Thy sons and thy daughters
> were eating and drinking wine in their eldest brother’s house:
> and, behold,
> there came a great wind from the wilderness,
> and smote the four corners of the house,
> and it fell upon the young men,
> and they are dead;
> and I only am escaped alone to tell thee.

===Chinese===
> 他还说话的时候，又有人来说：
> 你的儿女
> 正在他们长兄的家里吃饭喝酒：
> 不料，
> 有狂风从旷野刮来，
> 击打房屋的四角，
> 房屋倒在少年人身上，
> 他们就都死了；
> 惟有我一人逃脱，来报信给你。

===Original===
> Then Job arose, and rent his mantle, and shaved his head, and
> fell down upon the ground, and worshipped; and he said,
> Naked came I out of my mother’s womb,
> and naked shall I return thither:
> the Lord gave, and the Lord hath taken away;
> blessed be the name of the Lord.

===Chinese===
> 于是约伯起来，撕裂外袍，剃了头，
> 伏在地上敬拜；他说：
> 我赤身出于母胎，
> 也必赤身归回：
> 赏赐的是耶和华，收取的也是耶和华；
> 耶和华的名是应当称颂的。

===Original===
So the Adversary is foiled, and Job has not renounced God.
