A second Council is held in Heaven; and the Adversary, being questioned, has to admit Job’s integrity, but proposes a severer test:

> Skin for skin, yea, all that a man hath will he give for his life. But put forth thine hand now, and touch his bone and his flesh, and he will renounce thee to thy face.

===Chinese===
那对手这一局落了空：约伯没有弃掉上帝。

天上再开一次会议；那对手被质问之下，不得不承认约伯的正直，却提出一场更严酷的试验：

> 人以皮代皮，情愿舍去一切所有的去保全性命。如今你且伸手，伤他的骨头和他的肉，他必当面弃掉你。

===Original===
Again leave is given: and the Adversary smites Job with the most hideous and loathsome form of leprosy. His kinsfolk (as we learn later) have already begun to desert and hold aloof from him as a man marked out by God’s displeasure. But now he passes out from their midst, as one unclean from head to foot, and seats himself on the ash-mound—that is, upon the *mezbele* or heap of refuse which accumulates outside Arab villages.

===Chinese===
准许又给了：那对手便以最丑恶、最可憎的一种癞病击打约伯。他的亲族（我们后来得知）已经起头躲开他、疏远他，把他看作一个被上帝震怒标出来的人。此刻他便从他们中间出去——从头到脚是个不洁的人——坐在灰堆上，也就是坐在 *mezbele*，阿拉伯村庄外面堆积起来的垃圾堆上。

===Original===
> The dung, [says Professor Moulton,] which is heaped upon the *mezbele* of the Hauran villages is not mixed with straw, which in that warm and dry land is not needed for litter, and it comes mostly from solid-hoofed animals, as the flocks and oxen are left overnight in the grazing places. It is carried in baskets in a dry state to this place … and usually burnt once a month. … The ashes remain. … If the village has been inhabited for centuries the *mezbele* reaches a height far overtopping it. The winter rains reduce it into a compact mass, and it becomes by and by a solid hill of earth. … The *mezbele* serves the inhabitants for a watchtower, and in the sultry evenings for a place of concourse, because there is a current of air on the height. There all day long the children play about it; and there the outcast, who has been stricken with some loathsome malady, and is not allowed to enter the dwellings of men, lays himself down begging an alms of the passersby by day, and by night sheltering himself among the ashes which the heat of the sun has warmed.

===Chinese===
> 堆在浩兰（Hauran）村庄 *mezbele* 上的粪，[莫尔顿教授说，]并不掺杂稻草——在那温热干燥的地方，垫圈用不着稻草——而且多半出自硬蹄的牲口，因为羊群牛畜是整夜留在牧地的。粪干透之后用筐子运到这地方……通常每月焚烧一次。……灰烬留着。……倘若村庄已有几百年的住人历史，*mezbele* 便会高到远远盖过全村。冬季的雨水把它压结成密实的一团，久而久之便成了一座结实的土丘。……*mezbele* 供村民们当瞭望台，闷热的夜晚又当聚会之所，因为高处有风。孩子们整日在它周围玩耍；而被某种可憎的疾病击中的被逐者——不许他进人的宅子——便躺在那里，白天向过路人乞讨，夜里在给太阳晒暖的灰烬中栖身。

===Original===
Here, then, sits in his misery “the forsaken grandee”; and here yet another temptation comes to him—this time not expressly allowed by the Lord. Much foolish condemnation (and, I may add, some foolish facetiousness) has been heaped on Job’s wife. As a matter of fact she is *not* a wicked woman—she has borne her part in the pious and happy family life, now taken away: she has uttered no word of complaint though all the substance be swallowed up and her children with it. But now the sight of her innocent husband thus helpless, thus incurably smitten, wrings, through love and anguish and indignation, this cry from her:

> Dost thou still hold fast thine integrity? renounce God, and die.

===Chinese===
于是，这位愁苦中的「被弃的大人物」便坐在这里；而在这里，又一场试探临到他——这一回并没有得到耶和华明白的准许。多少愚蠢的谴诃（我还得添上一句：多少愚蠢的打诨）已经倾倒在约伯的妻子身上。其实她*并*不是坏女人——她在那虔敬而快乐的家庭生活里有她的本分，如今这一切都被夺去：家业尽被吞没、儿女一同丧命，她却不曾吐过一句怨言。可是此刻，眼见无辜的丈夫这般无助、这般病重难愈，那景象透过爱与痛楚与义愤，从她心里绞出这一声呼喊：

> 你仍然持守你的纯正么？你弃掉神，死了吧。

===Original===
But Job answered, soothing her:

> Thou speakest as one of the foolish women speaketh. What? shall we receive good at the hand of God, and shall we not receive evil?

===Chinese===
约伯却回答她，抚慰她说：

> 你说话，像愚顽的妇人所说的话。什么？难道我们从神手里得福，不也受祸么？

===Original===
So the second trial ends, and Job has sinned not with his lips.

But now comes the third trial, which needs no Council in Heaven to decree it. Travellers by the mound saw this figure seated there, patient, uncomplaining, an object of awe even to the children who at first mocked him; asked this man’s history; and hearing of it, smote on their breasts, and made a token of it and carried the news into far countries: until it reached the ears of Job’s three friends, all great tribesmen like himself—Eliphaz the Temanite, Bildad the Shuhite, and Zophar the Naamathite. These three made an appointment together to travel and visit Job. “And when they lifted up their eyes afar off, and knew him not, they lifted up their voice, and wept.” Then they went up and sat down opposite him on the ground. But the majesty of suffering is silent:

===Chinese===
第二场试炼就此结束，约伯没有在嘴唇上犯罪。

可是现在来了第三场试炼，无须天上的会议来颁令。从土丘旁经过的旅人，看见这个坐在那里的人物——坚忍，无怨，甚至起初嘲弄他的孩子们也对他心生敬畏；便问起这人的来历；听说了，就捶胸，做一个记号记载这事，把消息传到远国：直到传进约伯的三个朋友耳中——都是同他一样的大族长：提幔人以利法（Eliphaz the Temanite）、书亚人比勒达（Bildad the Shuhite）、拿玛人琐法（Zophar the Naamathite）。这三人约齐了，一同起行来探望约伯。「他们举目远远观看，竟认不出他来，就放声大哭。」于是他们走近前来，在地上对面坐下。但受难的威仪是静默的：

===Original===
> Here I and sorrows sit;
> Here is my throne, bid kings come bow to it. …

No, not a word. … And, with the grave courtesy of Eastern men, they too are silent:

> So they sat down with him upon the ground seven days and seven nights, and none spake a word unto him: for they saw that his grief was very great.

===Chinese===
> 我与哀伤同坐在这里；
> 这是我的宝座，传列王来向它下拜。……

不，一言不发。……于是，带着东方人庄重的礼数，他们也静默了：

> 他们就同他七天七夜坐在地上，没有一个人向他说一句话：因为他们看见他极其痛苦。

===Original===
The Prologue ends. The scene is set. After seven days of silence the real drama opens.

VI

Of the drama itself I shall attempt no analysis, referring you for this to the two books from which I have already quoted. My purpose being merely to persuade you that this surpassing poem can be studied, and ought to be studied, as literature, I shall content myself with turning it (so to speak) once or twice in my hand and glancing one or two facets at you.

===Chinese===
序幕终了。布景已定。静默七日之后，真正的戏剧开场。

VI

戏剧本身，我不拟试作分析；这方面请你们参看我方才引过的那两部书。我的目的只在说服你们：这首超迈的诗可以当作文学来研读，而且应当当作文学来研读；为此我只求把它（姑且这么说）在手中转动一两回，向你们闪出一两个棱面来。

===Original===
To begin with, then, you will not have failed to notice, in the setting out of the drama, a curious resemblance between Job and the *Prometheus* of Aeschylus. The curtain in each play lifts on a figure solitary, tortured (for no reason that seems good to us) by a higher will which, we are told, is God’s. The chorus of Sea-nymphs in the opening of the Greek play bears no small resemblance in attitude of mind to Job’s three friends. When Job at length breaks the intolerable silence with

> Let the day perish wherein I was born,
> And the night which said, There is a man child conceived.

he uses just such an outburst as Prometheus: and, as he is answered by his friends, so the Nymphs at once exclaim to Prometheus

> Seest thou not that thou hast sinned?

===Chinese===
那么，先从这一点说起：你们想必没有漏看，这出戏的布局里，约伯与埃斯库罗斯（Aeschylus）的《普罗米修斯》（Prometheus）有着奇妙的相似。两出戏的幕布升起时，台上的都是个孤独的人物，受着一种更高的意志的折磨——据告诉我们，那意志是上帝的——而折磨的缘由，在我们看来并不成话。希腊戏开场时的海仙女歌队，在心灵姿态上同约伯的三个朋友相去不远。当约伯终于冲破那不堪忍受的静默，喊出

> 愿我生的那日灭没，
> 愿那夜说：怀了男胎。

他用的正是普罗米修斯那样的爆发：他的朋友怎样应他，仙女们也立刻向普罗米修斯惊呼：

> 你看不见你已犯了罪么？

===Original===
But at once, for anyone with a sense of comparative literature, is set up a comparison between the persistent West and the persistent East; between the fiery energising rebel and the patient victim. Of these two, both good, one will dare everything to release mankind from thrall; the other will submit, and justify himself—mankind too, if it may hap—by submission.

===Chinese===
但立刻，在稍具比较文学意识的人眼里，便立起了一重比较：执拗的西方对执拗的东方；火烈奋发的反叛者对坚忍的受难者。这二者都是善的：一个敢于拿出一切，去把人类从奴役中解救出来；另一个则顺服，并且以顺服证明自己有理——若命运允许，连人类也一并如此。

===Original===
At once this difference is seen to give a difference of form to the drama. Our poem is purely static. Some critics can detect little individuality in Job’s three friends, to distinguish them. For my part I find Eliphaz more of a personage than the other two; grander in the volume of his mind, securer in wisdom; as I find Zophar rather noticeably a mean-minded greybeard, and Bildad a man of the stand-no-nonsense kind. But, to tell the truth, I prefer not to search for individuality in these men: I prefer to see them as three figures with eyes of stone almost expressionless. For in truth they are the conventions, all through—the orthodox men—addressing Job, the reality; and their words come to this:

> Thou sufferest, therefore must have sinned.
> All suffering is, must be a judgment upon sin.
> Else God is not righteous.

===Chinese===
立刻可以看出，这一分别也给了戏剧形式上的分别。我们的诗是纯粹静态的。有的批评家在约伯的三个朋友身上辨不出多少个性来区分他们。在我看来，以利法比另两位更成个人物：心胸的气象更宏大，智慧上更笃定；而琐法颇为扎眼地是个心地褊狭的老朽，比勒达则是个容不得半句废话的人物。但说老实话，我并不想在这些人身上搜求什么个性：我宁愿把他们看作三尊石目无光的像，几乎毫无表情。因为说真的，他们从头到尾就是那些程式——正统的人们——向约伯，那位「真实」，说话；他们的话归结起来便是：

> 你受苦，所以必定犯了罪。
> 一切受苦都是，必定是对罪的审判。
> 否则，神就不是公义的。

===Original===
They are statuesque, as the drama is static. The speeches follow one another, rising and falling, in rise and fall magnificently and deliberately eloquent. Not a limb is seen to move, unless it be when Job half rises from the dust in sudden scorn of their conventions:

> No doubt but *ye* are the people,
> And wisdom shall die with you!

or again

> Will ye speak unrighteously for God,
> And talk deceitfully for him?
> Will *ye* respect *his* person?
> Will *ye* contend for God?

===Chinese===
他们如雕像，正如这出戏是静态的。一篇篇演说相继而来，起起伏伏，那起伏宏伟而从容，雄辩滔滔。看不见一肢一动，除非约伯从尘土里蓦地半支起身，蔑视他们的程式：

> 无怪乎*你们*是子民，
> 智慧要同*你们*一同灭亡！

或者又喊：

> 你们要为神说不义的话，
> 为他说诡诈的言语么？
> *你们*要看*他的*情面么？
> *你们*要为他争论么？

===Original===
Yet—so great is this man, who has not renounced and will not renounce God, that still and ever he clamours for more knowledge of Him. Still getting no answer, he lifts up his hands and calls the great Oath of Clearance; in effect, “If I have loved gold overmuch, hated mine enemy, refused the stranger my tent, truckled to public opinion”:

> If my land cry out against me,
> And the furrows thereof weep together;
> If I have eaten the fruits thereof without money,
> Or have caused the owners thereof to lose their life:
> Let thistles grow instead of wheat,
> And cockle instead of barley.

===Chinese===
然而——这个人何等伟大：他不曾弃掉上帝，也不肯弃掉上帝，以至他仍旧、永远呼喊着要多一点对他的认识。依旧得不到回答，他便举起双手，发起那庄严的雪冤大誓；要旨说：「倘若我爱金子过了度，倘若我恨过仇敌，倘若我拒绝客居我帐棚的外人，倘若我向公共舆论屈膝」：

> 我若这地向我喊冤，
> 它的犁沟一同哭泣；
> 我若未给价值便吃了它的出产，
> 或叫原主丧了性命：
> 愿蒺藜长出来代替麦子，
> 愿恶草长出来代替大麦。

===Original===
With a slow gesture he covers his face:

> The words of Job are ended.

===Chinese===
他缓缓作势，掩住了脸：

> 约伯的话说完了。

===Original===
VII

They are ended: even though at this point (when the debate seems to be closed) a young Aramaean Arab, Elihu, who has been loitering around and listening to the controversy, bursts in and delivers his young red-hot opinions. They are violent, and at the same time quite raw and priggish. Job troubles not to answer: the others keep a chilling silence. But while this young man rants, pointing skyward now and again, we see, we feel—it is most wonderfully conveyed—as clearly as if indicated by successive stage-directions, a terrific thunderstorm gathering; a thunderstorm with a whirlwind. It gathers; it is upon them; it darkens them with dread until even the words of Elihu dry on his lips:

===Chinese===
VII

是说完了：尽管在这当口（辩论似乎已告收束），一个年轻的亚兰阿拉伯人，以利户（Elihu），——他一直在旁边逡巡、听这场争辩——突然闯进来，迸出他那一套年轻的、烧得正红的意见。那些意见是猛烈的，同时又生涩得很，还有几分道学气。约伯不屑于费神回答：其余的人守着一片冷得瘆人的沉默。可正当这年轻人高声咆哮、不时向天指指点点的时候，我们看见，我们感到——这一点传达得奇妙之至——一场可怖的雷雨正在聚拢来，清楚得仿佛一道接一道的舞台指示标出来的一般；一场携着旋风的雷雨。它聚拢来；它已临到他们；它用惧怕罩暗了他们，直到连以利户的话也在唇上干涸：

===Original===
> If a man speak, surely he shall be swallowed up.

It breaks and blasts and confounds them; and out of it the Lord speaks.

===Chinese===
> 人若开口，就必被吞没。

雷雨炸裂，轰击他们，把他们震伏；耶和华就从雷雨之中说话。

===Original===
Now of that famous and marvellous speech, put by the poet into the mouth of God, we may say what may be said of all speeches put by man into the mouth of God. We may say, as of the speeches of the Archangel in *Paradise Lost* that it is argument, and argument, by its very nature, admits of being answered. But, if to make God talk at all be anthropomorphism, here is anthropomorphism at its very best in its effort to reach to God.

===Chinese===
至于那篇由诗人放进上帝口中的著名而奇妙的演说，凡人放进上帝口中的演说所能说的话，对它都可以说。我们可以像论《失乐园》里天使长的演说那样说：它是论辩，而论辩按其本性是可以被驳答的。但，倘若叫上帝开口本身就是拟人论（anthropomorphism），那么这里有的是拟人论的极致——它竭尽全力企及上帝。

===Original===
> There is a hush. The storm clears away; and in this hush the voice of the Narrator is heard again, pronouncing the Epilogue. Job has looked in the face of God and reproached him as a friend reproaches a friend. Therefore his captivity was turned, and his wealth returned to him, and he begat sons and daughters, and saw his sons’ sons unto the fourth generation. So Job died, being old and full of years.

===Chinese===
> 万籁俱寂。风雨散去；在这静默里，重新听见叙述者的声音，宣读着尾声。约伯曾面对面见过上帝，像朋友责备朋友那样责备过他。因此他的被掳得以转回，家业重新归他，他又生了儿子女儿，得见儿子的儿子，直到四代。这样，约伯年纪老迈，日子满足而死。

===Original===
VIII

Structurally a great poem; historically a great poem; philosophically a great poem; so rendered for us in noble English diction as to be worthy in any comparison of diction, structure, ancestry, thought! Why should we not study it in our English School, if only for purpose of comparison? I conclude with these words of Lord Latymer:

===Chinese===
VIII

就结构说是一首伟大的诗；就历史说是一首伟大的诗；就哲学说是一首伟大的诗；又以高贵的英文辞采为我们译出，在辞采、结构、血统、思想的任何比较中都当得起！我们为什么不在我们的英文学院（School of English）里研读它呢——哪怕只为比较的目的？我以拉蒂默勋爵的这些话作结：

===Original===
> There is nothing comparable with it except the *Prometheus Bound* of Aeschylus. It is eternal, illimitable … its scope is the relation between God and Man. It is a vast liberation, a great gaol-delivery of the spirit of Man; nay, rather a great Acquittal.

===Chinese===
> 除埃斯库罗斯的《被缚的普罗米修斯》（Prometheus Bound）之外，无可与之相比。它是永恒的，无界无垠……它的范围是上帝与人之间的关系。这是一场浩大的解放，是人之灵的一场盛大的监狱开释（gaol-delivery）；不，毋宁说是一场盛大的开释无罪（Acquittal）。

## Lecture XI Of Selection / 第十一讲 论择书

===Original===
Wednesday, October 23, 1918

I

Let us hark back, Gentlemen, to our original problem, and consider if our dilatory way have led us to some glimpse of a practical solution.

We may restate it thus: Assuming it to be true, as men of Science assure us, that the weight of this planet remains constant, and is today what it was when mankind carelessly laid it on the shoulders of Atlas; that nothing abides but it goes, that nothing goes but in some form or other it comes back; you and I may well indulge a wonder what reflections upon this astonishing fact our university Librarian, Mr. Jenkinson, takes to bed with him. A copy of every book printed in the United Kingdom is—or I had better say, should be—deposited with him. Putting aside the question of what he has done to deserve it, he must surely wonder at times from what other corners of the earth Providence has been at pains to collect and compact the ingredients of the latest new volume he handles for a moment before fondly committing it to the cellars.

===Chinese===
1918 年 10 月 23 日，星期三

I

诸位先生，让我们回到最初的问题，看看我们这条迂缓的路，是否已把我们引向对一种切实可行的解决的惊鸿一瞥。

我们不妨把它重述如下：假定科学家的保证属实——这颗行星的重量恒定不变，今天仍是人类当初漫不经心把它搁上阿特拉斯（Atlas）肩头时的那个重量；凡存在者必逝去，凡逝去者又必以这样或那样的形式回来——那么你我尽可以纵情揣想：面对这一令人惊诧的事实，我们大学的图书馆长詹金森先生（Mr. Jenkinson）夜晚上床时，心里盘算的是些什么念头。凡在不列颠境内印行的书，每一本都有一份——或者我最好说，应当有一份——缴送到他那里存藏。且撇开他做了什么才配得上这桩差事不谈，他必定时常纳闷：天意是从地球上哪几个别的角落，费了怎样的辛苦，才收集、压紧成他手里这册最新书卷的原料——他把玩片刻，便一往情深地把它送进地窖。

===Original===
> “Locked up, not lost.”

Or, to take it in reverse—When the great library of Alexandria went up in flames, doubtless its ashes awoke an appreciable and almost immediate energy in the crops of the Nile Delta. The more leisurable process of desiccation, by which, under modern storage, the components of a modern novel are released to fresh unions and activities admits, as Sir Thomas Browne would say, a wide solution, and was just the question to tease that good man. Can we not hear him discussing it? “To be but pyramidally extant is a fallacy in duration. … To burn the bones of the King of Edom for lime seems no irrational ferity: but to store the back volumes of Mr. Bottomley’s *John Bull* a passionate prodigality.”

===Chinese===
> 「锁存，而非遗失。」

或者反过来想——当亚历山大里亚（Alexandria）的大图书馆腾起烈焰时，那堆灰烬无疑在尼罗河三角洲的庄稼地里唤起了一股可观而几乎立竿见影的地力。而在现代化的仓储之下，一部现代小说的诸种成分得以释放，去缔结新的结合、投入新的活动——那较为从容的枯干过程，正如托马斯·布朗爵士（Sir Thomas Browne）会说的，容得下一个宽泛的解答，而且恰恰是当年最能拨动那位好人、叫他不得安宁的问题。我们难道听不见他正讨论这个么？「仅仅以金字塔的形式存世，乃是时间中的一场谬见。……把以东王的骨头烧成石灰，看来算不得乖张的残暴；而囤积巴托姆利先生（Mr. Bottomley）《约翰牛》（*John Bull*）的过刊，却是一种狂热的挥霍。」

===Original===
II

Well, whatever the perplexities of our Library we may be sure they will never break down that tradition of service, help and courtesy which is, among its fine treasures, still the first. But we have seen that Mr. Jenkinson’s perplexities are really but a parable of ours: that the question, What are we to do with all these books accumulating in the world? really *is* a question: that their mere accumulation really *does* heap up against us a barrier of such enormous and brute mass that the stream of human culture must needs be choked and spread into marsh unless we contrive to pipe it through. That a great deal of it is meant to help—that even the most of it is well intentioned—avails not against the mere physical obstacle of its mass. If you consider an Athenian gentleman of the 5th century BC connecting (as I always preach here) his literature with his life, two things are bound to strike you: the first that he was a man of leisure, somewhat disdainful of trade and relieved of menial work by a number of slaves; the second, that he was surprisingly unencumbered with books. You will find in Plato much about reciters, actors, poets, rhetoricians, pleaders, sophists, public orators and refiners of language, but very little indeed about books. Even the library of Alexandria grew in a time of decadence and belonged to an age not his. Says Jowett in the end:

> He who approaches him in the most reverent spirit shall reap most of the fruit of his wisdom; he who reads him by the light of ancient commentators will have the least understanding of him.

> We see him [Jowett goes on] with the eye of the mind in the groves of the Academy, or on the banks of the Ilissus, or in the streets of Athens, alone or walking with Socrates, full of those thoughts which have since become the common possession of mankind. Or we may compare him to a statue hid away in some temple of Zeus or Apollo, no longer existing on earth, a statue which has a look as of the God himself. Or we may once more imagine him following in another state of being the great company of heaven which he beheld of old in a vision. So, (partly trifling but with a certain degree of seriousness,) we linger around the memory of a world which has passed away.

Yes, “which has passed away,” and perhaps with no token more evident of its decease than the sepulture of books that admiring generations have heaped on it!

===Chinese===
II

好吧，不论我们的图书馆有多少困惑，我们可以确信，它们永远压不垮那「服务、襄助与礼遇」的传统——在这馆中种种精美的宝藏之间，这一传统至今仍是头一件。但我们已经看到，詹金森先生的困惑其实只是一则关于我们自身的寓言：那个问题——世界上越积越多的这些书，我们拿它们怎么办？——实实在在「是」一个问题：它们的单纯堆积，实实在在在我们面前垒起一道障壁，其体量如此庞大、如此蛮横，若不设法用管道把它导流而过，人类文化的溪流就势必被堵死，漫溢成一片沼泽。这当中有大量书籍本意在于助人——甚至其中最大部分都出自好意——但在这体量那纯粹物理性的障碍面前，全然无济于事。设想你面前是一位公元前五世纪的雅典绅士，他（正如我在这台上素来宣讲的）把他的文学与他的生活连在一起，那么有两件事必然击中你：其一，他是个有闲的人，对营生略带轻蔑，家务贱役由一群奴隶替他代劳；其二，他身上的书累出奇地少。在柏拉图（Plato）笔下，你能找到许多关于诵诗人、演员、诗人、修辞学家、讼师、智术师、公众演说家和语言修饰家的谈论，关于书的，却实在寥寥。连亚历山大里亚图书馆，也是在一个衰颓的时代里长起来的，属于一个不属于他的时代。乔伊特（Jowett）在篇末说：

> 以最虔敬的精神走近他的人，将收获他智慧之果的大半；借着古代注家之光去读他的人，对他的理解将最少。

> 〔乔伊特接着说〕我们用心灵之眼看见他，在学园（Academy）的树荫里，在伊利索斯河（Ilissus）畔，在雅典的街市上，或独自一人，或与苏格拉底同行，满怀那些自此已成为人类共同财富的思想。我们也不妨把他比作一尊深藏于某座宙斯或阿波罗神庙中的雕像——那庙宇在世上已不复存在——而那雕像有着宛如神明本人的仪容。我们还可以再一次想象他，在另一种存在状态里，追随他当年在异象中得见的那支天上的伟大行列。就这样，（半是儿戏，却带着几分郑重，）我们围着一个已然逝去的世界之记忆流连。

是啊，「已然逝去」——而后世仰慕的一代代人堆积在它身上的那座书冢，或许正是它亡故的最显豁的凭证！

===Original===
III

In a previous lecture I referred you to the beautiful opening and the yet more beautiful close of the “Phaedrus.” Let us turn back and refresh ourselves with that Dialogue while we learn from it, in somewhat more of detail, just what a book meant to an Athenian: how fresh a thing it was to him and how little irksome.

Phaedrus has spent his forenoon listening to a discourse by the celebrated rhetorician Lysias on the subject of Love, and is starting to cool his head with a stroll beyond the walls of the city, when he encounters Socrates, who will not let him go until he has delivered up the speech with which Lysias regaled him, or, better still, the manuscript, “which I suspect you are carrying there in your left hand under your cloak.” So they bend their way beside Ilissus towards a tall plane tree, seen in the distance. Having reached it, they recline.

===Chinese===
III

在先前的一讲里，我曾请诸位留意《斐德罗篇》（“Phaedrus”）那美丽的开篇、以及更美丽的收尾。让我们折回去，就着那篇对话录恢复精神，同时从它那里、以稍详细一些的笔墨，弄清一本书对一位雅典人究竟意味着什么：它在他眼中是何等新鲜的一件物事，又是何等的不惹厌烦。

斐德罗（Phaedrus）消磨了一上午，听那位大名鼎鼎的修辞学家吕西阿斯（Lysias）讲论爱欲，此刻正要出城墙散步，让头脑凉爽凉爽，不想撞见苏格拉底（Socrates）；后者不放他走，除非他把吕西阿斯款待他的那篇讲辞交出来——或者更好，把抄本交出来，「我疑心那东西就揣在你左手边、斗篷底下」。于是两人沿着伊利索斯河畔，朝远处望见的一株高大的悬铃木走去。到了树荫下，便斜倚下来。

===Original===
> “By Hera,” says Socrates, “a fair resting-place, full of summer sounds and scents! This clearing, with the agnus castus in high bloom and fragrant, and the stream beneath the tree so gratefully cool to our feet! Judging from the ornaments and statues, I think this spot must be sacred to Acheloüs and the Nymphs. And the breeze, how deliciously charged with balm! and all summer’s murmur in the air, shrilled by the chorus of the grasshoppers! But the greatest charm is this knoll of turf—positively a pillow for the head. My dear Phaedrus, you have been a delectable guide.”

> “What an incomprehensible being you are, Socrates,” returns Phaedrus. “When you are in the country, as you say, you really are like some stranger led about by a guide. Upon my word, I doubt if you ever stray beyond the gates save by accident.”

> “Very true, my friend: and I hope you will forgive me for the reason—which is, that I love knowledge, and my teachers are the men who dwell in the city, not the trees or country scenes. Yet I do believe you have found a spell to draw me forth, like a hungry cow before whom a bough or a bunch of fruit is waved. For only hold up before me in like manner a book, and you may lead me all round Attica and over the wide world.”

So they recline and talk, looking aloft through that famous pure sky of Attica, mile upon mile transparent; and their discourse (preserved to us) is of Love, and seems to belong to that atmosphere, so clear it is and luminously profound. It ends with the cool of the day, and the two friends arise to depart. Socrates looks about him.

“Should we not, before going, offer up a prayer to these local deities?”

“By all means,” Phaedrus agrees.

===Chinese===
> 「以赫拉之名，」苏格拉底说，「这歇脚处真美，满是夏日的声响与芬芳！这片林间空地，穗花牡荆（agnus castus）开得正盛、香气袭人，树下的溪水浸脚，凉得叫人痛快！看这些陈设与雕像，我想这地方必是供奉阿刻罗俄斯（Acheloüs）与诸宁芙的圣所。再看这微风，馨香馥郁，多么宜人！满空中都是夏天的低语，被蚱蜢的合唱刺得又尖又亮！而最大的妙处是这一方草坡——简直就是给头颅预备的枕头。亲爱的斐德罗，你真是一位令人愉快的向导。」

> 「苏格拉底，你真是个让人摸不透的家伙，」斐德罗答道。「你到了乡下，正如你自己说的，活脱脱像个由向导牵着的异乡人。说老实话，除了纯属偶然，我疑心你从不曾信步走出城门。」

> 「你说得千真万确，我的朋友：至于缘由，还望你恕罪——那就是我热爱知识，而我的老师是住在城里的人，不是树木，不是乡野的景致。不过我确实相信，你找到了把我引出来的咒语，就像在一头饿牛面前摇动一根树枝、一串果子。因为只消照这样把一本书举到我面前，你就可以牵着我走遍阿提卡（Attica），走遍辽阔的世界。」

于是两人斜倚着倾谈，仰望穿过阿提卡那片著名的澄澈天空——一里又一里的透明；他们的谈话（留存至今）谈的是爱欲，而且看上去就属于那片大气——那大气何等清澈，何等明澈而深邃。谈话结束于昼间的凉意初起之时，两位朋友起身离去。苏格拉底环顾四周。

「走之前，我们不该向这地方的神明献上一段祷告吗？」

「当然要，」斐德罗应道。

===Original===
> *Socrates* (praying): “Beloved Pan, and all ye other gods who haunt this place, grant me beauty in the inward soul, and that the outward and inward may be at one! May I esteem the wise to be the rich; and may I myself have that quantity of gold which a temperate man, and he only, can carry. … Anything more? That prayer, I think, is enough for me.”

> *Phaedrus.* “Ask the same for me, Socrates. Friends, methinks, should have all things in common.”

> *Socrates.* “Amen, then. … Let us go.”

Here we have, as it seems to me, a marriage, without impediment, of wisdom and beauty between two minds that perforce have small acquaintance with books: and yet, with it, Socrates’ confession that anyone with a book under his cloak could lead him anywhere by the nose. So we see that Hellenic culture at its best was independent of book-learning, and yet craved for it.

===Chinese===
> *苏格拉底*（祷告）：「亲爱的潘神，以及诸位居于此地的其他神明，求你们赐我内在灵魂的美，好叫外在的与内在的合而为一！愿我尊智慧者为富有；愿我所有的黄金，不多不少，恰是一个有节制的人——也唯有有节制的人——所能携带的分量。……还有吗？我想，这番祷告于我已足够。」

> *斐德罗。*「苏格拉底，请替我也求同样的。朋友之间，我想，应当一切共享。」

> *苏格拉底。*「那么，阿门。……我们走吧。」

在我看来，这里有一场两心之间的智慧与美的联姻，全无阻碍——尽管这两颗心对书的相识必然有限；而与此同时，又有苏格拉底的自白：任何人只要斗篷底下夹着一本书，就能牵着他的鼻子走到任何地方。由此我们看到，希腊文化纵在其极盛之时也不依赖书本学问，却又渴求着书本学问。

===Original===
IV

When our own Literature awoke, taking its origin from the proud scholarship of the Renaissance, an Englishman who affected it was scarcely more cumbered with books than our Athenian had been, two thousand years before. It was, and it remained, aristocratic: sparingly expensive of its culture. It postulated, if not a slave population, at least a proletariat for which its blessings were not. No one thought of making a fortune by disseminating his work in print. Shakespeare never found it worth while to collect and publish his plays; and a very small sense of history will suffice to check our tears over the price received by Milton for *Paradise Lost*. We may wonder, indeed, at the time it took our forefathers to realise—or, at any rate, to employ—the energy that lay in the printing-press. For centuries after its invention mere copying commanded far higher prices than authorship.13 Writers gave “authorised” editions to the world sometimes for the sake of fame, often to justify themselves against piratical publishers, seldom in expectation of monetary profit. Listen, for example, to Sir Thomas Browne’s excuse for publishing *Religio Medici* (1643):

===Chinese===
IV

等到我们自己的文学醒转过来，从文艺复兴那骄傲的学术中发端时，一位爱好文学的英国人，比起两千年前的雅典人来，身上也多不了几本书的重负。这种文化当年是贵族的，此后也一直是：它开销起自身的教养来极为吝啬。它纵不以奴隶人口为前提，至少也以一个无产者阶级为前提——它的种种福分，原不是为了这个阶级预备的。谁也不曾想过靠印行自己的作品来发财。莎士比亚（Shakespeare）从来没觉得收集、出版他的剧本值得一为；而一点点历史常识，就足以叫我们止住为弥尔顿（Milton）出让《失乐园》（*Paradise Lost*）所得的那笔钱而洒的眼泪。说实在的，我们的先辈竟费了那么久的时光才认识到——或者至少说，才动用起——印刷机中蕴藏的能量，这倒真令人诧异。印刷术发明之后的几个世纪里，单是抄写一项，索价便远高于著述。¹³ 作家们把「授权」的版本交给世界，有时为博声名，常见为向盗印的书商自辩，极少指望金钱上的获利。比如，请听托马斯·布朗爵士为出版《医生的宗教信仰》（*Religio Medici*）（1643）所作的辩解：

===Original===
> Had not almost every man suffered by the press or were not the tyranny thereof become universal, I had not wanted reason for complaint: but in times wherein I have lived to behold the highest perversion of that excellent invention, the name of his Majesty defamed, the honour of Parliament depraved, the writings of both depravedly, anticipatively, counterfeitly imprinted; complaints may seem ridiculous in private persons; and men of my condition may be as incapable of affronts, as hopeless of their reparations. And truly had not the duty I owe unto the importunity of friends, and the allegiance I must ever acknowledge unto truth, prevailed with me; the inactivity of my disposition might have made these sufferings continual, and time that brings other things to light, should have satisfied me in the remedy of its oblivion. But because things evidently false are not only printed, but many things of truth most falsely set forth, in this latter I could not but think myself engaged. For though we have no power to redress the former, yet in the other, the reparation being within our selves, I have at present represented unto the world a full and intended copy of that piece, which was most imperfectly and surreptitiously published before.

> This I confess, about seven years past, with some others of affinity thereto, for my private exercise and satisfaction, I had at leisurable hours composed; which being communicated unto one, it became common unto many, and was by transcription successively corrupted, until it arrived in a most depraved copy at the press…14

===Chinese===
> 倘若不是几乎人人都吃过印刷的亏，倘若它的暴虐尚未成为普遍的灾殃，我原不缺抱怨的理由；但我生逢其时，亲眼得见那一项绝妙发明的极度败坏：陛下的名讳遭人污蔑，国会的尊严横遭玷辱，双方的文字都被人以歪曲的、抢先的、伪造的方式印行；在这等年月，私人的抱怨未免显得可笑；而我这等处境的人，对冒犯可以无动于衷，对昭雪也可以不抱希望。说实在的，若不是朋友们苦苦相劝、我理当领情的那份情义，连同我永远必须向真理承认的那份效忠，一并占了上风；以我天性的好静，这本可以成为一场无休无止的忍受，而时间——它终会使别的事物现出光来——原也该用遗忘这一剂药，打发得我称心满意。但既然显而易见的谬妄不仅有人刊印，连许多真实的文字也被极度歪曲地面世，这后一桩，我不能不认为自己与此攸关。因为前者我们虽无力挽回，后者——昭雪之权既在我自己手中——我便趁此时把那部作品的一份完整而合乎本意的抄本呈献于世：此前它被人以极不完整、私自偷运的方式出版过。

> 这篇文字我承认，是大约七年之前，连同另外几篇性质相类的，为我一己的修习与自娱，在闲散的时辰里写成的；它既传示于一人，便流布于众人，又在一次次转录中不断走样，直到以一份讹误至极的抄本落进印坊……¹⁴

===Original===
V

The men of the 18th century maintained the old tradition of literary exclusiveness, but in a somewhat different way and more consciously.

I find, Gentlemen, when you read with me in private, that nine out of ten of you dislike the 18th century and all its literary works. As for the Women students, they one and all abominate it. You do not, I regret to say, provide me with reasons much more philosophical than the epigrammatist’s for disliking Doctor Fell. May one whose time of life excuses perhaps a detachment from passion attempt to provide you with one? If so, first listen to this from Mr. and Mrs. Hammond’s book *The Village Labourer*, 1760–1832:

===Chinese===
V

十八世纪的人们守住了文学独占的旧传统，只是路数稍有不同，而且更出于自觉。

我发现，诸位先生，你们私下里同我读书时，十有八九不喜欢十八世纪及其全部文学作品。至于女学生，她们无一例外地憎恶它。我很遗憾地说，你们向我提供的理由，比那位打油诗人不喜欢费尔博士（Doctor Fell）的理由，哲学不了多少。可否容许一个到了这般年纪、大可以说已经超脱了激情的人，试着给你们提供一个理由？若可，请先听哈蒙德夫妇（Mr. and Mrs. Hammond）的书《乡村劳工》（*The Village Labourer*），1760–1832 中的一段：

===Original===
> A row of 18th century houses, or a room of normal 18th century furniture, or a characteristic piece of 18th century literature, conveys at once a sensation of satisfaction and completeness. The secret of this charm is not to be found in any special beauty or nobility of design or expression, but simply in an exquisite fitness. The 18th century mind was a unity, an order. All literature and art that really belong to the 18th century are the language of a little society of men and women who moved within one set of ideas; who understood each other; who were not tormented by any anxious or bewildering problems; who lived in comfort, and, above all things, in composure. The classics were their freemasonry. There was a standard for the mind, for the emotions, for taste: there were no incongruities.

> When you have a society like this, you have what we roughly call a civilisation, and it leaves its character and canons in all its surroundings and in its literature. Its definite ideas lend themselves readily to expression. A larger society seems an anarchy in contrast: just because of its escape into a greater world it seems powerless to stamp itself in wood or stone; it is condemned as an age of chaos and mutiny, with nothing to declare.

===Chinese===
> 一排十八世纪的房屋，或一间陈设着寻常十八世纪家具的房间，或一件有代表性的十八世纪文学作品，都立即传达出一种满足而圆整的感觉。这种魅力的秘密，并不在任何设计或表达的独特之美或高贵之中，而仅仅在于一种精妙的合度。十八世纪的心灵是一个统一体，一种秩序。一切真正属于十八世纪的文学与艺术，都是一个小小的男女社会的语言：这群人在同一套观念之内活动；他们彼此了解；没有任何焦灼或令人迷惘的问题折磨他们；他们活得安适，而超乎一切之上，活得从容。古典作品是他们的通行暗语。心智有标准，情感有标准，趣味有标准：没有不相称的东西。

> 当你拥有这样一个社会，你便拥有我们大而化之所谓的一种文明；它把自己的性格与法度留在四周的一切事物上，也留在它的文学里。它那些明确的观念，自身便宜于表达。一个更大的社会与之相比显得像一场无政府：恰恰因为它逃逸进一个更广阔的世界，它便似乎无力把自己钤印在木头或石头上；它被判定是一个混沌与变乱的时代，没有什么可资宣告。

===Original===
You do wrong, I assure you, in misprising these men of the 18th century. They reduced life, to be sure: but by that very means they saw it far more *completely* than do we, in this lyrical age, with our worship of “fine excess.” Here at any rate, and to speak only of its literature, you have a society fencing that literature around—I do not say by forethought or even consciously—but in effect fencing its literature around, to keep it in control and capable of an orderly, a nice, even an exquisite cultivation. Dislike it as you may, I do not think that any of you, as he increases his knowledge of the technique of English Prose, yes, and of English Verse (I do not say of English Poetry) will deny his admiration to the men of the 18th century. The strength of good prose resides not so much in the swing and balance of the single sentence as in the marshalling of argument, the orderly procession of paragraphs, the disposition of parts so that each finds its telling, its proper, place; the adjustment of the means to the end; the strategy which brings its full force into action at the calculated moment and drives the conclusion home upon an accumulated sense of *justice*. I do not see how any student of 18th century literature can deny its writers—Berkeley or Hume or Gibbon—Congreve or Sheridan—Pope or Cowper—Addison or Steele or Johnson—Burke or Chatham or Thomas Paine—their meed for this, or, if he be an artist, even his homage.

But it remains true, as your instinct tells you, and as I have admitted, that they achieved all this by help of narrow and artificial boundaries. Of several fatal exclusions let me name but two.

In the first place, they excluded the Poor; imitating in a late age the Athenian tradition of a small polite society resting on a large and degraded one. Throughout the 18th century—and the great Whig families were at least as much to blame for this as the Tories—by enclosure of commons, by grants, by handling of the franchise, by taxation, by poor laws in result punitive though intended to be palliative, the English peasantry underwent a steady process of degradation into serfdom: into a serfdom which, during the first twenty years of the next century, hung constantly and precariously on the edge of actual starvation. The whole theory of culture worked upon a principle of double restriction; of restricting on the one hand the realm of polite knowledge to propositions suitable for a scholar and a gentleman, and, on the other, the numbers of the human family permitted to be either. The theory deprecated enthusiasm, as it discountenanced all ambition in a poor child to rise above what Sir Spencer Walpole called “his inevitable and hereditary lot”—to soften which and make him acquiescent in it was, with a Wilberforce or a Hannah More, the last dream of restless benevolence.

===Chinese===
我向你们担保，你们轻看这些十八世纪的人，是看错了。诚然，他们把生活删削了：可恰恰借此，他们看得比我们远为「完整」——我们是活在一个抒情的时代，膜拜着「精致的过火」（fine excess）。单就这里而言，而且只就它的文学而言，你们看到的是一个社会把自己的文学圈围起来——我不是说出于深谋远虑，甚至不是出于有意——而是事实上圈围起来，好让它受控，可以有条不紊地、精细地、乃至精妙绝伦地加以耕种。随你们怎么不喜欢它，我相信你们中间没有一个人，随着他对英文散文技巧——是的，还有英文韵文技巧（我不是说英文诗歌）——的认识日渐加深，会拒绝把他的钦佩交给十八世纪的人们。好散文的力量，与其说在单个句子的摇曳与平衡，不如说在论点的整军布阵，段落的井然行进，各部分的部署——使每一部分都找到它切实的、恰当的位置；手段对目的的调适；还有那战略：在算准的时刻投入全部力量，并借着层层积聚的「公道」之感，把结论一举钉牢。我看不出，任何一个研究十八世纪文学的学生，怎能拒绝就这一点向它的作家们——贝克莱（Berkeley）或休谟（Hume）或吉本（Gibbon）——康格里夫（Congreve）或谢里丹（Sheridan）——蒲柏（Pope）或柯珀（Cowper）——艾迪生（Addison）或斯梯尔（Steele）或约翰逊（Johnson）——伯克（Burke）或查塔姆（Chatham）或托马斯·潘恩（Thomas Paine）——致以应得的报偿；倘若他本人是艺术家，甚至致以他的顶礼。

但有一点依然属实，正如你们的直觉告诉你们的，也正如我已承认的：这一切他们是借助狭窄而人为的疆界达成的。在若干个致命的排除之中，请容我只举两桩。

首先，他们把穷人排除在外；在一个晚近的时代里，仿效雅典的传统——一个小而文雅的社会，压在一个庞大而堕落的社团之上。纵贯十八世纪——在这件事上，辉格党的大世家与托利党难辞其咎，甚或尤甚——通过圈占公地，通过特许授田，通过操弄选举权，通过征税，通过那些本意在缓和、结果却是惩罚性的济贫法，英国的农家经历了一场稳步沦为农奴的过程：沦为一种农奴制——在下一世纪的头二十年里，它始终摇摇欲坠地悬在真实饥馑的边缘。那整套教养理论，运作于一条双重限制的原则之上：一方面把文雅知识的疆域限制在适合学者与君子的命题之内，另一方面，把获准为学者、或为君子的人类家庭成员的数目限制起来。这理论贬抑热情，一如它不赞许一个穷孩子的一切抱负——不甘于斯宾塞·沃尔波尔爵士（Sir Spencer Walpole）所谓「他那无可避免的、世袭的命分」的抱负；而对威尔伯福斯（Wilberforce）或汉娜·莫尔（Hannah More）来说，软化这道命分、叫孩子默认它，正是那颗不得安宁的仁心最后的好梦。

===Original===
VI

Also these 18th century men fenced off the whole of our own Middle English and medieval literature—fenced off Chaucer and Dunbar, Malory and Berners—as barbarous and “Gothic.” They treated these writers with little more consideration than Boileau had thought it worth while to bestow on Villon or on Ronsard—*enfin Malherbe*! As for Anglo-Saxon literature, one may, safely say that, save by Gray and a very few others, its existence was barely surmised.

You may or may not find it harder to forgive them that they ruled out moreover a great part of the literature of the preceding century as offensive to urbane taste, or as they would say, “disgusting.” They disliked it mainly, one suspects, as one age revolts from the fashion of another—as some of you, for example, revolt from the broad plenty of Dickens (Heaven forgive you) or the ornament of Tennyson. Some of the great writers of that age definitely excluded God from their scheme of things: others included God fiercely, but with circumscription and limitation. I think it fair to say of them generally that they hated alike the mystical and the mysterious, and, hating these, could have little commerce with such poetry as Crashaw’s and Vaughan’s or such speculation as gave ardour to the prose of the Cambridge Platonists. Johnson’s famous attack, in his *Life of Cowley*, upon the metaphysical followers of Donne ostensibly assails their literary conceits, but truly and at bottom rests its quarrel against an attitude of mind, in respect of which he lived far enough removed to be unsympathetic yet near enough to take denunciation for a duty. Johnson, to put it vulgarly, had as little use for Vaughan’s notion of poetry as he would have had for Shelley’s claim that it

===Chinese===
VI

再者，这些十八世纪的人还把我们整个中古英语文学与中世纪文学圈挡在外——把乔叟（Chaucer）、邓巴（Dunbar）、马洛礼（Malory）、伯纳斯（Berners）都圈挡在外——斥为野蛮与「哥特」。他们对待这些作家，并不比布瓦洛（Boileau）认为值得施于维庸（Villon）或龙萨（Ronsard）的多一点体贴——*enfin Malherbe*（马莱伯终于是马莱伯）！至于盎格鲁-撒克逊文学，我们尽可以有把握地说：除格雷（Gray）与极少数人之外，世人几乎猜想不到它的存在。

此外，他们还把上一个世纪文学的一大半排斥在外，说它冒犯了文雅的趣味，或者照他们的说法，「令人作呕」。这一点，你们是否觉得更难宽宥他们，那就见仁见智了。依我猜想，他们不喜欢它，主要就像一个时代厌弃另一个时代的时尚——比如你们中间的某些人，厌弃狄更斯（Dickens）的宽宏丰腴（上天宽恕你们），或丁尼生（Tennyson）的藻饰。那个时代的某些大作家，断然把上帝排除出他们的世界格局：另一些则激烈地把上帝包括进来，却是带着限定与制约。我想可以公道地总说一句：他们同等地憎恶神秘主义与幽玄难解之物；既憎恶这些，便与克拉肖（Crashaw）与沃恩（Vaughan）那样的诗歌、或曾给剑桥柏拉图学派（Cambridge Platonists）的散文注入热忱的那类玄思，少有往来。约翰逊（Johnson）在《柯莱传》（*Life of Cowley*）里对多恩（Donne）那些玄学派后继者的著名攻伐，表面上抨击的是他们的文学奇喻，实质上、根本上，它把怨气记在一种心态的名下；对这种心态，他生得够远，隔膜无感，却又生得够近，把声讨当成了本分。说得粗俗些，沃恩关于诗的那套见解，约翰逊用不上，正如他也用不上雪莱（Shelley）的那个主张——诗

===Original===
> feeds on the aëreal kisses

> Of shapes that haunt thought’s wildernesses,

and we have only to set ourselves back in Shelley’s age and read (say) the verse of Frere and Canning in *The Anti-Jacobin*, to understand how frantic a lyrist—let be how frantic a political figure—Shelley must have appeared to well-regulated minds.

===Chinese===
> 啜饮空中的亲吻，

> 那亲吻出自徘徊于思想荒野的诸般形体，

而我们只消把自己挪回雪莱的时代，去读（比如说）《反雅各宾报》（*The Anti-Jacobin*）上弗里尔（Frere）与坎宁（Canning）的诗句，便能明白：在守规矩的心智看来，雪莱该是一位何等癫狂的抒情诗人——更遑论一个何等癫狂的政治人物。

===Original===
VII

All this literature which our forefathers excluded has come back upon us: and concurrently we have to deal with the more serious difficulty (let us give thanks for it) of a multitude of millions insurgent to handsel their long-deferred heritage. I shall waste no time in arguing that we ought not to wish to withhold it, because we cannot if we would. And thus the problem becomes a double one, of *distribution* as well as of *selection*.

Now in the first place I submit that this *distribution* should be free: which implies that our *selection* must be confined to books and methods of teaching. There must be no picking and choosing among the recipients, no appropriation of certain forms of culture to certain “stations of life” with a tendency, conscious or unconscious, to keep those stations as stationary as possible.

===Chinese===
VII

我们先辈排除在外的这一切文学，已经回流到我们身上：与此同时，我们还须对付一个更严重的难题（让我们为此感谢天）——千百万大众正奋起要求开启他们被长期延宕的遗产。我不打算浪费时间去论证我们不该想扣住它不发，因为纵然想扣，我们也办不到。于是，问题成了双重的：既在于「分发」（*distribution*），也在于「拣择」（*selection*）。

首先，我主张这「分发」应当是自由的：这意味着我们的「拣择」必须只限于书籍与教学方法。在接受者中间不许挑挑拣拣，不许把某些形式的文化圈划给某些「生活等次」（stations of life），还不许——有意也罢，无意也罢——存着让那些等次尽量原地不动的倾向。

===Original===
Merely by clearing our purpose to this extent we shall have made no inconsiderable advance. For even the last century never quite got rid of its predecessor’s fixed idea that certain degrees of culture were appropriate to certain stations of life. With what gentle persistence it prevails, for example, in Jane Austen’s novels; with what complacent rhetoric in Tennyson (and in spite of Lady Clara Vere de Vere)! Let me remind you that by allowing an idea to take hold of our animosity we may be as truly “possessed” by it as though it claimed our allegiance. The notion that culture may be drilled to march in step with a trade or calling endured through the Victorian age of competition and possessed the mind not only of Samuel Smiles who taught by instances how a bright and industrious boy might earn money and lift himself out of his “station,” but of Ruskin himself, who in the first half of *Sesame and Lilies*, in the lecture “Of Kings’ Treasuries,” discussing the choice of books, starts vehemently and proceeds at length to denounce the prevalent passion for self-advancement—of rising above one’s station in life—quite as if it were the most important thing, willy-nilly, in talking of the choice of books. Which means that, to Ruskin, just then, it was the most formidable obstacle. Can we, at this time of day, do better by simply turning the notion out of doors? Yes, I believe that we can: and upon this credo:

*I believe that while it may grow—and grow infinitely—with increase of learning, the grace of a liberal education, like the grace of Christianity, is so catholic a thing—so absolutely above being trafficked, retailed, apportioned, among “stations in life”—that the humblest child may claim it by indefeasible right, having a soul.*

*Further, I believe that Humanism is, or should he, no decorative appanage, purchased late in the process of education, within the means of a few: but a quality, rather, which should, and can, condition all teaching, from a child’s first lesson in Reading: that its unmistakable hallmark can be impressed upon the earliest task set in an elementary school.*

===Chinese===
单是把我们的宗旨澄清到这个地步，我们就已是一个不小的进步。因为纵使上一世纪，也从未完全摆脱其前辈的那个定见：某种程度的教养，只与某种生活等次相配。它以何等温柔的执念，弥漫于简·奥斯汀（Jane Austen）的小说里；又以何等自鸣得意的修辞，流贯于丁尼生笔下（纵然有《克拉拉·维尔·德·维尔小姐》〔Lady Clara Vere de Vere〕那首诗在）！请容我提醒一句：任凭一个念头攫住我们的敌意，我们照样可能被它真正「附体」，一如它要求我们效忠。那种认为教养可以像操练新兵一样，教它跟某个行当或职业齐步走的观念，熬过了维多利亚时代的竞争岁月，不但附住了塞缪尔·斯迈尔斯（Samuel Smiles）的心思——他拿实例开课，教一个聪明勤快的男孩如何挣钱、如何把自己从他的「等次」里拔出来——而且附住了罗斯金（Ruskin）本人的心思：《芝麻与百合》（*Sesame and Lilies*）的前半篇，那篇《国王的宝库》（“Of Kings’ Treasuries”）的讲演，讨论择书之道，起手便慷慨激昂，长篇大论地声讨风靡一时的自我进阶热——爬出自己生活等次的热——仿佛谈择书时，不论你情不情愿，这才是顶要紧的事。这意味着，对彼时彼刻的罗斯金来说，它正是最可怕的障碍。到了今天这个时候，我们能不能做得更好，干脆把这观念扫地出门？能，我相信我们能：而且凭的是这样一篇信经：

*我相信：自由教育的恩典，固然可以随学问的长进而增长——并且无限地增长——却像基督教的恩典一样，是一件如此普泛的物事——断然高出于在诸「生活等次」之间被贩卖、零售、配给之上——以至最卑微的孩子，只要有一个灵魂，便可凭不可剥夺的权利认领它。*

*再者，我相信人文主义（Humanism）不是——或不应是——一种装饰性的附属物，须在教育的末段才购置得起、而且只有少数人购置得起；它毋宁是一种品质，应当、并且能够统摄一切教学，从孩子的识字第一课起：它那无可错认的印记，可以打在小学布置的最初课业上。*

===Original===
VIII

I am not preaching red Radicalism in this: I am not telling you that Jack is as good as his master: if he were, he would be a great deal better; for he would understand Homer (say) as well as his master, the child of parents who could afford to have him taught Greek. As Greek is commonly taught, I regret to say, whether they have learnt it or not makes a distressingly small difference to most boys’ appreciation of Homer. Still it does make a vast difference to some, and should make a vast difference to all. And yet, if you will read the passage in Kinglake’s *Eöthen* in which he tells—in words that find their echo in many a reader’s memory—of his boyish passion for Homer—and if you will note that the boy imbibed his passion, after all, through the conduit of Pope’s translation—you will acknowledge that, for the human boy, admission to much of the glory of Homer’s realm does not depend upon such mastery as a boy of fifteen or sixteen possesses over the original. But let me quote you a few sentences:

===Chinese===
VIII

我在这番话里鼓吹的并不是赤色激进主义：我不是在对你们说杰克与他的主人一样好：倘若当真一样好，他要好得多；因为他会（比如说）同他的主人一样懂得荷马——主人呢，是那对父母养得起、请得起人教他希腊文的孩子。我遗憾地说，照希腊文通常的那种教法，学过没学过，对多数孩子欣赏荷马所造成的差别，小得叫人丧气。尽管如此，它对某些孩子确实造成了天壤之别，而且本应对所有孩子都造成天壤之别。然而，你们若肯去读金莱克（Kinglake）《东方的踪迹》（*Eöthen*）里那段文字——他讲自己少年时对荷马的热恋，那字句在多少读者的记忆里有过回响——你们若再留意到：这孩子那腔热恋，说到底原是经蒲柏（Pope）译本这道管道吸进去的——你们便会承认，对一个人类的孩子来说，走进荷马王国的许多荣光，并不取决于十五六岁的孩子对原文所能有的那种掌握。且让我引你们听几句：

===Original===
> I, too, loved Homer, but not with a scholar’s love. The most humble and pious among women was yet so proud a mother that she could teach her firstborn son no Watts’s hymns, no collects for the day; she could teach him in earliest childhood no less than this—to find a home in his saddle, and to love old Homer, and all that old Homer sung. True it is, that the Greek was ingeniously rendered into English, the English of Pope even, but not even a mesh like that can screen an earnest child from the fire of Homer’s battles.

> I pored over the *Odyssey* as over a storybook, hoping and fearing for the hero whom yet I partly scorned. But the *Iliad*—line by line I clasped it to my brain with reverence as well as with love. …

> The impatient child is not grubbing for beauties, but pushing the siege; the women vex him with their delays, and their talking… but all the while that he thus chafes at the pausing of the action, the strong vertical light of Homer’s poetry is blazing so full upon the people and things of the *Iliad*, that soon to the eyes of the child they grow familiar as his mother’s shawl. …

> It was not the recollection of school nor college learning, but the rapturous and earnest reading of my childhood, which made me bend forward so longingly to the plains of Troy.

===Chinese===
> 我也爱荷马，但不是以一个学者的方式爱他。妇人之中最谦卑、最虔诚的一位，却又是那样一位心气骄傲的母亲：她不能教长子瓦茨的赞美诗，不能教他当日的祝文；她在孩子最早的年岁里所能教的，不下于此——在马鞍上安一个家，爱老荷马，爱老荷马歌唱过的一切。诚然，那希腊文被巧妙地移译成了英文，甚至是蒲柏的英文；可连这样一层罗网，也遮拦不住一个热切的孩子，挡不住荷马战火的灼烤。

> 我伏在《奥德赛》（*Odyssey*）上，像伏在一部故事书上，为那位我半带鄙夷的英雄时而企望、时而惊惧。可《伊利亚特》（*Iliad*）——我一字一行地把它捧进脑髓，怀着敬，也怀着爱。……

> 这性急的孩子并不是在刨寻妙语美景，而是在猛攻城池；那些女人的拖延、她们的絮语，惹他心烦……然而，正当他这样为行动的停滞焦躁之际，荷马诗中那道强烈的垂直光，正满蓬蓬地照着《伊利亚特》里的人物与事物，要不了多久，在孩子眼里，它们就熟悉得像他母亲的披肩了。……

> 使我如此热望地俯向特洛伊平原的，并不是对学堂或大学课业的追忆，而是我童年那如痴如醉、又恳切认真的阅读。

===Original===
IX

It is among the books then, and not among the readers, that we must do our selecting. But how? On what principle or principles?

Sometime in the days of my youth, a newspaper, *The Pall Mall Gazette*, then conducted by W. T. Stead, made a conscientious effort to solve the riddle by inviting a number of eminent men to compile lists of the Hundred Best Books. Now this invitation rested on a fallacy. Considering for a moment how personal a thing is Literature, you will promptly assure yourselves that there is—there can be—no such thing as the Hundred Best Books. If you yet incline to toy with the notion, carry it on and compile a list of the Hundred Second-best Books: nay, if you will, continue until you find yourself solemnly, with a brow corrugated by responsibility, weighing the claims (say) of Velleius Paterculus, Paul and Virginia and Mr. Jorrocks to admission among the Hundred Tenth-best Books. There is, in fact no positive hierarchy among the classics. You cannot appraise the worth of Charles Lamb against the worth of Casaubon: the worth of Hesiod against the worth of Madame de Sévigné: the worth of Théophile Gautier against the worth of Dante or Thomas Hobbes or Macchiavelli or Jane Austen. They all wrote with pens, in ink, upon paper: but you no sooner pass beyond these resemblances than your comparison finds itself working in impari materia.

Also why should the Best Books be one hundred in number, rather than ninety-nine or one hundred and ninety-nine? And under what conditions is a book a Best Book? There are moods in which we not only prefer Pickwick to the Rig-Vedas or Sakuntalà, but find that it does us more good. In our day again I pay all respect to Messrs. Dent’s Everyman’s Library. It was a large conception vigorously planned. But, in the nature of things, Everyman is going to arrive at a point beyond which he will find it more and more difficult to recognise himself: at a point, let us say, when Everyman, opening a new parcel, starts to doubt if, after all, it wouldn’t be money in his pocket to be Somebody Else.

===Chinese===
IX

那么，我们的拣择必须在书中间做，而不是在读者中间做。可是怎么做？依一条什么原则，或哪几条原则？

在我年轻岁月里的某一天，一家报纸——当时由 W. T. 斯特德（W. T. Stead）主持的《帕尔马尔公报》（*The Pall Mall Gazette*）——曾凭良心下过一番功夫去解这个谜：它邀请一批显赫人物，编拟「百部最佳书」的书单。而这份邀请建在一个谬见之上。只要稍稍想一想文学是一件何等个人化的东西，你们立刻就会让自己确信：所谓百部最佳书——这样东西并不存在，也不可能存在。倘若你们还想拿这个念头把玩一番，那就顺手接下去，编一份「百部次佳书」的书单；再不然，索性一直编下去，直到你们发现自己一脸庄严，眉心让责任心拧出了沟，正在掂量（比如说）维莱伊乌斯·帕特尔库鲁斯（Velleius Paterculus）、《保罗与薇吉妮》（*Paul and Virginia*）和乔罗克斯先生（Mr. Jorrocks）跻身「百部第十佳书」的资格。实际上，经典之中并没有一个实有的等第体系。你无法拿查尔斯·兰姆（Charles Lamb）的价值去对勘卡索邦（Casaubon）的价值：无法拿赫西俄德（Hesiod）的价值去对勘塞维尼夫人（Madame de Sévigné）的价值：无法拿泰奥菲尔·戈蒂耶（Théophile Gautier）的价值去对勘但丁（Dante）、托马斯·霍布斯（Thomas Hobbes）、马基雅维利（Macchiavelli）或简·奥斯汀（Jane Austen）的价值。他们全都用笔、蘸着墨、在纸上写作：可你一越过这些相似之点，你的比较便发觉自己是在不对等的材料（in impari materia）上行事了。

再说，最佳书凭什么非得是一百部这个数目，而不是九十九部，或一百九十九部？又在什么条件之下，一部书才算得一部最佳书？有些心境之中，我们不但更喜欢《匹克威克》（*Pickwick*）胜过《梨俱吠陀》（Rig-Vedas）或《沙恭达罗》（Sakuntalà），而且发现它于我们更有补益。到了我们这个时代，我对登特书局（Messrs. Dent）的「人人丛书」（Everyman’s Library）同样满怀敬意。那是一个宏大的构想，规划得也雷厉风行。但按事物的本性，「人人」（Everyman）终会走到某一点，越过这一点，他要认出自己便越来越难：走到某一点——不妨这么说——「人人」拆开一包新书时，开始起疑：归根结底，去做「别的某个人」（Somebody Else），口袋里岂不更进一笔？

===Original===
X

And yet, may be, *The Pall Mall Gazette* was on the right scent. For it was in search of masterpieces: and, however we teach, our trust will in the end repose upon masterpieces, upon the great classics of whatever Language or Literature we are handling: and these, in any language are neither enormous in number and mass, nor extraordinarily difficult to detect, nor (best of all) forbidding to the reader by reason of their own difficulty. Upon a selected few of these—even upon three, or two, or one—we may teach at least a surmise of the true delight, and may be some measure of taste whereby our pupil will, by an inner guide, be warned to choose the better and reject the worse when we turn him loose to read for himself.

To this use of masterpieces I shall devote my final lecture.

===Chinese===
X

然而，说不定，《帕尔马尔公报》倒嗅对了踪迹。因为它搜寻的是杰作：而不论我们怎样教，我们最终的信托终要安放在杰作上——安放在我们手中的无论哪种语言、哪种文学的伟大经典上：而这类东西，无论在哪种语言里，都既不数量浩繁、卷帙庞大，也不特别难于辨认，更（顶好的一点）不因其自身的难度而令读者望而却步。就从其中拣定的寥寥几部入手——哪怕三部、两部，乃至一部——我们至少能教给学生一种对真正喜悦的揣想，或许还有几分趣味的分寸；凭着这个内里的向导，等我们放手让他自己去读时，他会受其警醒：择其善者，弃其劣者。

杰作的这一用途，我将留待最后一讲来专门讨论。

## Lecture XII On the Use of Masterpieces / 第十二讲 论经典的使用

===Original===
Wednesday, November 6, 1918

I

===Chinese===
1918 年 11 月 6 日，星期三

I

===Original===
I do not think, Gentlemen, that we need to bother ourselves today with any definition of a “classic,” or of the stigmata by which a true classic can be recognised. Sainte-Beuve once indicated these in a famous discourse, “Qu’est-ce qu’un classique”: and it may suffice us that these include Universality and Permanence. Your true classic is *universal*, in that it appeals to the catholic mind of man. It is doubly *permanent*: for it remains significant, or acquires a new significance, after the age for which it was written and the conditions under which it was written, have passed away; and it yet keeps, undefaced by handling, the original noble imprint of the mind that first minted it—or shall we say that, as generation after generation rings the coin, it ever returns the echo of its father-spirit?

But for our purpose it suffices that in our literature we possess a number of works to which the title of classic cannot be refused. So let us confine ourselves to these, and to the question, How to use them?

===Chinese===
诸位先生，我想我们今天不必为「经典」（classic）的定义费神，也不必去考究那些用以辨认真经典的印记。圣伯夫（Sainte-Beuve）曾在那篇著名的讲演《何谓经典》（Qu’est-ce qu’un classique）里一一指陈过这些印记；而我们只消知道其中包含普遍性（Universality）与恒久性（Permanence），便已足够。真正的经典是*普遍的*（universal），因为它诉诸人类普遍共通的心灵。它又是双重的*恒久的*（permanent）：因为在为之写作的时代与写作的条件都已消逝之后，它依然有其意义，或者获得新的意义；并且，纵经摩挲把玩，它仍不失其当初铸币的心灵所打下的高贵原印——或者我们不妨说：一代又一代的人流转使用这枚钱币，它总归还响着其父本精神的回声？

但就我们的目的而言，只消这一点便够了：我们的文学里拥有若干容不得拒绝「经典」之名的作品。那么，就让我们把自己限定在这些作品上，限定在这个问题上：如何使用它们？

===Original===
Well, to begin with, I revert to a point which I tried to establish in my first lecture; and insist with all my strength that the first obligation we owe to any classic, and to those whom we teach, and to ourselves, is to treat it *absolutely*: not for any secondary or derivative purpose, or purpose recommended as useful by any manual: but at first solely to interpret the meaning which its author intended: that in short we should *trust* any given masterpiece for its operation, on ourselves and on others. In that first lecture I quoted to you this most wise sentence:

> That all spirit is mutually attractive, as all matter is mutually attractive, is an ultimate fact,

and consenting to this with all my heart I say that it matters very little for the moment, or even for a considerable while, that a pupil does not perfectly, or even nearly, understand all he reads, provided we can get the attraction to seize upon him. He and the author between them will do the rest: our function is to communicate and trust. In what other way do children take the ineffaceable stamp of a gentle nurture than by daily attraction to whatsoever is beautiful and amiable and dignified in their home? As there, so in their reading, the process must be gradual of acquiring an inbred monitor to reject the evil and choose the good. For it is the property of masterpieces that they not only raise you to

===Chinese===
那么，首先，我要回到我在第一讲里力图确立的一点上，并竭尽全力地主张：我们对任何一部经典、对我们所教的人、也对我们自己所负的头一项义务，是*绝对地*（absolutely）对待它：不是为任何次要的、派生的目的，也不是为任何手册举荐为有用的目的；而是首先只为解释作者原意所指的意义：一句话，我们应当*信任*（trust）任何一部杰作自身的运作——它在我们身上、也在他人身上的运作。在那第一讲里，我曾引给你们听这句极明智的话：

> 一切精神相互吸引，正如一切物质相互吸引，这是一个终极的事实，

我由衷赞同这句话，并且要说：学生不能完全领会——甚至近于领会——他所读的一切，这一时无关紧要，甚至在相当一段时期里无关紧要，只要我们能叫那吸引力攫住他。他与作者两人自会把其余的事办成：我们的职分只是传达与信任。孩子们除了靠着日日受家中一切美善、可亲、庄严之物的吸引，还能靠什么领受温柔教养那不可磨灭的印记呢？同在家里一样，在阅读里，养成一种内在的良知以拒恶择善，也必是一个渐进的过程。因为杰作的特性就在于此：它们不但把你举升到——

===Original===
> despise low joys, low Gains;
> Disdain whatever Cornbury disdains:

they are not only as Lamb wrote of the Plays of Shakespeare “enrichers of the fancy, strengtheners of virtue, a withdrawing from all selfish and mercenary thoughts, a lesson of all sweet and honourable thoughts and actions, to teach you courtesy, benignity, generosity, humanity”; but they raise your gorge to defend you from swallowing the fifth-rate, the sham, the fraudulent. *Abeunt studia in mores.* I cannot, for my part, conceive a man who has once incorporated the “Phaedo” or the *Paradiso* or *Lear* in himself as lending himself for a moment to one or other of the follies plastered in these late stern times upon the firm and most solid purpose of this nation—the inanities, let us say, of a Baby-Week. Or, for a more damnable instance, I think of you and me with Marvell’s great Horatian Ode sunk in our minds, standing today by the statue of Charles I that looks down Whitehall: telling ourselves of “that memorable scene” before the Banqueting House, remembering amid old woes all the glory of our blood and state, recollecting what is due even to ourselves, standing on the greatest site of our capital, and turning to see it degraded, as it has been for a week, to a vulgar raree-show. Gentlemen, I could read you many poor ill-written letters from mothers whose sons have died for England, to prove to you we have not deserved *that*, or the sort of placard with which London has been plastered,

> Dum domus Æneae Capitoli immobile saxum
> Accolet.

Great enterprises (as we know) and little minds go ill together. Someone veiled the statue. That, at least, was well done.

===Chinese===
> 鄙弃低下的欢愉，低下的所得；
> 凡康伯里（Cornbury）所鄙弃的，一并鄙弃：

它们不只是如查尔斯·兰姆（Lamb）论莎士比亚的戏剧时所说，是「想象力的增富者，德性的增强者，引人撤离一切自私与市侩的念头，是一切甜美而可敬的思想与行动的一课，教你谦恭、仁善、慷慨、人道」；它们还会叫你喉咙发紧，护住你，不容你咽下那五流的、假冒的、欺诈的货色。*Abeunt studia in mores.*（研习化入品性。）就我而论，我无法设想一个已经把《斐多篇》（Phaedo）、《天堂篇》（Paradiso）或《李尔王》（Lear）吸纳于自身的人，会有一刻俯就这些严酷日子里涂抹在这个国家坚定而最坚实的宗旨之上的种种愚行——譬如说，「婴儿周」（Baby-Week）那类无聊之举。或者，举一个更其可憎的例子：我想象你我心头沉潜着马弗尔（Marvell）那首伟大的贺拉斯体颂歌，今日站在查理一世（Charles I）那尊俯瞰白厅（Whitehall）的雕像旁：对自己复述宴会厅（Banqueting House）前「那难忘的一幕」，在旧日的哀恸中记起我们血脉与身分的一切荣光，想起甚至对我们自己也负有的一份本分，站在我们首都最重大的一片基址上，却一转身，眼看它一连一个星期被贬作一处庸俗的拉洋片杂耍。诸位先生，我可以读给你们听许多母亲写来的、笔拙字劣的信——她们的儿子已为英格兰捐躯——向你们证明我们不配受*这个*，也不配受伦敦满城张贴的那种招贴，

> *Dum domus Æneae Capitoli immobile saxum*
> *Accolet.*（只要埃涅阿斯的后裔还安居卡皮托利山上那不移的磐石，）

伟大的事业（人所共知）与猥琐的心胸是合不拢的。有人给雕像蒙了布。这件事至少做得好。

===Original===
I have not the information—nor do I want it—to make even a guess who was responsible for this particular outrage. I know the sort of man well enough to venture that he never had a liberal education, and, further, that he is probably rather proud of it. But he may nevertheless own some instinct of primitive kindliness: and I wish he could know how he afflicts men of sensitiveness who have sons at the War.

III

Secondly, let us consider what use we can make of even one selected classic. I refer you back to the work of an old schoolmaster, quoted in my first lecture:

> I believe, if the truth were known, men would be astonished at the small amount of learning with which a high degree of culture is compatible. In a moment of enthusiasm I ventured once to tell my “English set” that if they could really master the ninth book of *Paradise Lost*, so as to rise to the height of its great argument and incorporate all its beauties in themselves, they would at one blow, by virtue of that alone, become highly cultivated men. … More and more various learning might raise them to the same height by different paths, but could hardly raise them higher.

I beg your attention for the exact words: “to rise to the height of its great argument and *incorporate all its beauties in themselves*.” There you have it—“to incorporate.” Do you remember that saying of Wordsworth’s, casually dropped in conversation, but preserved for us by Hazlitt?—“It is in the highest degree unphilosophic to call language or diction the dress of our thoughts. … It is the *incarnation* of our thoughts.” Even so, I maintain to you, the first business of a learner in literature is to get complete hold of some undeniable masterpiece and incorporate it, incarnate it. And, I repeat, there are a few great works for you to choose from: works approved for you by ancient and catholic judgment.

===Chinese===
这一桩冒犯该由谁负责，我没有消息——也不想要消息——去作哪怕一个猜测。这一类人我见识得够多，敢断言：他从未受过自由教育（liberal education）；而且，他多半还颇为以此自负。但他身上或许仍有某种原始善心的本能：我但愿他能知道，他叫那些儿子正在战地的敏感之士受着怎样的熬煎。

III

第二，让我们想一想：哪怕只选定一部经典，我们又能拿它派上怎样的用场。请你们回到我第一讲里引过的一位老教师的那段文字：

> 我相信，倘若如实道来，人们会惊讶于：高度的文化修养竟能与何等少量的学问相容。我曾在一时兴起之际斗胆对我的「英文班」说：倘若他们能真正精通《失乐园》（*Paradise Lost*）第九卷，得以升抵其宏大立意的高度，并把它的全部美吸纳于自身，那么单凭这一点，他们便一举成为高度有修养的人。……再多、再博杂的学问，或许能经由不同的路径把他们带到同一高度，却几乎无法把他们抬得更高。

请你们留意那些确切的字眼：「得以升抵其宏大立意的高度，并把*它的全部美吸纳于自身*（incorporate all its beauties in themselves）。」要领就在这里——「吸纳于自身」（to incorporate）。你们还记得华兹华斯（Wordsworth）那句话么？那是谈话间随手说出、却由赫兹里特（Hazlitt）为我们保存下来的：「把语言或措辞称作我们思想的衣裳，是极不哲学的说法。……它是我们思想的*道成肉身*（incarnation）。」正是如此，我要向你们主张：文学学习者的头一桩正业，就是完完全全地把握某一部无可争议的杰作，把它吸纳于自身，让它化身于己。而且我再说一遍，可供你们拣选的伟大作品为数寥寥：都是经古老而普遍的公论为你们认可的作品。

===Original===
IV

But let us take something far simpler than the Ninth Book of *Paradise Lost* and more direct than any translated masterpiece can be in its appeal; something of high genius, written in our mother tongue. Let us take *The Tempest*.

Of *The Tempest* we may say confidently:

1. that it is a literary masterpiece: the last most perfect “fruit of the noblest tree in our English Forest”;

2. that its story is quite simple; intelligible to a child: (its basis in fact is fairytale, pure and simple—as I tried to show in a previous lecture);

===Chinese===
IV

但让我们取一件比《失乐园》第九卷远为简单的东西，一件比任何译作所能有的感染都更直接的东西；一件出诸高妙天才、以我们母语写成的作品。让我们取《暴风雨》（The Tempest）。

关于《暴风雨》，我们可以满有把握地说：

1. 它是一部文学杰作：是我们英国林中那棵最高贵的树上最后、最完美的一枚「果实」；

2. 它的故事十分简单，儿童也看得懂（它的事实根据纯然是童话——这一点我在以前的某一讲里试曾表明）；

===Original===
3. that in reading it—or in reading *Hamlet*, for that matter—the child has no sense at all of being patronised, of being “written down to.” And this has the strongest bearing on my argument. The great authors, as Emerson says, never condescend. Shakespeare himself speaks to a slip of a boy, and that boy feels that he *is* Ferdinand;

4. that, though Shakespeare uses his loftiest, most accomplished and, in a sense, his most difficult language: a way of talking it has cost him a lifetime to acquire, in line upon line inviting the scholar’s, prosodist’s, poet’s most careful study; that language is no bar to the child’s enjoyment: but rather casts about the whole play an aura of magnificence which, with the assistant harmonies, doubles and redoubles the spell. A child no more resents this because it is strange than he objects to read in a fairytale of robbers concealed in oil-jars or of diamonds big as a roc’s egg. When will our educators see that what a child depends on is imagination, that what he demands of life is the wonderful, the glittering, possibility?

===Chinese===
3. 在阅读它的时候——其实读《哈姆雷特》（Hamlet）也是一样——儿童全然没有受到居高俯就之感，没有「被写了一本降格的书来应付」之感。这一点与我的论证关系最切。伟大的作者，如爱默生（Emerson）所说，从不屈尊俯就。莎士比亚亲自对一个细弱的小男孩说话，那男孩便觉得自己*就是*腓迪南（Ferdinand）；

4. 诚然，莎士比亚用的是他最高华、最精纯、就某种意义说也最艰难的语言：一种花了他一辈子工夫才修得的说话方式，一行又一行，招惹着学者、音律家与诗人作最仔细的钻研；但这种语言并不妨碍儿童的欣赏，反倒给整出戏笼上一层壮丽的光晕，那光晕辅以和声的助力，把魔力加倍、又再加倍。儿童并不因为这语言陌生而嫌恶它，正如他不反对在童话里读到藏在油瓮里的强盗，或大如大鹏之蛋的钻石。我们的教育家几时才肯看到：儿童所倚仗的是想象力；他要求于人生的，是那神奇的、闪闪发光的可能性？

===Original===
Now if, putting all this together and taking confidence from it, we boldly launch a child upon *The Tempest* we shall come sooner or later upon passages that *we* have arrived at finding difficult. We shall come, for example, to the Masque of Iris, which Iris, invoking Ceres, thus opens:

> Ceres, most bounteous lady, thy rich leas
> Of wheat, rye, barley, vetches, oats and peas;
> Thy turfy mountains, where live nibbling sheep,
> And flat meads thatched with stover, them to keep:
> Thy banks with pionèd and twillèd brims,
> Which spongy April at thy hest betrims—
> To make cold nymphs chaste crowns; and thy broom-groves,
> Whose shadow the dismissèd bachelor loves,
> Being lass-lorn; thy pole-clipt vineyard;
> And thy sea-marge, sterile and rocky hard,
> Where thou thyself dost air—the Queen o’ th’ sky,
> Whose watry arch and messenger am I,
> Bids thee leave these. …

===Chinese===
那么，倘若我们把以上几点合在一处，并从中壮起胆来，放胆让一个孩子驶入《暴风雨》，那么或早或晚，我们总会撞上一些连*我们自己*也是到了如今才觉得困难的段落。比如，我们会读到伊里斯（Iris）的假面剧——伊里斯召请刻瑞斯（Ceres）时这样开场：

> 刻瑞斯，最丰厚的女神，你那丰沃的草场，
> 种着小麦、黑麦、大麦、巢菜、燕麦和豌豆；
> 你那生草的山峦，绵羊在那里啃牧，
> 你那铺着干草的平川，供它们栖宿：
> 你那蓬生枝条、织纹交错的河岸，
> 松软的四月奉你之命为它整饰——
> 好给清冷的仙女编贞洁的花冠；还有你那金雀花的树丛，
> 它的浓荫为失了姑娘的单身汉所爱；
> 你那剪去梢头的葡萄园；
> 还有你那海边的边地，硗瘠而多石坚硬，
> 你亲自在那里吐纳游息——那天空的女王，
> 我是她的水光拱门与信使，
> 她吩咐你离开这些。……

===Original===
The passage is undeniably hard for any child, even when you have paused to explain who Ceres is, who Iris, who the Queen o’ the sky, and what Iris means by calling herself “her watery arch and messenger.” The grammatical structure not only stands on its head but maintains that posture for an extravagant while. Naturally (or rather let us say, ordinarily) it would run, “Ceres, the Queen o’ the sky bids thee leave—thy rich leas, etc.” But, the lines being twelve-and-a-half in number, we get no hint of there being any grammatical subject until it bursts on us in the second half of line eleven, while the two main verbs and the object of one of them yet linger to be exploded in the last half-line, “Bids thee leave these.” And this again is as nothing to the difficulties of interpretation. “Dismissèd bachelor” may be easy; “pole-clipt vineyard” is certainly not, at first sight. “To make cold nymphs chaste crowns.” What cold nymphs? You have to wait for another fifty odd lines before being quite sure that Shakespeare means Naiads (and “What are Naiads?” says the child)—“temperate nymphs”:

> You nymphs, called Naiads, of the windring brooks,
> With your sedged crowns …

—and if the child demand what is meant by “pionèd and twillèd brims,” you have to answer him that nobody knows.

===Chinese===
这一段对任何儿童都无可讳言地难，纵使你已经停下来解释过刻瑞斯是谁、伊里斯是谁、天空的女王是谁，以及伊里斯自称「她的水拱与信使」是什么意思。这语法结构不但是拿大顶，而且把那个姿势维持了一个奢侈漫长的工夫。照自然（不如说照平常）的语序，本该说：「刻瑞斯，天空的女王吩咐你离开——你丰沃的草场，等等。」可这一段共计十二行半，我们直到第十一行的后半才蓦地撞见那个语法上的主语；而那两个主要动词，连同其中之一的宾语，还要再拖，直到最后半行「吩咐你离开这些」才炸了出来。而这一点比起解释上的种种难处，又什么都不是。「失了姑娘的单身汉」或许容易；「剪去梢头的葡萄园」初看之下却必定不易。「好给清冷的仙女编贞洁的花冠」。什么清冷的仙女？你得再等五十来行，才能确知莎士比亚指的是那伊阿得斯（Naiads）（那孩子要问了：「那伊阿得斯是什么呀？」）——「温文的仙女」：

> 你们这些绕曲溪流间的仙女，称作那伊阿得斯的，
> 头戴苇草之冠……

——而倘若孩子追问「蓬生枝条、织纹交错的河沿」是什么意思，你只好回答他：谁也不知道。

===Original===
These difficulties—perhaps for you, certainly for the young reader or listener—are reserved delights. My old schoolmaster even indulges this suspicion—“I never can persuade myself that Shakespeare would have passed high in a Civil Service Examination on one of his own plays.” At any rate you don’t *begin* with these difficulties: you don’t (or I hope you don’t) read the notes first: since, as Bacon puts it, “Studies teach not their own use.”

As for the child, he is not “*grubbing* for beauties”; he magnificently ignores what he cannot for the moment understand, being intent on *What Is*, the heart and secret of the adventure. He *is* Ferdinand (I repeat) and the isle is “full of voices.” If these voices were all intelligible, why then, as Browning would say, “the less Island it.”

===Chinese===
这些难处——对你们或许是，对年幼的读者或听者则必定是——是珍藏的乐事。我那位老教师甚至纵容这样一个疑心：「我从来无法说服自己相信：莎士比亚拿他自己的一个戏去考文官考试，能得高分。」无论如何，你不是从这些难处*开始*的：你不是（我希望你不是）先读注释：因为如培根（Bacon）所说，「学问并不教人它们自己的用处」。

至于那孩子，他并不是在*刨挖*美点；他气派十足地不理会自己眼下无法懂得的东西，一心贯注在*所是之物*（What Is）上，贯注在这场奇遇的心脏与秘密上。他*就是*腓迪南（我再说一遍），那座海岛「满处是声音」。倘若那些声音全都明白可解，那么，照布朗宁（Browning）会说的，「岛也就不那么成其为岛了」。

===Original===
V

I have purposely exhibited *The Tempest* at its least tractable. Who will deny that *as a whole* it can be made intelligible even to very young children by the simple process of reading it with them intelligently? or that the mysteries such a reading leaves unexplained are of the sort to fascinate a child’s mind and allure it? But if this be granted, I have established my contention that the Humanities should not be treated as a mere crown and ornament of education; that they should inform every part of it, from the beginning, in every school of the realm: that whether a child have more education or less education, what he has can be, and should be, a “liberal education” throughout.

Matthew Arnold, as everyone knows, used to preach the use of these masterpieces as prophylactics of taste. I would I could make you feel that they are even more necessary to us.

The reason why?—The reason is that every child born in these Islands is born into a democracy which, apart from home affairs, stands committed to a high responsibility for the future welfare and good governance of Europe. For three centuries or so it has held rule over vast stretches of the earth’s surface and many millions of strange peoples: while its obligations towards the general civilisation of Europe, if not intermittent, have been tightened or relaxed, now here, now there, by policy, by commerce, by dynastic alliances, by sudden revulsions or sympathies. But this War will leave us bound to Europe as we never have been: and, whether we like it or not, no less inextricably bound to foe than to friend. Therefore, I say, it has become important, and in a far higher degree than it ever was before the War, that our countrymen grow up with a sense of what I may call the *soul* of Europe. And nowhere but in literature (which is “memorable speech”)—or at any rate, nowhere so well as in literature—can they find this sense.

===Chinese===
V

我是特意把《暴风雨》最不驯服的一面陈给诸位看的。谁能否认：*就整体而言*，只消用「带着理解陪他们朗读」这么一个简单的法子，便能叫它连十分年幼的儿童也明白？谁又能否认：这样的朗读所留下未加解释的种种奥秘，恰是那种能迷住童心、又勾引它的奥秘？但倘若这一点获蒙承认，我便立住了我的论点：人文学科不该被当作教育单纯的冠冕与饰物；它们应当贯注于教育的每一部分，从起头便然，在这国土的每一所学校里都然：一个儿童所受的教育无论多些、少些，他所受的都能够、也应当彻头彻尾是一种「自由教育」（liberal education）。

马修·阿诺德（Matthew Arnold），众所周知，向来宣讲要把这些杰作充作品味的预防剂。我但愿能叫你们感到：对我们，它们甚至是更加必需的。

缘故何在？——缘故在于：生在这些岛上的每一个儿童，都生在一个民主国家里；这个国家除内政之外，还对欧洲未来的福祉与良治负有重大的责任。三百年来，它统治着地球表面广袤的地域和数以百万计的陌生民族；而它对欧洲整个文明所负的义务，虽非时断时续，却或紧或弛，此处彼处不定，随政策、随商务、随王朝联姻、随骤然的反感或同情而变。但这一场战争将使我们与欧洲结成前所未有之缚：并且，无论我们喜欢与否，我们对敌人也结着丝毫不亚于对朋友的、解不开的缚。所以我要说：我们的国人应当怀着一种我姑且称之为欧洲*灵魂*（soul）的意识长大成人——这件事如今已变得重要，其程度远过于战前的任何时代。而除了文学（文学是「令人难忘的言辞」（memorable speech））——或者无论如何，在任何地方都不如在文学里——他们再无处可以寻得这种意识。

===Original===
VI

There was, as we have seen, a time in Europe, extending over many centuries, when mankind dwelt under the preoccupation of making literature, and still making more of it. The 5th century BC in Athens was such a time; and if you will you may envy, as we all admire, the men of an age when to write at all was tantamount to asserting genius; the men who, in Newman’s words, “deserve to be Classics, both because of what they do and because they can do it.” If you envy—while you envy—at least remember that these things often paid their price; that the “Phaedo,” for example, was bought for us by the death of Socrates. Pass Athens and come to Alexandria: still men are accumulating books and the material for books; threshing out the Classics into commentaries and grammars, garnering books in great libraries.

There follows an age which interrupts this hive-like labour with sudden and insensate destruction. German tribes from the north, Turkish from the east, break in upon the granaries and send up literature in flames; the Christian Fathers from Tertullian to Gregory the Great (I regret to say) either heartily assisting or at least warming their benedictory hands at the blaze: and so thoroughly they do their work that even the writings of Aristotle, the Philosopher, must wait for centuries as “things silently gone out of mind or things violently destroyed” (to borrow Wordsworth’s fine phrase) and creep back into Europe bit by bit, under cover of Arabic translations.

===Chinese===
VI

我们方才已看到：欧洲曾有一段绵延许多世纪的光阴，人类在其间一心所系便是制作文学，而且还要制作得更多。公元前 5 世纪的雅典便是这样一个时代；你们若愿意，尽可以去羡慕——我们也无不赞叹——那个时代的人：生当彼时，只要动笔写作，就无异于宣称自己是天才；用纽曼（Newman）的话说，那些人「配得上称为经典，既因为他们所做的，也因为他们能做」。你们若羡慕——趁着还在羡慕——至少要记得：这些东西往往付出过它们的代价；譬如那篇《斐多篇》，就是以苏格拉底（Socrates）之死为我们换来的。别过雅典，来到亚历山大里亚（Alexandria）：人们仍在积累书籍与成书的材料；把经典打碾扬簸，做成注疏与文法书，把书籍收进宏大的图书馆。

接下来是一个时代，以突然而无知的毁灭打断了这种蜂房般的劳作。北边的日耳曼部落、东边的突厥部落，闯进谷仓，放起火来，把文学付与烈焰；而基督教的教父们，从德尔图良（Tertullian）到大格列高利（Gregory the Great）（说来令人遗憾），或由衷襄助，或至少在那片火光前烤着他们那双祝福的手：他们把这件事做得如此干净，以至连「那位哲学家」亚里士多德（Aristotle）的著作，也得以「无声地淡出记忆之物、或被暴力摧毁之物」的身分（借华兹华斯的名句）等上几个世纪，再借着阿拉伯文译本的掩蔽，一点一点爬回欧洲。

===Original===
The scholars set to work and begin rebuilding: patient, indefatigable, anonymous as the coral insects at work on a Pacific atoll—building, building, until on the near side of the gulf we call the Dark Age, islets of scholarship lift themselves above the waters: mere specks at first, but ridges appear and connect them: and, to first seeming, sterile enough:

> Nec Cereri opportuna seges, nec commoda Baccho—

but as they join and become a terra firma, a thin soil gathers on them God knows whence: and, God knows whence, the seed is brought, “it may chance of wheat, or of some other grain.” There is a price, again, for this resurrection: but how nobly, how blithely paid you may learn, without seeking recondite examples, from Cuthbert’s famous letter describing the death of Bede. Compare that story with that of the last conversation of Socrates; and you will surely recognise that the two men are brothers born out of time; that Bede’s work has been a legacy; that his life has been given to recreating—not scholarship merely nor literature merely—but, through them both, something above them both—the soul of Europe. And this may or may not lead you on to reflect that beyond our present passions, and beyond this War, in a common sanity Europe (and America with her) will have to discover that common soul again.

But eminent spirits such as Bede’s are, by their very eminence, less representative of the process—essentially fugitive and self-abnegatory—than the thousands of copyists who have left no name behind them. Let me read you a short paragraph from *The Cambridge History of English Literature*, Chapter 11, written, the other day, by one of our own teachers:

===Chinese===
学者们动手做工，开始重建：坚忍，不倦，无名，一如在太平洋一座环礁上做工的珊瑚虫——筑造，筑造，直到在我们称之为黑暗时代的这道海湾的近侧，学术的小洲一一浮出水面：起初不过几点斑痕，随后礁脊显现，把它们连缀起来：而乍看之下，也贫瘠得够呛：

> *Nec Cereri opportuna seges, nec commoda Baccho—*（既无宜于刻瑞斯的田畴，也无便于巴克科斯的园地，）

可一旦它们连成一片坚实的大陆，一层薄薄的土壤便聚集在上面——天知道从何而来；而种子也天知道从何而来被带到了那里，「或是麦子，或是别的谷子」。这场复活又一次是有代价的：但代价偿付得何等高贵、何等欣然，你们不必搜寻冷僻的例证，从库斯伯特（Cuthbert）那封记述比德（Bede）之死的著名书信里便可知道。把那个故事同苏格拉底最后谈话的故事对读，你们定会认出：这两个人是超越时代而生的兄弟；比德的工作是一笔遗产；他的一生献给了再造——不单是学术，也不单是文学——而是经由这两者，再造了高于两者的某物——欧洲的灵魂。这件事或许会、或许不会引你们进一步去想：在我们今日的种种激情之外，在这场战争之外，在一个共同的清明之中，欧洲（美国与她同行）终须重新寻回那个共同的灵魂。

但比德这样卓异的灵魂，正因其卓异，反倒不如那千千万万身后不曾留名的抄写者更能代表这一过程——这过程本质上倏忽无形、舍己忘我。让我给你们读一小段文字，出自《剑桥英国文学史》（The Cambridge History of English Literature）第 11 章，是不久前我们自己的某位教师写的：

===Original===
> The cloister was the centre of life in the monastery, and in the cloister was the workshop of the patient scribe. It is hard to realise that the fair and seemly handwriting of these manuscripts was executed by fingers which, on winter days, when the wind howled through the cloisters, must have been numbed by icy cold. It is true that, occasionally, little carrels or studies in the recesses of the windows were screened off from the main walk of the cloister, and, sometimes, a small room or cell would be partitioned off for the use of a single scribe. The room would then be called the Scriptorium, but it is unlikely that any save the oldest and most learned of the community were afforded this luxury. In these scriptoria of various kinds the earliest annals and chronicles in the English language were penned, in the beautiful and painstaking forms in which we know them.

If you seek testimony, here are the *ipsissima verba* of a poor monk of Wessobrunn endorsed upon his MS.:

> The book which you now see was written in the outer seats of the cloister. While I wrote I froze: and what I could not write by the beams of day I finished by candlelight.

===Chinese===
> 回廊是修道院生活的中心，而回廊里便是坚忍的抄经人的工场。很难想象，这些抄本上秀雅端正的字迹，是出于那样一些手指：冬日里，寒风在回廊间呼啸，那手指必已冻得麻木。诚然，偶尔也有窗龛深处隔屏出来的小读经座或小书斋，与回廊的主道隔开；有时也隔出一个小房间或斗室，供单独一位抄写者使用。这样的房间便叫作缮写室（Scriptorium），但除了会众里最年长、最有学问的少数人，恐怕谁也享受不到这份奢华。在各种这样的缮写室里，英文最早的编年史与史传被一笔一划写下，写成我们今日所见那美丽而煞费苦心的模样。

你们若要见证，这里便是韦索布伦（Wessobrunn）一位贫穷修士写在他抄本末尾的亲笔原话（*ipsissima verba*）：

> 你们如今看见的这部书，写在回廊外端的座席上。我一面写，一面冻僵：白天光线下写不完的，我便就着烛光写完。

===Original===
We might profitably spend—but today cannot spare—a while upon the pains these men of the Middle Ages took to accumulate books and to keep them. The chained volumes in old libraries, for example, might give us a text for this as well as start us speculating why it is that, to this day, the human conscience incurably declines to include books with other portable property covered by the Eighth Commandment. Or we might follow several of the early scholars and humanists in their passionate chasings across Europe, in and out of obscure monasteries, to recover the lost MSS. of the classics: might tell, for instance, of Pope Nicholas V, whose birth-name was Tommaso Parentucelli, and how he rescued the MSS. from Constantinople and founded the Vatican Library: or of Aurispa of Sicily who collected two hundred and thirty-eight for Florence: or the story of the *editio princeps* of the Greek text of Homer. Or we might dwell on the awaking of our literature, and the trend given to it, by men of the Italian and French renaissance; or on the residence of Erasmus here, in this university, with its results.

===Chinese===
关于这些中古人搜求书籍、保存书籍所费的苦心，我们本可以——只是今天腾不出工夫——花上一段光阴，大有裨益。比如，老图书馆里那些用锁链锁住的书册，既可就此给我们出一道讲题，也可引我们去玄想：何以直到今天，人类的良心总无可救药地不肯把书籍归入第八诫所涵盖的其他可携带财产之内。或者，我们不妨追随几位早期的学者与人文主义者，踏遍欧洲、出入于湮没无闻的修道院之间，热烈地追逐那失落的古典抄本：可以讲一讲，比如教皇尼古拉五世（Nicholas V）——他的本名叫托马索·帕伦图切利（Tommaso Parentucelli）——怎样把抄本从君士坦丁堡（Constantinople）抢救出来，又创立了梵蒂冈图书馆（Vatican Library）；或讲西西里的奥里斯帕（Aurispa），如何为佛罗伦萨（Florence）收得二百三十八部；或讲荷马（Homer）希腊文本初版（*editio princeps*）的故事。或者，我们也可以细说意大利与法兰西文艺复兴的诸人怎样唤醒了我们的文学、又给了它怎样的趋向；或者细说伊拉斯谟（Erasmus）客居此地、在这座大学里的居留及其成果。

===Original===
VII

But I have said enough to make it clear that, as we owe so much of our best to understanding Europe, so the need to understand Europe lies urgently today upon large classes in this country; and that yet, in the nature of things, these classes can never enjoy such leisure as our forefathers enjoyed to understand what I call the soul of Europe, or at least to misunderstand it *upon acquaintance*.

Let me point out further that within the last few months we have doubled the difficulty at a stroke by sharing the government of our country with women and admitting them to Parliament. It beseems a great nation to take great risks: to dare them is at once a sign and a property of greatness: and for good or ill—but for limitless good as we trust—our country has quietly made this enterprise amid the preoccupations of the greatest War in its annals. Look at it as you will—let other generations judge it as they will—it stands a monument of our faith in free self-government that in these most perilous days we gave and took so high a guerdon of trust in one another.

But clearly it implies that all the women of this country, down to the small girls entering our elementary schools, must be taught a great many things their mothers and grandmothers—happy in their generation—were content not to know.15

===Chinese===
VII

但我已说得够多，足以表明：既然我们最好的东西大半归功于对欧洲的理解，那么今日，理解欧洲的需要便急迫地落在这个国家的广大阶层身上；然而按事物的本性，这些阶层永远享受不到我们祖先那样的闲暇，去理解我所称的欧洲灵魂，或者至少在*熟识之后*（upon acquaintance）去误解它。

我还要进一步指出：就在最近几个月里，我们让妇女参预我们国家的治理、准许她们进入议会，一举把这个难度翻了一倍。伟大的民族冒大险才相称：敢于去冒，这既是大者的表征，也是大者的本性；是好是坏——但我们相信是无限的好——我们的国家已在它编年史上最大一场战争的诸务萦绕之中，不动声色地办成了这桩事业。你们尽可随己意看它——也任后世各代随己意评断它——它立在那里，是我们信奉自由自治的一座纪念碑：值此最危殆的日子，我们彼此交出、又彼此领受了那样高的一份信任的酬报。

但这分明意味着：这个国家的全部妇女，直到刚跨进我们小学门槛的小女孩，都必须受教于许许多多的事情——她们的母亲与祖母们（在她们那一代里有福了）安于不知的事情。15

===Original===
It cannot be denied, I think, that in the long course of this War, now happily on the point of a victorious conclusion, we have suffered heavily through past neglect and present nescience of our literature, which is so much more European, so much more catholic, a thing than either our politics or our national religion: that largely by reason of this neglect and this nescience our statesmen have again and again failed to foresee how continental nations would act through failing to understand their minds; and have almost invariably, through this lack of sympathetic understanding, failed to interpret us to foreign friend or foe, even when (and it was not often) they interpreted us to ourselves. I note that America—a country with no comparable separate tradition of literature—has customarily chosen men distinguished by the grace of letters for ambassadors to the Court of St. James—Motley, Lowell, Hay, Page, in our time: and has for her President a man of letters—and a professor at that!—whereas, even in these critical days, Great Britain, having a most noble cause and at least half-a-hundred writers and speakers capable of presenting it with dignity and so clearly that no neutral nation could mistake its logic, has by preference entrusted it to stunt journalists and film-artistes. If in these later days you have lacked a voice to interpret you in the great accent of a Chatham, the cause lies in past indifference to that literary tradition which is by no means the least among the glories of our birth and state.

===Chinese===
我想不能否认：在这场如今幸而临近胜利结局的战争的漫长进程中，我们因昔时对我国文学的忽视、因今日对我国文学的无知，吃了大亏——而比起我们的政治或我们的国教来，我国文学这东西要欧洲得多、也普遍（catholic）得多：大抵正因这种忽视与这种无知，我们的政治家们因为不解大陆各国的心灵，一次又一次未能预见它们将如何行动；又因缺乏这种同情的理解，几乎无一例外地，未能把我们解释给国外的朋友或敌人——甚至在（这并不多见的）他们把我们解释给我们自己的时候。我注意到，美国——一个并无相应独立文学传统的国家——向来拣选以文采著称的人出任驻圣詹姆斯宫（Court of St. James）的大使：就在我们这个时代，有莫特利（Motley）、洛厄尔（Lowell）、海（Hay）、佩奇（Page）：而她现任的总统便是一位文人——而且还是一位教授！反观英国，明明手握一项最高贵的事业，又有不下五六十位作家与演说家，满能把这事业庄严地陈述出来，又清晰得叫任何中立国都不致错认其逻辑，却在连这些危急的日子里，偏情愿把它托付给专耍噱头的新闻记者和电影艺人。近来的日子里，你们若苦于没有一副嗓音，用查塔姆（Chatham）那样的大声调替你们说话，那缘故就在昔日对那文学传统的漠不关心——而这传统，在我们血统与身分的一切荣光之中，绝不是最不足道的一项。

===Original===
VIII

Masterpieces, then, will serve us as prophylactics of taste, even from childhood; and will help us, further, to interpret the common mind of civilisation. But they have a third and yet nobler use. They teach us to lift our own souls.

For witness to this and to the way of it I am going to call an old writer for whom, be it whim or not, I have an almost 18th century reverence—Longinus. No one exactly knows who he was; although it is usual to identify him with that Longinus who philosophised in the court of the Queen Zenobia and was by her, in her downfall, handed over with her other counsellors to be executed by Aurelian: though again, as is usual, certain bold bad men affirm that, whether he was this Longinus or not, the treatise of which I speak was not written by any Longinus at all but by someone with a different name, with which they are unacquainted. Be this as it may, somebody wrote the treatise and its first editor, Francis Robertello of Basle, in 1554 called him Dionysius Longinus; and so shall I, and have done with it, careless that other MSS. than that used by Robertello speak of Dionysius or Longinus.
