Dionysius Longinus, then, in the 3rd century AD—some say in the 1st: it is no great matter—wrote a little book *ΠΕΡΙΨΠΣΟΥΣ* commonly cited as *Longinus on the Sublime*. The title is handy, but quite misleading, unless you remember that by “Sublimity” Longinus meant, as he expressly defines it, “a certain distinction and excellence in speech.” The book, thus recovered, had great authority with critics of the 17th and 18th centuries. For the last hundred years it has quite undeservedly gone out of vogue.

===Chinese===
VIII

可见，杰作可以充当我们品味的预防剂，从童年起便然；还能进一步帮我们解释文明的共同心灵。但它们还有第三种、也更高贵的用处。它们教我们举起自己的灵魂。

为给这一点、也给它的途径作见证，我要请出一位老作家——朗吉努斯（Longinus）；不论这算不算一时的偏爱，我对他怀着一种近乎 18 世纪式的崇敬。没有人确切知道他是谁；人们通常把他认作那位在芝诺比娅（Zenobia）女王宫廷里谈哲论道的朗吉努斯——女王败亡之际，把他连同她的其他谋臣一并交出，由奥勒良（Aurelian）处死。不过，又照例有某些胆大而促狭的先生们断言：且不论他是不是这位朗吉努斯，我所说的这篇论著压根不是任何一个朗吉努斯写的，而是出自另一个名字的人，至于那名字，他们自己也无从知晓。无论如何，总有人写了这篇论著；它的首位刊刻者、巴塞尔（Basle）的弗朗西斯·罗伯泰洛（Francis Robertello）于 1554 年称他作狄奥尼修斯·朗吉努斯（Dionysius Longinus）；我也就如此称呼，就此打住，至于罗伯泰洛所用抄本之外的其他抄本或言狄奥尼修斯、或言朗吉努斯，我一概不去理会。于是，狄奥尼修斯·朗吉努斯，于公元 3 世纪——有人说在公元 1 世纪：无关宏旨——写了一本小书 *ΠΕΡΙΨΠΣΟΥΣ*（《论崇高》，Peri Hypsous），通称《朗吉努斯论崇高》（Longinus on the Sublime）。这书名用起来顺手，却颇有误导，除非你记得：朗吉努斯所谓的「崇高」（Sublimity），照他自己明白的界定，是「言语中某种卓越与优美」。此书经这样重新发见之后，在 17、18 世纪的批评家中间享有极大权威。近一百年来，它却全然不该地失了时兴。

===Original===
It is (I admit) a puzzling book, though quite clear in argument and language: pellucidly clear, but here and there strangely modern, even hauntingly modern, if the phrase may be allowed. You find yourself rubbing your eyes over a passage more like Matthew Arnold than something of the 3rd century: or you come without warning on a few lines of “comparative criticism,” as we call it—an illustration from Genesis—“God said, Let there be Light, and there was Light” used for a specimen of the exalted way of saying things. Generally, you have a sense that this author’s lineage is mysterious after the fashion of Melchisedek’s.

Well, to our point—Longinus finds that the conditions of lofty utterance are five: of which the first is by far the most important. And this foremost condition is innate: you either have it or you have not. Here it is:

> Elsewhere, [says Longinus,] I have written as follows: “*Sublimity is the echo of a great soul.*” Hence even a bare idea sometimes, by itself and without a spoken word will excite admiration, just because of the greatness of soul implied. Thus the silence of Ajax in the underworld is great and more sublime than words.

===Chinese===
这本书（我承认）令人困惑，虽然论证与语言都极为清明：清澈见底，却时时处处出奇地现代——容我这么说的话——甚至现代得萦绕不去。你会发觉自己对某一段落直揉眼睛——它更像马修·阿诺德，而不像 3 世纪的东西：或者你会毫无预警地撞上几行我们今日所谓「比较批评」（comparative criticism）——一个取自《创世记》的例证——「神说，要有光，就有了光」——被拿来作崇高言说之法的标本。总的说来，你会有一种感觉：这位作者的世系，神秘得一如麦基洗德（Melchisedek）的世系。

好，回到正题——朗吉努斯认定，崇高言辞的条件有五：其中第一条远远最为紧要。而这居首的条件是天赋的：你或有，或没有。它是这样：

> 在别处，[朗吉努斯说，]我曾写下这样一句话：「*崇高是伟大灵魂的回声。*」因此，有时甚至连一个赤裸的观念，不着一字，单凭自身也能激起叹赏，只因它暗含着灵魂的伟大。例如，埃阿斯（Ajax）在冥府中的沉默，便伟大而比言语更崇高。

===Original===
You remember the passage, how Odysseus meets that great spirit among the shades and would placate it, would “make up” their quarrel on earth now, with carneying words:

> “Ajax, son of noble Telamon, wilt thou not then, even in death forget thine anger against me over that cursed armour. … Nay, there is none other to blame but Zeus: he laid thy doom on thee. Nay, come hither, O my lord, and hear me and master thine indignation.”
> So I spake, but he answered me not a word, but strode from me into the Darkness, following the others of the dead that be departed.

Longinus goes on:

> It is by all means necessary to point this out—that the truly eloquent must be free from base and ignoble (or ill-bred) thoughts. For it is not possible that men who live their lives with mean and servile aims and ideas should produce what is admirable and worthy of immortality. Great accents we expect to fall from the lips of those whose thoughts are dignified.

===Chinese===
你们记得那一段故事：奥德修斯（Odysseus）在亡魂中遇见那伟大的英灵，想安抚它，想用讨好讨饶的言语，把两人当年在人间的争端如今「和解」掉：

> 「高贵的忒拉蒙（Telamon）之子埃阿斯，难道你到了死里，还不肯为我那副该咒诅的铠甲息怒么。……不，怪只该怪宙斯（Zeus）：是他把厄运加在你身上。来吧，我的君上，听我一言，制住你的忿怒。」
> 我这样说了，他却一字不答，径自离开我，步入幽暗，随着其他死去的亡魂去了。

朗吉努斯接着写道：

> 无论如何必须指明这一点：真正雄辩之士，必得免于卑下与不肖（或曰缺乏教养）的念头。因为凡终生怀着鄙吝奴性的目的与观念而生活的人，绝不可能产出令人叹赏、配得不朽的东西。我们指望伟岸的语句，落自思想庄严者的唇边。

===Original===
Believe this and it surely follows, as concave implies convex, that by daily converse and association with these great ones we take their breeding, their manners, earn their magnanimity, make ours their gifts of courtesy, unselfishness, mansuetude, high seated pride, scorn of pettiness, wholesome plentiful jovial laughter.

> He that of such a height hath built his mind,
> And rear’d the dwelling of his soul so strong
> As neither fear nor hope can shake the frame
> Of his resolvèd powers, nor all the wind
> Of vanity or malice pierce to wrong
> His settled peace, or to disturb the same;
> What a fair seat hath he, from whence he may
> The boundless wastes and wilds of man survey!
>
> And with how free an eye doth he look down
> Upon these lower regions of turmoil!
> Where all the storms of passions mainly beat
> On flesh and blood; where honour, power, renown,
> Are only gay afflictions, golden toil;
> Where greatness stands upon as feeble feet
> As frailty doth; and only great doth seem
> To little minds, who do it so esteem. …
>
> Knowing the heart of man is set to be
> The centre of this world, about the which
> These revolutions of disturbances
> Still roll; where all th’ aspects of misery
> Predominate; whose strong effects are such
> As he must bear, being powerless to redress;
> And that, unless above himself he can
> Erect himself, how poor a thing is man!16

===Chinese===
相信这一点，便必然随之推出——一如凹面预设着凸面——通过与这些伟大者日日交谈、相与往还，我们承得他们的教养与风范，赚得他们的弘毅大度，把他们的馈赠化为我们自己的：谦恭、无私、温良，高踞的自尊，对褊琐的鄙夷，还有那有益而丰盈的快活笑声。

> 谁把心智筑到这样的高度，
> 把灵魂的居所砌得这般坚固，
> 以致恐惧与希望都摇不动那构架，
> 摇不动他决断了的诸般力量，
> 虚荣或恶意的风，也刺不进去
> 错伤他安顿的安宁，或把它搅扰；
> 他的座位何其佳美！从那里他可将
> 人类无边的荒土旷野尽收眼底！
>
> 而他俯视下面这片纷扰的低域，
> 目光又是何其自由！
> 那里，激情的一切风暴
> 主要敲打在血肉之上；那里，尊荣、权势、名声，
> 只是鲜艳的苦难、镀金的苦役；
> 那里，伟大所站的脚，同脆弱
> 一样虚软；而显得伟大的，
> 只在小的心目之中，因为是他们在这样看重它。……
>
> 他深知人心被安置为
> 这个世界的中心，纷扰的诸般轮转
> 绕着它不住滚转；
> 那里，惨境的一切形相
> 居临一切；其强横的后果，人无力匡救，
> 只得承受；而且知道，除非人能
> 在自己之上竖起自己，
> 人是何等贫乏的一件东西！16

===Original===
IX

If the exhortation of these verses be somewhat too high and stoical for you, let me return to Longinus and read you, from his concluding chapter, a passage you may find not inapposite to these times, nor without a moral:

> “It remains” [he says] “to clear up, my dear Terentianus, a question which a certain philosopher has recently mooted. I wonder,” he says, “as no doubt do many others, how it happens that in our time there are men who have the gift of persuasion to the utmost extent, and are well fitted for public life, and are keen and ready, and particularly rich in all the charms of language, yet there no longer arise really lofty and transcendent natures unless it be quite peradventure. So great and worldwide a dearth of high utterance attends our age. Can it be,” he continued, “we are to accept the common cant that democracy is the nursing mother of genius, and that great men of letters flourish and die with it? For freedom, they say, has the power to cherish and encourage magnanimous minds, and with it is disseminated eager mutual rivalry and the emulous thirst to excel. Moreover, by the prizes open under a popular government, the mental faculties of orators are perpetually practised and whetted, and as it were, rubbed bright, so that they shine free as the state itself. Whereas today,” he went on, “we seem to have learnt as an infant-lesson that servitude is the law of life; being all wrapped, while our thoughts are yet young and tender, in observances and customs as in swaddling clothes, bound without access to that fairest and most fertile source of man’s speech (I mean Freedom) so that we are turned out in no other guise than that of servile flatterers. And servitude (it has been well said) though it be even righteous, is the cage of the soul and a public prison-house.”
> But I answered him thus.—“It is easy, my good sir, and characteristic of human nature, to gird at the age in which one lives. Yet consider whether it may not be true that it is less the world’s peace that ruins noble nature than this war illimitable which holds our aspirations in its fist, and occupies our age with passions as with troops that utterly plunder and harry it. The love of money and the love of pleasure enslave us, or rather, as one may say, drown us body and soul in their depths. For vast and unchecked wealth marches with lust of pleasure for comrade, and when one opens the gate of house or city, the other at once enters and abides. And in time these two build nests in the hearts of men, and quickly rear a progeny only too legitimate: and the ruin within the man is gradually consummated as the sublimities of his soul wither away and fade, and in ecstatic contemplation of our mortal parts we omit to exalt, and come to neglect in nonchalance, that within us which is immortal.”

===Chinese===
IX

这几节诗的劝勉，若于你们未免太高亢、太斯多噶了些，那么让我回到朗吉努斯，从他结尾的一章里读一段给你们听：你们或许会觉得，它与当世并非不切，也并非不含一课教训：

> 「还剩一件事要澄清，」[他说，]「我亲爱的特伦提安努斯（Terentianus）：那是某位哲学家近来提出的一个问题。我不明白，」他说，「想必许多别人也不明白，何以我们这个时代，有这样一些人：他们劝服的本领达于极境，极宜于公共生活，敏锐干练，语言的一切魅力尤其丰赡，可是真正崇高超迈的天性却不再兴起，除非全凭偶然。我们这个时代，竟伴着这样一种遍及世界的崇高言辞的荒歉。这会不会是，」他接着说，「要我们接受那句滥调，说民主是天才的乳母，伟大的文人随民主共盛衰、同生死？因为，他们说，自由有涵养、激励恢弘心灵的力量，随着自由，热切的相互竞逐与争胜的渴望也播散开来。再者，平民政府之下奖励之门大开，演说家们的心智官能恒得练习、常被磨砺，可以说磨得锃亮，照得同国家本身一般自由明亮。而今天，」他继续说，「我们仿佛自幼课中学得了：奴役是生活的法则；当我们的思想还幼嫩的时候，就被种种仪轨习俗像襁褓一样裹起，被捆缚着，无从亲近人类言辞那至美至丰的泉源（我指自由），以致我们最终被造就的模样，除了奴颜的谄谀者再无其他。而奴役（这话说得好），纵使是公义的奴役，也是灵魂的牢笼、公共的监狱。」
> 但我这样回答他。——「非难自己身处的时代，我的好先生，是容易的，也是人性的常态。然而请想一想：败坏高贵天性的，与其说是世界的太平，不如说是这场无休无止的战争——它把我们的志向攥在拳心，又用种种激情占据我们的时代，如同用兵卒把时代劫掠蹂躏净尽。爱财与爱乐奴役着我们；或者不如说，可以这样说，把我们连身体带灵魂淹溺在它们的深处。因为庞大而不受节制的财富，与纵乐之欲结伴进军；一个开了家门或城门，另一个便随即进来驻下。久而久之，这两位便在人的心里筑起巢来，很快养出一窝太也名正言顺的后嗣：人内的毁坏便渐次完成，因为他灵魂中的崇高凋萎褪色；我们出神凝视自身必朽的部分，却忘了高举，进而漫不经心地怠慢了我们里面那不朽的东西。」

===Original===
I had a friend once who, being in doubt with what picture to decorate the chimneypiece in his library, cast away choice and wrote up two Greek words—*ΨΥΧΗΣ ἸΑΤΡΕΙΟΝ*; that is, the hospital—the healing-place—of the soul.

===Chinese===
我从前有一位朋友，拿不定该用一幅什么画来装点书房的壁炉架，索性撇开挑选，在架上题了两个希腊字——*ΨΥΧΗΣ ἸΑΤΡΕΙΟΝ*；意思是：灵魂的医所——灵魂的疗治之地。

===Chinese===
1. 请读者费心翻回本章开头，留意本篇讲演起首标着的那行日期。那时，我正同一个我认为彻头彻尾败坏的英文教学体系论战。那个体系后来已让位于另一个体系；倘若要我为后者辩护，我是准备说它好一些的。 ↩︎

===Original===
2. Do you remember, by the by, Samuel Rogers's lines on Lady Jane Grey? They have always seemed to me very beautiful:

 Like her most gentle, most unfortunate,

 Crown'd but to die—who in her chamber sate

 Musing with Plato, though the horn was blown,

 And every ear and every heart was won,

 And all in green array were chasing down the sun!

 ↩︎

===Chinese===
2. 顺带问一句：你可还记得塞缪尔·罗杰斯（Samuel Rogers）咏简·格雷夫人（Lady Jane Grey）的那几行诗？它们在我眼里从来都是极美的：

 一如她那般的至为温婉、至为不幸，

 加冕只为赴死——她独坐于闺房之中，

 与柏拉图一同沉思，尽管猎号已经吹响，

 人人侧耳，颗颗心都被猎事夺走，

 满眼绿衣猎装，正追逐着西沉的太阳！

 ↩︎

===Original===
3. *The Odyssey*, Book VI, lines 81–86. ↩︎

4. The reference given is *Zeitschr. f. Ethnologie*, XIX 30 ff. ↩︎

===Chinese===
3. 《奥德赛》（*The Odyssey*），Book VI, lines 81–86。 ↩︎

4. 所给出的出处是 *Zeitschr. f. Ethnologie*，XIX，第 30 页及以下。 ↩︎

===Original===
5. Why had he to swear this under pain of excommunication, when the lecturer could so easily keep a roll-call? But the amount of oath-taking in a medieval university was prodigious. Even college servants were put on oath for their duties: Gyps invited their own damnation, bed-makers kissed the book. Abroad, where examinations were held, the Examiner swore not to take a bribe, the Candidate neither to give one, nor, if unsuccessful, to take his vengeance on the Examiner with a knife or other sharp instrument. At New College, Oxford, the matriculating undergraduate was required to swear in particular not to dance in the College Chapel. ↩︎

===Chinese===
5. 既然主讲人本可轻而易举地点名查数，他何须冒着被逐出教门的惩戒立此誓言？不过，中世纪大学里立誓之繁多，实在骇人。连学院的仆役都要为各自的职守宣誓：杂役（gyps）立誓守职，无异于自请咒诅，铺床的婢女也要吻经为誓。在国外那些举行考试的地方，主考官宣誓不受贿赂；考生宣誓不行贿，并且倘若落第，也不得用刀子或其他利器向主考官寻仇。在牛津的新学院（New College, Oxford），注册入学的本科生还须特别宣誓：绝不在学院的礼拜堂里跳舞。 ↩︎

===Original===
6. Donne's "Sermon II preached at Pauls upon Christmas Day, in the Evening." 1624. ↩︎

===Original===
14. The loose and tautologous style of this Preface is worth noting. Likely enough Browne wrote it in a passion that deprived him of his habitual self-command. One phrase alone reveals the true Browne—that is, Browne true to himself: "and time that brings other things to light, should have satisfied me in the remedy of its oblivion. ↩︎

===Chinese===
14. 这篇序言松散而叠床架屋的文体，值得注意。布朗（Browne）多半是在一阵激情之中写下它的，那阵激情剥夺了他惯常的自制。唯独有一个短语，显露了真实的布朗——即忠于自己的布朗：「而时间既使别的事物重见天日，本该让我在补救它的湮没这件事上得到满足。 ↩︎

===Original===
15. Well! … my education is at last finished: indeed it would be strange, if, after five years' hard application, anything were left incomplete. Happily that is all over now; and I have nothing to do, but to exercise my various accomplishments.

 Let me see!—as to French, I am mistress of that, and speak it, if possible, with more fluency than English. Italian I can read with ease, and pronounce very well: as well at least, and better, than any of my friends; and that is all one need wish for in Italian. Music I have learned till I am perfectly sick of it. But … it will be delightful to play when we have company. I must still continue to practise a little;—the only thing, I think, that I need now to improve myself in. And then there are my Italian songs! which everybody allows I sing with taste, and as it is what so few people can pretend to, I am particularly glad that I can.

 My drawings are universally admired; especially the shells and flowers; which are beautiful, certainly; besides this, I have a decided taste in all kinds of fancy ornaments.

 And then my dancing and waltzing! in which our master himself owned that he could take me no further! just the figure for it certainly; it would be unpardonable if I did not excel.

 As to common things, geography, and history, and poetry, and philosophy, thank my stars, I have got through them all! so that I may consider myself not only perfectly accomplished, but also thoroughly well-informed.

 Well, to be sure, how much have I fagged through—; the only wonder is that one head can contain it all.

===Chinese===
15. 「好了！……我的教育总算完成了：确实，倘若五年刻苦用功之后，还有什么没有学全，那才怪呢。幸好如今一切都已经过去；我再没有什么事可做，只消把我的种种才艺一一施展出来。

 「让我想想！——至于法文，我已然精通，说起来即便说不上比英文更流利，也不相上下。意大利文我能毫不费力地阅读，发音也极好：至少同我任何一位朋友一样好，甚至更好；而意大利文求到这个地步，也就尽够了。音乐我一直学到腻烦欲呕。不过……家里有客的时候，弹奏起来总是件赏心乐事。我还得继续稍微练一练；——我想，这是如今我唯一还需要精进的东西。再说，还有我的意大利歌曲！人人都承认我唱得有情有味；既然能以此自许的人寥寥无几，我居然能唱，自然格外欢喜。

 「我的画人人称赏；尤其是那些贝壳和花卉；那当然是美的；此外，对于各样花哨的装饰小品，我也有一种断然不俗的品味。

 「再看我跳舞和跳华尔兹！连我们的教师本人也承认，他已经再没法教我什么了！我这身段确然再合适不过；倘若跳不出众，那才不可饶恕。

 「至于那些寻常科目——地理、历史、诗歌、哲学——谢天谢地，我全都修完了！这样，我不但可以自视为才艺完足，而且算得上学识淹博了。

 「唉，可不是么，我苦熬着学完了多少东西——；唯一的稀奇事是，一颗脑袋竟能装得下这许多。

===Original===
 I found this in a little book *Thoughts of Divines and Philosophers*, selected by Basil Montagu. The quotation is signed "J. T." I cannot trace it, but suspect Jane Taylor. ↩︎

===Chinese===
 我这段文字，抄自巴兹尔·蒙塔古（Basil Montagu）选编的一本小书《神学家与哲学家文思选》（*Thoughts of Divines and Philosophers*）。引文署名「J. T.」。我无从查考此人为谁，但疑心是简·泰勒（Jane Taylor）。 ↩︎

===Original===
16. Samuel Daniel, *Epistle to the Lady Margaret, Countess of Cumberland*. ↩︎

===Chinese===
16. 塞缪尔·丹尼尔（Samuel Daniel）：《致坎伯兰伯爵夫人玛格丽特夫人的书简》（*Epistle to the Lady Margaret, Countess of Cumberland*）。 ↩︎
